> [!definition] **The-Fluency-Trap|Fluency Illusion / The-Fluency-Metacognition-Paradox|Fluency-Metacognition Paradox** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** The phenomenon by which smooth, effortless processing of surface code and propositional text base generates subjective confidence in comprehension quality that does not accurately reflect the quality of the situation model constructed. Boundary condition: the illusion operates most powerfully when texts are linguistically familiar even when situationally novel — when the reader can parse the sentences easily while lacking the prior knowledge to integrate them into a coherent situation model.
>

The definition-of-competence dimension distinguishes whether the referent for competence evaluation is absolute/intrapersonal (mastery) or normative/interpersonal (performance). The valence dimension distinguishes whether the motivati...
>

> [!definition] **2×2 Achievement Goal Framework** *[needs-note]*
> The 2×2 framework organizes achievement goals along two orthogonal dimensions: (1) the *definition* dimension (mastery vs. performance — the competence standard used) and (2) the *valence* dimension (approach vs. avoidance — whether positive or negative possibilities regulate behavior). The resulting four goals are: **Mastery-Approach** (pursuing positive mastery outcomes — learning, development), **Mastery-Avoidance** (avoiding negative mastery outcomes — incompetence, failure to develop), **Performance-Approach** (pursuing positive performance outcomes — outperforming others, demonstratin...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[2×2-Achievement-Goal-Framework|2×2 Achievement Goal Framework]]*

> [!definition] **2×2 Framework** *[needs-note]*
> A theoretical model of achievement goals that crosses two dimensions — goal content (mastery vs. performance) with motivational valence (approach vs. avoidance) — to yield four distinct goal types: mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance. Currently the dominant framework in the field, though contested regarding the adaptive potential of performance-approach goals.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[2×2-Framework|2×2 Framework]]*

> An umbrella term for four related theoretical movements challenging Classical Cognitivism: Embodied cognition (the body shapes cognitive processes constitutively, not merely causally), Embedded cognition (cognitive systems are deeply adapted to and exploit environmental structures), Enacted cognition (cognition is brought forth through sensorimotor coupling, not representation of a pre-given world), and Extended cognition (cognitive processes can literally extend into the environment). The four Es are related but distinct: a theorist can accept embodied and embedded cognition without accept...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*

## _A

> [!definition] **Abduction** **[perm-note]**
> The logical form of inference that generates a hypothesis to explain a surprising observation. The abductive syllogism: if *H* were true, *C* would be expected; *C* is observed and surprising; therefore *H* is a conjecture worth investigating. Abduction is non-deductive (the conclusion does not follow necessarily), non-inductive (it does not generalize from observed frequencies), and generative (it produces genuinely new hypotheses). Synonymous in some contexts with *retroduction* (Peirce's earlier term) and with *inference to the best explanation* (the analytic philosophy reconstruction). ...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[abduction]]*

> [!definition] **Abductive Competence** **[perm-note]**
> The cluster of cognitive, epistemic, and motivational capacities that support effective abductive reasoning. These include: sensitivity to anomaly (noticing when observed data does not fit existing expectations); explanatory imagination (the ability to generate multiple candidate hypotheses); plausibility assessment (the ability to evaluate which hypotheses are more or less likely to explain the data given background knowledge); and tolerance for provisional commitment (the willingness to act on a hypothesis while holding it as revisable). Abductive competence is developed, not innate — it ...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[abductive-competence|Abductive Competence]]*

> [!definition] **Abductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> The logical form of inference that moves from a surprising or anomalous observation to the hypothesis that would, if true, render that observation intelligible or expected. Abduction does NOT establish that the hypothesis is true — only that it is worth investigating. It is the logic of hypothesis *generation*, not hypothesis *confirmation*. It is sometimes called *retroduction* in Peirce's earlier writings, and its more recent analytic reconstruction is known as [[inference-to-the-best-explanation|Inference to the Best Explanation]].
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[abductive-reasoning|Abductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **The Abductive Syllogism** **[perm-note]**
> Peirce's canonical form of the abductive syllogism:
> - The surprising fact *C* is observed.
> - But if *H* were true, *C* would be a matter of course.
> - Therefore, there is reason to suspect that *H* is true.
> This form is explicitly non-deductive: the conclusion does not follow necessarily from the premises. The warrant for the inference is not logical compulsion but *explanatory fit*. Importantly, *H* must be genuinely explanatory — not merely a restatement of *C*, and not an arbitrary conjecture, but a hypothesis that *makes* *C* intelligible.
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-abductive-syllogism|The Abductive Syllogism]]*

> [!definition] **Habit** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A learned disposition to repeat past responses in stable contexts. Habits are triggered by contextual cues that were associated with the behavior during prior repetitions, operate through non-declarative memory systems, and are relatively independent of current goal states and motivational conditions. What habits are NOT: deliberate choices, intention-driven actions, or behaviors that require ongoing motivational support to execute.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The definition's emphasis on contextual cues and goal-independence is the analytical key. Most popular habit ...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[habit]]*

> [!definition] **Habit Devaluation Paradigm** **[perm-note]**
> An experimental procedure in which the reward value associated with a habitual behavior is reduced *after* the habit is established, allowing researchers to test whether the behavior persists (indicating habit) or ceases (indicating goal-directedness). The persistence of behavior after devaluation is the operational criterion for habit in the behavioral neuroscience literature.
> 
> **See also:** [[cortico-striatal-learning-loops]], [[behavioral-modification]]
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[habit-devaluation-paradigm|Habit Devaluation Paradigm]]*

> [!definition] **Accessibility Heuristic** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>The accessibility heuristic</span> is the metacognitive strategy of basing monitoring judgments on the amount and quality of partial information that comes to mind during a retrieval attempt. More partial information → higher FOK → higher confidence that the full answer could be retrieved under different conditions (e.g., with better cues or on a recognition test).
> 
> [**Accessibility-Heuristic**:: The metacognitive strategy of inferring memory strength from the amount and quality of partial information available during retrieval — more accessible partial informa...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[accessibility-heuristic|Accessibility Heuristic]]*

> [!definition] **Accommodation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which an existing schema is modified to incorporate information that cannot be successfully assimilated. Accommodation involves structural change to the schema — adding slots, adjusting default values, splitting a schema into subcategories, or merging previously distinct schemas.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Accommodation presupposes a schema available for modification. Pure accommodation is *revision* of an existing structure. It cannot explain the creation of a schema where none existed before.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[schema-accommodation]], [[Assimilation-vs....
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[accommodation]]*

> [!definition] **Accretion** **[perm-note]**
> The most common and least disruptive mode of schema change. New information is added as *instances* or facts within the existing schema framework, without modifying the schema's structure. The schema's variables, slots, constraints, and default values remain unchanged; only the content instantiated in those slots is updated.
> 
> **Example:** Encountering a new restaurant (Thai food, $15 average price, 30-minute wait) adds an instance to the RESTAURANT schema without changing what a restaurant IS. The person now has more data points for what "a restaurant" involves, but the abstract schema is u...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[accretion]]*

> [!definition] **Accretion Disk** **[perm-note]**
> A flat, rotating structure of gas and dust orbiting a central protostar, formed when infalling material with angular momentum settles into Keplerian orbits rather than falling directly onto the stellar surface. The disk serves as a conduit for mass transfer onto the star, a launching platform for bipolar outflows, and the birthplace of planetary systems.
> 
> **Boundary:** Protostellar accretion disks are distinct from accretion disks around compact objects (black holes, neutron stars), which operate at much higher temperatures and energies.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The accretion disk...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[accretion-disk|Accretion Disk]]*

> [!definition] **Accretion, Tuning, and Restructuring** *[needs-note]*
> **Accretion** is the addition of new information to an existing schema without modifying the schema's fundamental structure — new facts or examples are stored as instances of the schema, filling its slots with new specific values. Most day-to-day learning is accretion: we learn that Abraham Lincoln was 6'4" without revising our schema for presidents or for historical figures. **Tuning** is the gradual refinement of a schema's parameters through experience — the slot values, default settings, and variable constraints are adjusted to fit the evidence more accurately. A novice chess player who...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Accretion,-Tuning,-and-Restructuring|Accretion, Tuning, and Restructuring]]*

> [!definition] **Accretion, Tuning, Restructuring** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition**: The three mechanisms of schema change. Accretion adds information to an existing schema without modifying its structure. Tuning adjusts variable constraints or default values. Restructuring creates qualitatively new schema organizations when existing schemas fail. After this re-examination: the restructuring mechanism is dramatically underdescribed by the term itself; genuine restructuring is better captured by conceptual change theory's revolutionary account.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The taxonomy that exposes the internal tension within schema theory — the mechanis...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Accretion,-Tuning,-Restructuring|Accretion, Tuning, Restructuring]]*

> [!definition] **Accuracy Constraint** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that elaborative interrogation benefits depend on the accuracy of the generated why-answers. Inaccurate elaborations, when generated with confidence, create interference: the incorrect causal account may be encoded alongside or instead of the target fact, producing distorted memory rather than enhanced retention.
> 
> **Boundary:** The accuracy constraint does not require that every generated answer be textbook-perfect; it requires that answers not be systematically misleading. A partially accurate why-answer (correct in mechanism, approximate in magnitude) typically still produce...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[accuracy-constraint|Accuracy Constraint]]*

> [!definition] **Achievement Goal** **[perm-note]**
> An achievement goal is a cognitive representation of the purpose a person is pursuing in an achievement context. It specifies what "success" means to the person, which in turn determines what counts as a competent performance, which tasks feel threatening or inviting, and which behaviours are rational responses to difficulty. Achievement goals are not merely preferences or wishes; they are organising frameworks that structure perception, attention, affect, and effort. The concept should not be confused with specific performance targets (e.g., "I want to score 90 on this test") — AGT goals a...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[achievement-goal|Achievement Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Achievement Goal Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework distinguishing between mastery goals (oriented toward developing competence) and performance goals (oriented toward demonstrating competence relative to others). Elliot's 2×2 extension adds approach-avoidance valence, yielding four goal types: mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, performance-avoidance. Extensive empirical evidence associates mastery-approach orientation with deeper learning, greater persistence, and more adaptive responses to failure.
> 
> **Boundary:** The mastery-performance distinction is not absolute; individuals may hold multiple goal orie...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation and self-regulation*
> *Permanent Note: [[achievement-goal-theory|Achievement Goal Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Tacit Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Knowledge that is genuine but resists full verbal articulation. Michael Polanyi's formulation: "we know more than we can tell." Paradigm cases include skilled motor performance (bicycle riding, instrument playing), expert perceptual discrimination (wine tasting, medical diagnosis), and domain-specific judgment that cannot be reduced to explicit rules. Tacit knowledge challenges any account of understanding that makes verbal explanation the criterion, since it demonstrates that understanding can be functionally robust — accurate, generative, transferable — without being propositionally artic...
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[tacit-knowledge|Tacit Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Action Phase Model** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework partitioning goal pursuit into four sequential phases — predecisional deliberation, postdecisional preplanning, actional execution, and postactional evaluation — each governed by a distinct mind-set and posing different regulatory demands. Implementation intentions are situated theoretically at the boundary between the second and third phases, where preplanning produces the cognitive structures that subsequently govern enactment.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Action Phase Model is a *functional* partition, not a claim that distinct neural systems implement each phase; the pha...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[action-phase-model|Action Phase Model]]*

> [!definition] **Activation** **[perm-note]**
> The retrieval of a relevant schema from long-term memory into an available state for use in ongoing comprehension or problem-solving, triggered by features of the incoming stimulus that match the schema's conditions of application.
> *See also*: [[spreading-activation]], [[prior-knowledge]], [[schema-theory]]
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[activation]]*

> [!definition] **Active Externalism** **[perm-note]**
> Clark and Chalmers's term for their own position: the view that the *vehicles* of cognitive states — the physical processes constituting cognition, not merely the contents they carry — can extend beyond the boundary of brain and body. Distinguished from passive externalism (content externalism) by its claim that the world plays an active, vehicle-level role in cognitive processes, not merely a background role in determining representational content.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[active-externalism|Active Externalism]]*

> [!definition] **Active Listening** **[perm-note]**
> A deliberate, effortful communicative practice involving sustained attention to a speaker's verbal and nonverbal signals, metacognitive monitoring of one's own interpretive biases, and explicit demonstration of comprehension through paraphrase and checking. Contrasts with pseudo-listening (appearing to listen while allocating cognitive resources elsewhere). See [[metacognition]], [[self-monitoring]], [[reflective-practice]].
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[active-listening|Active Listening]]*

> [!definition] **Active Word-Noticing Disposition** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A metacognitive stance toward reading in which the reader deliberately maintains attention to unfamiliar lexical items, attempts semantic inference from context, flags items for follow-up study, and monitors for vocabulary gaps rather than tolerating them as comprehension noise. Distinguished from passive reading by the deliberate allocation of attentional resources to word-level processing alongside passage-level comprehension. Not natural or automatic; a learnable disposition that requires explicit metacognitive development.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Identified in...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[active-word-noticing-disposition|Active Word-Noticing Disposition]]*

> [!definition] **Actively Open-Minded Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> A dispositional orientation toward thinking characterized by active seeking of evidence against one's current beliefs, willingness to revise positions in response to evidence, and resistance to premature closure. Activated by the Private Generative Space condition of silent reading; suppressed by social performance pressure and identity threat.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[actively-open-minded-thinking|Actively Open-Minded Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Actor-Observer Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The **actor-observer asymmetry** (Jones & Nisbett, 1971) is the tendency for actors — people performing a behavior — to attribute their own actions to situational factors, and for observers of the identical behavior to attribute it to the actor's dispositions. The asymmetry is theorized to reflect differential attentional focus: actors attend to the situational context from the inside; observers attend to the person who is perceptually salient against the situational background. Contemporary meta-analyses have identified this asymmetry as more reliable for negative behaviors and less univer...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[actor-observer-asymmetry|Actor-Observer Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive Expertise** **[perm-note]**
> A form of expertise that combines mastery of routine cases with the metacognitive monitoring and analytical capacity needed to recognize and reason productively about novel cases that fall outside the standard chunk store.
> 
> **Boundary:** Adaptive expertise requires the same chunking architecture as routine expertise, plus an additional layer of metacognitive monitoring that is not produced by ordinary deliberate practice and must be cultivated through training that explicitly includes non-routine cases.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Adaptive expertise is the path beyond the standard fa...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-expertise|Adaptive Expertise]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive Help-Seeking** **[perm-note]**
> The pursuit of help that enables continued independent work — a hint, a worked example, a conceptual framework, a clarifying question — as opposed to *executive help-seeking*, which seeks the answer itself in order to bypass the learning entirely. Adaptive help-seeking is one of the cleanest behavioral markers of mastery-approach orientation.
> 
> **Boundary:** Distinct from *executive help-seeking* (which substitutes external solution for internal learning) and from *help-avoidance* (the systematic non-seeking of available help, characteristic of performance-avoidance orientation).
> 
> **Report-S...
> 
> *Source: goal-orientation-theory-mastery-vs-performance-goals-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-help-seeking|Adaptive Help-Seeking]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive Help-Seeking vs. Avoidant Help-Seeking** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Adaptive help-seeking is the strategic use of assistance to acquire the understanding needed to achieve mastery — asking for explanation, requesting feedback, seeking clarification. Avoidant help-seeking is the avoidance of situations requiring assistance or the solicitation of answers rather than explanations, motivated by the desire to conceal incompetence.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The distinction operationalizes one of the three core mechanisms through which PAV goals suppress adaptive learning behavior. PAV learners are not less likely to need help; they are les...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-help-seeking-vs.-avoidant-help-seeking|Adaptive Help-Seeking vs. Avoidant Help-Seeking]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive Inference** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The output of a successful Self-Reaction process: a specific, strategy-level adjustment to the Forethought Phase architecture for the next cycle. **Boundary Conditions:** Distinct from vague general resolutions ("try harder next time"); must specify what will be done differently and why the identified attribution suggests this adjustment. **Report-Specific Significance:** The adaptive inference is the mechanism through which SRL develops over time — not through global willpower but through specific strategic refinements accumulated across cycles. **Operational Indicators:** ...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)* | *Domain: self-regulated-learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-inference|Adaptive Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive Self-Reaction** **[perm-note]**
> A response to self-evaluation that modifies the SRL system in ways likely to improve future performance: revising strategy selection, adjusting goal level, seeking additional background knowledge, changing environmental conditions, or sustaining a productive approach.
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all changes in response to Self-Reflection are adaptive. Lowering goals to match current performance (rather than raising performance to meet goals) is a change, but a defensive rather than an adaptive one. Adaptive reactions are characterised by *upward* regulatory pressure.
> 
> **See also:** [[The-SRL-Cycle-a...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-self-reaction|Adaptive Self-Reaction]]*

> [!definition] **Adaptive vs. Defensive Inference** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The two possible outcomes of the Self-Reaction subsystem. *Adaptive inference* leads the learner to adjust, persist, and re-engage with appropriate modifications. *Defensive inference* leads the learner to protect self-esteem through withdrawal, lowered standards, or avoidance — at the cost of learning and self-efficacy.
> **Operational Indicators:** Defensive inference can be disguised as wisdom ("I'm not ready for this yet") or self-care ("I need to come back to this when I'm better prepared"). The diagnostic question: does the inference generate a *specific, controllable ac...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[adaptive-vs.-defensive-inference|Adaptive vs. Defensive Inference]]*

> [!definition] **The Additivity Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> The claim that intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load are additive and jointly bounded by working memory capacity: IL + EL + GL ≤ WM Capacity. Kalyuga (2011) challenges this by arguing that germane and intrinsic load are not independently variable.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-additivity-assumption|The Additivity Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Advance Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> A pre-reading cognitive scaffold that provides an abstract, inclusive framework into which subsequent more specific content can be assimilated. Advance organizers activate relevant prior knowledge and provide an incomplete structural frame that incoming content elaborates.
> 
> **Boundary:** An advance organizer is not simply a summary of what follows (which would be a "forward organizer") but a more abstract, structural framework that provides organizing principles rather than content.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** An effective advance organizer leaves the reader with a sense of the shape of the...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[advance-organizer|Advance Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Advance-Organizer|Advance Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> Introductory material presented before a learning task that provides an abstract, conceptual framework into which the incoming information can be assimilated. In reading, previewing abstract, headings, and conclusions functions as an advance organizer by pre-activating relevant schema and reducing inference load during primary reading.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[advance-organizeradvance-organizer|Advance-Organizer|Advance Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Affective Intelligence Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework proposing that discrete emotional states — particularly the distinction between enthusiasm/aversion (disposition system) and anxiety (surveillance system) — have differential effects on political reasoning style. The central claim is that anxiety, unlike anger or enthusiasm, activates a surveillance mode that promotes deliberative information-seeking and reduces reliance on partisan heuristics. The theory directly challenges the assumption that emotional political engagement is ipso facto irrational.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[affective-intelligence-theory|Affective Intelligence Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Amotivating Events** **[perm-note]**
> [**Amotivating-Events-Definition**:: External events whose primary functional significance is to signal the person's incompetence, helplessness, or inability to achieve desired outcomes. Examples include consistently negative performance feedback, impossible task demands, demeaning evaluation, and environmental conditions that communicate futility. CET predicts that amotivating events undermine intrinsic motivation by damaging perceived competence — the felt sense of effectance — leading to resignation, withdrawal, and learned helplessness.]
> *See also: [[amotivation]], [[Learned-Helplessnes...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[amotivating-events|Amotivating Events]]*

> [!definition] **Amotivation** **[perm-note]**
> The absence of intentional motivation — behavior is not driven by any identified or intrinsic reason, or is not engaged with at all. The motivational state associated with the impersonal orientation. In OIT's motivational continuum, amotivation represents the complete absence of self-determination, contrasting with externally regulated, introjected, identified, integrated, and intrinsic motivation.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[amotivation]]*

> [!definition] **Analogical Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> Inductive inference based on structural similarity between cases: if situation A has property P and outcome X, and situation B shares the structural features relevant to P, then B is likely to also exhibit outcome X. Research by Dedre Gentner (Structure-Mapping Theory) shows that productive analogies align *relational structures* rather than surface features. Analogical reasoning underlies much expert problem-solving, pedagogical explanation, and hypothesis generation in science.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[analogical-reasoning|Analogical Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Analogical Representation** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive representation whose internal structure resembles the structure of what it represents — shares some non-trivial structural properties with its object. Mental models are held to be analogical: a model of "A is to the left of B" has A-token spatially arranged relative to B-token in a way that mirrors the described spatial relation. Contrast with propositional representations, which encode relations symbolically without structural mirroring.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[analogical-representation|Analogical Representation]]*

> [!definition] **Analysis** **[perm-note]**
> Analysis is the cognitive act of decomposing a complex claim, argument, text, or phenomenon into its component parts in order to examine their structure, their relationships, and their individual validity. In educational contexts, analysis involves identifying the explicit and implicit premises of an argument, mapping the logical relationships between claims, and distinguishing what is stated from what is inferred. Analysis is prior to evaluation — one cannot fairly assess what one has not first accurately understood.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[analysis]]*

> [!definition] **Handoff Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A structural feature of the SRL cycle document architecture that physically connects the adaptive inference output of a Self-Reflection session to the Forethought template of the next session, preventing inference evaporation. **Report-Specific Significance:** The most common failure point in SRL cycle implementation is the gap between generating an adaptive inference and actually applying it in the next Forethought Phase. The handoff mechanism closes this gap by requiring explicit capture and forward transfer. **Cross-References:** [[self-regulated-learning]], [[Implementat...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)* | *Domain: self-regulated-learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[handoff-mechanism|Handoff Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Andragogy** **[perm-note]**
> A theory of adult learning proposed by Malcolm Knowles that contrasts with pedagogy in six key assumptions: adults have a self-concept that moves from dependency toward self-direction as they mature; adults accumulate an expanding reservoir of experience that becomes an increasingly rich resource for learning; adults' readiness to learn is oriented toward the developmental tasks of their social roles; adults have a problem-centred rather than subject-centred orientation to learning; adults are internally rather than externally motivated to learn; and adults need to know why they are learnin...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[andragogy]]*

> [!definition] **Anomaly** **[perm-note]**
> An observation or datum that does not fit the expectations generated by an agent's current knowledge or background theory. Anomalies are the triggers of abductive reasoning — they create the occasion for hypothesis generation. Importantly, what counts as anomalous is theory-dependent (Hanson's *theory-ladenness of observation*): the same datum that is anomalous for one thinker may be entirely expected for another with different background knowledge. Developing sensitivity to anomaly — a richer sense of what should be surprising — is a core component of abductive competence development.
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[anomaly]]*

> [!definition] **Aporia** **[perm-note]**
> Literally, "impasse" or "without passage" (Greek: a- + poros). In Socratic philosophy, aporia is the state of productive intellectual impasse reached when a previously confident position has been shown to be inadequate, but before a replacement has been found. Aporia is not failure — in the Platonic dialogues, it is the precondition for genuine inquiry. Only someone who genuinely does not know what they thought they knew can begin to seek real understanding.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[aporia]]*

> [!definition] **TARGET Framework** **[perm-note]**
> An acronym identifying the key structural dimensions of learning environments that shape motivational climate: Task (design and complexity), Authority (distribution of decision-making power), Recognition (how achievement is acknowledged), Grouping (how learners are sorted and structured), Evaluation (how competence is assessed), and Time (how temporal pressure is structured). Used as an audit and design tool for motivational climate engineering.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[target-framework|TARGET Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Argument-Analysis** **[perm-note]**
> Structured evaluation of claims, reasons, assumptions, warrants, and implications. Relationship: Paul-Elder broadens argument analysis beyond form into purpose and perspective diagnostics.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[argument-analysis]]*

> [!definition] **Aristotle's Modes of Persuasion** *[needs-note]*
> **Logos** — the quality and force of the argument itself: the accuracy of the evidence, the validity of the inferences, the strength of the logical structure. **Ethos** — the audience's perception of the speaker's credibility, expertise, character, and good will. **Pathos** — emotional arousal that makes the audience more receptive to the speaker's conclusion. Effective persuasion typically requires all three: a technically sound argument (logos) delivered by a credible source (ethos) that engages the audience's genuine interests and values (pathos). Overreliance on any one mode at the expe...
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Aristotle's-Modes-of-Persuasion|Aristotle's Modes of Persuasion]]*

> [!definition] **Earned Security** **[perm-note]**
> [[adult-attachment-theory]] concept designating a pattern of secure adult attachment functioning achieved through reflective processing of insecure attachment history rather than through experiencing secure attachment from birth. Earned security is theoretically relevant for RMT because it suggests that internalization — the transformation from controlled to autonomous relational motivation — is possible at any developmental stage, not only in childhood. Therapeutic, romantic, and deep friendship relationships characterized by genuine autonomy-supportive responsiveness can provide the corre...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[earned-security|Earned Security]]*

> [!definition] **Articulation-as-Monitoring Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that the demand for explicit articulation in a communicable medium (written text) automatically triggers metacognitive monitoring processes that would not otherwise be engaged. The quality of written output provides "hard" metacognitive evidence — visible, persistent, and evaluable — as opposed to the "soft" evidence of internal feelings of knowing.
> 
> **Boundary:** Applies specifically to written articulation requiring genuine reformulation (knowledge transforming). Copying, summarizing without restructuring, or filling templates mechanically may not trigger the monitoring effe...
> 
> *Source: pkb-as-externalized-metacognition-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[articulation-as-monitoring-principle|Articulation-as-Monitoring Principle]]*

> [!definition] **The Articulatory Construction Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The principle, developed in the context of [[the-articulatory-construction-principle|articulatory construction theory]], that the act of formulating language — translating a mental representation into sequential, syntactically constrained prose — forces a kind of cognitive accountability that private thought does not. Thought can be vague, implicit, and logically inconsistent in ways that are invisible to the thinker. Language, which must be sequential and (to be coherent) must maintain logical consistency across sentences, exposes these gaps, forcing the writer either to resolve them or to...
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-articulatory-construction-principle|The Articulatory Construction Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Articulatory Construction Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that articulating knowledge in sequential, syntactically constrained language (as opposed to holding it in implicit mental representation) creates accountability constraints that expose logical gaps, inconsistencies, and missing connections invisible to private cognition. Writing is the primary vehicle for articulatory construction in contemporary PKB practice.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[articulatory-construction-principle|Articulatory Construction Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Articulatory Suppression** **[perm-note]**
> **Articulatory Suppression**: The concurrent production of an irrelevant spoken utterance (e.g., "the, the, the" or "blah, blah, blah") that prevents articulatory rehearsal, thereby blocking the refreshment of phonological store traces and eliminating the acoustic coding advantage for verbal material presentation. Articulatory suppression is the primary behavioral manipulation used to isolate phonological store contributions from rehearsal contributions in working memory experiments.
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[articulatory-suppression|Articulatory Suppression]]*

> [!definition] **Ease of Learning — EOL** *[needs-note]*
> An [[Ease-of-Learning|ease-of-learning]] judgment is a pre-acquisition metacognitive assessment of how difficult a particular item or body of material will be to learn. EOL judgments are based primarily on intrinsic cues (properties of the material) and prior experience with similar material, and they represent the meta-level's prediction of the effort that object-level processing will require.
> 
> **Boundary:** EOL is a prospective judgment made *before* learning, not a retrospective assessment of experienced difficulty during learning.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** EOL judgments illustr...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Ease-of-Learning-—-EOL|Ease of Learning — EOL]]*

> [!definition] **Ease-of-Learning Judgment — EOL** *[needs-note]*
> [[Ease-of-Learning-—-EOL|Ease-of-Learning judgments]] are prospective metacognitive assessments made before the acquisition of material, reflecting the meta-level's prediction about how easy or difficult the material will be to learn. EOL judgments are typically based on the meta-level's existing knowledge about similar material, the structural features of the to-be-learned items (word frequency, concreteness, inter-item associations), and the learner's prior experience with comparable tasks. EOL judgments influence the initial allocation of study time and the selection of learning strategi...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narens-metacognitive-control-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Ease-of-Learning-Judgment-—-EOL|Ease-of-Learning Judgment — EOL]]*

> [!definition] **Task Class** **[perm-note]**
> A category of learning tasks at the same level of complexity, within which all tasks address the same set of interacting elements but vary in surface features and context. Task classes are sequenced from simple to complex, with each new class introducing additional interacting elements. Within a task class, scaffolding is faded from high support (worked examples) to low support (conventional tasks).
> 
> **Boundary:** Task classes are not difficulty levels based on surface features or arbitrary grading criteria. They represent genuine differences in structural complexity defined by the number o...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[task-class|Task Class]]*

> [!definition] **Task Involvement** **[perm-note]**
> Task involvement is a motivational state in which the person's primary concern is mastering the task itself — understanding it, improving at it, making progress. In this state, the person is using their own prior performance or the inherent demands of the task as the standard of reference. Success is defined as learning, understanding, or improving, and effort is seen as the natural means of achieving these things. Task involvement is associated with deep engagement, challenge-seeking, and resilience in the face of failure.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[task-involvement|Task Involvement]]*

> [!definition] **Task Involvement vs. Ego Involvement** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** In [[john-g.-nicholls]]' original framework, task involvement and ego involvement represent two qualitatively distinct states of engagement with an achievement activity. Task involvement is a motivational state in which success is defined as mastery of the task — demonstrating learning, improvement, or competence relative to one's own prior performance. Ego involvement is a motivational state in which success is defined as demonstrating superior ability relative to others, or at minimum avoiding demonstration of inferior ability. This is NOT merely a difference in goal conte...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[task-involvement-vs.-ego-involvement|Task Involvement vs. Ego Involvement]]*

> [!definition] **Task Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The subcomponent of metacognitive knowledge concerning beliefs about how the characteristics of cognitive tasks — their structure, demands, complexity, format, and goals — influence the difficulty of processing and the strategies required for successful performance. Task knowledge includes understanding that recall is more demanding than recognition, that tasks with high element interactivity place greater demands on working memory, and that different assessment formats require different encoding strategies.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Task knowledge governs the quality of forethough...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[task-knowledge|Task Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Task Value** **[perm-note]**
> A learner's subjective evaluation of a learning task along three dimensions: intrinsic value (inherent enjoyment), utility value (perceived usefulness for future goals), and attainment value (importance for self-concept). Empirically, task value shows stronger relationships to behavioral outcomes (persistence, choice, help-seeking) than to cognitive strategy quality, suggesting it operates through a distinct motivational pathway from self-efficacy.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[task-value|Task Value]]*

> [!definition] **Aspiration Index** **[perm-note]**
> The primary psychometric instrument used to assess goal content in GCT research. Participants rate the importance of a range of goals spanning intrinsic and extrinsic domains, with researchers computing *relative* aspiration strength — the emphasis placed on extrinsic goals relative to intrinsic ones — as the primary predictor variable. Cross-culturally validated across 15+ nations (Grouzet et al., 2005).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[aspiration-index|Aspiration Index]]*

> [!definition] **Aspirational Ecology** **[perm-note]**
> **Aspirational Ecology** — A concept emerging from GCT research referring to the aggregate aspirational messages promoted by an environment (family, school, workplace, culture, digital platform). GCT research demonstrates that the aspirational ecology of an environment shapes the aspiration profiles of individuals within it, and therefore serves as a contextual determinant of well-being at the population level. See Sections 6, 8, Synthesis.
> **See also:** [[Social-Media-and-Well-being]], [[autonomy-support]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[aspirational-ecology|Aspirational Ecology]]*

> [!definition] **Assessment for Autonomy** **[perm-note]**
> Assessment for autonomy refers to the deliberate design of assessment and feedback systems whose primary purpose is to develop the learner's capacity for accurate self-evaluation, self-monitoring, and self-directed improvement, rather than primarily serving the institutional function of grading, ranking, or certification. The mechanism operates by progressively transferring the evaluative function — the capacity to judge the quality of one's own work against appropriate standards — from the assessor to the learner, mirroring the broader scaffolding-fading progression in the domain of evalua...
> 
> *Source: curriculum-design-for-lifelong-learning-and-self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: education — curriculum design*
> *Permanent Note: [[assessment-for-autonomy|Assessment for Autonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Assessment-Design** **[perm-note]**
> Construction of evaluation systems that validly capture targeted learning outcomes. Relevance: critical for avoiding rubric substitution and construct underrepresentation in critical thinking measurement.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[assessment-design]]*

> [!definition] **Assimilation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The cognitive process by which new information is interpreted through and incorporated into an existing schema without changing the schema's structure. Assimilation is schema-guided comprehension: the new instance is recognized as an example of an existing category.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Assimilation does not change the schema — it confirms it. When assimilation is the dominant process, learning is cumulative (more examples of a known category) but not structurally transformative. The schema that does the assimilating must pre-exist.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[Assimilation...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[assimilation]]*

> [!definition] **Assimilation vs. Accommodation** **[perm-note]**
> **[[assimilation]]** (schema-theoretic): New information is interpreted through the existing schema without modifying the schema's structure. Input is *slotted into* the existing framework. The schema remains unchanged; the representation of the input is shaped by the schema.
> 
> **[[schema-accommodation]]** (schema-theoretic): The schema itself is modified to better fit the input. This involves changing the schema's structure — its slots, defaults, constraints, or internal organization. Accommodation is computationally expensive, cognitively effortful, and episodically unstable (requires cons...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[assimilation-vs.-accommodation|Assimilation vs. Accommodation]]*

> [!definition] **Assimilation-Accommodation Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical regularity that assimilation (interpreting new information within existing schemas) is systematically easier, faster, and more likely than accommodation (modifying schemas in response to new information). Accommodation requires recognition of genuine anomaly, motivation to invest in restructuring, available alternative schemas, and sustained effort. The asymmetry explains why knowledge accumulation tends toward accretion by default and why deliberate instructional conditions are necessary to produce restructuring.
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[assimilation-accommodation-asymmetry|Assimilation-Accommodation Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Assumption 5: Rule/Model Dichotomy** **[perm-note]**
> The framing of reasoning research as a choice between rule-based (mental logic) accounts and model-based (mental models) accounts — treating these as mutually exclusive. This report's audit identifies this as a dissolved assumption: the dichotomy is probably a theoretical artifact rather than a genuine description of cognitive architecture.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[assumption-5-rulemodel-dichotomy|Assumption 5: Rule/Model Dichotomy]]*

> [!definition] **The Atkinson-Shiffrin Multi-Store Model** **[perm-note]**
> A structural theory of memory proposing three distinct storage systems: the sensory register (which holds precise but rapidly decaying percepts for fractions of a second), the short-term store (a limited-capacity buffer holding approximately 7±2 items for roughly 15-30 seconds without rehearsal), and the long-term store (an effectively unlimited repository of knowledge and experience). Information is thought to flow sequentially through these stores, with transfer from short-term to long-term memory mediated primarily by the duration and amount of rehearsal received in the short-term store....
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-atkinson-shiffrin-multi-store-model|The Atkinson-Shiffrin Multi-Store Model]]*

> [!definition] **Atomic Note** **[perm-note]**
> A note expressing a single, complete idea, claim, or argument at a level of granularity that permits it to be understood without requiring simultaneous reference to other notes. Atomic notes reduce element interactivity at the note level, support Zettelkasten linking practices, and minimize split-attention effects at retrieval. Distinguished from "outline notes" (which summarize a topic hierarchically) and "source notes" (which record bibliographic information from a single source).
> 
> **Boundary:** Atomicity is relative to conceptual level. A note on "photosynthesis" would not be atomic; a n...
> 
> *Attribution: 1976; popularized by Ahrens, 2017*
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[atomic-note|Atomic Note]]*

> [!definition] **Attainment Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The finding, most rigorously demonstrated by Niemiec, Ryan & Deci (2009), that attaining extrinsic goals produces little or no well-being gain and may increase ill-being, while attaining intrinsic goals produces reliable well-being gains. This finding directly challenges the folk-psychological assumption that achieving goals is generically good for well-being, revealing instead that the well-being "returns" of achievement depend on what was achieved.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[attainment-paradox|Attainment Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **The Attainment Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The **[[attainment-paradox]]** in Goal Contents Theory refers to the empirical finding that achieving extrinsic goals (wealth, fame, image) fails to produce lasting well-being benefits commensurate with the effort invested, and may in some cases be associated with *increased* ill-being indicators. The paradox is that "getting what you want" does not always make you better off — and the determining factor is whether what you wanted was intrinsically or extrinsically oriented.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-attainment-paradox|The Attainment Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **The Attention-Allocation Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The structural tension in incidental vocabulary learning in which: (a) the conditions that maximise accurate inferential processing (rich, redundant context) also allow shallow processing to succeed; and (b) the conditions that would force deep processing (sparse context) reliably produce inferential errors and comprehension failure. There is no naturalistic reading context that simultaneously forces deep, accurate semantic processing and supports adequate text comprehension — these two requirements are structurally in tension.
> 
> **Boundary:** The paradox applies to naturalistic, comprehensi...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-attention-allocation-paradox|The Attention-Allocation Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **The Hattie-Timperley Feedback Model** **[perm-note]**
> John Hattie and Helen Timperley's [[Hattie-&-Timperley-Feedback-Model]] distinguishes four levels at which feedback can be directed: (1) **Task level** — information about the correctness or quality of specific work products; (2) **Process level** — information about the processes and strategies underlying performance; (3) **Self-regulation level** — information that builds the learner's capacity for self-monitoring and self-correction; (4) **Self level** — praise or criticism directed at the person rather than their work. Research consistently shows that process-level and self-regulation-l...
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-hattie-timperley-feedback-model|The Hattie-Timperley Feedback Model]]*

> [!definition] **Attribute Substitution** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive process in which the cognitive system, faced with a difficult target question that is computationally demanding for Type 2, unconsciously substitutes an easier heuristic question that can be addressed by Type 1 pattern-matching, then delivers the answer to the heuristic question as if it were the answer to the original target question. The substitution is opaque to introspection — the agent does not experience having answered a different question, only having answered the question that was asked.
> 
> **Boundary:** Attribute substitution is not the same as ignorance or laziness. The...
> 
> *Source: dual-process-theory-kahneman-system-1-system-2-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribute-substitution|Attribute Substitution]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A causal explanation assigned by an observer (or actor) to account for an outcome — specifically, the assignment of that outcome to an internal source (properties of the person) or an external source (properties of the environment or situation). Attributions are not neutral descriptions; they carry implications about the future, about responsibility, and about appropriate responses.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The basic internal/external distinction is the foundation on which Weiner's three-dimensional model builds — but the analysis will show that locus alone is insu...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution and Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Causal attribution — the learner's account of why the outcome occurred — is the crucial cognitive event of Phase 4. Following Weiner's [[attribution-theory|attribution theory]], outcomes can be attributed to stable internal causes (ability), unstable internal causes (effort), stable external causes (task difficulty), or unstable external causes (luck). Adaptive attributional patterns, in which success is attributed to stable-internal factors (ability and strategy) and failure is attributed to unstable-internal factors (effort, strategy choice), support the maintenance of self-efficacy and u...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-model-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-and-self-regulation|Attribution and Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution Retraining** **[perm-note]**
> An intervention approach that specifically targets students' causal attributions for failure, attempting to shift them from stable/uncontrollable (ability) to unstable/controllable (effort, strategy) causes. Has stronger and more consistent empirical support than general mindset interventions, particularly when delivered in context and tied to specific performance domains.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-retraining|Attribution Retraining]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution Style** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to consistently favor particular attribution dimensions across different situations; a relatively stable individual difference variable. A *pessimistic attribution style* — characterized by internal, stable, global attributions for failure — is strongly associated with learned helplessness, depression, and academic underperformance. An *optimistic attribution style* — characterized by external, unstable, specific attributions for failure and internal, stable, global attributions for success — is associated with resilience, persistence, and academic achievement.
> 
> **Boundary:** A...
> 
> *Source: attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-style|Attribution Style]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution Style / Explanatory Style** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A habitual pattern of causal attribution across situations, characterized by three dimensions: internal/external, stable/unstable, global/specific. Depressive explanatory style (internal, stable, global attributions for negative events) predicts vulnerability to learned helplessness and depression.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Attribution style transforms the situational mechanism described by Weiner into an individual difference variable with developmental history and clinical implications.
> 
> **Operational Indicators:** Measured via the Attributional Style Questionnair...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-style-explanatory-style|Attribution Style / Explanatory Style]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework in motivational psychology explaining how people's causal explanations for success and failure shape their subsequent motivation. Key dimensions: locus (internal/external), stability (stable/unstable), and controllability (controllable/uncontrollable). Stable-internal-uncontrollable attributions (e.g., "I failed because I lack ability") produce learned helplessness; unstable-internal-controllable attributions (e.g., "I failed because I didn't use the right strategy") support persistence. The empirical foundation for Axiom 1 of this report.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-theory|Attribution Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Attribution-Dependent Emotion** **[perm-note]**
> An attribution-dependent emotion is an affective state whose specific quality is determined not by the success or failure of an outcome per se but by the causal attribution constructed to explain that outcome. Each dimension of the causal taxonomy maps onto a characteristic family of emotions: locus maps onto self-referent affects (pride, shame), stability maps onto future-oriented affects (hope, hopelessness), and controllability maps onto social-moral affects (anger, guilt, pity, gratitude). The mapping is systematic: changing the dimensional profile of an attribution changes the emotiona...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: social cognition & motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[attribution-dependent-emotion|Attribution-Dependent Emotion]]*

> [!definition] **The Attributional Bridge** **[perm-note]**
> The interpretive mechanism within the Self-Reflection Phase by which performance outcomes are translated into belief updates. The bridge is not a neutral conduit but an active interpreter whose architecture (attributional tendencies, evaluation standards) determines what motivational consequences performance outcomes have. Distinguishable from attribution per se by its functional role in the cycle's feedback dynamics.
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-attributional-bridge|The Attributional Bridge]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Retraining** **[perm-note]**
> **Attributional Retraining**: An intervention approach that redirects maladaptive causal attributions for failure — particularly fixed-ability attributions (internal, stable, uncontrollable) — toward controllable, unstable attributions (strategy, effort, approach). Produces improvements in persistence, self-efficacy, and academic performance independently of direct competence gains.
> See also: [[attribution-theory]], [[growth-mindset]], [[self-reflection-phase]], [[learned-helplessness]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-retraining|Attributional Retraining]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Style** **[perm-note]**
> **Attributional style** is an individual-difference variable capturing a person's characteristic or habitual pattern of causal attribution across domains and over time. Measured by tools such as the **Attributional Style Questionnaire** (ASQ; Peterson et al., 1982), attributional style assesses whether a person habitually makes Internal/External, Stable/Unstable, and Global/Specific attributions for positive and negative events. The most extensively researched attributional style is the **depressogenic attributional style** — the tendency toward Internal-Stable-Global attribution for negati...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-style|Attributional Style]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary used in assigning causes to academic outcomes, structured along three dimensions: *locus* (internal vs. external — "I failed" vs. "the text failed me"), *stability* (stable vs. unstable — "I always struggle" vs. "I struggled today"), and *controllability* (controllable vs. uncontrollable — "I didn't try hard enough" vs. "I can't do this"). Crucially, these dimensions are not purely cognitive constructs — they are encoded in the grammatical and lexical structure of the attributional statement itself.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the central empiri...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-vocabulary|Attributional Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Vocabulary Chain** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The sequence of vocabulary choices through which a learner assigns causal structure to an academic outcome, encoding locus (internal/external), stability (stable/unstable), and controllability (controllable/uncontrollable) in the specific words used. Critically, the chain is not merely a label applied to a pre-formed attribution — it is the primary medium through which the attribution is constructed. Example chain: "I failed this section because I lack the background knowledge" (internal, stable, low-controllability chain) vs. "I haven't yet built the background knowledge ne...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-vocabulary-chain|Attributional Vocabulary Chain]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Vocabulary Dimensions** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The three dimensions along which causal attributions vary and to which vocabulary items commit: *locus* (internal vs. external — whether the cause is within or outside the learner), *stability* (stable vs. unstable — whether the cause persists over time), and *controllability* (controllable vs. uncontrollable — whether the learner can modify the cause). Vocabulary pre-loads dimensional commitments: ability vocabulary encodes internal-stable-uncontrollable; strategy vocabulary encodes internal-unstable-controllable.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[attribution-theory]], [[Bernard-Wei...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-vocabulary-dimensions|Attributional Vocabulary Dimensions]]*

> [!definition] **Attributional Vocabulary Loop** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The self-amplifying cycle through which reflection-phase attribution vocabulary seeds forethought-phase goal vocabulary, which structures performance-phase monitoring, which generates outcome experiences that feed back into reflection-phase attribution vocabulary. The loop can run destructively (ability vocabulary → closed expectancy → vague goals → poor monitoring → poor outcomes → confirmed ability attribution) or constructively (strategy vocabulary → open expectancy → specific goals → precise monitoring → better outcomes → confirmed strategy attribution).
> 
> **Report-Specif...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[attributional-vocabulary-loop|Attributional Vocabulary Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Audience Simulation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive mechanism by which imagining writing for a naive reader forces implicit knowledge to become explicit, activating generative processing. The naive audience cannot be assumed to share the writer's prior knowledge, forcing the writer to justify inferences, define terms, and make logical connections explicit — all of which require genuine knowledge construction.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[audience-simulation-effect|Audience Simulation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Automatic-Assembly Fallacy** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The mistaken belief that adequate processing at a lower level of representational analysis automatically produces coherent integration at a higher level, without dedicated attention or strategy directed at that higher level. In reading, it takes the form of believing that sentence-level understanding produces situation model coherence automatically. Boundary condition: the Fallacy does not apply when the text is extremely simple, the prior knowledge is extremely rich, and the inferential demand is minimal — in those conditions, something like automatic assembly does occur.
> 
> ...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[automatic-assembly-fallacy|Automatic-Assembly Fallacy]]*

> [!definition] **Automaticity** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The property of a cognitive or behavioral process whereby it runs without conscious attentional control, consumes minimal working memory resources, is fast, and is obligatorily triggered by relevant stimuli. Automaticity is not binary but a matter of degree; processes become more automatic through consistent, repeated practice with stable stimulus-response mappings.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** [[automaticity]] is the functional outcome that successful [[habit-formation]] produces. The critical insight is that automaticity is stimulus-specificity dependent — a process ...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[automaticity]]*

> [!definition] **Automaticity Gradient** **[perm-note]**
> [**Automaticity-Gradient**:: The continuum from fully controlled (high [[central-executive]] demand, capacity-limited, conscious, effortful) to fully automatic (low CE demand, capacity-free, unconscious, effortless) processing. Most real-world tasks occupy a position along this gradient rather than sitting at either extreme. The position shifts with practice, fatigue, task complexity, and individual differences.]
> 
> *Source: walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[automaticity-gradient|Automaticity Gradient]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomous Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Motivation arising from volitional endorsement of one's actions, where the perceived locus of causality is internal. Includes intrinsic motivation (engaging for inherent interest/enjoyment) and well-internalised identified and integrated regulation. Distinguished from controlled motivation by the experience of willingness rather than pressure. Predicts deeper learning, greater creativity, more persistence under difficulty, and better well-being. *Note: SRL frameworks use the term "self-regulation" for the cognitive side of what SDT calls "autonomous motivation" — these terms overlap but are...
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomous-motivation|Autonomous Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomous Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The umbrella category in OIT encompassing identified regulation, integrated regulation, and intrinsic motivation — all forms of motivation in which the learner experiences the source of their engagement as arising from their own values and choices. Autonomous regulation is associated with deeper processing, greater persistence, higher wellbeing, and more positive academic emotions. The key functional marker is an internal perceived locus of causality.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomous-regulation|Autonomous Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomous Relationship Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> The motivational quality in which a person maintains a close relationship because they genuinely value it, because participation expresses their identity, or because the relationship is intrinsically rewarding. Distinguished from high commitment, which can co-occur with controlled motivation. Autonomous motivation predicts resilience in the face of relational challenge, positive responses to partner behavior, and lower psychological cost for equivalent relational investment.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomous-relationship-motivation|Autonomous Relationship Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomous vs. Controlled Commitment** **[perm-note]**
> A qualitative distinction within commitment specifying whether commitment arises from identification with the goal as expressing endorsed values (autonomous) or from external pressure, contingent self-worth, or fear of consequences (controlled).
> 
> **Boundary:** The distinction is qualitative rather than quantitative; commitments of equal quantitative strength can differ markedly in regulatory quality with consequences for performance, persistence, and well-being.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The refinement of Goal Setting Theory's commitment moderator most consequential for understandi...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomous-vs.-controlled-commitment|Autonomous vs. Controlled Commitment]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomous vs. Controlled Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> [[autonomous-motivation|Autonomous motivation]] encompasses intrinsic motivation and identified/integrated regulation — any form of motivation where the person experiences themselves as the origin of their action. [[controlled-motivation|Controlled motivation]] encompasses external and introjected regulation — any form where action is experienced as compelled by external pressure or internal compulsion. The [[relative-autonomy-index]] measures the balance between autonomous and controlled motivation. SDT's core empirical claim is that autonomous motivation consistently predicts better outco...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomous-vs.-controlled-motivation|Autonomous vs. Controlled Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomy** **[perm-note]**
> The psychological experience of volition and self-endorsement in behaviour — acting from one's own integrated values and interests rather than from external pressure or introjected obligation. Distinct from independence (one can autonomously choose to comply with others). Not equivalent to philosophical self-determination or full agency, though often treated as such in SDT discourse.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomy Need** **[perm-note]**
> The need for [[autonomy]] is the need to experience one's behavior as *volitional* — as emanating from one's own values, interests, and integrated sense of self rather than from external pressure or internal compulsion. Crucially, autonomy does NOT mean independence, self-reliance, or the absence of social influence. A person can willingly, genuinely internalize another's values and act on them in a fully autonomous manner. Conversely, a person can be physically independent and yet psychologically controlled by shame, guilt, or ego-involvement. The technical term for the underlying dimensio...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomy-need|Autonomy Need]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomy Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to orient toward and be regulated by intrinsic interest, personal value, and self-endorsed choice in one's interactions with the environment. Individuals high in autonomy orientation characteristically notice the interesting and meaningful aspects of tasks and situations, treat external feedback as informational rather than controlling, and experience their behavior as self-initiated and self-endorsed. The key boundary condition: autonomy orientation does NOT mean ignoring external demands or input. It means the *regulatory register* through which external information is proces...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomy-orientation|Autonomy Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Autonomy Support** **[perm-note]**
> A social-contextual condition in which choice is offered, rationale is provided, the actor's perspective is acknowledged, and pressure or coercion is minimised. The primary environmental lever for improving motivation quality in SDT research. Distinguished from permissiveness; autonomy support can include structure and clear expectations delivered in a non-controlling manner.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[autonomy-support|Autonomy Support]]*
