> **Epistemic Status:** Original synthesis — well-motivated by converging evid...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[comprehension-gateway-mechanism|Comprehension Gateway Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The process of evaluating one's own text comprehension during reading — detecting failures of understanding, inconsistencies between text and background knowledge, and gaps in situational coherence. Comprehension monitoring is a specific application of [[metacognitive-monitoring]] to the reading domain. It is distinguished from comprehension *testing* (external assessment of comprehension) by its real-time, self-directed character.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[comprehension-monitoring|Comprehension Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Comprehension Monitoring Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary used during in-progress reading to characterize one's current comprehension state. The granularity and structural affordances of this vocabulary determine the precision of metacognitive monitoring available to the reader. Coarse-grained vocabulary ("I get it" / "I don't get it") provides binary monitoring access. Fine-grained vocabulary ("I understand the claim structure but haven't yet mapped the evidence," "I'm missing the conceptual bridge between section two and three," "I've grasped the surface argument but the deeper logic is unclear") provides graduated...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[comprehension-monitoring-vocabulary|Comprehension Monitoring Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Conception of Ability** **[perm-note]**
> The implicit theoretical frame through which a learner construes what "ability" actually is — whether as a fixed entity that can be revealed but not changed, or as a malleable capacity that grows with effort and engagement. The conception of ability is the substrate from which goal orientations are adopted; differentiated (fixed) conceptions make performance orientations easily adoptable, while undifferentiated (malleable) conceptions support mastery orientations.
> 
> **Boundary:** Distinct from *self-efficacy* (belief about whether one can succeed at a specific task, given current ability) an...
> 
> *Source: goal-orientation-theory-mastery-vs-performance-goals-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[conception-of-ability|Conception of Ability]]*

> [!definition] **Conceptual Change** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: Deep learning that involves not accretion to existing conceptual frameworks but the restructuring or replacement of those frameworks with qualitatively different explanatory systems. Requires: dissatisfaction with current conception, intelligibility of the new conception, plausibility of the new conception, and fruitfulness of the new conception. NOT equivalent to Rumelhart's "restructuring" — it is more specific about the conditions and mechanism.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The reconstructive resource that provides what schema theory's own mechanism (accommodation) f...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[conceptual-change|Conceptual Change]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Knowledge about when, why, and under what conditions specific learning strategies are effective. Provides the causal vocabulary necessary for strategy-level attribution during the Self-Reflection Phase. Without conditional knowledge, learners are restricted to coarser attribution dimensions (ability, effort) that carry worse motivational consequences than strategy attributions.
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-metacognitive-knowledge|Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Positive Regard** **[perm-note]**
> [[conditional-positive-regard]] describes the experience of being valued by significant others contingent on meeting specific standards or performing specific behaviors. SDT research connects conditional regard to introjected regulation: when significant others' approval is contingent on outcomes, children and students internalize the pressure as an internal "should" (introjection) rather than as a genuine value (identification). [[conditional-regard]] is among the more reliably documented antecedents of contingent self-worth and controlled motivation.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-positive-regard|Conditional Positive Regard]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Prescription Problem** **[perm-note]**
> The structural challenge (identified in Phase VI) faced by any prescriptive framework that generates recommendations for supporting a developing system: prescriptions valid for early developmental states become counterproductive in later states, as the support needed for early-stage performance constrains late-stage expert performance. The expertise reversal effect is the CLT-specific instantiation of this more general problem.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-prescription-problem|Conditional Prescription Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional Regard** **[perm-note]**
> A parenting or teaching style in which approval, affection, or attention is given contingently — offered when the child meets the adult's expectations and withdrawn when they do not. Conditional regard is particularly effective at producing introjected regulation because it teaches the child to regulate themselves through the same approval-contingent mechanism the adult uses externally. Associated with contingent self-worth, performance anxiety, and the introjection trap.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-regard|Conditional Regard]]*

> [!definition] **Conditional-Positive-Regard** **[perm-note]**
> The relational pattern in which approval, warmth, and positive regard are made contingent on meeting performance standards. Research within SDT demonstrates that conditional positive regard is a primary driver of introjected (rather than identified or integrated) regulation, because it communicates that the person's worth is performance-contingent and thus creates the relational threat that makes releasing the introjection feel existentially dangerous.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[conditional-positive-regard]]*

> [!definition] **Confirmation Bias** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Confirmation Bias (Wason, 1960; Nickerson, 1998)
> **Definition:** The tendency to search for, interpret, favour, and recall information in ways that confirm or support one's prior beliefs or hypotheses. Operates in three forms: biased information search, biased interpretation of evidence, and biased memory retrieval.
> **Boundary conditions:** Not always irrational — in stable, high-validity environments, prior expectations are often reliable guides. Becomes problematic when applied to contested, uncertain, or identity-relevant claims.
> **Report significance:** Identified as the parad...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[confirmation-bias|Confirmation Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Conjunctive System Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> The proposal that the three basic psychological needs are jointly necessary for full psychological well-being and optimal functioning — meaning all three must be adequately satisfied simultaneously, and persistent deprivation of any one creates well-being costs not offset by amplified satisfaction of the others.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[conjunctive-system-architecture|Conjunctive System Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The set of processes by which newly formed, initially fragile memory representations are stabilized over time. Consolidation occurs at two levels: *synaptic consolidation* (cellular/molecular stabilization of individual synaptic changes, occurring over hours and requiring protein synthesis) and *systems consolidation* (gradual transfer of memory representation from hippocampus-dependent to neocortically distributed storage, occurring over weeks to years). Consolidation can be disrupted by trauma, sleep deprivation, or pharmacological intervention during the critical post-encoding window.
> 
> *...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Constitutive Vocabulary Function** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The property of vocabulary whereby the act of formulating an experience in specific words *partially creates* the psychological reality of that experience, rather than merely describing a pre-formed reality. Distinguished from *expressive vocabulary function* (vocabulary as symptom of prior state) and from *performative speech acts* (vocabulary that creates social facts). In the motivational inner speech context, constitutive vocabulary means that the words chosen to characterize a comprehension state, attribute a failure, or frame a goal are among the causes — not merely th...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[constitutive-vocabulary-function|Constitutive Vocabulary Function]]*

> [!definition] **Construction Problem** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The unresolved difficulty that schema theory cannot explain the origin of schemas without presupposing schemas. If all learning is mediated by schemas (through assimilation or accommodation), the formation of the first schema is inexplicable within schema theory's own resources. NOT merely an abstract philosophical puzzle; it reveals the limits of schema theory's foundational claim. After this re-examination, dissolved by the attractor account (schemas are emergent from statistical learning, not foundational primitives).
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Identified as the de...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[construction-problem|Construction Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Activation Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The categorical difference between (1) schema activation — fast, automatic, top-down retrieval and application of an existing schema guided by partial cues — and (2) schema construction — slow, effortful, bottom-up building of a new schema through analogy and restructuring. The asymmetry has both mechanistic and resource dimensions: different cognitive processes are at work, requiring different cognitive resources and different time scales.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** The asymmetry applies to cognitive schemas; related asymmetries have been identified in skill acquisition (bet...
> 
> *Attribution: original synthesis*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[construction-activation-asymmetry|Construction-Activation Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Integration** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A computational model of text comprehension proposing two cyclically alternating phases: construction (building an initial network of propositions from text plus associated prior knowledge activations) and integration (refining the network through spreading activation to produce a coherent representation). The situation model emerges from successful integration cycles.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Provides the mechanistic account of why the received view fails: poor situation models result from inadequate integration cycles, not inadequate construction — and integratio...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[construction-integration]]*

> [!definition] **Construction-Integration Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-phase computational model of discourse comprehension: the Construction phase activates relevant concepts and relationships from text and long-term memory, forming an initial (noisy, over-inclusive) network; the Integration phase settles this network through constraint satisfaction, producing a coherent mental representation. The model produces all three levels of representation: surface code, textbase, and situation model.
> 
> **Boundary:** The CI model is a computational model, not a phenomenological description. It does not imply that readers are conscious of construction and integrati...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[construction-integration-model|Construction-Integration Model]]*

> [!definition] **Constructionist Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A theory of inference generation specifying that causal antecedent inferences (explaining why events occurred) and goal-motive inferences are generated automatically during comprehension, while predictive and elaborative inferences are not. Based on three assumptions: reader goal (inference generation is goal-modulated), coherence (readers default to seeking coherent representations), and explanation (readers automatically seek causal explanations).
> 
> **See also:** [[situation-model]], [[self-explanation-effect]], [[metacognitive-scaffolding]]
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[constructionist-theory|Constructionist Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Constructive Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> The epistemic state at which a learner has sufficient prior knowledge to provide integration anchors for new material, but has not yet constructed a situation model for that material. The constructive threshold is the zone of maximum EI benefit: enough anchors for construction, enough novelty to require it.
> 
> **Boundary:** The constructive threshold is domain-specific. A reader may be above the constructive threshold for evolutionary biology but below it for quantum field theory — requiring different instructional strategies for each.
> 
> **See also:** [[prior-knowledge]], [[Region-of-Proximal-...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[constructive-threshold|Constructive Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivism** **[perm-note]**
> Constructivism, as an epistemological position, holds that knowledge is not discovered or received but actively constructed by the knower through interaction with the environment. Cognitive structures — schemas, concepts, frameworks — are not copies of reality but are built by the learner and continuously revised in response to experience. The key distinction is between learning as acquisition (the receipt of pre-formed knowledge packages) and learning as construction (the active building and rebuilding of meaning structures).
> 
> **Boundary:** Constructivism does not deny an external reality,...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[constructivism]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivism Defined** **[perm-note]**
> **Constructivism** is an epistemological and psychological framework holding that knowledge is not a fixed, external reality to be transmitted from teacher to learner, but an active construction of the knowing subject built through experience, prior knowledge, and — in social variants — collaborative interaction. The learner is not a passive vessel but an active meaning-maker who interprets, organizes, and transforms incoming information through existing cognitive structures. The claim is simultaneously epistemological (concerning the nature of knowledge) and psychological (concerning the m...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[constructivism-defined|Constructivism Defined]]*

> [!definition] **Constructivist Pedagogy** **[perm-note]**
> **Constructivist pedagogy** encompasses the family of instructional approaches, methods, and design principles derived from or consistent with constructivist learning theory. Common characteristics include: activating and connecting to prior knowledge; presenting learning tasks that create productive cognitive challenge (not passive reception); using authentic, complex, context-embedded problems rather than decontextualized exercises; supporting collaborative knowledge construction; requiring learners to make thinking visible through explanation, representation, and argument; using formativ...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[constructivist-pedagogy|Constructivist Pedagogy]]*

> [!definition] **Content-by-Motive Interaction** **[perm-note]**
> **Content-by-Motive Interaction** — A theoretical refinement within GCT recognizing that the well-being consequences of goal pursuit depend jointly on the *content* of the goal (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) and the *regulatory motive* underlying its pursuit (autonomous vs. controlled). A goal with extrinsic content pursued for autonomous reasons may have different well-being consequences than the same goal pursued for controlled reasons. See Section 7.
> **See also:** [[autonomous-motivation]], [[controlled-motivation]], [[regulatory-style]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[content-by-motive-interaction|Content-by-Motive Interaction]]*

> [!definition] **Context Collapse** **[perm-note]**
> The dissolution of audience specificity in digital communication, where messages intended for targeted audiences reach heterogeneous, unanticipated audiences simultaneously. A source of systematic communication failure in social media and other digital communication platforms.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-collapse|Context Collapse]]*

> [!definition] **Context Collapse in Digital Communication** **[perm-note]**
> *Context collapse* — a concept developed by danah boyd in social media research — describes the situation in digital communication where messages intended for specific audiences are instead visible to radically heterogeneous, unanticipated audiences simultaneously. The professional posting their political views on LinkedIn, the person whose private message is screenshotted and shared publicly, the speaker addressing one audience whose words are distributed to another: all experience context collapse. Managing context collapse requires either highly general communication (accessible and inno...
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-collapse-in-digital-communication|Context Collapse in Digital Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Context Inference** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process by which a reader uses surrounding textual information — syntactic cues, semantic associations, thematic expectations, world knowledge — to infer the approximate meaning of an unfamiliar word. The quality of a context for inference depends on: (1) how much relevant meaning-relevant information the surrounding text provides; (2) the distance between the target word and its inferential supports; (3) whether the meaning clues are explicit or require domain-specific background knowledge to interpret.
> 
> **Boundary:** Context inference is probabilistic and error-prone. Studie...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-inference|Context Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Context of Discovery vs. Context of Justification** **[perm-note]**
> A foundational distinction in [[philosophy-of-science|philosophy of science]], associated with [[hans-reichenbach|Hans Reichenbach]] (1938), between the *context of discovery* (the psychological and heuristic process by which hypotheses are generated) and the *context of justification* (the logical process by which hypotheses are tested and confirmed or disconfirmed). Peirce's abduction belongs primarily to the context of discovery, though [[inference-to-the-best-explanation|Inference to the Best Explanation]] attempts to give it a role in the context of justification as well. The distincti...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-of-discovery-vs.-context-of-justification|Context of Discovery vs. Context of Justification]]*

> [!definition] **Context Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Regulatory activities directed at managing features of the task and learning environment, including structuring the physical environment for study, managing social resources strategically, selecting and modifying tasks, and seeking or avoiding specific types of help. Context is treated as a first-class regulatory domain rather than a fixed background condition.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The most theoretically underspecified and empirically least-supported area in Pintrich's framework, but potentially the most consequential for learners whose learning failures are pr...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-regulation|Context Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Context-Action Encoding** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The neuropsychological process by which the basal ganglia habit system binds together the representation of a contextual cue and the motor/behavioral routine that has been consistently associated with it. The binding is strengthened by reward signals (dopaminergic) and weakened by inconsistency in the context-action association. Context-action encoding is the mechanism by which habits become context-triggered.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Context-action encoding is the specific mechanism that willpower-based approaches fail to engage. Repetition alone is insufficient; ...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-action-encoding|Context-Action Encoding]]*

> [!definition] **Context-Dependency of Habit** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical phenomenon, documented extensively by Wendy Wood and colleagues, in which habitual behavior is strongly bound to the specific contextual features present during habit formation — including location, time, social environment, and preceding events. The same individual may show dramatically different habit strength for the same behavior across different contexts.
> 
> **Boundary:** Context-dependency does not mean habits are infinitely fragile. Strong habits survive moderate context disruption; what matters is the degree of overlap between the original encoding context and the curren...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-dependency-of-habit|Context-Dependency of Habit]]*

> [!definition] **Context-Dependent Memory** **[perm-note]**
> A specific instantiation of the ESP in which the physical or environmental setting at retrieval matches the setting at encoding, producing better recall than retrieval in a novel environment. Demonstrated paradigmatically by Godden & Baddeley (1975) with underwater/land learning and testing.
> 
> **Boundary:** Context-dependent effects are one special case of the general ESP. The ESP also predicts semantic, associative, physiological, and cognitive context dependencies. Physical environmental context is the most concrete and experimentally controllable dimension.
> 
> **See also:** [[Encoding-Speci...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[context-dependent-memory|Context-Dependent Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Contextual Inference** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process by which a reader constructs a provisional meaning for an unfamiliar word by attending to surrounding words, sentence structure, discourse structure, and prior world knowledge. Contextual inference is the primary mechanism of incidental vocabulary acquisition in reading; it is selective (not all readers attempt it for all unknown words), fallible (contextual cues can be misleading), and cumulative (repeated partial inferences across multiple encounters eventually converge on accurate meaning).
> 
> **Boundary:** Contextual inference does not always produce accurate word me...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[contextual-inference|Contextual Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Contingent Self-Esteem** **[perm-note]**
> A form of self-regard that fluctuates with the degree to which one is meeting internalized conditions of worth derived from conditional positive regard in important relationships. Contingent self-esteem is inherently unstable, fragile, and anxiety-producing — requiring continuous monitoring of performance on the relevant conditions. Distinguished from unconditional self-worth, which is not dependent on any particular performance or approval and is associated with more stable, autonomous functioning.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[contingent-self-esteem|Contingent Self-Esteem]]*

> [!definition] **Contingent Self-Worth** **[perm-note]**
> The experience of one's self-esteem as conditional on outcomes in specific domains — on academic performance, others' approval, attractive appearance, or financial achievement. Theorized by Crocker and colleagues as a distinct motivational process, contingent self-worth is proposed by GCT as one mechanism through which extrinsic goal emphasis undermines well-being: when a person's sense of worth depends on extrinsic goal outcomes, failures threaten not just goals but identity.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[contingent-self-worth|Contingent Self-Worth]]*

> [!definition] **The Continuity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The [[Dewey's-Continuity-and-Interaction-Principles|continuity principle]] states that each experience carries forward the effects of previous experiences and modifies the conditions of future ones. Educationally, it implies that the quality of any learning experience must be evaluated not only by its immediate outcomes but by its long-term effects on the learner's openness to further experience, capacity for inquiry, and motivation for continued growth. A single miseducative experience — one that instills learned helplessness, [[conditional-positive-regard|contingent self-worth]], or fixed...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-continuity-principle|The Continuity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Continuity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** [[Dewey's-Continuity-and-Interaction-Principles|Continuity Principle]] (Dewey, 1938)
> **Definition:** The principle that every genuine experience both takes up elements from previous experiences and modifies the conditions under which subsequent experiences will be had. Experience is cumulative and directional — each moment of learning shapes what future learning is possible.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Continuity operates in both educative and miseducative directions — experiences can compound growth or compound stagnation with equal efficiency.
> **Report Significance:** Provides the ...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[continuity-principle|Continuity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Contract-Style Prompt Structure** **[perm-note]**
> The optimal system prompt follows a contract format:
> 
> ```markdown
> # [Agent Name] - [One-line role description]
> 
> ## Role
> You are [specific expertise/domain specialist].
> [1-2 sentences defining core responsibility]
> 
> ## When Invoked
> [Specific trigger conditions]
> [What situations require this agent]
> 
> ## Success Criteria
> This invocation succeeds when:
> - [Measurable outcome 1]
> - [Measurable outcome 2]
> - [Measurable outcome 3]
> 
> ## Workflow
> 1. [First step - be specific]
> 2. [Second step with tool usage if applicable]
> 3. [Analysis or processing step]
> 4. [Output generation step]
> 5. [Validation or revi...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[contract-style-prompt-structure|Contract-Style Prompt Structure]]*

> [!definition] **Control** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive control designates the regulatory activity through which the learner adjusts, modifies, or redirects her cognitive processes in response to monitoring signals. Control encompasses strategy selection and switching, effort regulation, attention reallocation, pacing adjustments, and help-seeking. Control is the effector component of the regulatory system: where monitoring detects discrepancies between current performance and desired performance, control enacts corrective responses.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive control, as technically defined, is a response to metacognitive monito...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[control]]*

> [!definition] **Control Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to orient toward and be regulated by external and internal controls: deadlines, rewards, surveillance, evaluations, and ego-involvement (the last being crucial). Individuals high in control orientation characteristically notice the evaluative and directive aspects of situations, treat feedback as indicative of their standing relative to standards or others, and experience their behavior as driven by demands they must satisfy. The critical conceptual point, which differentiates COT from [[locus-of-control|locus of control theory]], is that *control orientation includes internall...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[control-orientation|Control Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Control Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory phase during performance in which the learner deploys moves from a repertoire of strategies that adjust cognition (strategy switching, attentional reallocation), motivation (named [[motivational-regulation-strategies]]), behavior (effort adjustment, [[Academic-Help-Seeking|help-seeking]]), and context (environmental modification) in response to monitoring signals.
> 
> **Boundary:** Control is not the same as self-discipline or willpower; it is the diagnostic deployment of specific moves matched to specific monitoring signals, and habitual deployment of any single move regardless...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[control-phase|Control Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Control Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Scaffolding that targets the downward information flow in the Nelson-Narens model — helping learners translate accurate monitoring judgments into effective regulatory decisions. Examples: strategy menus with decision criteria, if-then regulatory protocols, structured response frameworks linked to monitoring outputs.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[control-scaffolding|Control Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Controllability** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Whether the cause is subject to volitional control by the actor or by others. Effort is the prototypical controllable cause; ability, luck, and task difficulty are paradigmatically uncontrollable. Critically, controllability is orthogonal to locus — an internal cause (ability) may be uncontrollable, while an external cause (another person's behavior toward you) may be controllable by them.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Controllability is the primary driver of guilt (for controllable failures attributed to self) and anger (for controllable failures attributed to others)....
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[controllability]]*

> [!definition] **The Controllability Dimension** **[perm-note]**
> **Controllability** refers to whether a causal factor is perceived to be subject to the person's voluntary agency. Note the critical word: *perceived*. Controllability is a phenomenological property of the attribution, not an objective property of the cause. The same factor — say, reading speed — may be construed as controllable by a person who believes in deliberate practice, or as uncontrollable by a person who believes reading speed is fixed by neurological inheritance. The perception of controllability, not its factual accuracy, governs the emotional and motivational consequences.
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-controllability-dimension|The Controllability Dimension]]*

> [!definition] **Controllability Dimension** **[perm-note]**
> The third dimension, classifying causes as controllable (effort, strategy) or uncontrollable (ability, luck, illness) by the individual. Primary determinant of social-moral affects: controllable failure causes generate anger (in observers) and guilt (in actors); uncontrollable failure causes generate pity (in observers) and shame (in actors).
> 
> **Boundary:** Controllability is a property of the *cause*, not the *outcome*. The question is not "Can you control what happened?" but "Can you control the factor that made it happen?"
> 
> **See also:** [[controllability]], [[the-controllability-dimensi...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: social cognition & motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[controllability-dimension|Controllability Dimension]]*

> [!definition] **Controllability-Moral Inference Bridge** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The automatic cognitive process by which perceived controllability of another person's negative condition triggers moral evaluation — specifically, reduced sympathy and increased anger — rather than purely descriptive causal cognition. The attribution and the moral judgment are generated in a single cognitive act rather than sequentially.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Reveals that Attribution Theory is simultaneously a descriptive motivational theory and an implicit moral philosophy — and that the normative use of controllability attributions in policy contexts is not a...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[controllability-moral-inference-bridge|Controllability-Moral Inference Bridge]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Motivation arising from external or introjected pressures — rewards, deadlines, approval-seeking, ego-involvement, guilt, shame. The perceived locus of causality is external (or internal but pressuring). Produces compliance and effort but degrades learning quality, creativity, and well-being under pressure conditions. *See also: [[undermining-effect]], [[introjected-regulation]], [[external-regulation]].*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[controlled-motivation|Controlled Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The umbrella category in OIT encompassing external and introjected regulation — all forms of motivation in which the learner experiences the source of their engagement as external pressure or internal compulsion (guilt, shame, ego-threat). Controlled regulation is associated with surface processing, performance orientation, higher anxiety, and reduced wellbeing. The functional marker is an external or impersonal perceived locus of causality.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[controlled-regulation|Controlled Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Relationship Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> The motivational quality in which a person maintains a close relationship due to external pressure, introjected guilt or obligation, fear of aloneness, or excessive sunk cost reasoning. Controlled motivation is NOT equivalent to dislike of the relationship — a person can be highly satisfied with a relationship while maintaining it for controlled reasons. The psychological cost of controlled motivation accrues through resource depletion and reduced autonomous functioning in other domains.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[controlled-relationship-motivation|Controlled Relationship Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlling Environment** **[perm-note]**
> An environment that pressures learners toward specific outcomes through rewards, punishments, surveillance, deadlines presented as sources of pressure, praise that is evaluative rather than informational, conditional regard (where teacher warmth is contingent on performance), and language that communicates "you must," "you should," or "I will reward/punish you for." Controlling environments may produce compliance, but the compliance comes at the cost of autonomous motivation, deeper processing, and wellbeing.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[controlling-environment|Controlling Environment]]*

> [!definition] **Controlling Events** **[perm-note]**
> [**Controlling-Events-Definition**:: External events whose primary functional significance is to pressure, direct, or coerce specific behavioral outcomes, thereby shifting the person's PLOC from internal to external. Examples include surveillance, competitive evaluation, imposed deadlines, contingent tangible rewards (especially task-contingent and completion-contingent), controlling interpersonal language ("you should," "you must"), and ego-involving instructions. CET predicts that controlling events reliably undermine intrinsic motivation by making the person feel like a "pawn" rather tha...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[controlling-events|Controlling Events]]*

> [!definition] **Core Definition: Epistemic Vigilance** **[perm-note]**
> 
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-epistemic-vigilance-and-epistemic-humility* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[core-definition-epistemic-vigilance|Core Definition: Epistemic Vigilance]]*

> [!definition] **Correspondent Inference Theory** **[perm-note]**
> **Correspondent inference theory** (Jones & Davis, 1965) is a model of person perception that addresses the conditions under which observers make **correspondent inferences** — inferring that a person's observed behavior reflects a stable, personal disposition. The theory holds that observers make dispositional attributions most readily when: the behavior produces distinctive (unique, non-redundant) effects; the behavior is socially non-common (uncommon); and the behavior is non-role-prescribed (not required by the social position). Low social desirability of the behavior also amplifies dis...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[correspondent-inference-theory|Correspondent Inference Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Corroboration** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's term for the epistemic standing earned by a hypothesis that has survived rigorous attempts at falsification. A highly corroborated hypothesis is not *verified* — it has not been confirmed as certainly true — but it has demonstrated its robustness by repeatedly surviving tests designed to refute it. Corroboration is explicitly not a measure of probability of truth but of tested resilience.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[corroboration]]*

> [!definition] **Cortico-Striatal Learning Loops** **[perm-note]**
> The basal ganglia organize into parallel loops connecting specific cortical regions through striatum to specific motor/cognitive outputs. The **motor loop** (motor cortex → putamen → GPi/SNr → thalamus → motor cortex) supports motor sequence learning. The **cognitive loop** (dlPFC → caudate → GPi/SNr → thalamus → dlPFC) supports cognitive skill automatization. These loops enable both motor and cognitive automaticity through common computational principles.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[cortico-striatal-learning-loops|Cortico-Striatal Learning Loops]]*

> [!definition] **Counterexample Search** **[perm-note]**
> The third and most demanding stage of the MMT inference cycle, in which the reasoner attempts to construct an alternative model consistent with the premises but inconsistent with the candidate conclusion. If a counterexample model is successfully constructed, the candidate conclusion is rejected or revised. Failure to engage in counterexample search — often due to working memory limitations or task demands — is the principal source of deductive error in the MMT account.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[counterexample-search|Counterexample Search]]*

> [!definition] **Coupling-Constitution Fallacy** **[perm-note]**
> The alleged error of inferring, from the fact that two systems are causally coupled, that one thereby constitutes part of the other. Adams and Aizawa argue that Clark and Chalmers commit this fallacy: because Otto's notebook is causally coupled to his cognitive processes, they infer it constitutes part of his cognitive system. The objection insists that causal relevance and constitutive membership are distinct relations, and that establishing the former does not establish the latter. The mark of the cognitive — whatever distinguishes genuine cognitive processes from causally relevant non-co...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[coupling-constitution-fallacy|Coupling-Constitution Fallacy]]*

> [!definition] **Covariation Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The **covariation principle** — articulated by Harold Kelley (1967) — holds that people attribute behavior to the cause with which it most consistently co-occurs across conditions. Kelley formalized this through an analogy with ANOVA experimental design: people behave as intuitive scientists, varying the potential causal factors of Persons (consensus), Entities (distinctiveness), and Occasions (consistency) and attributing behavior to whichever factor shows the strongest covariation with the behavior. High consensus + high distinctiveness + high consistency = entity (situational) attributio...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[covariation-principle|Covariation Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Coverage Ratio** **[perm-note]**
> The proportion of running word tokens in a text that fall within the reader's known vocabulary. A coverage ratio of 98% means the reader knows 98 out of every 100 word tokens encountered. The vocabulary threshold hypothesis holds that fluent, unassisted comprehension requires a coverage ratio approaching 95–98%.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** Approximately 5–7 unknown words per typical page of prose in a given reader constitutes a rough indicator of the productive acquisition band (95–97% coverage).
> 
> **See also:** [[simple-view-of-reading]], [[cognitive-load-theory]], [[schema-theory]]
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[coverage-ratio|Coverage Ratio]]*

> [!definition] **Coverage Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> The proportion of running words in a text that a reader must already know before the incidental vocabulary acquisition mechanism operates efficiently. Estimated at approximately 98% for general adult text — meaning no more than 1 unknown word in 50 running words. Reflects the working memory demands of simultaneous text comprehension and vocabulary inference.
> 
> **Boundary:** This is a population average for skilled adult readers in general-topic text. Individual thresholds vary with fluency, topic familiarity, and morphological competence. The threshold applies to running-word coverage (with ...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[coverage-threshold|Coverage Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **The Coverage Threshold Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical claim that efficient incidental vocabulary acquisition from reading requires that a reader already knows approximately 98% of the running words in a text — equivalently, that no more than about 1 unknown word occurs per 50 running words. Below this threshold, comprehension degrades sufficiently that the cognitive resources necessary for inferring unfamiliar word meanings are unavailable.
> 
> **Boundary:** The 98% figure is a population average estimated from controlled studies. Individual thresholds vary with reading skill, topic familiarity, and text genre. Some research suggest...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-coverage-threshold-hypothesis|The Coverage Threshold Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Ecphory** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which a retrieval cue interacts with a stored memory trace (engram) to produce a recollective experience. Ecphory is not simply "trace activation" — it is a specific kind of interaction in which the retrieval cue modifies and shapes the process of bringing the memory to consciousness. The cue does not merely trigger the trace; it participates in constructing what is remembered.
> 
> **Boundary:** Ecphory as defined by Tulving is not the same as consciousness of a memory. Ecphory is the process; the resulting conscious experience (if it occurs) is ecphoric information or "retrieva...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[ecphory]]*

> [!definition] **Craving** **[perm-note]**
> The anticipatory motivational state triggered by a habit cue — the desire for the reward before any action has occurred. Craving is proposed to be the motivational bridge between cue and routine, and the primary source of the "pull" that makes established habits feel compelling rather than merely automatic.
> 
> **See also:** [[intrinsic-motivation]], [[extrinsic-motivation]], [[cortico-striatal-learning-loops]]
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[craving]]*

> [!definition] **The Critical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Examines whose interests the received view serves, what perspectives it excludes, and what social or political dimensions are rendered invisible by the dominant framework. This lens asks: What has been stripped from schema theory in the process of formalizing it, and why does that matter?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-critical-lens|The Critical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Critical thinking is "purposeful, self-regulatory judgment which results in interpretation, analysis, evaluation, and inference, as well as explanation of the evidential, conceptual, methodological, criteriological, or contextual considerations upon which that judgment is based." This definition, produced by expert consensus across disciplines, emphasises that CT is not merely logical analysis but a self-aware, self-correcting cognitive activity — one that monitors and adjusts its own processes.
> 
> *Attribution: Delphi Report*
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[critical-thinking|Critical Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking as Metacognitively-Regulated Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> **[[critical-thinking|Critical Thinking]]** is defined operationally as the deliberate application of reasoning skills and strategies that increase the probability of reaching sound conclusions through purposeful, reflective judgment, mediated by metacognitive monitoring and control processes that regulate strategy selection, execution monitoring, and error correction. This definition emphasizes that critical thinking is fundamentally a *metacognitive process* rather than simply a collection of reasoning techniques, distinguishing skilled critical thinkers not by their possession of reasoni...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[critical-thinking-as-metacognitively-regulated-reasoning|Critical Thinking as Metacognitively-Regulated Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking — Delphi Consensus** *[needs-note]*
> The [[delphi-report]] defines critical thinking as "purposeful, self-regulatory judgment which results in interpretation, [[analysis|analysis]], [[evaluation|evaluation]], and [[inference|inference]], as well as explanation of the evidential, conceptual, methodological, criteriological, or contextual considerations upon which that judgment is based. CT is essential as a tool of inquiry" (Facione, 1990, p. 3). This definition was the product of a two-year Delphi process involving forty-six expert panelists drawn from philosophy, education, social science, and physical science.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Critical-Thinking-—-Delphi-Consensus|Critical Thinking — Delphi Consensus]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking — The Definitional Landscape** *[needs-note]*
> [[critical-thinking]] has been defined in numerous ways across the philosophical, psychological, and educational literatures. [[john-dewey]] (1910) defined [[reflective-thinking|reflective thought]] as "active, persistent, and careful consideration of any belief or supposed form of knowledge in the light of the grounds that support it and the further conclusions to which it tends." [[robert-ennis]] (1987) offered the influential definition: "reasonable reflective thinking focused on deciding what to believe or do." The [[delphi-report|APA Delphi consensus]] ([[Peter-Facione]], 1990) charact...
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[Critical-Thinking-—-The-Definitional-Landscape|Critical Thinking — The Definitional Landscape]]*

> [!definition] **Critical-Thinking-Pedagogy** **[perm-note]**
> Instructional approaches designed to cultivate reasoning quality and reflective judgment. In Paul-Elder practice, best implemented through infusion across disciplinary contexts.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[critical-thinking-pedagogy]]*

> [!definition] **Cross-Phase Vocabulary Cascade** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The mechanism by which the vocabulary deployed in one phase of Zimmerman's SRL cycle becomes the lexical input for the next phase's characteristic processes. Vocabulary in reflection seeds vocabulary available in forethought; vocabulary in forethought shapes vocabulary available for monitoring; monitoring vocabulary determines the vocabulary of outcome interpretation. The cascade means that vocabulary deficits in any phase propagate through the entire cycle, and vocabulary interventions in any phase have effects throughout the cycle.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[Zimmerman's-Cycl...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[cross-phase-vocabulary-cascade|Cross-Phase Vocabulary Cascade]]*

> [!definition] **Crystallization Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The level of accumulated pre-schema knowledge at which a set of loosely organized elements rapidly consolidates into a coherent, retrievable schema. Pre-threshold states are characterized by slow, effortful, element-by-element processing; post-threshold states by fluent, whole-schema activation.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** The crystallization threshold is domain-specific and learner-specific. Threshold conditions include: varied-context exposure, generative description tasks, and explicit structure-finding exercises.
> 
> **Operational Indicators:** Threshold crossing is marked by...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[crystallization-threshold|Crystallization Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Cue** **[perm-note]**
> An environmental or internal stimulus that reliably precedes and elicits a habitual routine. Cues may be external (a location, time of day, other people, preceding events) or internal (an emotional state, a bodily sensation). The cue-routine association is the core learned unit of a [[habit]].
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all cues are consciously noticed. Much habit-triggering occurs sub-attentionally — the individual executes the routine without having registered the cue as salient. This is precisely why habit modification requires deliberate cue identification work.
> 
> **See also:** [[Behavioral-Modi...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[cue]]*

> [!definition] **Cue Utilisation** **[perm-note]**
> The cue utilisation approach holds that metacognitive judgments are based on heuristic cues — intrinsic, extrinsic, and mnemonic — rather than on direct access to the state of the underlying cognitive process. This approach, developed most fully by Asher Koriat, provides the mechanistic account of *how* the monitoring channel operates: the meta-level integrates available cues into a monitoring judgment, and the accuracy of that judgment depends on the diagnostic validity of the cues utilised.
> 
> **Boundary:** Cue utilisation is a mechanistic theory of monitoring, not a model of control. It ex...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[cue-utilisation|Cue Utilisation]]*

> [!definition] **Cue Validity** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a monitoring cue (e.g., processing fluency, familiarity, FOK) accurately predicts the cognitive state being assessed (e.g., comprehension depth, future retrievability). High-validity cues produce accurate monitoring; low-validity cues systematically miscalibrate monitoring judgments regardless of monitoring effort.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[cue-validity|Cue Validity]]*

> [!definition] **Cue-Encoding Bottleneck** **[perm-note]**
> A proposed integrative hypothesis, surfaced originally in this synthesis, holding that the most common single point of failure for implementation intentions is the quality of cue specification — that vague, abstract, or context-dependent cues fail to acquire the perceptual discriminability required for reliable detection, regardless of how strong the underlying goal commitment is. The hypothesis predicts that practitioner training in cue-encoding skill should outperform other forms of implementation-intention coaching.
> 
> **Boundary:** The hypothesis is novel to this synthesis and has not bee...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[cue-encoding-bottleneck|Cue-Encoding Bottleneck]]*

> [!definition] **Cue-Utilization Framework** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>The cue-utilization framework</span> (Koriat, 1997) holds that [[metacognitive-judgments|metacognitive judgments]] are based on heuristic cues drawn from three sources: intrinsic properties of the material, extrinsic properties of the study conditions, and mnemonic properties of the learner's own processing experience. The framework explains both why monitoring is sometimes accurate (the cues often do correlate with actual learning) and why it systematically fails (the correlation is imperfect and the cues are subject to specific misalignment conditions).
> 
> [**C...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[cue-utilization-framework|Cue-Utilization Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Cultural Neutrality Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The implicit assumption in the formalized, computational version of schema theory that schemas are culturally universal cognitive structures — that everyone's "restaurant schema" or "narrative schema" is essentially similar in structure, differing only in content. After this re-examination: this assumption is false, and its embedding in the dominant account has political consequences for whose prior knowledge counts as legitimate in instructional contexts.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Exposed by the critical lens challenge; connects this cognitive science debate to broa...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[cultural-neutrality-assumption|Cultural Neutrality Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Curriculum Design** **[perm-note]**
> Curriculum design refers to the deliberate, systematic process of planning, structuring, and organising the elements of a learning experience — including content selection, sequencing, pedagogy, assessment, and resource allocation — to achieve specified learning outcomes. It is distinguished from "curriculum" (which can refer simply to the content or syllabus) by its emphasis on the intentional engineering of the learning environment.
> 
> **Boundary:** Curriculum design is not synonymous with teaching. A curriculum can be well-designed and poorly taught, or poorly designed and salvaged by an e...
> 
> *Source: curriculum-design-for-lifelong-learning-and-self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: education — curriculum design*
> *Permanent Note: [[curriculum-design|Curriculum Design]]*

> [!definition] **Curse of Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive bias in which possessing knowledge makes it systematically difficult to accurately model not having it, producing vocabulary miscalibration, skipped inferential steps, and expert-novice communication failures. A primary cause of poor communication from experts to non-expert audiences. Requires deliberate perspective-taking and vocabulary calibration to overcome.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[curse-of-knowledge|Curse of Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A three-phase model of [[self-regulated-learning]] — Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection — organized as a feedback cycle in which each phase's outputs function as inputs to subsequent phases. The cyclical claim (that self-reflection quality shapes subsequent forethought quality) distinguishes it from sequential or modular accounts of SRL.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The cycle's architectural claim — that phase-transition quality, not phase-content quality alone, determines SRL development — is the central analytical focus of this report.
> 
> **Operational Indic...
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[cyclical-model-of-self-regulated-learning|Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Cyclical SRL Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Cyclical SRL Model** (Zimmerman): A three-phase recursive model of self-regulated learning — Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection — in which each cycle's outputs (especially attributional inferences) feed forward to shape the regulatory quality of subsequent cycles. The recursiveness explains why SRL capacity is compounding: each high-quality cycle improves subsequent cycles.
> See also: [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning]], [[forethought-phase]], [[performance-phase]], [[self-reflection-phase]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[cyclical-srl-model|Cyclical SRL Model]]*

## _D

> [!definition] **Declarative Memory** **[perm-note]**
> [[declarative-memory|Declarative memory]] refers to the long-term memory system responsible for the conscious, intentional recollection of facts and events. It is termed "declarative" because its contents can, in principle, be stated or "declared" — they are accessible to conscious awareness and can be verbally reported. Declarative memory is further subdivided into [[episodic-memory|episodic memory]] (memory for personally experienced events, located in a specific spatial and temporal context) and [[semantic-memory|semantic memory]] (memory for general knowledge, facts, concepts, and meani...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[declarative-memory|Declarative Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative Memory / Explicit Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The LTM system supporting conscious, intentional recollection of facts (semantic memory) and personally experienced events (episodic memory). Contents are accessible to awareness and can be verbally reported.
> 
> **Boundary:** Does not include procedural skills, conditioning, priming, or other forms of non-declarative memory, which operate outside conscious awareness and are expressed through performance.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The declarative/non-declarative distinction is the most fundamental division within LTM, with direct implications for instructional design.
> 
> **See also:** [...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[declarative-memory-explicit-memory|Declarative Memory / Explicit Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Propositional knowledge about cognitive processes, one's own cognitive characteristics, and the cognitive demands of different task types. Declarative metacognitive knowledge is explicit — it can be stated, communicated, and examined — and relatively stable across time and context. Its primary function is to provide a cognitive map of the terrain that strategic regulation must navigate.
> 
> **Boundary:** Declarative knowledge is inert without procedural and conditional knowledge to activate and deploy it. Knowing that distributed practice is superior to massed practice does not produce distrib...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[declarative-metacognitive-knowledge|Declarative Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative Schema** **[perm-note]**
> A schema encoding propositional knowledge about entities, events, and relationships — the knowing-that dimension of organized knowledge. Amenable to direct instruction. Contrasted with procedural schemas.
> *See also*: [[declarative-schemas]], [[procedural-schemas]], [[john-anderson]]
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[declarative-schema|Declarative Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge** *[needs-note]*
> **Declarative Metacognitive Knowledge:** What one knows *about* cognition — knowledge about oneself as a learner ("I tend to skim when tired and miss important qualifications"), about task demands ("dense theoretical texts require slower reading and more monitoring than narrative texts"), and about strategies ("elaborative interrogation produces deeper understanding than passive re-reading").
> 
> **Procedural Metacognitive Knowledge:** Knowledge of *how* to implement cognitive strategies — knowing not just that elaborative interrogation exists, but how to conduct it effectively during actual r...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Declarative,-Procedural,-and-Conditional-Metacognitive-Knowledge|Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Schemas** *[needs-note]*
> **[[declarative-schemas]]** represent *what* knowledge — facts, concepts, objects, entities, and their properties. The schema for "bird" contains the slots SIZE, PLUMAGE, LOCOMOTION, HABITAT, etc. with default values (small, feathered, flies, trees).
> 
> **[[procedural-schemas]]** represent *how* knowledge — sequences of actions for achieving goals. The schema for "boiling water" specifies: fill kettle → place on heat source → wait for boiling → pour. The slots are ACTION-STEPS, SEQUENCE-CONSTRAINTS, and GOAL-STATE.
> 
> **Conditional schemas (also called pragmatic reasoning schemas)** represent *...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Declarative,-Procedural,-and-Conditional-Schemas|Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Schemas]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative/Procedural/Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Three types of metacognitive knowledge: *Declarative* (knowing *about* oneself, tasks, and strategies), *Procedural* (knowing *how* to implement strategies), and *Conditional* (knowing *when* and *why* to use specific strategies). All three must be developed for expert self-regulation.
> **Operational Indicators:** A learner with strong declarative knowledge knows that elaborative interrogation promotes deeper understanding. A learner with strong procedural knowledge can actually implement it effectively during reading. A learner with strong conditional knowledge knows when to...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[declarativeproceduralconditional-metacognitive-knowledge|Declarative/Procedural/Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Decoding** **[perm-note]**
> The translation of printed symbols (graphemes) into their phonological equivalents (phonemes) and from there to word-level representations. Decoding is the primary focus of early reading instruction and is the foundation on which fluency and comprehension are built. Decoding accuracy (getting the correct phonological representation) is distinguished from decoding automaticity (getting it without consuming working memory resources).
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[decoding]]*

> [!definition] **Deductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> Deductive reasoning is a form of inference in which a conclusion is drawn from premises in such a way that, if the premises are true, the conclusion *must* be true. The relationship between premises and conclusion is one of logical necessity — the conclusion is contained within, or guaranteed by, the premises. Deductive reasoning moves from the general to the specific: from universal rules or principles to particular instances or applications. It is fundamentally distinct from reasoning in which the conclusion is merely probable, plausible, or well-supported by evidence. **What it is NOT**:...
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[deductive-reasoning|Deductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Deep Processing** **[perm-note]**
> **[[deep-processing]]** — A cognitive engagement pattern characterized by elaborative learning strategies: connecting new information to prior knowledge, generating examples, questioning implications, and seeking structural understanding rather than surface memorization. GCT research demonstrates that intrinsic goal framing promotes deep processing while extrinsic goal framing produces equivalent rote retention without deep processing gains. See Section 6.
> **See also:** [[elaboration]], [[Bloom's-Taxonomy]], [[metacognition]]
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[deep-processing|Deep Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Deep Processing / Elaborative Encoding** **[perm-note]**
> The encoding of information at a semantic, relational, and meaningful level, as contrasted with shallow processing of phonological or orthographic features. Deep processing is associated with more durable memory traces, greater resistance to interference, and superior transfer.
> 
> **Boundary:** Depth of processing is better understood as a continuum of elaborative richness than as a binary shallow/deep distinction. EI induces processing toward the deep-elaborative end of this continuum, but the richness of the elaboration is itself variable.
> 
> **Common Misconception:** Deep processing does not...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[deep-processing-elaborative-encoding|Deep Processing / Elaborative Encoding]]*

> [!definition] **Default Value Assignment** **[perm-note]**
> The automatic process by which unfilled schema slots are populated with typical, expected values in the absence of specific input information. Default value assignment is the source of schema-driven inference generation during comprehension and is a primary mechanism of reconstructive memory errors (when default inferences are misremembered as explicitly stated input).
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[default-value-assignment|Default Value Assignment]]*

> [!definition] **Default Values** **[perm-note]**
> Schema-supplied values for unspecified slot contents — the schema's built-in expectations about the most probable instantiation of a variable in the absence of explicit information. The source of schema-driven inference generation.
> *See also*: [[Schema-Theory-Bartlett,-Rumelhart]], [[pragmatic-reasoning-schema]]
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[default-values|Default Values]]*

> [!definition] **Default-Interventionist Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A model of how Type 1 and Type 2 processes interact in which Type 1 generates a default response to nearly every cognitive situation, and Type 2 intervenes only when triggered by specific conditions — perceived difficulty, conflict between competing intuitions, explicit instruction to be careful, time pressure that forces a halt, or the activation of metacognitive monitoring. In the absence of such triggers, the default Type 1 output is endorsed and acted upon without further scrutiny.
> 
> **Boundary:** This is not a claim that Type 1 always runs first in some absolute temporal sense — for som...
> 
> *Source: dual-process-theory-kahneman-system-1-system-2-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[default-interventionist-architecture|Default-Interventionist Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Defeasibility** **[perm-note]**
> A property of justification or reasons: a justification J for belief B is *defeasible* if there is some additional evidence E such that J together with E no longer justifies B. The concept captures the fallibilist claim that justification is always provisional. A defeasible reason is a genuine reason — it actually supports the belief — but it can be "defeated" by defeating evidence. This is to be contrasted with deductive entailment, which is indefeasible: if premises P1 and P2 deductively entail conclusion C, no additional information can make C false given P1 and P2 (though it might give ...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[defeasibility]]*

> [!definition] **Defeat / Defeater** **[perm-note]**
> A defeater for a belief is any evidence or consideration that rationally requires the holder of that belief to revise it. *Rebutting defeaters* provide direct evidence against the target belief. *Undercutting defeaters* provide evidence against the reliability or applicability of the justification for the belief. The defeater framework, developed by John Pollock, is the primary formal vocabulary for understanding how fallibilist revision works.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[defeat-defeater|Defeat / Defeater]]*

> [!definition] **Defensive Communication** **[perm-note]**
> Communication behavior triggered by perceived evaluation, control, or hidden strategy from a conversational partner — characterized by protective self-justification, selective attention to confirming information, and resistance to new framing. Distinguished from supportive communication which triggers openness, problem-orientation, and genuine information exchange. Defensive communication forecloses the grounding process that effective communication requires.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[defensive-communication|Defensive Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Defensive Inference** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A specific sub-process of the Self-Reflection Phase in which learners respond to negative self-evaluation by adopting self-protective attributional strategies and behavioral adjustments that preserve self-esteem at the cost of learning. Contrasted with *adaptive inference*, which involves modifying strategy and increasing effort in response to failure. Defensive inference (e.g., attributing failure to task difficulty or bad luck, reducing effort to preserve ability-attribution, avoiding similar tasks in the future) breaks the Self-Reflection → Forethought feedback link and p...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[defensive-inference|Defensive Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Degenerate Cycle** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An SRL cycle in which the functional connections between phases are absent or minimal — the learner enters the performance phase without meaningful goal setting or strategic planning (no forethought), and exits with global evaluative self-reflection ("that was hard/easy") rather than causal, strategy-specific self-reflection. A degenerate cycle proceeds through three temporal phases without the inter-phase signal transmission that makes the cycle self-modifying.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The degenerate cycle is proposed as the primary explanation for why SRL trainin...
> 
> *Attribution: Original Terminology*
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[degenerate-cycle|Degenerate Cycle]]*

> [!definition] **Deliberate Practice** **[perm-note]**
> [[deliberate-practice|Deliberate practice]] is a specific type of effortful activity, distinct from mere repetition or routine performance, that is designed to improve specific aspects of performance. Its defining characteristics include: (a) a well-defined performance goal that targets specific aspects of the task; (b) full concentration and effort — the activity is not inherently enjoyable and requires the allocation of attentional resources; (c) immediate and informative feedback that allows the practitioner to detect errors and adjust performance; (d) iterative refinement through repeti...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[deliberate-practice|Deliberate Practice]]*

> [!definition] **Deliberative Democracy** **[perm-note]**
> A normative model of democratic governance emphasizing the role of reasoned, inclusive, and open public deliberation in the formation of legitimate collective decisions. Empirically, deliberative democratic theory has inspired a research program on "mini-publics" — citizens' assemblies, deliberative polls, participatory processes — that tests the conditions under which ordinary citizens engage in high-quality political reasoning. Fishkin's Deliberative Polling research is the most systematic empirical programme in this tradition.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[deliberative-democracy|Deliberative Democracy]]*

> [!definition] **Delphi Consensus Definition** **[perm-note]**
> "Critical thinking is the process of purposeful, self-regulatory judgment. This process gives reasoned consideration to evidence, contexts, conceptualizations, methods, and criteria... CT is pervasive and self-rectifying human phenomenon...
