The ideal critical thinker is habitually inquisitive, well-informed, trustful of reason, open-minded, flexible, fair-minded in evaluation, honest in facing personal biases, prudent in making judgments, willing to reconsider, clear about issues, orderly in complex matters, diligent in seeking relevant information, reasonable in the selection of criteria, ...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[delphi-consensus-definition|Delphi Consensus Definition]]*

> [!definition] **Delphi Report** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Delphi Report ([[Peter-Facione|Facione]], 1990)
> **Definition:** A consensus statement on critical thinking produced by a panel of 46 CT experts using the Delphi method. Defines CT as "purposeful, self-regulatory judgment" and identifies six core skills (interpretation, analysis, evaluation, inference, explanation, self-regulation) and seven dispositional categories.
> **Boundary conditions:** Represents expert consensus of a specific historical moment (1988-1989); some scholars (notably Richard Paul) consider it insufficiently structural.
> **Report significance:** Provides the PENCRI...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[delphi-report|Delphi Report]]*

> [!definition] **Demand-Architecture Isomorphism** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that the level of cognitive architecture engaged in processing a text or task is isomorphic to the level required to satisfy the task's demand structure. Under this principle, a task requiring textbase-level response produces textbase-level encoding regardless of effort; a task requiring situation model construction forces that level of encoding.
> 
> **Boundary:** This principle operates at the level of task design, not individual learner effort. Additional effort on a textbase-level task does not produce situation model encoding; only restructuring the demand does.
> 
> **See also:*...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[demand-architecture-isomorphism|Demand-Architecture Isomorphism]]*

> [!definition] **Depth of Processing** **[perm-note]**
> A framework holding that memory retention is a function of the depth at which information is processed during encoding, with semantic processing (connecting meaning to existing knowledge) producing more durable representations than phonological processing (attending to sound) or orthographic processing (attending to form). Applied to vocabulary learning: processing a word's meaning in relation to existing semantic knowledge produces more durable lexical representations than processing only its form or approximate contextual approximation.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Depth" in this framework refers to e...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[depth-of-processing|Depth of Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Depth of Word Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The multidimensional representation of a lexical item in long-term memory, comprising at minimum: (1) *form knowledge* — orthographic and phonological representation; (2) *meaning knowledge* — core denotative meaning, semantic range, and connotative associations; (3) *use knowledge* — syntactic behaviour, collocational constraints, register appropriateness, and pragmatic application. Full productive mastery requires all three dimensions to be robustly encoded and fluently accessible; receptive reading comprehension requires a lower threshold, but even this threshold is higher than is common...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[depth-of-word-knowledge|Depth of Word Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **The Depth-Frequency Multiplier** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that in lexical learning (and, by extension, in other domains of knowledge acquisition), the learning value of a given number of exposures scales non-linearly with the depth of processing at each exposure. A few deep encounters reliably outperform many shallow ones in producing durable, precise, integrated lexical knowledge. The "multiplier" is the ratio of retention per hour under deep processing versus shallow processing conditions, which research suggests is substantially greater than 1 — often estimated at 3:1 or higher.
> 
> **Boundary:** This is a theoretical construct integ...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-depth-frequency-multiplier|The Depth-Frequency Multiplier]]*

> [!definition] **Description Field Components** **[perm-note]**
> An effective description contains:
> 
> **1. Invocation triggers** (when to use this agent):
> ```yaml
> description: Use PROACTIVELY when designing APIs, defining endpoints, or planning REST architectures.
> ```
> 
> **2. Domain keywords** (what this agent knows):
> ```yaml
> description: Expert in React, TypeScript, component architecture, hooks, and modern frontend patterns.
> ```
> 
> **3. Task verbs** (what actions this agent performs):
> ```yaml
> description: Reviews code for security, analyzes vulnerabilities, audits dependencies, scans for OWASP Top 10.
> ```
> 
> **4. Exclusions** (when NOT to use):
> ```yaml
> descri...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[description-field-components|Description Field Components]]*

> [!definition] **Desirable Difficulties** **[perm-note]**
> Learning conditions that impair immediate acquisition performance but substantially enhance long-term retention and transfer. Paradigmatic examples include spaced rather than massed practice, interleaved rather than blocked practice, testing rather than restudy, and reducing contextual cues. The "desirable" qualifier distinguishes these difficulties from undesirable ones (extraneous load): desirable difficulties impair performance by strengthening encoding processes (retrieval effort, generative processing, contextual variability), not by wasting cognitive resources on task-irrelevant proce...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[desirable-difficulties|Desirable Difficulties]]*

> [!definition] **Developmental Arc Model** **[perm-note]**
> A four-stage model of metacognitive scaffold progression in PKB systems: (1) Structured Dependence — full scaffolding for all regulatory functions; (2) Guided Autonomy — selective fading of mastered functions; (3) Self-Directed Monitoring — independent regulation with occasional scaffold use; (4) Reflective Mastery — learner designs and modifies own scaffolding systems. The arc is domain-specific and non-linear.
> 
> **Boundary:** The model describes a developmental trajectory, not a typology of learners. A single learner may occupy different stages across different domains simultaneously. The ...
> 
> *Source: pkb-metacognitive-scaffolding-for-study-and-planning-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: cognitive science — metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[developmental-arc-model|Developmental Arc Model]]*

> [!definition] **Developmental Need Satisfaction History** **[perm-note]**
> The accumulation of need-satisfaction and need-frustration experiences across development that is theorized in COT to be the primary determinant of adult causality orientations. Not directly measured in GCOS studies but operationalized through retrospective self-report of parental and educational environment in some studies.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[developmental-need-satisfaction-history|Developmental Need Satisfaction History]]*

> [!definition] **Differentiated Ability Concept** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The cognitive developmental achievement, typically consolidated in middle childhood, by which children come to understand that effort and ability are independent causes of performance outcomes, such that high effort required to succeed can imply lower ability relative to others who succeed with less effort. This understanding is the developmental precondition for ego involvement, as it enables the inference that failure implies low ability.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The differentiated ability concept is the developmental foundation of the approach-avoidance asymmetry...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[differentiated-ability-concept|Differentiated Ability Concept]]*

> [!definition] **Differentiated Conception of Ability** **[perm-note]**
> In a differentiated conception of ability, effort and ability are understood as *separate* entities that can partially cancel each other out. If I have to try very hard to accomplish something that others accomplish easily, this is evidence that my ability is lower, not higher. Under this conception, demonstrating high ability means achieving more with less effort (or the same outcomes with less effort) relative to others. This is the conception of ability operative in *ego involvement*, and it is cognitively demanding — children typically do not fully develop it until late childhood or ear...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[differentiated-conception-of-ability|Differentiated Conception of Ability]]*

> [!definition] **DIME Annotation Protocol** **[perm-note]**
> A reading annotation system derived from the Event Indexing Model's five situational dimensions, designed to externalize the situation model rather than the textbase. Annotations are organized around five types: Causal chain annotations (→ markers for causal relationships); Temporal gap annotations (T? for unclear sequence); Spatial annotations (S: for spatial configuration); Intentional annotations (I: for agent goals); Entity annotations (E: for state tracking). Includes compression annotations at section ends as quality checks.
> 
> **Boundary:** Original synthesis by this report. Well-motiv...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[dime-annotation-protocol|DIME Annotation Protocol]]*

> [!definition] **Direct-Access Model** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>The direct-access model of metacognitive monitoring</span> posits that metacognitive judgments result from privileged introspective access to memory trace strength. On this model, learners can directly "read" whether a memory trace is strong or weak, present or absent.
> 
> **Status:** Falsified. The accumulated evidence shows that metacognitive judgments are based on heuristic cues that only partially correlate with actual memory strength. If direct access were available, the systematic calibration errors documented at Level 1 would be inexplicable — there would b...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[direct-access-model|Direct-Access Model]]*

> [!definition] **Directive Context** **[perm-note]**
> A naturally occurring textual context that provides sufficient semantic, syntactic, and pragmatic information for a reader to infer the meaning of an unfamiliar word with reasonable precision. Contrasted with non-directive contexts (which neither help nor hinder inference) and misdirective contexts (which actively support incorrect inferences). Research suggests that directive contexts constitute a minority of naturally occurring contexts in which unfamiliar words appear.
> 
> **Boundary:** Context directiveness is not binary — it exists on a continuum from maximally misdirective to maximally d...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[directive-context|Directive Context]]*

> [!definition] **Directive Function** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which a committed goal organizes attention preferentially toward goal-relevant activities and away from goal-irrelevant activities, including the suppression of competing tasks, the filtering of competing stimuli, and the privileged occupancy of working memory by goal-related representations.
> 
> **Boundary:** The directive function operates on activities the performer recognizes as relevant to the goal; activities whose relevance is not perceived will not be selected even if they would advance the goal, which is one mechanism by which goals can paradoxically narrow performanc...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[directive-function|Directive Function]]*

> [!definition] **Disciplinary Literacy** **[perm-note]**
> Disciplinary literacy refers to the discipline-specific reading competencies required to comprehend and critically evaluate texts within a particular academic field — including knowledge of disciplinary text genres (research articles, theoretical reviews, case studies), disciplinary vocabulary, disciplinary epistemological norms (what counts as evidence, how claims are warranted), and disciplinary rhetorical conventions (how arguments are structured, what objections must be addressed). Disciplinary literacy is distinct from general academic reading ability and must be developed separately f...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[disciplinary-literacy|Disciplinary Literacy]]*

> [!definition] **Disclosure-Responsiveness Cycle** **[perm-note]**
> The reciprocal relational dynamic in which personal disclosure by one partner creates an opportunity for demonstrating responsiveness; high-quality responsiveness from the receiving partner reinforces the disclosure partner's sense of being genuinely known and cared for; this enhanced sense of being known generates motivation for further authentic disclosure; and the cycle continues. The disclosure-responsiveness cycle, when functioning well, creates a progressive deepening of authentic relational connection and increasing perceived partner responsiveness over relational time. *Break points...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[disclosure-responsiveness-cycle|Disclosure-Responsiveness Cycle]]*

> [!definition] **Discovery Learning** **[perm-note]**
> An instructional approach in which learners are required to discover principles, rules, or relationships for themselves through exploration, experimentation, and inductive reasoning, rather than being provided with them through direct instruction. Discovery learning has a Piagetian warrant (self-generated activity promotes accommodation) but a contested empirical record — particularly for novice learners who lack the prior knowledge to guide productive exploration. Bruner is its most prominent advocate; Ausubel and, later, Kirschner, Sweller, and Clark are its most prominent critics.
> 
> **See...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[discovery-learning|Discovery Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Discrepancy Reduction Model** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>The discrepancy reduction model</span> of [[metacognitive-regulation|metacognitive control]] proposes that learners allocate study time by monitoring the discrepancy between their current perceived state of learning and their desired target state, then directing effort toward items and topics where the discrepancy is largest. Study terminates when the discrepancy reaches an acceptable threshold or when external constraints intervene.
> 
> [**Discrepancy-Reduction-Model**:: The model of metacognitive control proposing that study time allocation is driven by the perc...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[discrepancy-reduction-model|Discrepancy Reduction Model]]*

> [!definition] **Disposition** **[perm-note]**
> A stable cognitive tendency or habit of mind that inclines a person toward or away from CT engagement. Key CT dispositions include inquisitiveness, truth-seeking, open-mindedness, analyticity, systematicity, self-confidence in reasoning, and cognitive maturity. Dispositions are distinguishable from skills: a person may have the skill to think critically without the disposition to deploy it, and vice versa.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[disposition]]*

> [!definition] **Dissolved Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> In the Assumption Audit framework of this report: a previously accepted assumption that does not survive scrutiny and cannot be retained even in modified form. Distinguished from Revised assumptions (contain truth but need modification) and Retained assumptions (survive scrutiny with full support). Two assumptions in this report were dissolved: Assumption 5 (domain-generality) and Assumption 6 (brief intervention transfer).
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[dissolved-assumption|Dissolved Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Distinctiveness** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which an encoding stands out as unique relative to the surrounding set of encodings. A distinctive encoding is processed in a way that differentiates it from similar items, reducing interference and improving retrieval specificity. Together with elaboration, distinctiveness was incorporated into the LOP framework to explain within-level retention differences that simple depth accounts could not address.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[distinctiveness]]*

> [!definition] **Distributed Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework holding that cognitive processes are distributed across individuals, artefacts, and environmental structures in sociotechnical systems, and that the appropriate unit of cognitive analysis is the system as a whole rather than the individual mind within it. Developed through ethnographic study of real-world cognitive systems (naval navigation, airline cockpits, scientific laboratories). Convergent with but methodologically distinct from Extended Mind Theory: Hutchins focuses on social-material systems; Clark and Chalmers focus on individual organism-environment coupl...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[distributed-cognition|Distributed Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Distributed Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A conception of self-regulation in which the regulatory functions described by Zimmerman (monitoring, standard-setting, strategic adjustment) are partially or primarily instantiated in tools, artifacts, other people, and environmental structures rather than in the individual learner. Distributed regulation is not a failure of self-regulation but an alternative architecture for achieving regulatory functions. [[situated-cognition]] and [[situated-learning]] frameworks emphasize distributed regulation; the Zimmerman model, by contrast, treats environmental scaffolds as *suppor...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[distributed-regulation|Distributed Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Distributed Representation** **[perm-note]**
> In cognitive neuroscience, a distributed representation is one in which a concept or memory is encoded not by a single neuron or discrete storage location but by the pattern of activation across a large population of neurons. Each neuron participates in the representation of many different memories, and each memory involves the coordinated activation of many different neurons. This encoding scheme provides tremendous storage efficiency and natural generalization (similar memories produce overlapping patterns) but also introduces the potential for interference when patterns overlap excessive...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[distributed-representation|Distributed Representation]]*

> [!definition] **Hedonic Adaptation** **[perm-note]**
> The well-documented tendency for people to return to a stable baseline level of well-being following positive or negative life events. Related to the attainment paradox in GCT: extrinsic goal attainment may fail to produce durable well-being gains partly because of hedonic adaptation, while intrinsic goal pursuit may be more resistant to adaptation because its well-being benefits are process-based (ongoing during pursuit) rather than outcome-based (concentrated at the moment of attainment).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[hedonic-adaptation|Hedonic Adaptation]]*

> [!definition] **Dopamine Prediction Error** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The difference between the actual reward received and the reward predicted by current context-action associations. Positive prediction error (better than expected) strengthens the context-action association; negative prediction error (worse than expected) weakens it; no prediction error (exactly as expected) produces no associative change. The brain's dopaminergic system encodes prediction errors, not rewards per se.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The prediction error mechanism explains the [[hedonic-adaptation]] problem in habit maintenance (expected rewards generate no...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[dopamine-prediction-error|Dopamine Prediction Error]]*

> [!definition] **Double-Loop Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Chris Argyris's concept for learning that examines and revises the governing assumptions and values underlying behavior, as distinguished from single-loop learning that adjusts behavior within existing frameworks. Achievable primarily through dialogic communication and inquiry; blocked by defensive reasoning patterns. See [[Argyris-Double-Loop-Learning]], [[double-loop-learning]], [[reflective-practice]].
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[double-loop-learning|Double-Loop Learning]]*

> [!definition] **The Doubtful Situation** **[perm-note]**
> The "doubtful situation" is Dewey's term for the triggering condition of genuine inquiry: a situation that is ambiguous, problematic, or resistant to existing habits of response. It is not subjectively created by performing Cartesian doubt; it is objectively encountered when the organism's purposes meet resistance in the environment. [[productive-struggle|Productive struggle]] and [[productive-failure|productive failure]] — contemporary instructional techniques — are essentially methods for engineering genuine doubtful situations within educational contexts.
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-doubtful-situation|The Doubtful Situation]]*

> [!definition] **Dual Register Diagnostic** **[perm-note]**
> A practical diagnostic criterion for classifying EI questions: a question is in Register 1 if it can be answered by someone who knows the text but has no other domain knowledge; it is in Register 2 if answering requires integrating text propositions with world knowledge not present in the text.
> 
> **Boundary:** Register classification is not binary in all cases; some questions fall between registers or shift register depending on the reader's prior knowledge level. The diagnostic is most useful as a design principle.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** Apply the test: "Could a reader with only this t...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[dual-register-diagnostic|Dual Register Diagnostic]]*

> [!definition] **Dual-Process Theory** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Dual-Process Theory (Kahneman, 2011; Stanovich, 2011; Evans, 2008)
> **Definition:** A cognitive framework positing two fundamentally different modes of processing: [[system-1-and-system-2|System 1]] (fast, automatic, intuitive, low-effort) and System 2 (slow, deliberate, analytical, effortful). Critical thinking is primarily a System 2 activity that involves overriding or supplementing System 1 outputs.
> **Boundary conditions:** The dichotomy is a simplification; actual processing involves continuous interaction between systems. Not all System 1 processing is error-prone, and not al...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[dual-process-theory|Dual-Process Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Dual-System Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The framework distinguishing Type 1 (automatic, fast, associative, context-sensitive) from Type 2 (controlled, slow, rule-based, effortful) processing. Habits are Type 1 productions; deliberate behavior change requires Type 2 intervention. The critical dynamic is that Type 2 processes can initiate new behavioral patterns but are too cognitively expensive to sustain them; enduring behavioral change requires the transition of control to Type 1 processes.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The dual-system framing explains why willpower-based strategies produce initial behavior ...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[dual-system-architecture|Dual-System Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Dual-Task Interference** **[perm-note]**
> The degradation in performance on one or both tasks when two tasks are performed concurrently, attributable to competition for shared cognitive resources or structural bottlenecks in central processing. The magnitude of interference depends on the degree to which the tasks overlap in their resource demands — tasks that draw on different processing codes, modalities, or stages may show minimal interference, while tasks competing for the same central executive resources show substantial performance costs.
> 
> **Boundary:** Dual-task interference is not inevitable. When one or both tasks are suff...
> 
> *Source: walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[dual-task-interference|Dual-Task Interference]]*

> [!definition] **Educative vs. Miseducative Experience** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Educative/Miseducative Experience (Dewey, 1938)
> **Definition:** An experience is educative when it simultaneously has intrinsic worth and promotes further growth — when it enriches the learner's capacity for future experience. An experience is miseducative when it arrests or distorts growth, even if immediately pleasant or measurably productive. The distinction is evaluative and consequentialist: judgments depend on long-term developmental trajectories.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Not all engaging experiences are educative; not all difficult experiences are miseducative. The criterio...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[educative-vs.-miseducative-experience|Educative vs. Miseducative Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Dyslexia** **[perm-note]**
> Dyslexia is a specific learning disability neurobiological in origin. It is characterized by difficulties with accurate and/or fluent word recognition and by poor spelling and decoding abilities. These difficulties typically result from a deficit in the phonological component of language that is often unexpected in relation to other cognitive abilities and the provision of effective classroom instruction.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[dyslexia]]*

## _F

> [!definition] **Fallibilism** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical thesis that any belief, however well-justified, could in principle be false, and that the evidence or reasoning supporting it could be overturned by subsequent inquiry. Fallibilism does not entail that our beliefs *are* false or probably false, nor that they are unjustified — only that their justification is inherently defeasible. The thesis applies paradigmatically to empirical knowledge but has been extended by various philosophers to moral, mathematical, and logical domains. Fallibilism is a thesis about the *epistemic status* of beliefs, not a prediction about how ofte...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[fallibilism]]*

> [!definition] **Falsificationism** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's philosophy of science, proposed as an alternative to inductivism: science proceeds not by accumulating confirming instances but by formulating bold, falsifiable hypotheses and subjecting them to rigorous attempts at disconfirmation. A hypothesis gains credibility by surviving falsification attempts, not by accumulating positive evidence. Influential in science education for its emphasis on testability and its recognition of the logical asymmetry between confirmation and refutation; criticized by Bayesian philosophers for not describing actual scientific practice accurately.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[falsificationism]]*

> [!definition] **Far Transfer** **[perm-note]**
> The application of learned principles or skills to domains structurally different from the learning context. Distinguished from near transfer (application in similar contexts). Far transfer requires abstraction of structural principles beyond the specific surface features of the learning context.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[far-transfer|Far Transfer]]*

> [!definition] **Far-Transfer** **[perm-note]**
> Application of learned principles to structurally different domains. Relevance: primary criterion for whether critical thinking instruction changes real-world judgment.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[far-transfer]]*

> [!definition] **Feed-Forward** **[perm-note]**
> The prospective, action-guiding dimension of feedback: information specifying what the learner should do next, which strategies to deploy, and how to approach the next iteration of the task. Distinguished from backward-facing evaluation of what was done. Effective feedback integrates backward diagnosis with forward navigation, but should weight the latter more heavily.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[feed-forward]]*

> [!definition] **Feeling of Knowing** **[perm-note]**
> A phenomenological signal indicating that information is known even when it cannot currently be retrieved. FOK judgments are moderately valid predictors of eventual retrieval success but are subject to systematic biases, including source-based familiarity inflation. See [[Feeling-of-Knowing-—-FOK]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[feeling-of-knowing|Feeling of Knowing]]*

> [!definition] **Feeling of Knowing — FOK** *[needs-note]*
> A [[feeling-of-knowing|feeling of knowing]] is a metacognitive monitoring event that occurs when retrieval has been attempted but has failed, and the individual nevertheless senses that the unretrieved information is stored in memory and would be recognised if encountered. FOK judgments are driven by the accessibility of partial information related to the target rather than by the actual availability of the target itself.
> 
> **Boundary:** FOK is distinct from tip-of-the-tongue (TOT) states, though the two often co-occur. TOT involves the specific sense that retrieval is imminent; FOK is a bro...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Feeling-of-Knowing-—-FOK|Feeling of Knowing — FOK]]*

> [!definition] **Effectance** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective experience of being effective in interaction with the environment — the psychological state of genuine competence satisfaction. Effectance is not the same as objective performance: a student can perform well without experiencing effectance (if success feels attributable to luck, scaffolding, or low task difficulty) and can experience effectance from failure in the right challenge context (when effort was appropriate and feedback is informational). The SDT competence need is for effectance experience, not for objective achievement.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[effectance]]*

> [!definition] **Effectance Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> [**Effectance-Motivation-Definition**:: Robert White's (1959) concept of an innate motivational propensity to interact effectively with the environment — to explore, manipulate, master, and understand. White proposed effectance as a non-drive-based motivation that is intrinsically satisfying, contrasting it with Hull's drive reduction theory. Effectance motivation is a direct theoretical ancestor of CET's perceived competence construct and of BPNT's competence need.]
> *See also: [[effectance-motivation]], [[competence]], [[basic-psychological-needs-theory]]*
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[effectance-motivation|Effectance Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Effective Communication** **[perm-note]**
> A joint cognitive achievement in which both sender and receiver successfully construct shared understanding, alignment of intent and interpretation, and appropriate relational calibration — requiring not just technical message transmission but grounding, common ground maintenance, emotional attunement, and metacognitive monitoring from both parties.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[effective-communication|Effective Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Efficacy Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> The degree of correspondence between a person's efficacy beliefs and their actual capability for the relevant performance, where well-calibrated beliefs match capability, overconfident beliefs exceed capability, and underconfident beliefs fall below capability. Calibration is itself a function of the person's history of efficacy-relevant feedback, the accuracy with which they have processed that feedback, and the specificity of the domain within which the calibration is being assessed.
> 
> **Boundary:** Calibration is not the same as accuracy of self-perception in general — it specifically con...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[efficacy-calibration|Efficacy Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Efficacy Hierarchy** **[perm-note]**
> The hierarchically organized structure in which a person's efficacy beliefs are arranged across levels of generality, with broader beliefs at higher levels constraining but not fully determining the narrower beliefs at lower levels. Movement up or down the hierarchy is mediated by the person's analysis of structural similarity between the levels, with greater analyzed similarity producing tighter coupling between adjacent levels.
> 
> **Boundary:** The hierarchy is not a strict containment relationship in which lower-level beliefs are subsets of higher-level beliefs — the relationship is more p...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[efficacy-hierarchy|Efficacy Hierarchy]]*

> [!definition] **Effort Abandonment** **[perm-note]**
> The monitoring-regulation decoupling mechanism in which a comprehension failure is correctly detected, an appropriate fix-up strategy is identified, but the reader declines to deploy it because the perceived effort cost exceeds the perceived value of correction. A motivational regulation failure rather than a monitoring or strategy knowledge failure. Addressed by interventions that explicitly alter the perceived ROI of fix-up strategy deployment.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[effort-abandonment|Effort Abandonment]]*

> [!definition] **Effort Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> A behavioral-area component in Pintrich's SRL framework referring to learners' ability to manage their effort and attention during learning in the face of distractions, difficult material, or flagging motivation. One of the most practically significant MSLQ subscales because it predicts persistence under adversity more directly than motivational belief subscales.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[effort-regulation|Effort Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Effort-Strategy Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary linking effort and strategy in attributional inner speech. Pure effort attribution ("I just need to try harder") can produce burnout in the absence of strategic adaptation. Effort-strategy vocabulary couples effort-attribution with strategic-attribution: "I need more effort *and* a better strategy," "working harder on this *approach* vs. working harder in general." This vocabulary captures the full causal chain of academic mastery — effort is necessary but insufficient without strategic flexibility.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[attribution-theory]], [[Growth-Minds...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[effort-strategy-vocabulary|Effort-Strategy Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Figure Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon that in syllogistic reasoning, the arrangement of terms across the two premises (the "figure" of the syllogism) systematically affects difficulty and error rates, even when the logical form is held constant. The MMT account explains figure effects as consequences of the different mental model constructions induced by different term arrangements — some arrangements make it harder to combine the information from the two premises into a coherent model.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[figure-effect|Figure Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Filter Bubble / Information Cocoon** **[perm-note]**
> A metaphor for the self-reinforcing information environment that results when users preferentially consume identity-confirming political content, amplified by algorithmic content personalization. The filter bubble concept (Pariser, 2011) emphasizes algorithmic filtering; the information cocoon concept (Sunstein, 2017) emphasizes voluntary selective exposure. Empirical research has complicated both accounts, finding that cross-cutting information exposure is more common than the metaphors suggest, but that exposure does not reliably produce belief updating.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[filter-bubble-information-cocoon|Filter Bubble / Information Cocoon]]*

> [!definition] **Fix-Up Strategy** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory action deployed in response to a detected comprehension failure. Fix-up strategies range on a continuum from local (re-reading a sentence) to global (returning to an earlier passage, seeking background knowledge, constructing a summary diagram). Strategy activation failure — the inability to select an appropriate fix-up strategy when a failure has been detected — is one of the three mechanisms of monitoring-regulation decoupling identified in this analysis.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[fix-up-strategy|Fix-Up Strategy]]*

> [!definition] **Fixed Mindset** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that intelligence and ability are fixed traits that cannot be substantially changed. Associated in Dweck's framework with challenge-avoidance, ability attribution for failure, and helpless response to difficulty. In this report's reconstruction, "fixed mindset behavior" is better understood as the result of stable-and-uncontrollable failure attributions combined with performance goal orientation, not as the downstream effect of a coherent intelligence-immutability belief.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[fixed-mindset|Fixed Mindset]]*

> [!definition] **Flavell's Metacognitive Framework** *[needs-note]*
> Flavell distinguished **[[metacognitive-knowledge|Metacognitive Knowledge]]** (stable declarative knowledge about persons, tasks, and strategies — knowing that re-reading is an ineffective consolidation strategy, for instance) from **[[metacognitive-experiences|Metacognitive Experiences]]** (in-the-moment feelings of knowing, confusion, or fluency that trigger regulatory responses during active learning). This distinction is foundational to SRL theory: metacognitive knowledge provides the repertoire from which the learner selects strategies; metacognitive experiences are the monitoring sign...
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Flavell's-Metacognitive-Framework|Flavell's Metacognitive Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Flavell's Metacognitive Taxonomy** *[needs-note]*
> [[Flavell's-Metacognitive-Framework|Flavell's framework]] organized metacognition into four interacting components: metacognitive knowledge (beliefs about one's own cognition, about tasks, and about strategies), [[metacognitive-experience|metacognitive experiences]] (conscious feelings and judgments that accompany cognitive activity), goals or tasks (the objectives that drive cognitive engagement), and actions or strategies (the behavioral responses deployed to achieve those objectives). The taxonomy was explicitly modular: each component could be studied in relative isolation, and the prim...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narens-metacognitive-control-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Flavell's-Metacognitive-Taxonomy|Flavell's Metacognitive Taxonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Fleshing Out** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which an initially minimal mental model is enriched with background knowledge from long-term memory to represent additional properties of the described situation. Fleshing out is necessary for realistic comprehension and everyday inference, but it also introduces the possibility of model enrichment from incorrect or biased background beliefs. Distinguished from schema activation in that fleshing out produces a unique representational token for a specific situation rather than importing a pre-stored template.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[fleshing-out|Fleshing Out]]*

> [!definition] **Flooding** **[perm-note]**
> A physiological state of emotional overwhelm during interpersonal conflict characterized by elevated heart rate (typically above ~100 bpm), cortisol release, attentional narrowing, and impaired complex information processing — which directly disables perspective-taking, nuanced response generation, and cooperative communication behaviors. Physiological self-soothe (genuine rest break of ~20 minutes) is required to restore communicative capacity from flooding.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[flooding]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive error in which ease of information processing (fluency) is mistaken for depth of understanding. Readers experiencing the fluency illusion terminate comprehension effort prematurely because the subjective experience of reading feels easy.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Fluency Illusion is distinct from the fluency itself — fluency (automatic decoding) is valuable and should be developed. The Illusion is the *misinterpretation* of fluency as comprehension.
> 
> **Etymology:** From Latin *fluere* (to flow) — the metaphor of information "flowing" easily, which is mistaken for understanding.
> 
> ...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[fluency-illusion|Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **The Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The systematic misperception that familiarity with information constitutes understanding of it. When reading, the subjective experience of "getting it" is strongly influenced by prior exposure to vocabulary and concepts, even when genuine comprehension is absent. This causes systematic overconfidence in comprehension assessments that do not use retrieval-based testing.
> **Operational Indicators:** The Fluency Illusion is active when a learner reports high comprehension confidence immediately after reading but cannot reconstruct core claims from memory without consulting the t...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-fluency-illusion|The Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **The Fluency Illusion in Academic Reading** **[perm-note]**
> The [[fluency-illusion|Fluency Illusion]] is the systematic tendency to overestimate comprehension of text that has been repeatedly processed, because the *ease of processing* (fluency) is misinterpreted as evidence of *depth of understanding* (comprehension). Familiar text feels more comprehensible even when conceptual understanding has not improved — because perceptual fluency (faster word recognition, smoother syntactic parsing) is a salient, cognitively available cue that is consciously experienced, while comprehension depth (situation model quality) is not directly introspectable. The ...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-fluency-illusion-in-academic-reading|The Fluency Illusion in Academic Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which reading fluency — the smooth, automatic processing of text at the lexical and syntactic levels — suppresses comprehension monitoring signals at the situational coherence level. Arises because the metacognitive system uses processing fluency as a proxy for comprehension success, generating false-positive comprehension signals when fluent decoding coexists with situation-model incoherence. A specific instantiation of the [[fluency-illusion]] in the context of comprehension monitoring.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[fluency-trap|Fluency Trap]]*

> [!definition] **The Fluency Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive error in which the subjective fluency of a writing activity is mistaken for evidence of strong encoding. Summarisation and note-taking from open sources are the primary fluency traps in the writing-to-learn domain, because they produce fluent output that reflects the source's structure rather than the learner's knowledge transformation.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-fluency-trap|The Fluency Trap]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency-Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The pervasive metacognitive error in which familiarity with recently processed material is misinterpreted as comprehension. The fluency illusion is the primary mechanism by which re-reading produces the feeling of learning without producing genuine encoding. It is the most consequential comprehension monitoring failure in academic reading.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[fluency-illusion]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency-Illusion|Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive error of conflating the subjective ease of processing (processing fluency) with actual comprehension depth. In reading, speed-trained readers often experience high processing fluency — smooth, fast reading — while their situation model construction has degraded. The illusion is produced because monitoring failure prevents awareness of the comprehension deficit.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[fluency-illusionfluency-illusion|Fluency-Illusion|Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **Foregrounding Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical finding that information causally relevant to current events and protagonist goals is more accessible in working memory during reading than other text content, even when the other content is equally important by global coherence standards. Reflects working memory's selective maintenance of the current-state situation model representation.
> 
> **Research Status:** Well-established through multiple experimental paradigms; consistent with both the Landscape Model and Event Indexing Model predictions.
> 
> **See also:** [[working-memory-in-reading]], [[situation-model]], [[Working-Memory...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[foregrounding-effect|Foregrounding Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Forethought Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The first of three phases in Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model, occurring before a learning task. Involves task analysis (goal-setting, strategic planning) and motivational belief activation (self-efficacy, outcome expectations, goal orientation). Quality of forethought determines the quality of subsequent performance and self-reflection phases. *SDT connection: autonomous motivation is the motivational substrate that enables productive forethought; controlled motivation or amotivation produces defensive or avoidant forethought.*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[forethought-phase|Forethought Phase]]*

> [!definition] **The Forethought Phase** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The first phase of the [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-SRL-Model]], occurring *prior to* any learning effort. It comprises two major subsystems — Task Analysis and Motivational Beliefs — whose function is to prepare both the cognitive architecture and the motivational substrate required for effective self-regulation during the subsequent Performance Phase.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This phase is the design specification for the reading session that follows. When it is high-quality, the Performance Phase has clear direction, appropriate resources, and sufficient motivational ...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-forethought-phase|The Forethought Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Forethought-Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The first phase of Zimmerman's SRL cycle, encompassing Task Analysis (goal-setting and strategic planning) and Motivational Beliefs (self-efficacy, outcome expectancy, goal orientation, and intrinsic interest). The quality of the Forethought Phase is the single strongest predictor of subsequent self-regulatory effectiveness.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[forethought-phase]]*

> [!definition] **Forgetting** **[perm-note]**
> Forgetting is the loss of access to previously encoded information. It may result from trace decay (the hypothesised deterioration of memory traces over time due to metabolic processes), from [[Interference-Theory|interference]] (the disruption of target memories by competing memories acquired before or after them), from cue-dependent inaccessibility (the absence of effective retrieval cues), or from motivated suppression. Contemporary research emphasises that most everyday forgetting reflects retrieval failure rather than storage loss — the memory persists but cannot be accessed under curr...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[forgetting]]*

> [!definition] **Formative Assessment** **[perm-note]**
> Assessment activity carried out by teachers and/or by students that provides information to be used as feedback to modify the teaching and learning activities in which they are engaged. The defining feature is *real-time instructional adjustment*: information gathered is used to adapt the learning process while it is still ongoing, in contrast to summative assessment which reports learning after the fact. Formative assessment is distinguished not by its format but by its *function* — the same quiz can be formative or summative depending on whether its results shape what happens next.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[formative-assessment|Formative Assessment]]*

> [!definition] **Formative Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback provided *during* a learning episode, while the learner still has time and opportunity to act on it and close the gap between current and desired performance. Contrasted with summative feedback, which reports performance after the fact. The defining feature is not format or content but *functional timing*: it must arrive when it can still influence the course of learning.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[formative-feedback|Formative Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **The Four Areas of Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> *Cognition:* the strategic and metacognitive operations the learner deploys on the material itself, ranging from low-level rehearsal to high-level critical evaluation.
> *Motivation and Affect:* the regulation of the beliefs and emotions that determine what the learner is willing and able to do, including efficacy beliefs, value perceptions, goal orientations, and academic emotions.
> *Behavior:* the observable allocation of effort, time, and help-seeking — the layer at which cognitive and motivational regulation become visible as study activity.
> *Context:* the modification of the immediate lea...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-four-areas-of-regulation|The Four Areas of Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **The Four Components of 4C/ID** **[perm-note]**
> The [[four-component-instructional-design|Four Component Instructional Design]] model specifies four interrelated components for designing training programs for complex learning:
> 
> 1. **Learning Tasks** — Authentic whole-task experiences organized in task classes of increasing complexity, providing the backbone of the learning process through which learners develop and integrate knowledge, skills, and attitudes.
> 2. **Supportive Information** — The bridge between what learners already know and what they need to know to perform non-recurrent aspects of learning tasks, supporting schema constru...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-four-components-of-4cid|The Four Components of 4C/ID]]*

> [!definition] **The Four-by-Four Matrix** **[perm-note]**
> The framework's organizing structure: four phases (forethought, monitoring, control, reaction-and-reflection) crossed with four areas (cognition, motivation/affect, behavior, context), producing sixteen cells each of which names a specific class of regulatable activity.
> 
> **Boundary:** The matrix is an analytical decomposition, not a description of separable processes; the cells interact continuously in actual regulation, and the matrix's value lies in its diagnostic and pedagogical utility, not in any claim that the cells are psychologically discrete.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The ...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-four-by-four-matrix|The Four-by-Four Matrix]]*

> [!definition] **Four-Component Instructional Design** **[perm-note]**
> A holistic instructional design model that organizes instruction around four interrelated components — learning tasks, supportive information, procedural information, and part-task practice — designed to address the challenge of teaching complex cognitive skills by integrating schema construction for non-recurrent aspects with schema automation for recurrent aspects within a single coherent framework.
> 
> **Boundary:** The 4C/ID model is not a general-purpose curriculum framework or a theory of learning. It is a prescriptive design model specifically targeted at complex cognitive skills charac...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[four-component-instructional-design|Four-Component Instructional Design]]*

> [!definition] **Four-Component Instructional Design / 4C/ID** **[perm-note]**
> An instructional design model for complex skills, grounded in CLT, specifying four components: (1) whole-task learning tasks; (2) supportive information (schema-building); (3) procedural information (just-in-time guidance for routine procedures); (4) part-task practice (for automating critical components). The model operationalizes CLT principles for complex vocational and professional training domains and represents the most elaborated CLT-derived design framework.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[four-component-instructional-design-4cid|Four-Component Instructional Design / 4C/ID]]*

> [!definition] **Frame-Before-Fill Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The directional asymmetry in situation model construction: a structural frame (causal, architectural, or schematic) can accept and organize incoming details, but an accumulation of unframed details cannot generate a structural frame. Situation models must be constructed top-down (frame first, details second) because the reverse direction does not produce coherent mental models.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Frame-Before-Fill" is not an instruction to learn abstractions before specifics in all cases. It is specifically a claim about the construction of mental models: the organizing structure must precede ...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[frame-before-fill-asymmetry|Frame-Before-Fill Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Frederic Bartlett** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: British psychologist whose *Remembering* (1932) established that memory is reconstructive and culturally guided. Coined the term "schema" in its psychological sense. Distinguished from the later formalization tradition: Bartlett's schemas were culturally embedded, socially constituted, and involved in active reconstruction — not abstract, amodal data structures. His original data showed cultural specificity; the formalization tradition stripped this dimension.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Founding figure whose work was transformed in transmission — the cultural critique...
> 
> *Attribution: 1969*
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[frederic-bartlett|Frederic Bartlett]]*

> [!definition] **Free-Choice Behavior Paradigm** **[perm-note]**
> The free-choice behavioral paradigm is the primary experimental operationalization of intrinsic motivation in CET research. Following an experimental manipulation (typically involving reward or feedback conditions), participants are left in a room with the target activity available along with competing options, and an experimenter covertly measures the amount of time participants spontaneously spend with the activity during an ostensibly unobserved period. The theoretical assumption is that time spent freely engaging with the activity — without any ongoing external contingency — indexes the...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[free-choice-behavior-paradigm|Free-Choice Behavior Paradigm]]*

> [!definition] **Free-Choice Behaviour** **[perm-note]**
> The dependent variable most commonly used in CET research to measure intrinsic motivation: the amount of time a person voluntarily spends with an activity when they are not required to do so, typically measured during a period when the experimenter is absent and no external contingencies are operating. Free-choice behaviour is considered a more direct behavioural measure of intrinsic motivation than self-reported interest, because it does not depend on verbal self-report. However, it also has limitations: it may reflect habit or accessibility rather than intrinsic motivation, and it is diff...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[free-choice-behaviour|Free-Choice Behaviour]]*

> [!definition] **Free-Choice Paradigm** **[perm-note]**
> [**Free-Choice-Paradigm-Definition**:: The primary experimental methodology in CET research, in which participants work on an interesting activity under varying conditions (reward, no reward, feedback types), then are left alone in a "free-choice period" during which they can continue the activity, switch to alternative activities, or do nothing. Time spent on the target activity during this unsupervised period provides a behavioral index of intrinsic motivation — what the person genuinely chooses to do when unconstrained by external contingency. Developed by Deci (1971) and validated again...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[free-choice-paradigm|Free-Choice Paradigm]]*

> [!definition] **Frequency-Indexed Dual-Channel Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A reconstructed model of vocabulary acquisition that assigns different acquisition strategies to different vocabulary frequency bands and knowledge dimensions. *Channel A (Deliberate Instruction)* handles high-to-mid-frequency vocabulary (approximately the 1–9,000 word family range) using spaced retrieval practice, frequency-ranked word lists, and morphological instruction to establish the vocabulary threshold. *Channel B (Extensive Reading with Active Noticing)* handles consolidation, collocational enrichment, and exposure to contextually embedded low-frequency vocabulary —...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[frequency-indexed-dual-channel-model|Frequency-Indexed Dual-Channel Model]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Integration** **[perm-note]**
> The organisation of domain knowledge in ways that make it productively usable — not merely stored but structured so as to enable inference, prediction, error-detection, and analogical extension. Functional integration is contrasted with information storage: the same facts can be stored without integration (rote memorisation) or integrated in ways that generate new outputs. Understanding, on the functional integration account, is about the *organisation* of knowledge rather than its *content*.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[functional-integration|Functional Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Psychology** **[perm-note]**
> The approach to psychology that emphasizes the adaptive functions of mental processes and consciousness in enabling organisms to survive and thrive in their environments, rather than merely analyzing the structure or contents of mental states. James's functionalism contrasted sharply with [[structuralism]] practiced by [[edward-titchener|Edward Titchener]] and instead aligned with [[Darwinian]] evolutionary principles by treating psychological capacities as solutions to adaptive problems.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-william-james* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[functional-psychology|Functional Psychology]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Significance** **[perm-note]**
> The psychological meaning an event carries for the person who experiences it, specifically with respect to two possible aspects: the **controlling aspect** (the degree to which the event is experienced as pressuring the person to behave in a particular way, implying that the event is managing or directing behaviour) and the **informational aspect** (the degree to which the event is experienced as providing competence-relevant feedback, conveying information about how well one is doing relative to a standard). Every external event — reward, praise, deadline, feedback, surveillance — carries ...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[functional-significance|Functional Significance]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Significance — Informational, Controlling, Amotivating** *[needs-note]*
> See full definition in Phase II. Functional significance is the perceived motivational role of an external event — the function the agent interprets the event as serving. Three aspects are identified: informational (competence-affirming, autonomy-preserving), controlling (pressure toward specific outcomes), and amotivating (competence-disconfirming). The dominant aspect in an individual's interpretation determines the motivational consequence of the event.
> 
> **See also:** [[autonomy-support]], [[controlling-environment]], [[autonomy-supportive-environment]], [[reward-contingency-types]]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Functional-Significance-—-Informational,-Controlling,-Amotivating|Functional Significance — Informational, Controlling, Amotivating]]*

> [!definition] **The Fundamental Attribution Error** **[perm-note]**
> The **Fundamental Attribution Error** (FAE) is the systematic tendency to overemphasize dispositional (personal) causes and underemphasize situational (environmental) causes when explaining another person's behavior. It manifests as a reflexive tendency to attribute behavior to "what kind of person they are" rather than "what kind of situation they were in." The FAE is most pronounced when the behavior is negative, when the observer has limited information about situational constraints, and when the observer is culturally positioned within an individualistic framework.
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-fundamental-attribution-error|The Fundamental Attribution Error]]*

> [!definition] **Fundamental Attribution Error** **[perm-note]**
> The **Fundamental Attribution Error** (Ross, 1977) is the systematic human tendency to over-attribute another person's behavior to stable dispositional properties and under-attribute it to situational forces. The "fundamental" designation reflects the claim that this is not a peripheral error but a deep architectural feature of the person-perception system — perhaps driven by attentional salience (persons are perceptually figural against situational backgrounds) and by cultural frameworks (particularly in individualistic societies) that conceptualize behavior as primarily person-caused. The...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[fundamental-attribution-error|Fundamental Attribution Error]]*

> [!definition] **Fundamental Attribution Error / Correspondence Bias** **[perm-note]**
> The systematic tendency for observers to overweight dispositional factors and underweight situational factors when explaining another person's behaviour. "Fundamental" in the sense of being pervasive in Western cognitive ecology, though culturally variable rather than cognitively universal.
> 
> **Boundary:** Applies to explanations of *others'* behaviour, not one's own (which follows actor-observer asymmetry patterns). More accurately a bias than an "error," since dispositional attribution is sometimes appropriate.
> 
> **See also:** [[fundamental-attribution-error]], [[the-fundamental-attribution...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: social cognition & motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[fundamental-attribution-error-correspondence-bias|Fundamental Attribution Error / Correspondence Bias]]*

## _G

> [!definition] **Garrison's Comprehensive Model of Self-Directed Learning** *[needs-note]*
> Garrison proposed that self-directed learning involves the integration of three overlapping dimensions: self-management (the external management of learning activities and resources, including the social setting), self-monitoring (the cognitive processes of metacognitive monitoring and reflection), and motivation (the decision to enter and persist in learning, including task value and self-efficacy). The model's critical contribution was its insistence that all three dimensions must be addressed simultaneously — self-direction cannot be reduced to any single dimension alone.
> 
> **Boundary:** ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[Garrison's-Comprehensive-Model-of-Self-Directed-Learning|Garrison's Comprehensive Model of Self-Directed Learning]]*

> [!definition] **General Causality Orientations Scale** **[perm-note]**
> The primary measurement instrument for causality orientations. Presents 12 vignettes of motivationally relevant situations, each with three response options keyed to autonomy, control, and impersonal orientations. Respondents rate each option on a 7-point scale (non-ipsative), yielding three independent subscale scores. Reliability coefficients typically .70-.82. Validated across multiple cultural contexts with generally consistent factor structure.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[general-causality-orientations-scale|General Causality Orientations Scale]]*

> [!definition] **Generation Advantage** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical phenomenon whereby self-generated responses produce stronger memory traces than equivalent passively received responses. In the EI context, the generation advantage is the finding that learner-produced elaborations outperform experimenter-provided elaborations even when the provided elaborations are accurate and plausible.
> 
> **Boundary:** The generation advantage in EI is not purely the generic generation effect — it specifically reflects the construction premium: the causal-integration process required to generate the elaboration produces richer encoding than merely recognizin...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[generation-advantage|Generation Advantage]]*

> [!definition] **Generation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical finding that information actively generated by the learner — produced in response to a partial cue or constructive prompt — is remembered significantly better than the same information passively received through reading.
> 
> **Boundary:** The generation effect does not apply when the generation task is so difficult that it prevents acquisition of the target information. The optimal generation difficulty is within the learner's reach — effortful but achievable.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** The generation effect is leveraged when the reader pauses to produce their own explanation, p...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[generation-effect|Generation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Generation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust finding that memory for material is superior when the learner generates the material (completes a partial prompt, creates an example, explains in their own words) compared to when they simply read or copy the material. The generation advantage is not merely about effort — it is about the specific cognitive processes triggered by the need to produce rather than consume. Generation activates semantic and associative networks in ways that passive reading does not.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-generation-effect|The Generation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Calibration Test** **[perm-note]**
> A comprehension check that requires the learner to *produce* something beyond what the text provided, thereby testing genuine understanding rather than surface recognition. Examples include generating a novel example, reconstructing a causal mechanism from memory, connecting the content to a different domain, or formulating an objection to the text's argument. Generative tests defeat the fluency illusion because fluent reading does not provide the resources needed to generate novel production.
> 
> **Boundary:** Generative tests are not the same as comprehension questions that can be answered b...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[generative-calibration-test|Generative Calibration Test]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Cognition Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A structural configuration of four cognitive conditions — freed output channels, self-paced recursive engagement, private hypothesis space, and active metacognitive monitoring — that together make evaluative, analytical cognition cognitively affordable. Proposed in this analysis as the unifying framework for understanding why silent reading (and analogous practices) produces critical analysis under some conditions but not others.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[generative-cognition-architecture|Generative Cognition Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **The Generative Constraint** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that a writing technique's learning yield is proportional to the extent it forces the learner to generate, rather than reproduce, cognitive content. Generation requires active retrieval from long-term memory, construction of connections between ideas, or production of explanations, examples, or applications that cannot simply be copied from available input. Techniques high on the generative constraint produce durable learning; techniques low on it produce accurate records with poor retention.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-generative-constraint|The Generative Constraint]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Learning Strategy** **[perm-note]**
> A deliberate cognitive procedure that a learner employs to actively construct, transform, organize, or connect new information in ways that promote meaning-making and durable retention. Generative strategies are distinguished from organizational or mnemonic strategies by their emphasis on constructive connection to existing knowledge (integration) rather than surface-level reorganization. Effective generative strategies require the learner to perform cognitive work beyond what the text or presentation itself provides — generating inferences, elaborations, structural representations, or conn...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[generative-learning-strategy|Generative Learning Strategy]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Learning Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework holding that meaningful learning occurs through the active generation of relationships between new information and existing knowledge structures. Learning is not passive reception but active construction: the learner must generate the connections, inferences, elaborations, and integrations that give new material meaning. Writing facilitates generative learning insofar as it functions as a *generative activity* — one that requires the learner to produce rather than reproduce, to construct rather than transcribe.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[generative-learning-theory|Generative Learning Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Learning Theory (GLT) — Wittrock's Original Formulation** *[needs-note]*
> A theory of learning and comprehension proposing that meaningful understanding arises from the learner's generative cognitive activity: the active construction of relationships between new information and existing knowledge, experiences, and mental models. Learning is characterized not by the acquisition of information presented externally but by the learner's self-generated mental elaborations, organizational structures, and integrative connections. Coined by Merlin C. Wittrock (1974).
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Generative-Learning-Theory-GLT-—-Wittrock's-Original-Formulation|Generative Learning Theory (GLT) — Wittrock's Original Formulation]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Processing** **[perm-note]**
