> Cognitive processing that requires the learner to produce — rather than reproduce — connections, inferences, elaborations, and integrations between new information and existing knowledge structures. The active ingredient in learning activities that produce durable encoding.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[generative-processing|Generative Processing]]*

> [!definition] **The Generativity Diagnostic** **[perm-note]**
> An original framework from this analysis for evaluating any writing technique's learning potential across four dimensions: source status (closed vs. available), organisation authority (writer-generated vs. source-provided), connection mandate (connections required vs. recording only), predictive commitment (commitment required vs. optional).
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-generativity-diagnostic|The Generativity Diagnostic]]*

> [!definition] **The Generativity Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The apparent contradiction between Generative Learning Theory's prescription that active generation promotes learning and Cognitive Load Theory's finding that excessive cognitive demand impairs learning. The paradox is resolved by distinguishing between types of cognitive load: *germane* cognitive load (effort directly invested in schema construction, which is what GLT promotes) and *extraneous* cognitive load (effort wasted on processing irrelevant elements of the learning environment, which CLT advises minimizing). Generative activities that increase germane load facilitate learning; acti...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-generativity-paradox|The Generativity Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **Genetic Epistemology** **[perm-note]**
> Genetic epistemology is Piaget's term for his program of empirically investigating the origins and development of knowledge — the study not of what knowledge is but of how it comes to be. The term "genetic" is used in its older sense of "developmental" or "generative" rather than its contemporary biological sense. The program's ambition was nothing less than a naturalistic account of the growth of human knowledge across the lifespan and across the history of science.
> 
> **See also:** [[genetic-epistemology]], [[jean-piaget]], [[Piaget's-Constructivism]], [[Piaget's-Developmental-Stages]]
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[genetic-epistemology|Genetic Epistemology]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> Originally proposed as cognitive demand devoted to schema formation and automation — the "good" load that directly produces learning. Later reformulated by [[john-sweller|Sweller]] (2010) as not a distinct third type of load at all, but rather a label for how available working memory resources (after intrinsic and extraneous demands are managed) are deployed. This reformulation effectively eliminates germane load as a measurable, distinct construct, reducing the framework to a bipartite model in practice even if not in name.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[germane-cognitive-load|Germane Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Cognitive Load (Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas, 1998) — Original Formulation** *[needs-note]*
> In the original three-component model, germane load referred to the cognitive effort specifically dedicated to schema formation and automation — the "good" load associated with active learning processes. The idea was that once extraneous load was minimized, designers could deliberately increase germane load (e.g., by introducing variability, by requiring self-explanation) to promote deeper schema formation. This construct became theoretically problematic (see Phase III) and was reconceptualized around 2010: subsequent accounts treat what was called germane load as a resource allocation desc...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Germane-Cognitive-Load-Sweller,-van-Merriënboer-&-Paas,-1998-—-Original-Formulat|Germane Cognitive Load (Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas, 1998) — Original Formulation]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Cognitive Load / Germane Processing** **[perm-note]**
> The portion of working memory processing dedicated to constructing and automating schemas from the current material. In the original three-load framework, germane load was distinguished from intrinsic load as a separate category. In the revised framework, it is understood as the productive component of intrinsic load — the processing that results in durable learning. Germane processing is the cognitive goal of good instructional design and, by extension, good PKM design.
> 
> **Boundary:** Germane processing cannot be directly observed or measured — it is inferred from learning outcomes. The su...
> 
> *Source: pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[germane-cognitive-load-germane-processing|Germane Cognitive Load / Germane Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Processing** **[perm-note]**
> In the reconstructed bipartite CLT, germane processing is not a distinct load type but a design goal: ensuring that the working memory capacity available after intrinsic and extraneous demands are managed is directed toward schema construction rather than wasted. This represents a conceptual downgrade from the original germane load construct (a measurable quantity of working memory devoted to schema formation) to a design directive (arrange instruction so that available capacity is used productively). The downgrade is warranted by the measurement independence problem identified in Phase III...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[germane-processing|Germane Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Git-Based Workflow** **[perm-note]**
> **Branch-per-agent pattern**:
> ```
> main
> ├── feature/payment-system
> │   ├── feature/payment-system-requirements (requirements-analyst)
> │   ├── feature/payment-system-architecture (system-architect)
> │   └── feature/payment-system-implementation (backend-engineer)
> ```
> 
> Each agent works in its own branch, main agent merges when complete.
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[git-based-workflow|Git-Based Workflow]]*

> [!definition] **GitHub Copilot** **[perm-note]**
> GitHub Copilot is an AI-powered code completion and generation tool that operates as a VS Code extension, using large language models trained on vast repositories of public code to predict and suggest code based on the current file's context — the code already written, the comments describing intent, the imported libraries, and the project's broader structure. Unlike traditional autocomplete, which matches against a fixed list of known symbols, Copilot generates novel code that it predicts will accomplish what the developer intends, producing suggestions that range from completing a partial...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: software engineering*
> *Permanent Note: [[github-copilot|GitHub Copilot]]*

> [!definition] **Global Coherence** **[perm-note]**
> Global coherence refers to the unity and consistency of the mental representation as a whole — the degree to which all elements of the constructed representation fit together into a unified, non-contradictory situation model. Global coherence is a situation-model-level property: it requires that the reader have integrated not just adjacent sentences but all relevant portions of the text with each other and with prior knowledge into a representation in which the described agents, events, causal sequences, spatial contexts, and temporal structure form an integrated whole.
> 
> **Boundary:** Globa...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[global-coherence|Global Coherence]]*

> [!definition] **Ego Involvement** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational state in which self-worth is contingent on performance outcomes — performance becomes a test of the person's value or adequacy rather than an expression of their interests. Ego involvement is theoretically related to the controlling pathway in CET: events that create ego involvement (typically comparative evaluation, normative feedback, and high-stakes assessment) introduce a self-esteem regulation layer that makes the activity extrinsically salient and shifts PLOC external. Harackiewicz and Manderlink (1984) showed that ego-involving praise ("you're much better than average"...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[ego-involvement|Ego Involvement]]*

> [!definition] **Ego-Involving Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback that triggers self-evaluation processes by implicating the learner's identity, worth, or ability. Ego involvement is produced by person-directed language, normative social comparisons, and high-stakes evaluative contexts. Under ego-involved conditions, learners tend to process feedback through the lens of self-protection rather than learning, prioritising consistent self-image over accurate performance information.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[ego-involving-feedback|Ego-Involving Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A goal, in the context of [[personal-knowledge-management|personal knowledge management]], denotes an internally represented desired state toward which cognitive and behavioural activity is directed, encompassing not merely the specification of a target outcome but the establishment of evaluative criteria, temporal parameters, and regulatory mechanisms through which progress is monitored and behaviour is adjusted. Unlike the commonsense notion of a goal as a simple wish or intention, the construct as it operates in the motivation sciences carries with it an entire architecture of self-regul...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation and self-regulation*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Commitment** **[perm-note]**
> **Goal Commitment**: The determination to attain a goal and refusal to lower or abandon it despite obstacles. Functions as the primary moderator of the goal difficulty-performance relationship: the performance benefits of challenging goals are realized only when commitment remains high. Moderated by [[self-efficacy]], perceived goal importance, and degree of autonomous vs. controlled regulation.
> See also: [[goal-setting-theory]], [[self-determination-theory]], [[implementation-intention]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-commitment|Goal Commitment]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Construction Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary used to construct and articulate goals across the three hierarchical levels of SRL goal architecture: superordinate (why am I engaging with this domain — regulatory vocabulary), intermediate (what do I want from this reading — mastery orientation vocabulary), and subordinate (how will I engage with this passage — strategy vocabulary). The quality of goal vocabulary at each level cascades downward through the hierarchy, with superordinate regulatory vocabulary being the highest-leverage point for vocabulary intervention.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[Goal-Setting-Th...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-construction-vocabulary|Goal Construction Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> Goal content refers to the *qualitative nature* of what a personal goal is *about* — the kind of outcome or state it aims toward. Goal Contents Theory specifically distinguishes between **intrinsic goal content** (outcomes that are considered directly satisfying and that tend to satisfy [[basic-psychological-needs|basic psychological needs]] as a matter of their inherent nature) and **extrinsic goal content** (outcomes that are primarily valued for the social approval, comparative advantage, or material rewards they bring, rather than for their direct satisfaction of psychological needs).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-content|Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Content Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A sub-theory of [[self-determination-theory|Self-Determination Theory]] proposing that personal goals differ in their qualitative content — what they aim toward — and that this qualitative dimension independently predicts psychological well-being. Distinguishes intrinsic goal content (personal growth, meaningful relationships, community contribution, physical health) from extrinsic goal content (financial success, fame, attractive appearance), with the hypothesis that intrinsic goal emphasis supports and extrinsic goal emphasis undermines the satisfaction of basic psychological needs.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-content-theory|Goal Content Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Contents** **[perm-note]**
> The substantive aspirations that people pursue, classified as either intrinsic (personal growth, community contribution, meaningful relationships, health) or extrinsic (wealth, fame, attractive appearance, social power). [[goal-contents-theory]] proposes that intrinsic goal contents are directly linked to basic need satisfaction, while extrinsic goal contents function as need substitutes that fail to deliver genuine well-being even when achieved.
> 
> **Boundary:** Goal content theory addresses *what* people pursue, not *why* they pursue it. A person can pursue an intrinsic goal (e.g., personal...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-contents|Goal Contents]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Contents Theory** **[perm-note]**
> **[[goal-contents-theory]]** — One of the six mini-theories within [[self-determination-theory]] that focuses on the *content* of goals (what people pursue) rather than the process, structure, or expectancy of goal pursuit. GCT distinguishes between intrinsic goal contents (directly satisfying basic psychological needs) and extrinsic goal contents (dependent on external contingencies and social comparison). The theory's central prediction is that the relative centrality of intrinsic vs. extrinsic aspirations predicts well-being outcomes independent of goal attainment, expectancy, or effort....
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-contents-theory|Goal Contents Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Contents Theory — Core Definition** *[needs-note]*
> **Goal Contents Theory (GCT)** is a mini-theory within [[self-determination-theory]] that distinguishes between two qualitatively different classes of life aspiration based on their typical relationships to [[basic-psychological-needs-theory|basic psychological need]] satisfaction. **Intrinsic goal content** includes aspirations for personal growth, meaningful relationships (close affiliation), community contribution, and physical health — goals whose pursuit and attainment tend to directly express or satisfy the needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness. **Extrinsic goal content** in...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Goal-Contents-Theory-—-Core-Definition|Goal Contents Theory — Core Definition]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Conversion** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which a performance-approach goal orientation converts toward performance-avoidance dynamics under conditions of competitive threat, low perceived competence, or repeated relative failure. Goal conversion involves not simply a shift in goal content but a shift in the underlying regulatory mode — from approach to avoidance — while the performance standard (normative comparison) is maintained.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Goal conversion is the primary mechanism explaining the context-dependent character of PAP goal effects and the theoretical basis for the...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-conversion|Goal Conversion]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Difficulty** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a goal exceeds what the performer would otherwise produce in its absence; operationalized in research as the percentage of subjects able to achieve the goal.
> 
> **Boundary:** Difficulty is bounded above by perceived attainability — a goal exceeding what the performer believes possible collapses commitment rather than intensifying effort.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Difficulty supplies the energetic gradient that mobilizes resources beyond default operating level; its calibration relative to ability and self-efficacy is the most consequential setting in goal design.
> ...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-difficulty|Goal Difficulty]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Framing** **[perm-note]**
> The language and rationale used to describe the *purpose* of a goal or task, independent of the task's objective content. Intrinsic goal framing emphasizes personal growth, interest, understanding, or relational value; extrinsic goal framing emphasizes grades, external evaluation, career advancement, or social comparison. Experimental manipulation of goal framing has been shown to causally affect learning depth, motivation quality, and transfer performance (Vansteenkiste et al., 2004, 2007).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-framing|Goal Framing]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Framing Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The specific vocabulary used to formulate learning goals in the forethought phase of SRL, which encodes the goal's orientation (mastery/approach vs. performance/avoidance), its specificity, its challenge level, and its temporal framing. Goal framing vocabulary is constitutive of the motivational orientation under which reading proceeds. A goal formulated as "I want to understand the core argument of this chapter well enough to apply it" encodes mastery-approach orientation, transformation as success criterion, and active agency. A goal formulated as "I need to get through th...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-framing-vocabulary|Goal Framing Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Intention** **[perm-note]**
> A volitional commitment to achieve a desired end state, expressed in the form *"I intend to achieve Z."* Goal intentions occupy the predecisional and post-actional phases of the action phase model and concern *what* the actor wants; implementation intentions concern *when, where, and how* the corresponding behavior will be enacted.
> 
> **Boundary:** Goal intentions alone are insufficient for reliable behavior change; the literature consistently shows that strong goal intentions only weakly predict behavior in the absence of supporting implementation intentions.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-intention|Goal Intention]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational construct distinguishing between mastery/learning goals (seeking to develop competence) and performance goals (seeking to demonstrate competence). Mastery goals promote deeper processing, persistence after failure, challenge-seeking, and long-term skill development. Performance goals promote performance under low-difficulty conditions but often produce avoidance under threat. Identified in this report as the proximate motivational variable mediating mindset effects.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-orientation|Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Proximity** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The temporal distance between the goal and its achievement. *Proximal goals* are achievable within a single session; *distal goals* are achievable across multiple sessions or over longer timeframes.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** For reading complex academic texts, the research strongly supports emphasizing proximal goals over distal ones during the Forethought Phase. A proximal goal like "I will be able to explain the distinction between declarative and procedural metacognitive knowledge in my own words by the end of this session" generates more sustained motivation and...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-proximity|Goal Proximity]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Setting Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically derived proposition that conscious goals — defined as desired outcomes one is committed to attaining — regulate performance through their effects on attention, effort, persistence, and task strategy, with performance increasing as a positive linear function of goal difficulty so long as the person possesses the requisite ability and is committed to the goal.
> 
> **Boundary:** The theory addresses conscious, accepted goals on tasks where the performer has sufficient ability and where strategy is not the rate-limiting factor; it does not directly address subconscious goals, goals...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-setting-theory|Goal Setting Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Specificity** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a goal identifies precise standards for performance, acceptable outcomes, or behavioural targets. In [[goal-setting-theory|goal-setting theory]], specificity operates as a variance-reduction mechanism: specific goals reduce the range of acceptable performance levels, thereby eliminating the ambiguity that allows self-serving interpretations of "good enough." Within PKM contexts, specificity manifests as the precision with which a learning goal defines what understanding looks like — the difference between "learn about metacognition" (vague) and "articulate the Nelson-Nar...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-specificity|Goal Specificity]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Structure** **[perm-note]**
> The achievement goal framework embedded in a learning or organizational environment — the pattern of practices, evaluation criteria, recognition systems, and competitive norms that collectively define what "success" means within that environment and thereby shape which goals participants adopt.
> 
> **See also:** [[mastery-goal-orientation]], [[performance-goal-orientation]], [[autonomy-supportive-environment]], [[instructional-design]]
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-structure|Goal Structure]]*

> [!definition] **Goal-Directed Behavior** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Action that is sensitive to the current value of the outcome (outcome-sensitive) and to the current belief that the action produces the outcome (action-outcome contingency-sensitive). Goal-directed behavior diminishes when the outcome is devalued or when the causal link is disrupted. Controlled by prefrontal-dorsomedial striatal circuits. Contrasted with habitual behavior, which is outcome-insensitive.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The goal-directed system is the scaffolding system in habit formation. Understanding its properties explains both why willpower strategies w...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-directed-behavior|Goal-Directed Behavior]]*

> [!definition] **Goal-Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational disposition toward achievement goals, distinguishing mastery orientation (developing competence) from performance orientation (demonstrating competence). Goal orientation shapes how outcomes are interpreted, how difficulty is responded to, and whether self-regulatory processes are adaptive or defensive.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Goal-Setting Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A theory of motivation and performance positing that specific, difficult goals lead to higher performance than vague or easy goals, provided that the individual has adequate ability, feedback, and goal commitment. The theory's core mechanism is that goals direct attention, energise effort, promote persistence, and stimulate strategy development.
> 
> **Boundary:** Goal-setting theory addresses the *structure* of goals (specificity, difficulty) but does not adequately address the *quality* of motivation underlying goal pursuit — a gap filled by Self-Determination Theory.
> 
> **Report-Specific Signi...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation and self-regulation*
> *Permanent Note: [[goal-setting-theory|Goal-Setting Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Egocentric-Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Habitual tendency to interpret evidence through self-serving bias and identity protection. In Paul-Elder analysis, a primary obstacle to intellectual humility and fairness.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[egocentric-thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Gravitational Collapse** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which a body of matter contracts under the influence of its own gravitational attraction, occurring when self-gravity overwhelms all opposing forces — principally thermal gas pressure, turbulent motions, magnetic pressure, and rotational support. In the context of star formation, gravitational collapse refers to the contraction of a dense molecular cloud core from an initial state of approximate equilibrium to a condition of ever-increasing central density and temperature, ultimately leading to the formation of a protostar.
> 
> **Boundary:** Gravitational collapse in star format...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[gravitational-collapse|Gravitational Collapse]]*

> [!definition] **Gricean Cooperative Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The pragmatic assumption — held by all conversational participants — that speakers are making honest, relevant, informative, and clear contributions to the exchange. Violations of this principle generate *implicature*: the receiver infers the intended meaning from the gap between what was said and what the cooperative principle would predict. Foundation of modern pragmatics. (Grice, 1975)
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[gricean-cooperative-principle|Gricean Cooperative Principle]]*

> [!definition] **GRM Triad — Goal-Regulation-Metacognition Triad** *[needs-note]*
> A triadic model proposing that effective goal-directed learning in PKM depends on the dynamic interaction of three mutually constitutive processes: goal formulation, self-regulatory execution, and metacognitive monitoring. Unlike linear models that treat these as sequential stages, the GRM Triad conceives them as simultaneously active vertices of a system, each capable of initiating change in the others. The model predicts specific failure modes when any vertex is underdeveloped.
> 
> **Boundary:** The GRM Triad is a theoretical synthesis, not an empirically tested model. It organises existing ...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation and self-regulation*
> *Permanent Note: [[GRM-Triad-—-Goal-Regulation-Metacognition-Triad|GRM Triad — Goal-Regulation-Metacognition Triad]]*

> [!definition] **Grounding** **[perm-note]**
> The interactive process by which conversational partners establish mutual knowledge — shared understanding of what has been said, meant, acknowledged, and accepted — through contingent responses, clarification requests, acknowledgment signals, and iterative checking. Distinguished from mere transmission by its requirement for active participation from the receiver, not just the sender. (Clark & Wilkes-Gibbs, 1986)
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[grounding]]*

> [!definition] **Grow's Staged Self-Directed Learning Model** *[needs-note]*
> A developmental model describing four stages through which learners progress: Stage 1 (Dependent — requires authority/coach), Stage 2 (Interested — responds to motivator/guide), Stage 3 (Involved — benefits from facilitator), Stage 4 (Self-Directed — uses consultant/delegator). The model's critical insight is that mismatches between learner stage and teaching style produce predictable dysfunction.
> 
> **Boundary:** Stages are domain-specific, not global. A learner may be at Stage 4 in one domain and Stage 1 in another. The model describes a developmental trajectory, not a fixed categorization....
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[Grow's-Staged-Self-Directed-Learning-Model|Grow's Staged Self-Directed Learning Model]]*

> [!definition] **Growth** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Growth (Dewey, 1916)
> **Definition:** The ongoing expansion of the capacity for further experience and inquiry. For Dewey, growth is both the means and the end of education — there is no terminal state toward which education aims, only the continuous enhancement of the organism's ability to engage intelligently with a changing environment.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Growth is not directionless — it is constrained by the demands of intelligent engagement with genuine problems and by the requirements of democratic social life. But it is not directed toward a pre-specified curriculum or...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[growth]]*

> [!definition] **Growth Mindset** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that intelligence and ability are malleable attributes that can be developed through effort, effective strategy, and persistence. Associated in Dweck's framework with challenge-seeking, effort attribution, and mastery-oriented response to failure. Contrasted with fixed mindset. As analyzed in this report: a culturally influential packaging of attribution-theory insights about failure response, with overstated causal claims and an underspecified mechanism.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[growth-mindset|Growth Mindset]]*

> [!definition] **Guidance Fading Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The CLT-derived instructional principle prescribing that the level of instructional scaffolding and support should decrease progressively as learner expertise grows, to avoid the expertise reversal effect. Operationalized most concretely in the [[faded-worked-example|faded worked example]] paradigm, where successive problems in a sequence include progressively fewer completed steps, tracking the learner's schema development. Supported by empirical research on faded examples and consistent with the expertise reversal mechanism.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[guidance-fading-principle|Guidance Fading Principle]]*

> [!definition] **The Guilt-Shame Asymmetry in Motivational Function** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** In Weiner's framework, guilt and shame are both negative self-directed emotions but are generated by different attribution profiles and have opposite motivational implications. Guilt — generated by controllable internal causal attributions — is motivationally facilitative because it implies that the cause is changeable. Shame — generated by uncontrollable internal causal attributions — is motivationally paralyzing because it implies that the cause is fixed.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The practical implication is that not all negative self-directed affect is motivatio...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-guilt-shame-asymmetry-in-motivational-function|The Guilt-Shame Asymmetry in Motivational Function]]*

## _I

> [!definition] **ICAP Framework** **[perm-note]**
> The [[icap-framework]] classifies cognitive activities by their depth and the nature of learner engagement: Passive (receiving information without overt activity), Active (manipulating information physically without necessarily changing it mentally), Constructive (generating new knowledge beyond what was presented), and Interactive (engaging in dialogue that produces knowledge through socio-cognitive conflict). Each mode predicts different learning outcomes, with Constructive and Interactive modes producing significantly better understanding and transfer. Silent reading is *not* automatical...
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[icap-framework|ICAP Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Identified Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory style in which the person has consciously endorsed a behavior or value as personally important — they "identify" with it as their own. Identified regulation is autonomous: the person acts from their own valuation rather than from external pressure or internal approval-seeking. It is distinct from integrated regulation in that the value, while endorsed, may not yet be fully synthesized with the person's broader self-conception. It is the first autonomous regulatory style on OIT's continuum.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[identified-regulation|Identified Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Identified-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by conscious valuing — the person has endorsed the goal or activity as personally important, even if it is not intrinsically enjoyable. The [[perceived-internal-locus-of-causality|locus of causality]] has shifted inward: the person acts because *they* see the value, not because an external agent rewards them for it or because they fear internal sanction. Identified regulation is typically associated with substantially better outcomes than introjection — greater persistence, higher quality engagement, and better psychological wellbeing.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[identified-regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Tier 2 Academic Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> Tier 2 vocabulary consists of high-frequency, general academic words that appear across disciplines and academic registers but rarely in conversational speech — examples include *analyse, synthesis, mechanism, paradigm, infer, criterion, implicit, hypothesis, juxtapose, ambiguous*. Tier 2 words are the connective tissue of academic argument, carrying logical, rhetorical, and epistemic relationships between ideas. Developing academic readers typically lack both the breadth and depth of Tier 2 vocabulary required for full academic text comprehension, making Tier 2 instruction the highest-prio...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[tier-2-academic-vocabulary|Tier 2 Academic Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Hierarchical Chunk Structure** **[perm-note]**
> The organization of chunks into nested levels in which higher-order chunks are themselves composed of lower-order chunks, allowing the cognitive system to operate at the level of granularity appropriate to the current task while preserving access to finer-grained components when needed. Hierarchy is what permits experts to reason about strategic-level patterns without losing tactical-level detail.
> 
> **Boundary:** Hierarchy in this sense is not the same as a simple categorization tree, because the categorization tree treats higher levels as abstractions of lower levels, while a chunk hierarch...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[hierarchical-chunk-structure|Hierarchical Chunk Structure]]*

> [!definition] **Hierarchical Goal Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A multi-level goal structure for learning, comprising distal learning goals (broad capabilities being developed over extended time), session outcome goals (what will be produced in a specific session), and proximal process goals (the specific strategies to be enacted during performance). Each level serves a distinct motivational and regulatory function; the proximal process goals are most important for moment-to-moment learning quality.
> 
> **Boundary:** Goal hierarchy does not mean goal quantity. Having many goals at all three levels simultaneously can impose its own cognitive load and dilute...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[hierarchical-goal-architecture|Hierarchical Goal Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **High Performance Cycle** **[perm-note]**
> The integrative dynamic model proposed by Locke and Latham (1990, refined 2002) in which specific, difficult, accepted goals — supported by adequate moderators — activate the four mediating mechanisms, produce performance, generate intrinsic and extrinsic rewards proportional to that performance, and thereby reinforce commitment to subsequent goals in a self-sustaining loop.
> 
> **Boundary:** The cycle's self-sustaining dynamics depend on rewards being commensurate with achievement, on attribution of success to internal causes, and on the maintenance of perceived attainability across cycles; e...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[high-performance-cycle|High Performance Cycle]]*

> [!definition] **High-Validity Environment** **[perm-note]**
> A decision environment characterised by stable causal structure (the same inputs reliably produce the same outputs) and reliable feedback (the agent can observe whether their judgments were correct, in a timeframe and with sufficient signal-to-noise to support learning). Intuitive expertise can be developed through practice in high-validity environments because the patterns Type 1 learns to match are the patterns that actually predict the outcomes the agent cares about.
> 
> **Boundary:** A high-validity environment is not the same as a comfortable or familiar environment; it is one in which th...
> 
> *Source: dual-process-theory-kahneman-system-1-system-2-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[high-validity-environment|High-Validity Environment]]*

> [!definition] **The Eight Elements of Thought** **[perm-note]**
> 1. **[[Purpose-in-Reasoning|Purpose]]** — Every act of reasoning has a goal, objective, or function. Reasoning is always directed.
> 2. **[[Question-at-Issue|Question at Issue]]** — Every act of reasoning attempts to address a question, problem, or issue.
> 3. **[[Information]]** — Every act of reasoning uses data, facts, observations, or experiences as its evidential base.
> 4. **[[Interpretation-and-Inference|Interpretation and Inference]]** — Every act of reasoning involves drawing conclusions, making interpretations, and giving meaning to data.
> 5. **[[Concepts]]** — Every act of reasoning emp...
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-eight-elements-of-thought|The Eight Elements of Thought]]*

> [!definition] **Tight-Loop Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The implicit architectural commitment in the [[zimmerman-srl-model]] that the three phases form a *functionally integrated* feedback loop in which information flows reliably from Performance Phase monitoring through Self-Reflection Phase evaluation to Forethought Phase modification. The assumption is architectural (embedded in the circular representation of the model) rather than propositional (stated as a testable claim). The dialectical process shows this assumption does not survive scrutiny in its strong form; a conditional version — that tight coupling is *possible* give...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[tight-loop-assumption|Tight-Loop Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Illusion of Explanatory Depth** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive phenomenon documented by Rozenblit and Keil (2002): people systematically overestimate their understanding of causal/mechanical systems before attempting to explain them. When asked to explain in detail, the overestimation collapses. The illusion arises because familiarity with the *function* of a system (what it does) is mistaken for understanding of its *mechanism* (how and why it does it). The phenomenon demonstrates that the *feeling* of understanding is an unreliable guide to its presence.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[illusion-of-explanatory-depth|Illusion of Explanatory Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Illusion of Knowing / Illusion of Comprehension** **[perm-note]**
> The illusion of knowing is a metacognitive failure mode in which a learner's subjective feeling of understanding a concept or text is not matched by their actual ability to use, apply, transfer, or accurately reproduce that knowledge when tested. The illusion arises from monitoring systems that use *processing fluency* (ease and speed of recognition and processing) as a proxy for *understanding depth* — a proxy that is accurate when familiar material is actually well-understood but is systematically misleading when familiarity reflects prior exposure rather than deep encoding. The illusion ...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[illusion-of-knowing-illusion-of-comprehension|Illusion of Knowing / Illusion of Comprehension]]*

> [!definition] **Impersonal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to orient toward the environment as if one's behavior were beyond intentional control — as if outcomes are determined by forces (internal or external) that cannot be managed. Individuals high in impersonal orientation characteristically experience themselves as incapable of producing desired outcomes, attribute performance to luck, fate, or overwhelming circumstances, and often disengage from effortful pursuit. Impersonal orientation is theoretically associated with [[amotivation]] in OIT's motivational continuum and with [[learned-helplessness|Learned Helplessness]] in Seligma...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[impersonal-orientation|Impersonal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Implementation Intention** **[perm-note]**
> **Implementation Intention** (Gollwitzer, 1999): A planning strategy that specifies the situation-behavior link for goal enactment in "if-then" format: "If [critical situational cue], then [goal-directed response]." The mechanism is prospective memory automatization: by pre-linking the behavioral response to a specific situational cue at encoding, the detection of that cue at retrieval automatically activates the intended behavior without requiring conscious deliberation. This bypasses the effortful access problem that causes non-implementation of mere goal intentions.
> 
> Boundary conditions:...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[implementation-intention|Implementation Intention]]*

> [!definition] **Implementation Intentions** **[perm-note]**
> Specific "if-then" plans that link a situational cue to a planned behavioral response, delegating action initiation from conscious deliberation to environmental detection. Substantially increase follow-through on intentions by automating the initiation decision.
> 
> **Boundary:** Most effective for simple, well-defined behaviors; less effective for complex behaviors requiring flexible adaptation.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Provides a concrete, evidence-based technique for supporting PKM habit formation (Section 6).
> 
> **See also:** [[implementation-intentions]], [[habit-formation]], [[Go...
> 
> *Source: pkb-pkm-planning-and-cognitive-science-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[implementation-intentions|Implementation Intentions]]*

> [!definition] **Implicit Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> An implicit theory of intelligence is an individual's naïve belief about the fundamental nature of intellectual ability — specifically, whether it is a fixed entity of limited stock (entity theory) or a malleable quality that can be developed through effort and learning (incremental theory). These beliefs are "implicit" in the sense that they function as background assumptions that organize goal pursuit, attribution, and persistence, rather than as explicit philosophical positions the person articulates or defends.
> 
> **Boundary:** Implicit theories of intelligence are not beliefs about one's...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[implicit-theory-of-intelligence|Implicit Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Acquisition from Context** **[perm-note]**
> The accrual of lexical knowledge as an unintended byproduct of reading for comprehension. Distinguished from *intentional learning* by the learner's primary goal (comprehension, not word learning) and from *rote memorisation* by the naturalness of the acquisition context.
> 
> **Boundary:** Incidental does not mean passive in the sense of zero attention. Some attention to the word form is necessary; "incidental" refers to the *goal structure*, not the attentional allocation.
> 
> **See also:** [[incidental-learning]], [[metacognition-in-learning]], [[levels-of-processing-theory]]
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[incidental-acquisition-from-context|Incidental Acquisition from Context]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Learning that occurs without explicit intent to memorize, typically studied using cover stories that direct participants' attention away from any awareness that a memory test will follow. LOP research demonstrated that incidental semantic learners often remember as much as intentional learners, supporting the claim that processing depth, not intent, is the primary determinant of retention.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[incidental-learning|Incidental Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Vocabulary Acquisition** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which word meanings are acquired as a by-product of comprehension-focused reading or listening, without deliberate intention to learn the words encountered. Distinguished from *intentional vocabulary learning*, which involves explicit study (word cards, memorization, dictionary use) directed at acquiring specific words. The received view holds that incidental acquisition from reading is the dominant vocabulary learning mechanism for mature readers; the dialectical challenge concerns the efficiency and scope of this mechanism.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The entire deba...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[incidental-vocabulary-acquisition|Incidental Vocabulary Acquisition]]*

> [!definition] **The Incremental Knowledge Model** **[perm-note]**
> A model of vocabulary knowledge according to which knowing a word is not binary (known or unknown) but involves gradual acquisition across multiple independent dimensions: (1) spoken/written form, (2) word parts and morphology, (3) syntagmatic relationships (collocations, typical sentence positions), (4) paradigmatic relationships (synonyms, antonyms, hyponyms), (5) semantic range and polysemy, (6) register and contextual appropriateness, (7) frequency and availability for production. Nation (2001) identifies nine aspects of word knowledge across three dimensions of form, meaning, and use, ...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-incremental-knowledge-model|The Incremental Knowledge Model]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Knowledge Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A model of vocabulary knowledge according to which knowing a word involves gradual acquisition across multiple independent dimensions: spoken/written form, word parts, syntagmatic relationships (collocations), paradigmatic relationships (synonyms/antonyms), semantic range and polysemy, register and contextual appropriateness, and availability for production. Each dimension can be known receptively (recognition in context) or productively (availability for use). Word knowledge is not binary (known/unknown) but a probability distribution across dimensions and modalities.
> 
> **Re...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[incremental-knowledge-model|Incremental Knowledge Model]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that ability is malleable — capable of development through effort, strategy, and learning. Under incremental theory, performance outcomes are *feedback about current level of competence*, not diagnostic verdicts about fixed traits. Failure is informative rather than definitional; effort is a path to growth rather than evidence of low ability. Incremental theorists tend to seek challenge, persist in the face of difficulty, and process negative feedback as useful data.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[incremental-theory|Incremental Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> Dweck's technical term for the belief that intelligence is malleable and develops through effort. The theoretical basis for growth mindset interventions. This report finds the evidence for its cross-domain stability and its causal independence from experience to be weak.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[incremental-theory-of-intelligence|Incremental Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Individual Interest** **[perm-note]**
> A relatively stable personal predisposition to re-engage with a particular class of content, objects, or activities, characterized by stored value (the content is found personally significant), stored knowledge (accumulated understanding of the domain), and positive affect (engagement is experienced as intrinsically rewarding). Individual interest is distinguished from situational interest (triggered by environmental features of specific texts or contexts) by its relative context-independence: individual interest in a domain motivates seeking out domain-relevant material even in the absence...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[individual-interest|Individual Interest]]*

> [!definition] **Individualism-Collectivism and Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> **Individualistic** cultural frameworks — common in North American and Western European contexts — conceptualize the person as a discrete, autonomous agent whose behavior primarily reflects internal properties (traits, abilities, intentions, values). This cultural self-model primes dispositional attribution: when a person acts in a particular way, the natural causal search targets the person's internal properties. **Collectivistic** cultural frameworks — more common in East Asian, South Asian, and many African contexts — conceptualize the person as fundamentally embedded in relational, hier...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[individualism-collectivism-and-attribution|Individualism-Collectivism and Attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Inductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> A form of inference that moves from particular observations or instances to general conclusions, principles, or hypotheses. Unlike deductive reasoning, which guarantees its conclusions if its premises are true, inductive reasoning produces conclusions that are *probable* rather than *certain* given the evidence. The strength of an inductive argument is a matter of degree — it can be stronger or weaker — and even a maximally strong inductive argument can, in principle, be overturned by new evidence. Inductive reasoning is also called *ampliative* inference because the conclusion contains inf...
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inductive-reasoning|Inductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Inert Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Inert knowledge refers to information that is acquired and stored but cannot be activated or deployed in novel problem contexts. It is the characteristic product of education organized around transmission of fixed content rather than around genuine inquiries. Pragmatist pedagogy aims specifically at the transformation of [[prior-knowledge|prior knowledge]] from inert reception to active competence — knowledge that functions as a tool for dealing with problems.
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[inert-knowledge|Inert Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Infallibilism** **[perm-note]**
> The contrasting view that genuine knowledge requires certainty — that a knower can only be said to *know* something if their justification is strong enough to exclude the possibility of error. On this view, if there is any chance (however remote) that a belief is false, it does not qualify as knowledge in the strict sense. [[descartes]]'s method of doubt in the *Meditations* (1641) is the classical expression: he withholds the label "knowledge" from any belief that can be doubted. Many ordinary language uses of "know" implicitly invoke infallibilist standards.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[infallibilism]]*

> [!definition] **Inference** **[perm-note]**
> Inference is the cognitive act of drawing conclusions that go beyond what is explicitly stated, using available evidence and reasoning to arrive at justified beliefs. In education, inference involves identifying what follows from the evidence, recognising alternative interpretations, and understanding the degree of certainty warranted by the evidence at hand. Inference is not guessing — it is reasoned extension from known to unknown, constrained by the quality of available evidence.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inference]]*

> [!definition] **Inference Generation in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> An inference is cognitive content that is activated during reading but is not explicitly stated in the text. Inference generation is the process by which readers go beyond the literal text to construct implicit meaning. Graesser, Singer, and Trabasso's constructionist theory distinguishes between two primary inference classes: *coherence inferences* (generated automatically to maintain local and global coherence of the text) and *elaborative inferences* (generated selectively to enrich the situation model with relevant world knowledge).
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all inferences are equally likely o...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[inference-generation-in-reading|Inference Generation in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Inference to the Best Explanation** **[perm-note]**
> The analytic philosophy reconstruction of Peircean abduction, understood as the inferential practice of selecting the hypothesis that, if true, would best explain the observed data according to criteria including explanatory power, fit with background knowledge, simplicity, and what Lipton calls "loveliness" (depth of illumination). IBE is simultaneously a description of how reasoners actually infer and a normative standard for how they should infer. Subject to the *bad lot problem* (van Fraassen): even the best available hypothesis may not be good in any absolute sense if the pool of candi...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inference-to-the-best-explanation|Inference to the Best Explanation]]*

> [!definition] **Inferential Bridging** **[perm-note]**
> The generation of implicit inferences needed to establish coherence between textual propositions that are not directly connected by explicit linguistic devices. A core component of situation model construction, requiring activation of world knowledge and causal reasoning. Inferential bridging demand is the primary determinant of text difficulty beyond the sentence level.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[inferential-bridging|Inferential Bridging]]*

> [!definition] **Information Transfer Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment works by generating accurate data about a student's current knowledge state, and delivering that data to the student (and teacher) in a form that can be acted upon to close the learning gap. The operative mechanism is the quality and accessibility of the information.
> 
> **Core Assumption:** Students who receive accurate, timely, specific feedback will use it to adjust their learning behavior. The bottleneck is informational: if the right information is present, improvement follows.
> 
> **Design Implication:** Optimize assessment accuracy, feedba...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[information-transfer-model|Information Transfer Model]]*

> [!definition] **Information Transfer Model (ITM)** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment works primarily through the delivery of accurate, timely, specific information about a student's performance gap. The operative variable is information quality and accessibility. **Boundary conditions:** not wrong, but insufficient; treats the monitoring system as a reliable conduit for information rather than as a variable in its own right.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The foil against which the MCM is developed. The ITM explains some variance but cannot explain the implementation gap or the differential effectiveness findings.
> 
> **Cr...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Information-Transfer-Model-ITM|Information Transfer Model (ITM)]]*

> [!definition] **The Information-Processing Foundation** **[perm-note]**
> The information-processing approach to cognition, which dominated [[cognitive-psychology]] from the late 1950s onward, treated the mind as a system that transforms, stores, and retrieves information according to computational principles. This framework imported concepts from communications engineering and computer science — channels, capacity limits, buffers, serial versus parallel processing — and applied them to the empirical study of human perception, attention, and memory. The first rigorous psychological application of these concepts to memory came from [[george-miller]] (1956), who de...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-information-processing-foundation|The Information-Processing Foundation]]*

> [!definition] **Informational Events** **[perm-note]**
> [**Informational-Events-Definition**:: External events whose primary functional significance is to provide genuine, non-pressuring information about the person's competence, mastery, or effectiveness. Examples include positive feedback framed as competence acknowledgment rather than evaluative pressure, optimal challenge calibration, and autonomy-supportive communication that conveys trust in the person's capacity. CET predicts that informational events maintain or enhance intrinsic motivation by affirming perceived competence within an autonomy-supportive context.]
> *See also: [[Information...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[informational-events|Informational Events]]*

> [!definition] **Informational Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback whose primary functional significance is informational — it conveys genuine competence-relevant information to the recipient in a context where PLOC remains internal. Informational feedback is characterised by specificity (pointing to particular aspects of performance), mastery-referencing (comparing performance to personal standards or task standards rather than to other people), process orientation (attributing performance to strategies and effort rather than global ability), and non-controlling language (describing rather than evaluating). CET predicts that informational feedbac...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[informational-feedback|Informational Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Initial Mass Function** **[perm-note]**
> A statistical description of the distribution of stellar masses at the time of birth within a population of newly formed stars. The IMF describes the relative number of stars born in each mass range — it does not describe the mass of any individual star but rather the probability distribution from which individual stellar masses are drawn.
> 
> **Boundary:** The IMF is a population-level description, not a prediction for individual star-forming events. It is also a birth mass function — it does not account for mass lost during stellar evolution.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The IMF repres...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[initial-mass-function|Initial Mass Function]]*

> [!definition] **Inner Speech** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Inner speech is the internalized, abbreviated, and predicate-dominant form of external speech that serves the self-regulatory function of mediating between thought and action. Distinguished from verbal thinking (internal monologue with full sentence structure) and from subvocalization (motor-level phonological encoding), inner speech in [[vygotsky]]'s account is functionally social — it is internalized dialogue — and carries the compressed semantic architecture of the social speech from which it was derived. **Boundary condition**: inner speech is not the same as *all* menta...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inner-speech|Inner Speech]]*

> [!definition] **Inoculation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework for building cognitive resistance to misinformation, modeled on medical vaccination. Pre-emptive exposure to weakened forms of misinformation techniques — accompanied by explicit identification of the persuasion tactic employed — generates cognitive "antibodies" that reduce susceptibility to subsequent misinformation encounters. Inoculation theory grounds "prebunking" interventions, which aim to expose manipulation tactics before they are used, rather than "debunking" interventions, which correct misinformation after belief has formed.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inoculation-theory|Inoculation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Inquiry-Based Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A family of pedagogical approaches in which students are the primary agents of inquiry: they formulate or are presented with questions, generate hypotheses, design or conduct investigations, and draw conclusions from evidence. IBL is grounded philosophically in Dewey's *How We Think* (1910) and developed empirically through decades of science education research. In its most abductive instantiation, IBL deliberately creates anomalies and withholds pre-formed explanations to ensure that students engage in genuine hypothesis generation rather than simply verifying known outcomes.
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[inquiry-based-learning|Inquiry-Based Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Instance Theory Core Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> Instance theory proposes that each encounter with a stimulus creates a separate memory trace (instance) that can be retrieved in response to subsequent encounters. With sufficient practice, memory retrieval of solutions from past instances becomes faster than executing the algorithm de novo, leading to a race between algorithmic processing and memory retrieval. When memory consistently wins this race, performance appears automatic.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[instance-theory-core-mechanism|Instance Theory Core Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Instructional Efficiency Ratio** **[perm-note]**
> Proposed reconceptualization of germane load as a ratio: the proportion of total cognitive investment devoted to element interactivity at an appropriate expertise level. Replaces "increase germane load" with "optimize the instructional efficiency ratio" as the design prescription.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[instructional-efficiency-ratio|Instructional Efficiency Ratio]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Development Environment** **[perm-note]**
> An Integrated Development Environment is a software application that consolidates the core tools of software development — a source code editor, build automation tools, a debugger, and often intelligent code completion — into a single unified interface, so that the developer need not switch between separate applications for writing, running, testing, and debugging code. The critical distinction is not the presence of any single feature but the *integration* between features: the ability for the debugger to highlight the exact line in the editor where an error occurred, for the code completi...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: software engineering*
> *Permanent Note: [[integrated-development-environment|Integrated Development Environment]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Development Environment (IDE) vs. Code Editor** *[needs-note]*
> An IDE is a software application that provides comprehensive facilities for software development — editor, compiler/interpreter, debugger, build automation, and project management — in a unified package, where all tools are designed to work together by the same vendor. A code editor is a text editor enhanced with programming-oriented features (syntax highlighting, code completion, extension support) that achieves IDE-like capabilities through modular extensions rather than monolithic integration. VS Code occupies the intersection: architecturally a code editor, functionally approaching an I...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: software engineering*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Development-Environment-IDE-vs.-Code-Editor|Integrated Development Environment (IDE) vs. Code Editor]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The most autonomous form of extrinsic motivation, in which identified goals have been synthesized with the coherent self-structure and other central personal values. [[Integrated-Regulation-(Motivation)]] shares many qualities with intrinsic motivation but differs in that the activity serves values or goals the person endorses rather than being inherently interesting in itself. Integration is most fully developed in mature adults and is associated with stable, conflict-free regulation of the broadest range of personally endorsed values.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[integrated-regulation|Integrated Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The most autonomous form of extrinsic motivation. The person has assimilated the identified value into a coherent sense of self — the activity is pursued not merely because it is valued in isolation but because it coheres with the person's broader values, goals, and sense of identity. Integration differs from identification in its *systemic* character: identified regulation can be isolated and context-specific, while integrated regulation is woven into the self-concept as a whole. Integration is associated with the best outcomes short of intrinsic motivation and, unlike intrinsic motivation...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[integrated-regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Integration** **[perm-note]**
> The fullest form of internalization, in which an externally-sourced regulation has been fully assimilated into the person's coherent sense of self — their identity, values, and other regulations. Integration is not merely endorsing a value as important (that is identification); it is the synthesis of that value with the whole person. Integrated values do not feel like additions to the self; they feel like expressions of it.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[integration]]*

> [!definition] **Integration Anchor** **[perm-note]**
> The prior knowledge schema or structure that provides the connection point for situation model integration. When a reader encounters a text proposition and constructs a situation model, they are attaching the proposition to an existing schematic structure — the integration anchor — that provides the causal-relational context in which the proposition becomes meaningful.
> 
> **Boundary:** Integration anchors are not simply related prior knowledge. They must provide causal or relational structures into which the new proposition can be meaningfully connected. Loosely related information does not s...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[integration-anchor|Integration Anchor]]*

> [!definition] **Integration-Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The structural paradox within OIT in which genuine identification with an externally sourced regulation requires a prior base of felt autonomy — the very resource that controlled motivational environments deplete. This paradox implies that motivation change cannot begin at the internalization step but must first attend to autonomy restoration.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[integration-paradox]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Humility** **[perm-note]**
> An intellectual virtue consisting in the accurate recognition of one's epistemic limitations, susceptibility to error, and dependence on others for knowledge. Intellectual humility is the dispositional correlate of fallibilism: if we could be wrong, intellectual integrity demands that we hold beliefs with appropriate tentativeness, acknowledge the force of opposing arguments, and remain genuinely open to revision. Distinct from intellectual timidity (excessive self-doubt) and intellectual arrogance (unwarranted certainty).
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intellectual-humility|Intellectual Humility]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Standards** **[perm-note]**
> A set of criteria — clarity, accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, significance, and fairness — used to evaluate the quality of reasoning. Applying intellectual standards to the elements of reasoning (purpose, question, information, inference, concepts, assumptions, implications, perspective) constitutes the core act of critical evaluation in the Paul-Elder framework. The standards are domain-transferable, though their application requires domain knowledge.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intellectual-standards|Intellectual Standards]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Virtues** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Intellectual Virtues ([[Jason-Baehr|Baehr]], 2011; [[Heather-Battaly|Battaly]], 2015; [[Linda-Zagzebski|Zagzebski]], 1996)
> **Definition:** Character traits that make their possessor an excellent epistemic agent — traits that reliably contribute to the formation and maintenance of true beliefs and the avoidance of error. Include [[intellectual-humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], [[Intellectual-Empathy]], [[Intellectual-Integrity]], and [[Intellectual-Perseverance]].
> **Boundary conditions:** Intellectual virtues can conflict (e.g., intellectual courage vs. intellectual humility); ...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[intellectual-virtues|Intellectual Virtues]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual-Standards** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluative criteria for reasoning quality, including clarity, accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, significance, and fairness. Significance: transforms descriptive analysis into normative appraisal.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[intellectual-standards]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual-Traits** **[perm-note]**
> Dispositional virtues required for strong-sense critical thinking, such as [[intellectual-humility]], [[Intellectual-Courage]], and fair-mindedness. Significance: links reasoning quality to character development.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[intellectual-traits]]*

> [!definition] **Intent-Code-Understanding Cycle** **[perm-note]**
> [**Intent-Code-Understanding-Cycle**:: A learning framework proposed in this report (confidence 2/5) describing the Copilot-mediated development process as a five-step cycle: (1) formulate intent in natural language, (2) receive generated code from Copilot, (3) encounter unfamiliar constructs, (4) develop understanding through explanation or documentation, (5) modify the generated code to test and extend understanding. The critical learning moment is Step 5 — modification — which forces active application rather than passive acceptance.]
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intent-code-understanding-cycle|Intent-Code-Understanding Cycle]]*

> [!definition] **Intention-Action Gap** **[perm-note]**
> **Intention-Action Gap**: The systematic failure of sincerely held behavioral intentions to translate into corresponding actions. Not a motivational deficit but an architectural one: the absence of specific cognitive and volitional structures (implementation intentions, action orientation, habit cues) that bridge aspiration and execution. Central to understanding why SRL cannot be reduced to motivation alone.
> See also: [[implementation-intention]], [[self-regulated-learning]], [[Volitional-Control]]
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[intention-action-gap|Intention-Action Gap]]*

> [!definition] **Intention-Behavior Gap** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically documented and theoretically central phenomenon that strong intentions to perform a behavior only weakly predict whether the behavior will actually be performed; meta-analytic estimates suggest intentions account for less than thirty percent of the variance in subsequent behavior. The intention-behavior gap is the problem implementation intentions were designed to address.
> 
> **Boundary:** The intention-behavior gap is not a uniform phenomenon; its magnitude varies systematically with behavior type, time horizon, and individual differences.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** T...
>
