> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[intention-behavior-gap|Intention-Behavior Gap]]*

> [!definition] **Interacting Elements** **[perm-note]**
> [**Interacting-Elements**:: Elements that cannot be understood in isolation — understanding any one element requires simultaneous consideration of its relationships to other elements. The number of interacting elements that must be simultaneously processed defines the level of element interactivity. Example: Understanding $F = ma$ requires simultaneously processing the concepts of force, mass, and acceleration, plus their multiplicative relationship.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[interacting-elements|Interacting Elements]]*

> [!definition] **Interaction Principle** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** [[Dewey's-Continuity-and-Interaction-Principles|Interaction Principle]] (Dewey, 1938)
> **Definition:** The principle that experience is always the product of the transaction between internal conditions (the learner's dispositions, knowledge, motivations, and purposes) and external conditions (the physical, social, and intellectual environment). Genuine education requires attending to both simultaneously.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Neither purely learner-centered nor purely environment-centered approaches satisfy the interaction principle — both poles must be addressed in their dynami...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[interaction-principle|Interaction Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Interleaving Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that mixing different problem types, topics, or subject matters within a study session produces substantially better performance on delayed tests and transfer problems than presenting all instances of one type before moving to the next (blocked practice), despite blocked practice producing better within-session performance.
> 
> **Boundary:** The interleaving advantage is largest for discriminable categories with overlapping surface features; it requires sufficient prior familiarity with each category to make discrimination a cognitively meaningful operation rather than random guess...
> 
> *Source: strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science / pkb design*
> *Permanent Note: [[interleaving-effect|Interleaving Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Internalisation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which externally regulated behaviours are gradually taken in and re-regulated by the self — moving along the self-determination continuum from external regulation toward identified regulation and ultimately integrated regulation. While CET specifically addresses intrinsic motivation (the far end of the intrinsic pole), Organismic Integration Theory (another SDT sub-theory) addresses internalisation of extrinsic motivation. The two processes are related: creating conditions that support autonomy and competence facilitates both intrinsic motivation (when activities are inherent...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[internalisation]]*

> [!definition] **Internalism** **[perm-note]**
> The position that the intrinsic, physical properties of a system's internal states — specifically, states inside the skin or skull of the organism — are both necessary and sufficient to determine the content and character of mental states. Internalism is the target of both content externalism (Putnam, Burge) and vehicle externalism (Clark, Chalmers). NOT the same as claiming that nothing external is causally relevant to cognition — only that the cognitive system itself is internally bounded.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[internalism]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization** **[perm-note]**
> The developmental process by which external regulations are progressively transformed into personally owned values, identities, and motivational orientations. Moves through external → introjected → identified → integrated regulation, with intrinsic motivation as an independent endpoint. *SRL parallel: the developmental progression from other-regulated to self-regulated learning is the cognitive-processual side of the same developmental event.*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[internalization]]*

> [!definition] **The Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> [[organismic-integration-theory]] describes how externally prompted behavior can be taken in and made progressively more self-determined. The continuum runs from [[external-regulation|external regulation]] (compliance to obtain reward or avoid punishment) through [[introjected-regulation|introjected regulation]] (taking in but not accepting external pressure; ego-involvement, "should") to [[identified-regulation|identified regulation]] (endorsing the value of an activity even if not intrinsically enjoyable) to [[integrated-regulation|integrated regulation]] (full congruence of the activity'...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-internalization-continuum|The Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's model of how external regulation is progressively taken over by internal regulatory processes, from external regulation (behaviour driven by external rewards/punishments) through introjection (partially internalised but controlled by internal pressure/guilt), identification (valued and self-endorsed), integration (fully assimilated into the self), to intrinsic motivation (activity is intrinsically rewarding). The reconstruction treats this as an empirically supported graded ordering while contesting its characterisation as a strict continuum with intrinsic motivation as its endpoint.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[internalization-continuum|Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **The Internalization Phenomenon** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon by which a behavior originally performed in response to external pressure or contingency comes to be performed without such pressure, with apparent self-initiation, persistence in the absence of monitoring, and integration into the person's voluntary activity stream. The surface description is silent about the regulatory mechanism — it describes only what an observer can witness from outside.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-internalization-continuum-deep-dive-2026-04-21* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-internalization-phenomenon|The Internalization Phenomenon]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization-Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> The progression from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration to intrinsic motivation — not a spectrum of motivation amounts but a spectrum of degrees of integration between a regulatory process and the self-system. Position on the continuum reflects both the phenomenological locus of causality and the degree of structural integration of the regulation into the self.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[internalization-continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Interstellar Medium** **[perm-note]**
> The matter and radiation that exist in the space between star systems within a galaxy. Composed primarily of hydrogen (~70% by mass) and helium (~28%), with trace amounts of heavier elements and microscopic dust grains, the ISM exists in multiple phases — hot ionised gas, warm neutral gas, cold neutral gas, and molecular gas — each characterised by different temperatures, densities, and physical behaviours.
> 
> **Boundary:** The ISM does not include the circumstellar material gravitationally bound to individual stars, nor does it include the intergalactic medium between galaxies.
> 
> **Report-Spe...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[interstellar-medium|Interstellar Medium]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive demand inherent in the complexity of the material itself, determined by the number of information elements that must be simultaneously processed and the degree to which those elements interact with each other. Called "intrinsic" because it cannot be altered by instructional design without fundamentally changing what is being learned — though it can be managed by sequencing, scaffolding, and prerequisite instruction that builds relevant schemas before exposure to high-complexity material. Key technical term: **element interactivity** — the degree to which elements must be proce...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-cognitive-load|Intrinsic Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Cognitive Load in PKM** **[perm-note]**
> [**Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load-in-PKM**:: The cognitive demand imposed by the inherent complexity and element interactivity of the knowledge domain being studied, which cannot be reduced through PKM system design but can be managed through sequencing, chunking, and progressive schema construction.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-cognitive-load-in-pkm|Intrinsic Cognitive Load in PKM]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> The four canonical intrinsic goal domains are: (1) **Personal growth** — developing oneself, learning new things, increasing self-understanding and psychological depth; (2) **Meaningful relationships** — building deep, authentic, mutually supportive connections with others; (3) **Community contribution** — working to make a positive difference in one's broader community or society; and (4) **Physical health and fitness** — maintaining and developing one's physical vitality and well-being. What unites these four domains, according to GCT, is that pursuing them tends to directly satisfy the b...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-goal-content|Intrinsic Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Engagement in an activity for its own inherent interest, enjoyment, or satisfaction, independent of separable outcomes. Phenomenologically characterised by interest, absorption, and the experience of the activity as its own reward. Distinct from integrated regulation in the reconstructed framework, where the self-activity relationship differs categorically: intrinsic motivation involves the activity itself as rewarding; integrated regulation involves self-coherent pursuit of valued but non-intrinsically-interesting outcomes.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-motivation|Intrinsic Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Goal Contents** **[perm-note]**
> Intrinsic goals orient the individual toward outcomes that directly satisfy basic psychological needs — personal growth (competence), meaningful relationships (relatedness), community contribution (relatedness and autonomy), and health (competence). Extrinsic goals orient the individual toward outcomes that serve as substitutes for or proxies of need satisfaction — wealth, fame, and image — which provide external indicators of worth rather than genuine need fulfillment.
> 
> **Boundary:** GCT does not claim that earning money or caring about appearance is inherently harmful. It claims that the ...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-vs.-extrinsic-goal-contents|Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Goal Contents]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic-Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Engagement driven by inherent satisfaction in the activity itself — the activity is its own reward. This is the form of motivation that first drove Deci and Ryan's research program, and it remains the paradigm case of self-determined motivation. However, [[cognitive-evaluation-theory]] established that intrinsic motivation is fragile: it can be undermined by the wrong kinds of external events. This fragility is central to understanding the practical implications of the continuum.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[intrinsic-motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory style in which the learner is driven by internalized pressures: guilt, shame, ego-threat, or the need to maintain contingent self-esteem. Introjected regulation is classified as controlled even though the source of pressure is internal, because the learner is not freely choosing engagement — they are responding to internal compulsions that feel obligatory rather than chosen. Students who study primarily to avoid feeling like a failure are exhibiting introjected regulation.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[introjected-regulation|Introjected Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by partially internalized external constraints, now represented *within* the person as ego-involving demands. The person acts to avoid shame, protect [[self-esteem|self-esteem]], or maintain conditional approval — but these demands are experienced as pressures, not as genuine expressions of the self. Introjection is the theoretical lynchpin of the internalization analysis: it is the site where external regulation enters the person but does not become part of the self. It is internal in location but external in phenomenological character.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[introjected-regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjection** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory style in which an external rule or value has been taken in but not genuinely made one's own — the person regulates themselves through internalized approval-seeking or shame-avoidance. Introjected regulation is internal in *location* (the pressure comes from within) but external in *quality* (its source is an internalized critic rather than a genuine value). Associated with ego-involvement, contingent self-esteem, performance anxiety, and — counterintuitively — psychological costs that may exceed those of external regulation.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[introjection]]*

> [!definition] **Hippocampal Reinstatement** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon, documented via fMRI, whereby the pattern of neural activity during successful memory retrieval recapitulates the pattern present during the original encoding episode. The degree of reinstatement correlates with the quality and richness of recollective experience. Provides neural-level support for the ESP's cue-engram resonance model.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reinstatement is a correlate of successful retrieval, not its full explanation. The mechanism by which reinstatement is achieved (hippocampal pattern completion) is an active area of investigation. The relationship between reinsta...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[hippocampal-reinstatement|Hippocampal Reinstatement]]*

> [!definition] **Isolated Elements** **[perm-note]**
> [**Isolated-Elements**:: Elements that can be learned independently of one another, without reference to other elements. Each element can be processed serially in working memory, one at a time. Example: Vocabulary items in a foreign language, where knowing the word for "cat" does not require simultaneously knowing the word for "dog."]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[isolated-elements|Isolated Elements]]*

> [!definition] **Isolated Elements Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The instructional finding (Pollock, Chandler, & Sweller) that for material whose total element interactivity exceeds working-memory capacity, learning is improved by initially presenting elements in isolation — temporarily suppressing the relational structure — before re-introducing the relations once the elements have been encoded. See [[isolated-elements]].
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-element-interactivity-deep-dive-2026-04-20* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[isolated-elements-effect|Isolated Elements Effect]]*

## _J

> [!definition] **Jeans Instability** **[perm-note]**
> The condition under which a region of self-gravitating gas becomes unstable to gravitational collapse, occurring when the region's mass exceeds the Jeans mass — the critical mass at which self-gravity overwhelms thermal pressure. The Jeans mass depends on the temperature and density of the gas: lower temperatures and higher densities reduce the threshold, making collapse easier.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Jeans criterion applies to a simplified system (uniform, non-rotating, non-magnetised gas) and must be modified to account for turbulence, magnetic fields, and rotation in realistic astrophysical ...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[jeans-instability|Jeans Instability]]*

> [!definition] **Judgment of Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A metacognitive judgment made by a learner about how well they have encoded material — "I understand this," "I would recall this on a test," "I am confident about this claim." JOLs are systematically inflated by passive study strategies that produce fluency without genuine understanding. Generative strategies tend to produce more accurate JOLs because they require actual retrieval and production, revealing comprehension failures that re-reading masks. Accurate JOLs are essential for effective self-regulated learning. See also: [[metacognition]], [[metacognitive-regulation]], [[Self-Regulate...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[judgment-of-learning|Judgment of Learning]]*

> [!definition] **The Judgment of Learning (JOL) Problem** *[needs-note]*
> Judgments of Learning are the metacognitive assessments learners make about how well they have learned material — "I understand this," "I could explain this," "I would get this right on a test." Research consistently shows that JOLs made immediately after studying are systematically overconfident, particularly for material studied through passive reading. Re-reading a passage increases fluency, and learners confuse fluency with understanding. Generative strategies — particularly self-testing and self-explanation — tend to produce more accurate JOLs because they require the learner to actual...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Judgment-of-Learning-JOL-Problem|The Judgment of Learning (JOL) Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Judgment of Learning — JOL** *[needs-note]*
> A [[judgment-of-learning|judgment of learning]] is a metacognitive monitoring event in which the learner assesses the likelihood that a recently studied item will be successfully recalled at a future test. JOLs can be made immediately after study (immediate JOLs) or after a delay (delayed JOLs). The accuracy of JOLs depends critically on the cue basis: immediate JOLs rely on short-term memory cues and tend to be less accurate; delayed JOLs rely on retrieval attempts from long-term memory and are substantially more accurate.
> 
> **Boundary:** JOLs are prospective judgments about *future* perfor...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Judgment-of-Learning-—-JOL|Judgment of Learning — JOL]]*

## _K

> [!definition] **Knowing-That / Knowing-How** **[perm-note]**
> Gilbert Ryle's distinction between two types of knowledge: *knowing-that* (propositional knowledge: knowing that Paris is the capital of France) and *knowing-how* (procedural knowledge: knowing how to ride a bicycle). Ryle argued that knowing-how is not reducible to knowing-that — you cannot derive skilled performance from a set of propositions, however complete. This distinction is directly relevant to the tacit knowledge problem: expert skill represents knowing-how that cannot be converted into knowing-that without loss.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowing-that-knowing-how|Knowing-That / Knowing-How]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge of Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Knowledge of Cognition** encompasses three subtypes of metacognitive knowledge that inform strategic deployment decisions. This represents the "database" that metacognitive control queries when selecting strategies.
> 
> **Declarative Knowledge**: Awareness of yourself as a learner—your strengths ("I excel at spatial reasoning"), weaknesses ("I struggle with probabilistic thinking"), and preferences ("I need concrete examples before abstraction"). This self-model enables resource pre-allocation: if you know probability is a weakness, you budget extra time and deploy verification strategies wh...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-of-cognition|Knowledge of Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer retrieves and transcribes existing knowledge without encountering resistance between the content knowledge and the rhetorical demands of the writing situation. Produces fluent output without reorganising the underlying knowledge representation.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-telling|Knowledge Telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> The foundational distinction in composition research between two fundamentally different writing processes. *Knowledge telling* is the retrieval and transcription of existing knowledge representations — the writer asks "what do I know about this?" and writes what comes to mind. *Knowledge transforming* involves a dialectical interaction between the writer's content knowledge and their rhetorical problem space — writing to an audience, for a purpose, under constraints that force the writer to evaluate, reorganize, and sometimes revise what they believe. Knowledge transforming cannot occur wi...
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-telling-vs.-knowledge-transforming|Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer's content knowledge is pressed against the rhetorical demands of the writing situation, producing a dialectical interaction that reorganises understanding. Requires some form of communicative purpose that the writer's current knowledge cannot straightforwardly satisfy.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-transforming|Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Activation Gap [Original Construct — Speculative Synthesis]** *[needs-note]*
> The systematic failure of stored, declaratively accurate metacognitive knowledge to activate in the relevant regulatory contexts during task performance, even when monitoring conditions that should trigger activation are present. Hypothesized to result from three contributing mechanisms: indexing insufficiency (declarative knowledge indexed to instruction contexts rather than task-embedded monitoring signals), proceduralization deficit (insufficient supported practice to proceduralize declarative knowledge), and monitoring-knowledge decoupling (weak associative links between monitoring stat...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Activation-Gap-Original-Construct-—-Speculative-Synthesis|Knowledge-Activation Gap [Original Construct — Speculative Synthesis]]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the writer retrieves and transcribes information already present in working memory or available in external sources, without significantly transforming, reorganising, or extending their understanding. The output accurately reflects pre-existing knowledge; the process adds nothing to it. Novice writers and most conventional note-taking operate in this mode.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the compositional process itself generates new understanding — through the need to resolve contradictions, fill explanatory gaps, reformulate confusing ideas, or integrate concepts that did not previously connect in the writer's mental model. Expert writers move between the knowledge space and the rhetorical space in a recursive dialogue that transforms both. Learning is a byproduct of this dialectic.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[knowledge-transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Krashen's Input Hypothesis** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** The theoretical claim that language acquisition — including vocabulary acquisition — occurs through exposure to *comprehensible input* slightly beyond the learner's current level (*i+1*). On this view, explicit instruction plays a minor role; the primary acquisition mechanism is the processing of large quantities of meaningful, comprehensible input. Applied to vocabulary, the hypothesis predicts that extensive reading is the primary and most efficient vocabulary acquisition mechanism.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The proximate theoretical source of the received view un...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Krashen's-Input-Hypothesis|Krashen's Input Hypothesis]]*

## _L

> [!definition] **Elaboration** **[perm-note]**
> Within the semantic processing level, the degree of richness, complexity, and associative spread with which a stimulus is encoded. More elaborated encodings recruit more prior knowledge nodes and create more retrieval pathways. Distinct from processing depth: elaboration modulates trace durability *within* a level, while depth determines the *class* of processing (structural, phonemic, semantic).
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaboration]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Encoding** **[perm-note]**
> A memory encoding strategy in which new information is processed by relating it to existing knowledge — generating connections, examples, questions, and explanatory structures. Elaborative encoding produces richer and more retrievable memory traces than maintenance encoding (rehearsal) or perceptual encoding (surface-level attention). It is the mechanism underlying the levels-of-processing effect.
> 
> **Boundary:** Elaborative encoding requires that existing knowledge exist to connect to. In genuinely novel domains with no relevant prior schemas, elaborative encoding may not be possible and ba...
> 
> *Source: pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-encoding|Elaborative Encoding]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Inference** **[perm-note]**
> An inference generated during reading that goes beyond what is necessary for local text coherence, adding information from the reader's prior knowledge to enrich the mental model. Distinct from bridging inferences (necessary for coherence) and evaluative inferences (assessing text quality or truth). Elaborative inferences require cognitive resources and constructive engagement.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-inference|Elaborative Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Interrogation** **[perm-note]**
> A reading and study strategy in which the learner generates answers to "why" questions about factual material being studied. The standard question format is "Why is it true that [fact]?" or "Why would [X] produce [Y]?" EI is distinguished from other question-prompting techniques by its specific causal-explanatory demand.
> 
> **Boundary:** EI does not refer to all question-asking during reading. "What" questions, "when" questions, and "how many" questions are not EI questions. The defining feature is the demand for a causal or functional explanation, not merely the question format.
> 
> **Operation...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-interrogation|Elaborative Interrogation]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Rehearsal** **[perm-note]**
> Processing that increases depth by connecting a stimulus to existing knowledge, generating implications, creating associative links, or embedding it in meaningful context. In contrast to maintenance rehearsal, elaborative rehearsal moves processing down (deeper) the levels-of-processing continuum and produces meaningfully improved long-term retention. This distinction between the two types of rehearsal is one of LOP's most practically significant contributions.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-rehearsal|Elaborative Rehearsal]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Richness** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a generated why-answer specifies a precise, mechanistic, and well-differentiated causal account, as contrasted with vague, tautological, or general explanatory claims. Elaborative richness is the within-deep-processing variable that determines the magnitude of EI's retention benefit: richer elaborations produce superior retention, particularly on transfer and inference measures.
> 
> **Boundary:** Elaborative richness is not merely answer length. A short, precise mechanistic account may be more elaboratively rich than a verbose but vague explanation.
> 
> **Research Status:** Th...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-richness|Elaborative Richness]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An inference that goes beyond what the text explicitly states to elaborate the situation model with information derived from prior knowledge — for example, inferring that a character is angry because the text described them slamming a door. Boundary condition: elaborative inferences are not required for text-base construction but are necessary for rich situation model construction; they are not generated automatically by most readers.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The primary cognitive mechanism bridging the text base to the situation model; its absence is the proximate...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[elaborative-inferenceelaborative-inference|Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Language Server Protocol** **[perm-note]**
> The Language Server Protocol is a standardized communication protocol between a code editor (the client) and a language analysis engine (the server) that enables language intelligence features — code completion, error detection, go-to-definition, symbol search, refactoring — to be developed once for a language and used by any editor that supports the protocol. The protocol uses JSON-RPC messages to communicate between processes, with the language server performing heavy computational analysis asynchronously while the editor handles user interaction. For Python in VS Code, the language serve...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: software engineering*
> *Permanent Note: [[language-server-protocol|Language Server Protocol]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Contract** **[perm-note]**
> A negotiated agreement between learner and facilitator that specifies learning objectives, strategies, resources, evidence of accomplishment, and evaluation criteria, all co-determined by the learner. The [[learning-contracts|learning contract]] functions as a curriculum mechanism for developing self-regulatory capacity through the process of negotiation and self-directed planning.
> 
> **Boundary:** Learning contracts are most effective during the Negotiated Autonomy phase of the Scaffolding-Sovereignty Progression. Deployed before the learner has sufficient metacognitive capacity, they produc...
> 
> *Source: curriculum-design-for-lifelong-learning-and-self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: education — curriculum design*
> *Permanent Note: [[learning-contract|Learning Contract]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Contracts** **[perm-note]**
> Negotiated agreements between learner and facilitator specifying learning goals, strategies and resources, evidence of learning, and evaluation criteria. The primary pedagogical instrument for operationalising SDL process in formal educational settings, learning contracts externalise and make visible the planning and goal-setting activities that natural SDL performs implicitly. Effective learning contracts are genuinely negotiated rather than merely approved; require iterative revision as learning proceeds; and are evaluated against jointly agreed criteria rather than normative benchmarks. ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[learning-contracts|Learning Contracts]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Projects** **[perm-note]**
> A concept from Allen Tough's empirical research designating any deliberate effort lasting at least seven hours to gain and retain specific knowledge or skills. Research using structured interviews found that average adults undertake roughly five to ten learning projects per year, approximately seventy percent of which are self-planned rather than institutionally directed. Learning projects data established the empirical foundation for the claim that SDL is not merely an educational ideal but a description of how the majority of adult learning actually occurs.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[learning-projects|Learning Projects]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Task** **[perm-note]**
> Authentic whole-task experiences organized into task classes of increasing complexity, forming the backbone of a 4C/ID training blueprint. Each learning task requires learners to integrate and coordinate all constituent skills of the target complex skill. Within each task class, scaffolding (via worked examples, completion tasks, and conventional tasks) is faded from high support to full independence.
> 
> **Boundary:** Learning tasks are not exercises that isolate individual skill components. Their defining characteristic is the integration of multiple skill elements within a single performanc...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[learning-task|Learning Task]]*

> [!definition] **Element** **[perm-note]**
> [**Element-in-CLT**:: An element is anything that has been or needs to be learned — any unit of information that must be processed in working memory. For a novice, elements are typically the smallest meaningful components of the learning material. For an expert, a schema that encapsulates multiple lower-level elements functions as a single element.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[element]]*

> [!definition] **Element Interactivity** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which elements of a task or concept must be processed simultaneously rather than sequentially to be understood. High element interactivity means many elements must be held in mind at once; low element interactivity allows processing one element at a time. Element interactivity is the primary determinant of intrinsic cognitive load. Critical problem: "element" is not formally defined in CLT, and what counts as an element depends on the learner's existing schemas — creating the circularity we will examine in Phase III.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[element-interactivity|Element Interactivity]]*

> [!definition] **Elements of Thought** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Elements of Thought ([[Richard-Paul|Paul]] & [[Linda-Elder|Elder]], 2001)
> **Definition:** Eight universal structures present in all reasoning: purpose, question at issue, information, interpretation/inference, concepts, assumptions, implications/consequences, and point of view. Critical analysis involves examining each element for quality using Intellectual Standards.
> **Boundary conditions:** The framework provides structural analysis tools but requires domain knowledge for meaningful application. Not all reasoning tasks engage all eight elements equally.
> **Report significance:** ...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[elements-of-thought|Elements of Thought]]*

> [!definition] **Elenchus** **[perm-note]**
> The Socratic method of cross-examination: a systematic procedure in which the Questioner draws out the interlocutor's commitments through apparently innocent questions, then demonstrates that those commitments, taken together, generate a contradiction or inadequacy. The elenchus does not refute from the outside; it shows the interlocutor that their own beliefs are internally inconsistent. Vlastos distinguishes the *elenchus* (which aims at exposing contradictions) from the *maieutic method* (which aims at helping the interlocutor give birth to insight).
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[elenchus]]*

> [!definition] **Teleological Directionality** **[perm-note]**
> The implicit or explicit assumption in a developmental theory that development has a natural endpoint toward which it tends under appropriate conditions. In OIT, the teleological directionality is the assumption that internalization naturally tends toward integration. Philosophically, teleological directionality in developmental theories should be examined for: (1) the degree to which the "natural endpoint" is empirically demonstrated vs. normatively prescribed; (2) whether the endpoint reflects universal human development or the theorist's cultural context; and (3) whether the directionali...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[teleological-directionality|Teleological Directionality]]*

> [!definition] **Levels of Processing** **[perm-note]**
> [[levels-of-processing]] theory proposes that memory and comprehension are byproducts of the depth at which stimulus features are processed — from shallow, phonological-structural processing through to deep, semantic-elaborative processing. Deep processing involves extracting meaning, making connections to prior knowledge, and generating inferences. [[shallow-processing]] involves attending to surface features without integration. Critical analysis requires deep, elaborative processing; it cannot emerge from shallow engagement.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[levels-of-processing|Levels of Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Levels of Processing Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework proposing that the durability of memory traces is a function of the depth at which information is processed during encoding. Shallow processing (attending to sensory or perceptual features such as letter shapes or sounds) produces weaker, less durable traces. Deeper processing (attending to semantic, conceptual, and associative features) produces stronger, more durable traces. Elaborative rehearsal — actively connecting new information to existing knowledge — represents the deep end of the processing continuum.
> 
> **Boundary:** LOP has been criticized for circularity (defining dep...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[levels-of-processing-theory|Levels of Processing Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Automaticity** **[perm-note]**
> The state in which word recognition (mapping orthographic form to phonological and semantic representations) requires minimal attentional resources. Lexical automaticity is achieved through massive exposure to a word form across varied contexts. It is a graded property — recognition can be partially automatized — not a binary state.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[lexical-automaticity|Lexical Automaticity]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The multi-dimensional representation of a word in long-term memory, comprising at minimum four layers of knowledge that are acquired incrementally and through different types of exposure. Full lexical knowledge of a word includes knowing its phonological form, its orthographic form, its morphological family, its syntactic behavior, its primary and secondary meanings, its collocational patterns (what words it typically occurs near), its register constraints (formal vs. informal), and its conceptual referent. Importantly, these layers are not acquired simultaneously — reading may contribute d...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[lexical-knowledge|Lexical Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Quality** **[perm-note]**
> The precision and completeness of a word's representation in long-term memory, encompassing orthographic, phonological, morphological, semantic, and syntactic information. High-quality lexical representations are retrieved rapidly and automatically during reading, consuming minimal working memory. Low-quality representations require effortful processing that competes with comprehension.
> 
> **Boundary:** Lexical quality is a continuum, not a binary. The same word may have high-quality representation in one person's lexicon and low-quality in another's — even if both individuals would pass a vo...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[lexical-quality|Lexical Quality]]*

> [!definition] **Lexicon** **[perm-note]**
> 
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[lexicon]]*

> [!definition] **Lexicon: Core Terms** **[perm-note]**
> 1. **[[cognitive-load-theory]]** — An instructional design theory based on the premise that [[working-memory]] has limited capacity, and that instructional materials must be designed to manage this constraint for effective [[schema-construction]].
> 
> 2. **[[intrinsic-cognitive-load]]** — The portion of cognitive load attributable to the inherent complexity of the material being learned, determined by [[element-interactivity]] relative to the learner's existing [[schemas]].
> 
> 3. **[[extraneous-cognitive-load]]** — The portion of cognitive load attributable to the design of the learning environm...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[lexicon-core-terms|Lexicon: Core Terms]]*

> [!definition] **Lifelong Learning** **[perm-note]**
> [[lifelong-learning|Lifelong learning]] refers to the ongoing, voluntary, and self-motivated pursuit of knowledge and competence across the entire span of a person's life, encompassing formal, non-formal, and informal learning contexts. Unlike education conceived as a bounded phase of preparation for adult life, lifelong learning operates on the premise that the capacity and need for learning does not diminish after formal schooling but continues — and in many respects intensifies — as individuals encounter new roles, new technologies, new domains of knowledge, and new challenges that their...
> 
> *Source: curriculum-design-for-lifelong-learning-and-self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: education — curriculum design*
> *Permanent Note: [[lifelong-learning|Lifelong Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Local Coherence** **[perm-note]**
> Local coherence refers to the connectedness of adjacent or nearby textual units — the degree to which each sentence or clause can be linked to its immediate neighbors through shared reference, causal connection, or explicit discourse connectors. Local coherence is maintained when the reader can track how the current sentence relates to the previous one. It is primarily a textbase-level property and is supported by surface cues: repeated nominals, pronoun referents, temporal connectors ("then," "subsequently"), and causal connectors ("therefore," "consequently").
> 
> **Boundary:** Local coheren...
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[local-coherence|Local Coherence]]*

> [!definition] **Local vs. Global Coherence** **[perm-note]**
> **Local coherence**: the connectedness of adjacent text units — whether each sentence links to its immediate neighbors. A textbase-level property. **Global coherence**: the unity and consistency of the situation model as a whole — whether all representation elements integrate into a non-contradictory simulation. A situation-model-level property. A text can be locally coherent throughout while failing to produce global coherence.
> 
> **See also:** [[textbase]], [[situation-model]], [[schema-theory]]
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[local-vs.-global-coherence|Local vs. Global Coherence]]*

> [!definition] **Locus of Causality** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The location of a cause as internal to the person (ability, effort, mood, strategy) or external to the person (task difficulty, luck, other people's actions). This dimension is closely related to but distinct from [[perceived-locus-of-causality]] in SDT — the SDT construct refers to whether the *regulation of behavior* feels internally initiated, whereas Weiner's locus refers to the *location of the cause* of an outcome.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Locus primarily determines the emotional valence of self-directed emotions — specifically pride and shame. Internal attri...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[locus-of-causality|Locus of Causality]]*

> [!definition] **The Locus of Causality Dimension** **[perm-note]**
> The [[locus-of-causality]] dimension classifies causes as either internal to the person (ability, effort, mood, personality) or external to the person (task difficulty, luck, other people's actions, institutional factors). This dimension is the direct descendant of [[fritz-heider]]'s original internal/external distinction, though Weiner refined it by distinguishing locus of causality from locus of control — a distinction that matters because a cause can be internal (it resides within me) without being controllable (I cannot change it).
> 
> **Boundary:** Locus of causality should not be confuse...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: social cognition & motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-locus-of-causality-dimension|The Locus of Causality Dimension]]*

> [!definition] **Locus of Control** **[perm-note]**
> An attributional construct measuring the degree to which individuals believe outcomes are caused by their own behavior (internal LOC) versus external forces (external LOC). Distinguished from control orientation: LOC concerns causal attribution; control orientation concerns motivational register. The distinction is sharpest for the case of internally-controlled motivation (guilt, shame, ego-involvement), which implies internal LOC but control orientation.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[locus-of-control|Locus of Control]]*

> [!definition] **Long-Term Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive system responsible for the long-term storage of knowledge, skills, procedures, and episodic memories. Unlike working memory, long-term memory has no known capacity limit and no intrinsic decay rate for well-consolidated memories. Information in long-term memory is organized into networks of interconnected structures. For CLT, the crucial feature of long-term memory is that its contents — particularly schemas — can be activated and used in working memory without consuming working memory capacity in proportion to their complexity.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[long-term-memory|Long-Term Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Long-Term Working Memory** **[perm-note]**
> A functional architecture, developed through extended domain-specific practice, in which long-term memory serves as an extended workspace through retrieval cues maintained in standard working memory. The expert constructs a network of cue-content associations — a *retrieval structure* — that allows large amounts of domain-relevant information to be accessed rapidly enough to function as if it were in working memory, even though the actual storage is in long-term memory.
> 
> **Boundary:** Long-term working memory is not a separate memory system. It is an emergent functional architecture that us...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[long-term-working-memory|Long-Term Working Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Helpless Response Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational response pattern triggered by achievement difficulty or failure in performance-goal conditions, characterized by effort withdrawal, maladaptive attributions to stable factors (low ability), deteriorating performance, and negative affect — the opposite of the mastery-oriented response of maintained effort and adaptive strategy change.
> 
> **See also:** [[helpless-response-pattern]], [[learned-helplessness]], [[attribution-theory]], [[performance-goal-orientation]]
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[helpless-response-pattern|Helpless Response Pattern]]*

## _M

> [!definition] **Main Sequence** **[perm-note]**
> The long, stable phase of stellar evolution during which a star generates energy through hydrogen fusion in its core, maintaining hydrostatic and thermal equilibrium. The main sequence is represented as a diagonal band on the [[Hertzsprung-Russell-Diagram|Hertzsprung-Russell diagram]], where stars spend the great majority (roughly 90%) of their lifetimes.
> 
> **Boundary:** The main sequence is a phase of evolution, not a type of star — all hydrogen-fusing stars pass through it, but they eventually exhaust their core hydrogen and evolve off the main sequence.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** ...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[main-sequence|Main Sequence]]*

> [!definition] **Maintenance Rehearsal** **[perm-note]**
> The repetitive cycling of information at a constant level of processing depth — typically at the phonemic level (saying a phone number over and over to keep it in mind). Craik and Lockhart proposed that maintenance rehearsal produces minimal improvement in long-term retention because it does not increase processing depth; it merely sustains the trace at a shallow level. This was a direct challenge to the rehearsal-based account in the [[atkinson-shiffrin-multi-store-model|Atkinson-Shiffrin Multi-Store Model]].
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[maintenance-rehearsal|Maintenance Rehearsal]]*

> [!definition] **Major Critical Thinking Assessment Instruments** **[perm-note]**
> - **Watson-Glaser Critical Thinking Appraisal (WGCTA)**: The oldest standardized CT test (1940, revised multiple times). Measures five dimensions: inference, recognition of assumptions, deduction, interpretation, and evaluation of arguments. Widely used in professional settings. Reflects a skills-focused conceptualization.
> - **Cornell Critical Thinking Tests (CCTT)**: Developed by Ennis and colleagues. Level X (secondary) and Level Z (advanced/college). Measures induction, deduction, credibility, assumption identification, and meaning.
> - **California Critical Thinking Skills Test (CCTST)**:...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[major-critical-thinking-assessment-instruments|Major Critical Thinking Assessment Instruments]]*

> [!definition] **Manipulative Abduction** **[perm-note]**
> A form of abductive inference that occurs through and in the course of physical manipulation of the environment — experimentation, prototyping, tool use, diagrammatic reasoning. Distinct from *sentential abduction* (hypothesis formation through linguistic or logical deliberation) and *model-based abduction* (hypothesis formation through mental model manipulation). Manipulative abduction grounds the epistemological value of hands-on learning: physical interaction with materials can generate hypotheses that purely mental deliberation would not produce, because the material world offers unexpe...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[manipulative-abduction|Manipulative Abduction]]*

> [!definition] **Mark of the Cognitive** **[perm-note]**
> The property or properties that distinguish genuinely cognitive processes from merely causally relevant ones. Identifying the mark of the cognitive is the central challenge posed by critics of Extended Mind Theory. Adams and Aizawa propose intrinsic, non-derived representational content as the mark; Clark rejects this criterion as theoretically unmotivated and argues that functional role provides the appropriate criterion. The debate about the mark of the cognitive has proved productive: it has forced more precise accounts of what cognitive status means.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mark-of-the-cognitive|Mark of the Cognitive]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Climate** **[perm-note]**
> A learning environment whose configuration of structural features (tasks, authority distribution, recognition criteria, grouping practices, evaluation methods, time organization) jointly elicits mastery-approach goal orientation in the learners operating within it. The configuration is *compositional*: changing some dimensions while leaving others in place tends to produce attenuated effects.
> 
> **Boundary:** Distinct from *mastery learning* (Bloom's instructional approach) and from *individualized instruction* (which can be implemented in either climate type). Climate is about the meaning th...
> 
> *Source: goal-orientation-theory-mastery-vs-performance-goals-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-climate|Mastery Climate]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Climate vs. Performance Climate** **[perm-note]**
> A *mastery climate* is a learning environment whose configuration of structural features (tasks, authority distribution, recognition criteria, grouping practices, evaluation methods, time organization) jointly elicits mastery-approach goal orientation in the learners operating within it. A *performance climate* is a learning environment whose configuration jointly elicits performance orientation, typically with a bias toward the avoidance variant in learners whose ability self-concept is fragile. Climates are not merely backdrops to individual orientation but active producers of orientation...
> 
> *Source: goal-orientation-theory-mastery-vs-performance-goals-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-climate-vs.-performance-climate|Mastery Climate vs. Performance Climate]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Experience** **[perm-note]**
> In Bandura's framework, an actual experience of successfully performing a challenging task, which is the most powerful source of self-efficacy. Contrasted with vicarious experience, verbal persuasion, and physiological states as sources of efficacy information. This report's reconstruction identifies mastery experience architecture as more fundamental to growth mindset outcomes than mindset instruction.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-experience|Mastery Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A mastery goal is an achievement goal in which the person defines success as developing competence — acquiring new skills, deepening understanding, or improving relative to one's own prior performance. The standard of reference is intrapersonal and/or task-inherent. Mastery goals are sometimes called *learning goals* (Dweck's terminology) or *task goals* (Nicholls's terminology). They are associated with the adoption of deep cognitive strategies, help-seeking when confused, persistence in the face of difficulty, positive affect during challenge, and long-term retention. Under mastery-orient...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-goal|Mastery Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal / Mastery-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> An achievement goal in which success is defined by self-improvement, task mastery, or competence development relative to prior performance or absolute task standards. In the 2×2 framework, the approach form specifically represents orientation toward positive mastery outcomes.
> 
> **Common Misconception:** Mastery goals do not preclude high absolute performance standards; they merely specify that success is self-referential or task-referenced rather than normatively comparative.
> 
> **See also:** [[mastery-goal-orientation]], [[mastery-approach-goal]], [[intrinsic-motivation]], [[Task-Involvement]...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-goal-mastery-approach-goal|Mastery Goal / Mastery-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A goal orientation focused on developing competence, understanding, and learning — where success is defined as improved understanding rather than relative performance. Associated with deeper processing, greater persistence, and more adaptive responses to failure than performance goal orientations.
> **Operational Indicators:** A learner with an active mastery orientation frames comprehension failures as information about process, seeks to understand *why* a concept is difficult, and experiences setback as a learning signal rather than a verdict about ability.
> **Cross-Reference...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-goal-orientation|Mastery Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal Orientation in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> A mastery goal orientation toward academic reading is characterized by the reader's primary concern with developing genuine understanding, expanding knowledge, and improving reading skill — with success defined as personal improvement and deepened comprehension rather than outperformance of others. Mastery-oriented readers interpret difficulty as a signal to try different strategies; they persist longer under comprehension failure; and they are more likely to engage in deep processing activities (elaboration, self-explanation, questioning) because these activities serve understanding rather...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-goal-orientation-in-reading|Mastery Goal Orientation in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Grammar** **[perm-note]**
> A linguistic register for evaluative prompts, self-talk, and feedback that frames evaluation in terms of growth, understanding, and capability development rather than judgment, comparison, or performance adequacy. Questions in mastery grammar ask "what did you learn?" and "where is your growing edge?" rather than "how well did you do?" and "was that good enough?" Mastery grammar activates mastery-approach goal orientation through repeated use.
> 
> **Boundary:** Mastery grammar is not euphemistic or imprecise. It does not avoid honest evaluation of performance — it reframes the evaluative purpo...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-grammar|Mastery Grammar]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery vs. Performance Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> Two contrasting orientations toward learning tasks: mastery orientation focuses on developing competence and understanding (with errors interpreted as information), while performance orientation focuses on demonstrating competence and outperforming others (with errors interpreted as evidence of inadequacy).
> 
> **Boundary:** The orientations are not personality traits but situationally adoptable framings; the framework treats the choice of orientation as a regulatory move available within the forethought phase.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The orientation construct that the framework tre...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-vs.-performance-goal-orientation|Mastery vs. Performance Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Striving to develop competence, to learn, to master tasks, and to improve one's own performance. The standard is absolute or self-referential; success means doing better than one's prior performance or achieving task mastery. This is the approach pole of the mastery dimension.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** MAP goals are the most uniformly beneficial goal type in the empirical literature — the one cell of the 2×2 where the evidence across outcomes, populations, and contexts is most consistent.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-approach-goal|Mastery-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Avoidance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Striving to avoid failure to learn, to avoid forgetting previously mastered material, to avoid performing worse than one's prior standard. The standard is self-referential, but the motivational orientation is avoidant. This is the rarest and most theoretically novel cell of the 2×2.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** MAV goals were [[john-elliot|Andrew Elliot]]'s theoretical contribution — the cell that had no home in earlier frameworks. They appear predominantly in perfectionist individuals and in older adults concerned with maintaining existing competencies.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-avoidance-goal|Mastery-Avoidance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Experience Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary used in post-reading self-reflection to characterize and encode prior comprehension successes in a way that contributes to [[self-efficacy]] accumulation. "I understood that" produces a weaker efficacy deposit than "I worked through the conceptual difficulty in that section and reached understanding" — the second encodes the effort-success connection and the strategy-deployment that will transfer to future difficult passages. Mastery experience vocabulary is the self-narration through which difficult comprehension successes become efficacy-building events.
> 
> **...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-experience-vocabulary|Mastery-Experience Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Oriented Response Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A behavioral pattern in which a learner responds to failure or setback by maintaining task engagement, trying new strategies, showing neutral or positive affect, and maintaining or improving performance on subsequent problems. Characteristic of growth-mindset learners; associated with mastery goal orientation in Achievement Goal Theory.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mastery-oriented-response-pattern|Mastery-Oriented Response Pattern]]*

> [!definition] **Mathemagenic Activities** **[perm-note]**
> Coined by Rothkopf (1970), referring to learner behaviors that generate learning — literally "give birth to learning." Wittrock used this concept to ground GLT: the learner's own cognitive activities, not the information encountered, are the proximal cause of learning. Mathemagenic activities include generating questions, making predictions, creating summaries, constructing visual representations, and any other behaviors that require active construction of meaning. The concept foregrounds learner activity rather than instructional design as the primary cause of learning outcomes.
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[mathemagenic-activities|Mathemagenic Activities]]*

> [!definition] **Matthew Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The compounding advantage in reading development named after the Gospel of Matthew ("to him who has, more will be given"): readers who read more develop larger vocabularies, enabling more fluent decoding, freeing more cognitive resources for meaning-making, increasing comprehension and motivation, leading to more reading. Relevant to critical analysis development through its underlying mechanism of vocabulary-mediated working memory availability.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[matthew-effect|Matthew Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Matthew Effect in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Stanovich's (1986) application of the sociological "Matthew Effect" (from Matthew 25:29 — "to him who has, more will be given") to reading development. Readers who start with larger vocabularies read more fluently, which produces more enjoyable reading experiences, which produces more reading, which produces more vocabulary — while readers who start with smaller vocabularies find reading effortful, read less, acquire less vocabulary, and fall further behind. The result is a *divergent trajectory*: vocabulary-rich and vocabulary-poor learners do not converge on the same vocabulary ceiling th...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-matthew-effect-in-reading|The Matthew Effect in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Matthew Effect in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> The compounding inequality in reading development whereby early reading skill advantages generate increased reading volume, vocabulary growth, and world knowledge, creating a positive feedback loop that accelerates skill development in already-skilled readers — while early skill deficits generate reading avoidance, reduced word exposure, and slower vocabulary and knowledge growth, creating a negative feedback loop. Named after the biblical observation that "to he who has, more shall be given."
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[matthew-effect-in-reading|Matthew Effect in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Matthew-Effect-in-Reading** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The self-amplifying advantage in reading development by which good readers, through extensive reading, develop richer vocabulary and prior knowledge that makes subsequent reading easier and more productive, while poor readers develop less, making subsequent reading harder — resulting in compounding divergence between strong and weak readers over time. Named after the biblical principle that advantages accrue to those who already have them.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Explains why situation model deficits are not static problems but self-sustaining ones; prior knowledg...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[matthew-effect-in-reading]]*

> [!definition] **Embedded-Processes Model** **[perm-note]**
> A theoretical framework in which working memory is not a separate structural system but rather the product of attentional processes operating on the temporarily activated subset of long-term memory. The model distinguishes between a broad activated region (unlimited in principle but subject to temporal decay) and a narrow focus of attention (limited to approximately 3–4 items).
> 
> **Boundary:** This model does not deny domain-specific processing differences but attributes them to long-term memory representations and attentional strategies rather than to dedicated architectural subsystems.
> 
> **...
> 
> *Source: working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[embedded-processes-model|Embedded-Processes Model]]*

> [!definition] **Embodied Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The theoretical and empirical program holding that cognitive processes are grounded in sensorimotor experience — that concepts, schemas, and knowledge structures are constituted by patterns of embodied simulation rather than abstract amodal representations. After this re-examination: the primary philosophical challenge to schema theory's representationalist foundation; not merely a different vocabulary for the same phenomenon.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Motivates the primary philosophical challenge to schema theory.
> **Cross-References**: [[embodied-cognition]], [[Embo...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[embodied-cognition|Embodied Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **MCII** **[perm-note]**
> A compound intervention combining Oettingen's mental-contrasting protocol (vividly imagining a desired future, then contrasting it with present obstacles to produce expectancy-dependent commitment) with Gollwitzer's implementation-intention specification (forming if-then plans for goal-relevant behavior). MCII routinely produces larger behavioral effects than either constituent alone because the two interventions address complementary failure modes — commitment formation and commitment translation — that together constitute the full goal-pursuit cycle.
> 
> **Boundary:** MCII is not a guarantee...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mcii]]*

> [!definition] **Meaningful Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of learning in which new information is intentionally related to existing knowledge structures in the learner's cognitive field, producing genuine integration between new and existing concepts. Meaningful learning contrasts with *rote learning*, in which material is memorized in isolation without connection to existing knowledge — producing fragile, non-transferable, rapidly decaying retention. Ausubel argued that almost all formal instruction produces rote learning by default because it fails to establish the cognitive conditions required for assimilation: relevant anchoring ideas m...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[meaningful-learning|Meaningful Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> [[memory-consolidation|Memory consolidation]] refers to the time-dependent processes by which recently encoded memory traces are transformed from a labile, disruption-sensitive state to a more stable, disruption-resistant state. Consolidation operates at two distinguishable levels: [[synaptic-consolidation|synaptic consolidation]], which involves molecular and cellular changes at the synapse and unfolds over minutes to hours; and [[systems-consolidation|systems consolidation]], which involves the gradual reorganization of memory representations across brain regions and may extend over weeks...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[memory-consolidation|Memory Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Durability** **[perm-note]**
> The persistence of a memory trace over time, understood within the levels of processing framework as a direct function of the depth of processing applied during encoding — not of storage location, rehearsal frequency, or conscious intention to remember. A durably encoded memory is one produced by elaborative, semantic, or otherwise meaningful cognitive engagement with the target material.
> 
> **Boundary:** Memory durability in this framework is specifically about the encoding determinants of trace strength. It does not directly address retrieval-context effects, which became the focus of the [...
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[memory-durability|Memory Durability]]*

> [!definition] **Memory System** **[perm-note]**
> A memory system is a functionally and neurologically distinct set of processes and representations that stores, organizes, and retrieves information by different mechanisms, supports different types of knowledge, and dissociates from other systems under conditions of amnesia, development, or neurological damage. The term was systematized by [[endel-tulving]] (1985), who argued that memory systems differ in their brain structures, acquisition procedures, representational formats, and retrieval mechanisms — not merely in content.
> 
> **Boundary:** The term does not mean simply "a type of memory ...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[memory-system|Memory System]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Trace / Engram** **[perm-note]**
> The physical substrate of a stored memory in neural tissue. The concept of the engram — a specific, localizable neural change corresponding to a specific memory — was sought for decades following Semon's (1904) coinage of the term. Modern neuroscience understands the engram not as a single localized pattern but as a *distributed pattern of synaptic weights* across networks of neurons, reconstituted by the reactivation of (a subset of) the neurons active during encoding. [[memory-trace-durability]] varies with the depth of encoding, emotional salience, frequency of reactivation, and the time...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[memory-trace-engram|Memory Trace / Engram]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Trace Durability** **[perm-note]**
> In the LOP framework, the persistence and accessibility of a memory over time, understood as a function of encoding quality rather than trace strength in a simple quantitative sense. Deeper processing does not simply create "more" of the same kind of trace — it creates a qualitatively different trace, characterized by richer elaboration, more retrieval routes, and greater resistance to interference. The concept is deliberately distinguished from mere rehearsal frequency.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[memory-trace-durability|Memory Trace Durability]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Contrasting** **[perm-note]**
> A self-regulatory technique in which the actor vividly imagines a desired future outcome and then deliberately contrasts it with the present obstacles standing in the way; the contrast produces expectancy-dependent commitment, with strong commitment when the goal is judged feasible and disengagement when it is judged infeasible.
> 
> **Boundary:** Mental contrasting alone does not engineer the cue-triggered enactment that implementation intentions provide; its primary function is commitment formation, not action initiation.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Mental contrasting is the upstream c...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-contrasting|Mental Contrasting]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Effort** **[perm-note]**
