> A subjective rating of the cognitive effort invested in a task, typically measured on a 9-point scale. The primary practical measure of cognitive load in CLT research. Distinct from cognitive load (a theoretical construct), performance (an outcome), and learning (a long-term outcome).
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-effort|Mental Effort]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Image** **[perm-note]**
> A quasi-pictorial representation that preserves perceptual surface features — the appearance of an object from a specific viewpoint. Mental images are viewpoint-dependent and encode phenomenal properties (colour, texture, apparent size). Johnson-Laird argues that images are *representations of models from a particular perspective*, not a separate representational format.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-image|Mental Image]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Model** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive representation of a state of affairs that is: (a) structurally analogous to that state of affairs rather than merely encoding propositional descriptions of it; (b) constructed from semantic content rather than syntactic form; (c) token-based (containing representations of specific individuals, not just variables); and (d) capable of underwriting inference by inspection rather than rule application. Distinguished from: (i) propositional representations, (ii) images in the narrow visual sense, (iii) "mental model" in the folk-cognitive or HCI sense.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-model|Mental Model]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Models** **[perm-note]**
> Internal representational structures that capture the states of affairs described by premises in a deductive argument. On Johnson-Laird's Mental Models Theory, deductive reasoning proceeds by (a) constructing one or more mental models of the premises, (b) drawing a putative conclusion that is true in the initial model, and (c) checking whether alternative models of the premises exist in which the conclusion is false. Reasoning difficulty correlates with the number of models required to represent the premises. What it is NOT: mental models are not visual images, though they may have visuospa...
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-models|Mental Models]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Simulation** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process of running a mental model forward in time to predict consequences, analogous to a physical simulation. Mental simulation is implicated in mechanical reasoning (imagining how gears turn), spatial reasoning (imagining how a figure would look rotated), causal reasoning (imagining what would happen if a component failed), and counterfactual reasoning (imagining what would have happened under different conditions). Mental simulation is closely related to — and may be partially explained by — embodied simulation in [[Perceptual Symbol Systems]].
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mental-simulation|Mental Simulation]]*

> [!definition] **Mentalization** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to understand behavior — one's own and others' — in terms of underlying mental states: thoughts, feelings, beliefs, wishes, and intentions. Developed theoretically by Peter Fonagy and Anthony Bateman, mentalization is understood as a developmental achievement that requires a sufficiently secure relational environment to emerge robustly. Failures of mentalization — defaulting to concrete, non-reflective, or hyperactivated modes of experiencing — are central to severe personality disorder, attachment disorganization, and trauma. Mentalization-Based Treatment (MBT) explicitly targ...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mentalization]]*

> [!definition] **Meta-Level** **[perm-note]**
> The [[Meta-Level-Object-Level-Model|meta-level]] is the higher-order component of the Nelson–Narrens architecture that contains a dynamic model of the object-level's current state. It receives information from the object-level through the monitoring channel and issues control directives that modify object-level processing. The meta-level is not a homunculus; it is itself a cognitive process, constrained by the cues available to it and subject to systematic biases.
> 
> **Boundary:** The meta-level is not coextensive with consciousness. Metacognitive monitoring can occur without conscious awaren...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[meta-level]]*

> [!definition] **Meta-Level/Object-Level Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> The meta-level/object-level architecture is the formal structure through which Nelson and Narrens propose that [[metacognition|metacognition]] operates. It posits two interacting levels of cognitive representation: an **object-level**, which carries out cognitive operations such as encoding, retrieval, and problem-solving, and a **meta-level**, which contains a dynamic, often imperfect model of the object-level's current state. Information flows between these levels in two directions: **monitoring** ascends from the object-level to the meta-level, informing the latter about what is happenin...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[meta-levelobject-level-architecture|Meta-Level/Object-Level Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognition** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognition, in the context of critical thinking, refers to the capacity to think about one's own thinking — to monitor, evaluate, and regulate one's cognitive processes. A critical thinker is not merely someone who reasons well; they are someone who is aware of *how* they reason, who can detect when their own reasoning is going wrong, and who can deliberately adjust. Metacognition is, in this sense, the executive capacity that makes sustained critical thinking possible. The term was formally introduced by John Flavell in 1976 and has since become a cornerstone of learning science.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognition]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Accuracy** **[perm-note]**
> The degree of correspondence between a learner's metacognitive judgments (confidence, judgments of learning, feelings of knowing) and their actual cognitive performance. A well-calibrated learner knows when they know something and knows when they do not. Metacognitive accuracy is conceptually distinct from metacognitive *level* — a learner can be highly confident (high level) while being systematically wrong about their actual knowledge state (poor accuracy). The Zimmerman model requires not merely that learners monitor, but that their monitoring *accurately reflects* their cognitive state ...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-accuracy|Metacognitive Accuracy]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Bootstrapping Problem** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive bootstrapping problem refers to the apparent paradox of attempting to improve a self-monitoring system from within that system: the cognitive processes required to assess and improve one's own metacognitive accuracy are themselves governed by the metacognitive system being assessed. If one's metacognitive monitoring is inaccurate, using that inaccurate monitoring to assess its own accuracy may generate self-validating but not genuinely diagnostic assessments.
> 
> **Boundary:** The metaphor of "bootstrapping" captures the circularity but should not be interpreted as implying t...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-bootstrapping-problem|Metacognitive Bootstrapping Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The degree of alignment between a learner's subjective judgments of their own learning (e.g., confidence in comprehension) and their actual learning performance (e.g., tested comprehension). Well-calibrated learners know what they know and what they don't — which enables accurate goal-setting, appropriate help-seeking, and effective allocation of study time. Poorly calibrated learners — who are systematically overconfident — allocate insufficient time to areas of genuine confusion and sufficient time to areas of already-adequate understanding.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-calibration|Metacognitive Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Calibration Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment works by correcting students' metacognitive miscalibration — aligning their monitoring accuracy (their felt sense of knowing) with their actual knowledge state. The operative mechanism is not information delivery but monitoring recalibration.
> 
> **Core Assumption:** Students cannot reliably act on feedback about their knowledge state if their monitoring system is inaccurate. Before information can be incorporated, the system that registers whether the information is needed must be calibrated.
> 
> **Design Implication:** Prioritize assessment des...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-calibration-model|Metacognitive Calibration Model]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Calibration Model (MCM)** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** The view that formative assessment's operative mechanism is the correction of metacognitive miscalibration — aligning students' monitoring accuracy with their actual knowledge state. The operative variable is calibration receptivity (the accuracy of the monitoring system *before* feedback is processed). **Boundary conditions:** most powerful for learners in the intermediate zone — enough knowledge to study but not enough to be accurately calibrated; less distinctive for expert learners with already-good calibration.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[nelson-narens-model]], [[Metacogni...
> 
> *Source: formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Calibration-Model-MCM|Metacognitive Calibration Model (MCM)]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Control** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive control refers to the processes by which the meta level modifies the operations of the object level in response to monitoring assessments. Control involves four operations: initiation of cognitive processes, continuation of ongoing processes, termination of processes, and re-selection of strategies or procedures. Control information flows *downward* from meta level to object level, in contrast to monitoring, which flows *upward*.
> 
> **Boundary:** Control is distinct from monitoring. Failures of metacognitive performance frequently involve not failures of monitoring per se but fa...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-control|Metacognitive Control]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Experience** **[perm-note]**
> In-the-moment affective or cognitive signals about the quality of one's current cognitive processing — feelings of knowing, of confusion, of fluency, or of effort. Serve as the monitoring signals that activate control processes. Inaccurate metacognitive experiences (feeling that you understand when you don't) constitute the primary failure mode of metacognitive monitoring.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-experience|Metacognitive Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Experiences** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive experiences are conscious phenomenological states that arise during cognitive processing and that carry information about the progress, quality, and appropriateness of cognitive activity. They include feelings of knowing (the sense that one knows something that cannot be immediately retrieved), judgments of learning (assessments of how well material has been acquired), feelings of difficulty (the sense that a task is more demanding than expected), feelings of confidence (assessments of the correctness of a response), and feelings of familiarity (the sense that material has bee...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-experiences|Metacognitive Experiences]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Externalization** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive externalization is the process of rendering internal metacognitive processes — monitoring judgments, knowledge-state representations, and control decisions — in external, persistent, inspectable, and manipulable form through tools, artifacts, or systems. A PKB is arguably the most comprehensive metacognitive externalization system available to individual learners, because it externalizes not just isolated judgments but the *entire monitoring-control architecture*.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive externalization is distinct from [[cognitive-offloading]], which refers to the use of...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-externalization|Metacognitive Externalization]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Externalization Spectrum** **[perm-note]**
> A three-level developmental model describing increasingly deliberate metacognitive design in PKB systems: Level 1 (Implicit) — the PKB passively accumulates traces of metacognitive processes; Level 2 (Structural) — the PKB architecture deliberately embodies metacognitive knowledge; Level 3 (Reflective) — the practitioner maintains an explicit metacognitive layer within the PKB, making the system a metacognitive development environment.
> 
> **Boundary:** The levels are not mutually exclusive but cumulative — Level 3 includes the benefits of Levels 1 and 2 while adding deliberate metacognitive r...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-externalization-spectrum|Metacognitive Externalization Spectrum]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Feelings** **[perm-note]**
> Phenomenological, affective states that arise during cognitive processing and carry implicit information about the quality and progress of that processing. Unlike metacognitive knowledge (explicit, declarative) or regulation (deliberate, procedural), metacognitive feelings are automatic, immediate, and valenced — they feel positive or negative. A feeling of non-comprehension (a subjective sense of confusion when reading a dense passage) carries both cognitive information ("I am not understanding this") and motivational signal ("what do I do now?").
> 
> *Source: metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-feelings|Metacognitive Feelings]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Inertia** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive inertia refers to the developmental state in which cognitive strategies and metacognitive knowledge exist in a learner's repertoire but are not spontaneously deployed in appropriate contexts without explicit prompting or scaffolding. Young children may know, when asked, that they should check their work or verify their understanding, but they do not spontaneously perform these regulatory behaviors without adult prompting. Metacognitive development, on this account, involves the progressive internalization and automatization of regulatory behaviors that were initially triggered...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-inertia|Metacognitive Inertia]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Infrastructure** **[perm-note]**
> The proposed reconceptualization of metacognitive skill as a system of interdependent components — monitoring cue ecology, threshold calibration, and strategic repertoire — rather than a unitary competence. Infrastructure must be *built* (through practice, calibration, and explicit instruction) rather than *taught* (through awareness alone).
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-infrastructure|Metacognitive Infrastructure]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Judgments** **[perm-note]**
> [[metacognitive-judgments|Metacognitive judgments]] are the products of the monitoring channel — explicit or implicit assessments that the meta-level generates about the state of the object-level. In the Nelson–Narrens taxonomy, these judgments are classified by their temporal relationship to the cognitive process they monitor. **Pre-acquisition judgments** include [[Ease-of-Learning|ease-of-learning]] (EOL) assessments. **During-acquisition judgments** include [[judgment-of-learning|judgments of learning]] (JOLs). **During-retrieval judgments** include [[feeling-of-knowing|feelings of know...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-judgments|Metacognitive Judgments]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Stable, declarative knowledge about persons (how learners differ), tasks (how task characteristics affect difficulty), and strategies (which approaches are effective for which purposes). Provides the repertoire from which the self-regulatory control phase draws. Necessary but insufficient for effective SRL — must be accompanied by accurate metacognitive experiences to trigger its deployment.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-knowledge|Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Knowledge of Reading** **[perm-note]**
> The reader's stable, long-term knowledge about reading as a cognitive activity, including declarative knowledge (facts about reading strategies and text genres), procedural knowledge (how to execute specific strategies), and conditional knowledge (when and why particular strategies are appropriate). Metacognitive reading knowledge is necessary but not sufficient for metacognitive reading practice; the conditional dimension — strategic appropriateness knowledge — is the most critical and most commonly underdeveloped component.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive reading knowledge is not identical w...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-knowledge-of-reading|Metacognitive Knowledge of Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Knowledge — Flavell's Tripartite Taxonomy** *[needs-note]*
> Metacognitive knowledge refers to stably held, often consciously accessible beliefs about cognition, organized along three axes: (1) **Person knowledge** — what one knows about oneself and others as cognitive agents, including relative strengths, limitations, and universal cognitive characteristics; (2) **Task knowledge** — what one knows about how different task features (novelty, complexity, goal structure) affect cognitive demands and appropriate approaches; (3) **Strategy knowledge** — what one knows about cognitive and metacognitive strategies, including their existence, relative effec...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Knowledge-—-Flavell's-Tripartite-Taxonomy|Metacognitive Knowledge — Flavell's Tripartite Taxonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The ongoing process of tracking one's own cognitive states during learning — assessing comprehension, judging learning progress, comparing performance against goals. Quality of monitoring determines calibration accuracy (whether one accurately knows what one knows) and thus the quality of adaptive regulatory responses. *SDT connection: competence-need frustration degrades monitoring quality by inducing ego-protective rather than accuracy-seeking processing.*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-monitoring|Metacognitive Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring Accuracy** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>[[metacognitive-accuracy|Metacognitive monitoring accuracy]]</span> refers to the correspondence between a person's subjective judgments about their own cognitive states (confidence, learning, comprehension, memory strength) and their actual cognitive performance on relevant tasks. It is the fidelity of the signal that the [[metacognitive-monitoring|meta-level monitoring system]] sends about [[regulation-of-cognition|object-level cognitive processes]].
> 
> [**Metacognitive-Monitoring-Accuracy**:: The degree of correspondence between subjective judgments about one'...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy|Metacognitive Monitoring Accuracy]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring Accuracy / Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> Monitoring accuracy (also called calibration in contexts of confidence-performance correspondence) refers to the degree to which a learner's metacognitive assessments — their JOLs, FOK judgments, comprehension monitoring signals, and performance predictions — correspond to actual cognitive performance outcomes. A highly calibrated monitor shows a high positive correlation between subjective assessments and objective performance. Calibration is psychometrically assessable and has been shown to be improvable through targeted feedback and training.
> 
> **Boundary:** Monitoring accuracy is not a s...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration|Metacognitive Monitoring Accuracy / Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive monitoring in the context of reading is the ongoing process by which a reader evaluates their own comprehension state — tracking whether they are understanding, identifying specific loci of confusion or failure, and assessing the quality of their developing situation model against standards of coherence and accuracy. It encompasses both *metacognitive knowledge* (knowing that comprehension can fail, knowing what strategies are available to repair it, knowing how reading tasks differ) and *metacognitive regulation* (deploying, adjusting, and evaluating comprehension strategies ...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-monitoring-in-reading|Metacognitive Monitoring in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring in Self-Directed Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive monitoring refers to the learner's ongoing assessment of their own cognitive states — their current level of understanding, the accuracy of their knowledge, the effectiveness of their current learning strategy, and the distance between their present comprehension and their learning goal. In self-directed learning contexts, metacognitive monitoring replaces the feedback loop that formal assessment typically provides, requiring the learner to generate internal signals of comprehension or confusion that would otherwise come from instructor evaluation.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-monitoring-in-self-directed-learning|Metacognitive Monitoring in Self-Directed Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Reflection** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognitive reflection is the deliberate, typically offline, evaluative process of thinking systematically about one's own cognitive processes — particularly about understanding, learning, reasoning, and problem-solving — with the explicit purpose of generating improved knowledge of how one thinks and producing adaptive changes in future cognitive activity. It is distinguished by its retrospective orientation, its evaluative character, its effortful and intentional engagement, and its functional aim of cognitive improvement rather than mere self-awareness.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive ref...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-reflection|Metacognitive Reflection]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The control processes that *respond* to monitoring signals: planning before a task, selecting and adjusting strategies during it, and evaluating outcomes after it. Regulation is the action component. Without it, monitoring is merely self-observation. See [[metacognitive-regulation]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-regulation|Metacognitive Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Externally provided support structures that target the monitoring and/or control components of the metacognitive system, enabling learners to perform metacognitive functions they cannot yet perform reliably independently. Distinguished from content scaffolding by its target (the metacognitive system rather than domain knowledge) and from generic support by its function (developing internal capacity rather than merely enabling task completion).
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-scaffolding|Metacognitive Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Scaffolding Design Triangle** **[perm-note]**
> A three-dimensional framework for designing PKBs that function as metacognitive development environments: (1) Monitoring Fidelity — generating accurate, diagnostic signals about knowledge state; (2) Control Precision — providing a rich vocabulary of regulatory interventions; (3) Reflective Integration — supporting metacognitive scrutiny of one's own knowledge management practices. Effective design attends to all three dimensions simultaneously.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Triangle is a design framework, not a theory of metacognitive mechanisms. It describes what a metacognitively effective PKB shoul...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-scaffolding-design-triangle|Metacognitive Scaffolding Design Triangle]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Within Pintrich's framework, the cognitive-area component that encompasses awareness, knowledge, and regulation of one's cognitive processes: planning (setting goals, activating prior knowledge, selecting strategies), monitoring (comprehension checking, tracking cognitive progress), and regulation (revising strategies, allocating attention). Treated as a sub-domain of the Cognition area in the 4×4 matrix.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-self-regulation|Metacognitive Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Sensitivity** **[perm-note]**
> [[metacognitive-accuracy|Metacognitive sensitivity]] refers to the ability of the metacognitive system to discriminate between correct and incorrect cognitive outputs. It is typically measured using signal detection theory metrics such as meta-d' (meta-d-prime), which quantifies the extent to which metacognitive confidence tracks first-order accuracy after controlling for task performance. A high meta-d' indicates that the meta-level is successfully distinguishing between correct and incorrect responses; a low meta-d' indicates that the meta-level's monitoring signals are poorly calibrated ...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-sensitivity|Metacognitive Sensitivity]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Sensitivity — meta-d'** *[needs-note]*
> Metacognitive sensitivity, measured by the meta-d' statistic, quantifies the ability of the metacognitive system to discriminate between correct and incorrect cognitive outputs. It is derived from signal detection theory and controls for first-order task performance, isolating the specifically metacognitive component of confidence judgments.
> 
> **Boundary:** Meta-d' measures resolution (relative accuracy), not calibration (absolute accuracy).
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Meta-d' provides a formal, quantitative measure of monitoring quality that complements the judgment-based measures de...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Sensitivity-—-meta-d'|Metacognitive Sensitivity — meta-d']]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Sovereignty** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to function as one's own epistemologist — to monitor, evaluate, and regulate one's learning processes and outcomes without dependence on external assessment. Encompasses three interacting dimensions: metacognitive knowledge (knowing about one's own cognition), metacognitive monitoring (real-time awareness of comprehension and performance), and metacognitive control (strategic adjustment based on monitoring outputs).
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive sovereignty does not imply infallibility. It describes the capacity for honest self-assessment, including the ability to recognize the ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-sovereignty|Metacognitive Sovereignty]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The control dimension of metacognition — the processes by which the learner modifies their cognitive activity based on monitoring information. Metacognitive regulation includes planning (Forethought), monitoring (Performance), and evaluation (Self-Reflection), and it operates continuously across all three phases of the SRL cycle.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[metacognitive-regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Mindset Intervention** **[perm-note]**
> A brief educational program (typically one to three hours) designed to shift students' implicit theories of intelligence from entity to incremental theories, typically by teaching about neuroplasticity, providing examples of growth, and reframing challenge as opportunity. This report finds that such interventions have not reliably demonstrated meaningful academic outcome effects in independent replications.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mindset-intervention|Mindset Intervention]]*

> [!definition] **Mindware** **[perm-note]**
> The rules, procedures, conceptual frameworks, and analytical tools a person has internalised and made available for Type 2 deployment when needed. Mindware determines what Type 2 processing can actually do when it is engaged — a person who has not internalised base-rate reasoning cannot apply it; a person who has not learned the expected-value calculation cannot perform it under time pressure. Mindware gaps are a distinct source of judgment failure from cognitive-miserliness failures.
> 
> **Boundary:** Not synonymous with "knowledge" in general; mindware specifically denotes procedural and con...
> 
> *Source: dual-process-theory-kahneman-system-1-system-2-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mindware]]*

> [!definition] **Modality Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that learning from a visual presentation accompanied by auditory narration typically exceeds learning from the same visual presentation accompanied by written text. Explained by the dual-channel architecture of working memory: audio + visual distributes load across the phonological loop and visuospatial sketchpad simultaneously, while written text + visual overloads the visual channel. Conditional on learner expertise, content complexity, and presentation pace.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[modality-effect|Modality Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Modality Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The [[modality-effect]] refers to the finding that presenting information through both auditory and visual channels — rather than through a single channel — can increase effective working memory capacity by distributing processing across the [[phonological-loop]] and [[visuospatial-sketchpad]]. When a visual diagram is accompanied by spoken narration rather than written text, the dual-channel presentation reduces the load on any single subsystem and facilitates the integration of complementary information.
> 
> **Boundary:** The modality effect requires that the two sources provide genuinely co...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-modality-effect|The Modality Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Model Multiplicity** **[perm-note]**
> The number of distinct mental models required to fully represent the possibilities consistent with a set of premises. Model multiplicity is the primary predictor of reasoning difficulty in MMT: problems requiring only one model are systematically easier than those requiring two or three. Multiplicity places demands on working memory (all models must be held active during counterexample search) and on the cognitive effort required for comprehensive exploration of the possibility space.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[model-multiplicity|Model Multiplicity]]*

> [!definition] **Model Selection Decision Tree** **[perm-note]**
> ```
> Is this task complex reasoning or novel problem-solving?
> ├─ YES → Use Opus 4 (deepest reasoning)
> └─ NO → Is routine/repetitive work with clear patterns?
>     ├─ YES → Use Haiku 4.5 (3x cost savings, 2x speed)
>     └─ NO → Use Sonnet 4.5 (balanced default)
> 
> Special cases:
> - Orchestrator/coordinator: Sonnet 4.5 (needs maximum context understanding)
> - Quality validation: Opus 4 (critical quality gate)
> - Worker agents: Haiku 4.5 (volume tasks)
> ```
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[model-selection-decision-tree|Model Selection Decision Tree]]*

> [!definition] **Model-Completeness Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The hypothesis, central to Johnson-Laird's performance theory, that reasoning errors arise from constructing an *incomplete* set of models — failing to consider all logically possible models of the premises. A conclusion appears to hold because it holds in the partial model set constructed, but fails because a non-constructed model falsifies it. The primary explanation for the difficulty effects and error rates in syllogistic and propositional reasoning.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[model-completeness-hypothesis|Model-Completeness Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Moderator-Conditionality** **[perm-note]**
> The property of a behavioral science finding whereby the effect holds only under specific conditions defined by moderating variables — variables that affect the direction or magnitude of a relationship. Many sweeping behavioral science claims (including SDT's undermining effect, stereotype threat, ego-depletion, and others) are categorical in their popular formulation but moderator-conditional in their careful empirical form. The gap between categorical transmission and moderator-conditional evidence is a structural problem in science dissemination.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[moderator-conditionality]]*

> [!definition] **Modus Ponens** **[perm-note]**
> *If P then Q. P. Therefore, Q.* This is the most basic and psychologically robust valid inference form. If the antecedent (P) is affirmed and the conditional link holds, the consequent (Q) must follow. Example: "If it is raining, the ground is wet. It is raining. Therefore, the ground is wet." Modus ponens is so psychologically natural that it is often described as an "inference ticket" — humans execute it automatically, without deliberate effort.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[modus-ponens|Modus Ponens]]*

> [!definition] **Modus Tollens** **[perm-note]**
> *If P then Q. Not-Q. Therefore, not-P.* This form denies the consequent to deny the antecedent. It is logically valid but psychologically more demanding than modus ponens, because it requires reasoning backward through the conditional. Example: "If it is raining, the ground is wet. The ground is not wet. Therefore, it is not raining." Modus tollens is central to falsificationist science: if a theory predicts a result and the result does not occur, the theory must be revised.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[modus-tollens|Modus Tollens]]*

> [!definition] **Molecular Cloud** **[perm-note]**
> A large, cold, dense region of the interstellar medium in which hydrogen exists predominantly as H₂ molecules rather than individual atoms. Typical molecular clouds span tens of light-years, contain masses equivalent to thousands of solar masses, and maintain internal temperatures of 10–20 kelvin. The largest examples, known as Giant Molecular Clouds (GMCs), can extend over hundreds of light-years and contain up to several million solar masses of material. They are the exclusive sites of star formation in the present-day universe.
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all dense gas qualifies as a molecular cl...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[molecular-cloud|Molecular Cloud]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring as Vocabulary-Dependent Signal Generation** **[perm-note]**
> Monitoring is the regulatory activity by which the learner's cognitive, motivational, behavioral, and contextual states become inputs to the control subsystem; its quality depends both on the sensitivity of the underlying monitoring mechanisms and on the vocabulary the learner possesses for naming what monitoring detects. Monitoring failure can occur because the mechanism failed to register a relevant change, because the change was registered but not named, or because the named signal was misinterpreted in a way that generated an inappropriate control response.
> 
> **Boundary:** Monitoring is ...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-as-vocabulary-dependent-signal-generation|Monitoring as Vocabulary-Dependent Signal Generation]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Gap** **[perm-note]**
> The documented discrepancy between what monitoring reveals (a comprehension failure) and what regulation enacts (a suboptimal response such as re-reading). The monitoring gap is evidence for coupling failure distinct from monitoring inaccuracy. See [[monitoring-gap]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-gap|Monitoring Gap]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory phase during performance in which the learner continuously compares cognitive, motivational, behavioral, and contextual states against the standards forethought established, generating signals that condition control decisions.
> 
> **Boundary:** Monitoring is not the same as awareness of one's own thoughts; it is the technical activity by which specific regulatable states become available as control inputs. Monitoring quality depends both on mechanism sensitivity and on the vocabulary the learner possesses for naming what monitoring detects.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-phase|Monitoring Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Scaffolding that targets the upward information flow in the Nelson-Narens model — helping learners generate more accurate, explicit, or frequent meta-level representations of their cognitive state. Examples: prediction prompts, summary generation requirements, calibration exercises, conceptual checkpoints.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-scaffolding|Monitoring Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Vocabulary Sensitization** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which goal vocabulary in the forethought phase tunes the monitoring system to track specific types of comprehension cues during the performance phase. Mastery-oriented goal vocabulary sensitizes monitoring to understanding cues (noticing non-comprehension, noticing inferential gaps); performance/completion-oriented vocabulary sensitizes monitoring to completion cues (progress through the text, time elapsed). The type of cue the monitoring system tracks determines what gets flagged for repair and what gets passed over silently.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[Achievem...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-vocabulary-sensitization|Monitoring Vocabulary Sensitization]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A theoretical framework describing metacognition as an interaction between two levels: the object level (where cognitive operations occur) and the meta level (which monitors and controls object-level operations). Information flows upward through monitoring (meta level receives status reports from object level) and downward through control (meta level issues instructions to object level).
> 
> **Boundary:** The model is a functional description, not a neuroanatomical claim. "Object level" and "meta level" are not discrete brain regions but functional roles in a dynamic system.
> 
> **Report-Specific...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-control-architecture|Monitoring-Control Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which a detected discrepancy between current performance and the goal standard (monitoring) triggers a specific regulatory response (control). The coupling can decouple — accurate monitoring can fail to trigger control response due to attribution failure, motivational withdrawal, or insufficient strategy repertoire — and the quality of this coupling is the proposed critical bottleneck in the SRL cycle.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-control-coupling|Monitoring-Control Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control Hierarchy** **[perm-note]**
> The monitoring-control hierarchy is a two-level cognitive architecture proposed by Nelson and Narens to formalize the metacognitive system. The *object level* is where direct cognitive work occurs — reading, calculating, problem-solving, memorizing. The *meta-level* is where cognitive processes are represented, assessed, and managed. Monitoring is the upward flow from object-level processes to meta-level representations: the meta-level receives information about how object-level processing is proceeding. Control is the downward flow from meta-level representations to object-level processes:...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-control-hierarchy|Monitoring-Control Hierarchy]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control Loop** **[perm-note]**
> The recursive cognitive process through which learners monitor their own comprehension and performance (generating an internal assessment signal) and then exercise control over their learning strategies in response to that signal. Monitoring generates information about the current state of learning; control translates that information into strategic action.
> 
> **Boundary:** The monitoring-control loop is not a conscious deliberative process in all cases; much metacognitive monitoring operates at a pre-reflective level, producing feelings of knowing or not-knowing that influence behavior witho...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-control-loop|Monitoring-Control Loop]]*

> [!definition] **The Monitoring-Control Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-level architecture in which (1) the *object level* represents cognitive processing — in reading, the processes of word identification, syntactic parsing, inference generation, and situation model construction; (2) the *meta level* monitors the object level and can issue control signals to it; and (3) crucially, these levels communicate through two distinct channels: **monitoring** flows upward (from object to meta) and **control** flows downward (from meta to object). [[monitoring-control-coupling]] occurs when a monitoring signal reliably activates a corresponding control response. [...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-monitoring-control-model|The Monitoring-Control Model]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control-Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The critical link in the Nelson-Narens model between the monitoring process (tracking actual cognitive states) and the control process (adjusting cognitive operations in response to monitoring). When monitoring is inaccurate (as in the fluency illusion), control interventions are misdirected — more effort is applied to strategies that are not producing comprehension.
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-applied-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-control-coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Regulation Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The functional link between metacognitive *monitoring* (the flow of information from object-level processing to meta-level awareness) and metacognitive *control* (the flow of regulatory decisions from meta-level to object-level processing). The [[nelson-narens-model]] treats monitoring and control as conceptually distinct operations that operate through separable mechanisms. The Zimmerman model implicitly assumes tight coupling: monitoring during the Performance Phase generates data that smoothly flows into the Self-Reflection Phase's self-evaluation, which then flows into modified Forethou...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-regulation-coupling|Monitoring-Regulation Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Regulation Decoupling** **[perm-note]**
> The failure of detected comprehension failures to trigger corresponding regulatory actions. Distinguished from monitoring failure (not detecting a failure) by the presence of a monitoring signal that does not result in regulatory action. The three mechanisms identified in this analysis are signal threshold failure, strategy activation failure, and effort abandonment. Central concept from [[monitoring-control-coupling]] theory (Nelson & Narens, 1990) applied to the reading domain.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[monitoring-regulation-decoupling|Monitoring-Regulation Decoupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring–Control Coupling** *[needs-note]*
> [[monitoring-control-coupling|Monitoring–control coupling]] refers to the degree to which metacognitive monitoring information is effectively translated into metacognitive control actions. Tight coupling means that accurate monitoring leads to adaptive control (e.g., allocating more study time to items judged as poorly learned). [[monitoring-regulation-decoupling|Decoupling]] occurs when monitoring information is available but is not acted upon, or when control actions do not correspond to monitoring signals.
> 
> **Boundary:** Coupling is a relational property of the monitoring–control system,...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring–Control-Coupling|Monitoring–Control Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring–Control Loop** *[needs-note]*
> A two-level cognitive architecture in which an *object level* performs primary cognitive work (in reading: decoding, parsing, integration) while a *meta level* maintains a representation of the object level's state and intervenes when needed. *Monitoring* is the upward information flow from object to meta. *Control* is the downward regulatory flow from meta to object. The two flows together form a feedback loop whose stability and accuracy determine whether self-regulated cognition is possible at all.
> 
> **Boundary:** This is not the same as introspection in the philosophical sense, nor is it...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring–Control-Loop|Monitoring–Control Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Morphological Awareness** **[perm-note]**
> Explicit or implicit knowledge of the morphological structure of words — their decomposition into meaningful units (morphemes) including roots, prefixes, and suffixes — and the ability to use this knowledge to generate provisional meanings for unfamiliar derived and compound words. Morphological awareness functions as a *force multiplier* on vocabulary breadth because knowledge of a single morpheme enables probabilistic inference across all words sharing that morpheme.
> 
> **Boundary:** Morphological inference does not always produce accurate meanings. English morphology is historically layere...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[morphological-awareness|Morphological Awareness]]*

> [!definition] **Morphological Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Knowledge of the component meaningful units (morphemes) that form words — including derivational affixes (prefixes and suffixes that change word meaning or part of speech) and root words (often from Latin or Greek). Morphological knowledge allows readers to analyze unfamiliar words as combinations of known parts, generating precise meaning hypotheses rather than approximate contextual guesses.
> 
> **Boundary:** Morphological knowledge is distinct from phonological awareness (which concerns sound structure) and orthographic knowledge (which concerns spelling patterns). It operates at the level ...
> 
> *Source: reading-vocabulary-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: cognitive-science-of-reading*
> *Permanent Note: [[morphological-knowledge|Morphological Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Emotion Mediation in Attribution** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The structural claim that the causal path from attribution to behavior is not direct but runs through specific discrete emotions generated by dimensional classification. The attribution generates the emotion; the emotion drives the behavior. Interventions that change attributions without producing affective change fail to propagate through to behavior.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the most theoretically important and most frequently overlooked feature of Weiner's mature model.
> 
> **Operational Indicators:** Demonstrated by showing that controlling for emotion eli...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[emotion-mediation-in-attribution|Emotion Mediation in Attribution]]*

> [!definition] **Emotional Granularity** **[perm-note]**
> The degree of precision with which an individual distinguishes between their emotional states — ranging from coarse two-dimensional categorization (good/bad, activated/deactivated) to fine-grained differentiation among specific emotion concepts (frustrated vs. anxious vs. disappointed vs. resentful). High emotional granularity is associated with better emotion regulation outcomes and more accurate communicative encoding of emotional states.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[emotional-granularity|Emotional Granularity]]*

> [!definition] **Emotional Granularity in Communication** **[perm-note]**
> [[emotional-granularity]] — the capacity to differentiate one's own emotional states with fine-grained precision rather than coarse categories (not just "I'm upset" but "I feel defensive and a little ashamed") — significantly improves the quality of emotional communication. Research by Lisa Feldman Barrett shows that individuals with high emotional granularity handle adversity more effectively, have better-regulated emotional states, and communicate their emotional experiences more accurately. The communicative implication: vague emotional language ("I feel like you're not listening") is fa...
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[emotional-granularity-in-communication|Emotional Granularity in Communication]]*

> [!definition] **Motivated Academic Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Motivated academic reading refers to the self-initiated, sustained engagement with expository, argumentative, or scholarly texts in which the reader actively processes content for meaning, pursues understanding beyond surface recall, and maintains this engagement under conditions of cognitive effort and difficulty. It is distinguished from both passive reading (attention without comprehension pursuit) and compliance-driven reading (reading to satisfy external requirements rather than internal understanding goals).
> 
> **Boundary:** The term does not presuppose enjoyment in every reading sessio...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivated-academic-reading|Motivated Academic Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Motivated Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> A pattern of reasoning in which prior commitments, identity stakes, or desired conclusions drive the direction of information search and evaluation, while the appearance of dispassionate inference is maintained. Kunda's formulation distinguishes motivated reasoning from biased reasoning simpliciter: motivated reasoning involves the deployment of genuine cognitive capacity in the service of directional goals. The motivation operates by constraining the stopping point of evidential search — accepting the first conclusion consistent with the desired outcome rather than the most evidentially su...
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivated-reasoning|Motivated Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Affordance** **[perm-note]**
> Adapted from Gibson's (1979) ecological psychology concept of affordance (the action possibilities that an environment offers to a particular perceiver). In the context of COT, motivational affordances are the motivationally relevant features of an environment that an individual perceives and can act on. Different orientations make different motivational affordances salient: autonomy orientation makes intrinsic interest and choice affordances salient; control orientation makes evaluative and directive affordances salient.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-affordance|Motivational Affordance]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Belief** **[perm-note]**
> A learner's judgment, belief, or perception about themselves or the task that energizes and directs goal-directed behavior. Distinguished from cognitive strategies (which are what learners do) and from motivation constructs like drive (which are not domain-specific). Includes self-efficacy, task value, goal orientation, and control beliefs.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-belief|Motivational Belief]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Climate** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The goal-relevant structure of an achievement environment — the constellation of signals, practices, and evaluative procedures that implicitly communicate which type of achievement is valued, how success is defined, and how ability is evaluated. A mastery climate emphasizes personal improvement, effort, and learning; a performance climate emphasizes normative comparison, relative ability, and competitive success.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Motivational climate is the primary environmental lever for shaping goal adoption distributions — it determines the goal type ecol...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-climate|Motivational Climate]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Deployment Problem** **[perm-note]**
> The Motivational Deployment Problem refers to the structural gap between a learner's *knowledge* of effective learning strategies (knowing that retrieval practice, spacing, and self-explanation produce superior retention) and their *consistent behavioral deployment* of those strategies. The gap is not informational; the learner knows what works. It is motivational, phenomenological, and systemic: the strategies that work best produce the most aversive immediate subjective experience (failure, confusion, forgetting), creating a negative incentive structure that systematically drives learners...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science & educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-deployment-problem|Motivational Deployment Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Ecology** **[perm-note]**
> The interconnected system of motivational conditions — including intrinsic interest, identity commitment, social belonging, practical relevance, and competence satisfaction — that together create a self-sustaining motivational system characterised by diversity, redundancy, development, social embeddedness, and metacognitive awareness.
> 
> **Boundary:** The ecological metaphor suggests dynamics (compensatory mechanisms, resilience through diversity) that have not been empirically tested in learning motivation contexts.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The second original contribution of this ...
> 
> *Source: curriculum-design-for-lifelong-learning-and-self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: education — curriculum design*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-ecology|Motivational Ecology]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Grammar Framework** **[perm-note]**
> A structural template for PKM goal formulation: *[Understanding verb] + [domain/concept specification] + [depth criterion as capability] + [autonomy marker for method] + [temporal integration rather than deadline]*. Designed to satisfy the specificity requirements of goal-setting theory while embedding the autonomy markers, mastery orientation, and calibration-improving criteria that SDT, AGT, and metacognitive theory prescribe.
> 
> **Boundary:** A linguistic tool for *expressing* authentic learning orientations, not for manufacturing them. When linguistic surface and motivational depth are in...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-grammar-framework|Motivational Grammar Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Lexicon** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The structured vocabulary repertoire that constitutes a learner's inner speech architecture for self-regulated academic engagement — specifically the vocabulary available for attributional narration, regulatory self-direction, self-efficacy evaluation, and goal construction. Distinct from domain vocabulary (vocabulary for content understanding) and strategy vocabulary (vocabulary for cognitive operations). The Motivational Lexicon is the set of words available for narrating, evaluating, and directing the motivational experience of learning itself.
> 
> **Report-Specific Signific...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-lexicon|Motivational Lexicon]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Quality — SDT Continuum** *[needs-note]*
> SDT defines motivation quality in terms of regulatory style along a continuum from external regulation (acting to obtain external rewards or avoid punishment) through introjected regulation (acting to protect self-esteem or gain internal ego rewards) through identified regulation (acting because the activity is personally valued even if not inherently enjoyable) to integrated regulation (acting because the activity is fully congruent with one's deeper values and identity) to intrinsic motivation (acting for the inherent interest and enjoyment of the activity itself).
> 
> **Boundary:** SDT does...
> 
> *Source: motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science & educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Motivational-Quality-—-SDT-Continuum|Motivational Quality — SDT Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The deliberate attempt by learners to initiate, maintain, or modify motivational states during learning — including monitoring motivational decline, attributing it appropriately, and deploying repair strategies such as self-consequating, interest enhancement, goal-oriented self-talk, and environmental structuring. Distinct from the more common framing of motivation as a relatively stable input to learning.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the most theoretically contentious component of Pintrich's framework. The evidence base exists but relies heavily on self-report...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-regulation|Motivational Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational-Regulatory Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> An original synthesis proposed in this analysis: the claim that cognitive regulation requires functional motivational architecture as a necessary precondition, but not vice versa. Motivational dysregulation cascades into cognitive regulatory failure (fragile strategy use, degraded monitoring quality, maladaptive persistence); cognitive strategy limitation does not equivalently cascade into motivational dysregulation. This asymmetry implies that motivational architecture is the more foundational of the two regulatory domains for sustained deep engagement with challenging learning tasks.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[motivational-regulatory-asymmetry|Motivational-Regulatory Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **The Empirical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Scrutinizes what the evidence base actually supports, disentangling what schema research shows from what popular summaries claim. This lens asks: Where is the evidence genuinely strong, and where does the evidence support a narrower or more conditional claim than the received view asserts?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-empirical-lens|The Empirical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Empirically Supported Treatment** **[perm-note]**
> A treatment protocol that meets specified criteria for having demonstrated effectiveness in controlled research, typically including at least two independent randomized controlled trials showing superiority to a waiting list, placebo, or alternative treatment, conducted with a clearly defined patient population using a replicable treatment manual. The EST framework was developed by APA Division 12's Task Force on the Promotion and Dissemination of Psychological Procedures. Criticism of the framework focuses on: the artificial conditions of RCTs relative to real-world practice, the neglect o...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[empirically-supported-treatment|Empirically Supported Treatment]]*

> [!definition] **Template** **[perm-note]**
> A higher-order memory structure built from frequently co-occurring chunks that integrates a stable core of relationships with variable slots that accept context-specific values. Templates allow experts to handle variations within a familiar pattern type without rebuilding the underlying structure for each new instance.
> 
> **Boundary:** Templates are not generic schemas in the broad sense; they are domain-specific structures that emerge from the chunking process and that retain the recognizable signature of the patterns from which they were built.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Templates e...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[template]]*

> [!definition] **Template Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A refinement of early chunk models proposing that expert chunks are not rigid, fixed patterns but structured templates with variable *slots* that can be filled with different specific values while the overall structure remains constant. Templates bridge the chunking tradition and [[schema-theory|schema theory]] by introducing the flexibility necessary to account for expert performance in novel situations.
> 
> **Boundary:** Templates are more specific than schemas (which organize entire categories of experience) but more flexible than the fixed chunks of the original Simon-Chase model. They rep...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[template-theory|Template Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Temporal Marker Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The specific temporal words and grammatical structures that encode the stability dimension of attributions: "always" and "never" (high stability, fixed); "so far," "until now," "not yet" (moderate stability, developmental trajectory); "today," "in this session," "with this type of text" (low stability, situationally bounded). Temporal marker vocabulary is among the highest-leverage vocabulary categories for SRL because it directly encodes the fixed/growth mindset distinction at the grammatical level.
> 
> **Cross-References:** [[attribution-theory]], [[growth-mindset]], [[Fixed-...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[temporal-marker-vocabulary|Temporal Marker Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Temporal Separation Model** **[perm-note]**
> The proposed strategy of separating walking and reading in time rather than combining them, assigning each activity to its optimal cognitive role: walking for arousal priming, creative incubation, and [[memory-consolidation|memory consolidation]]; desk-based reading for comprehension, [[schema-construction|schema construction]], and generative PKB processing.
> 
> **Boundary:** Applies specifically to high-element-interactivity material requiring deep comprehension. For low-demand or review-level reading, concurrent walking-reading remains defensible.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This rep...
> 
> *Source: walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[temporal-separation-model|Temporal Separation Model]]*

> [!definition] **MSLQ** **[perm-note]**
> Pintrich's major assessment instrument for self-regulated learning. Measures both motivational components (value, expectancy, affect) and cognitive/metacognitive strategy use across multiple subscales. Used extensively in research on SDT-SRL integration studies. The motivational subscales partially operationalise SDT constructs; the cognitive subscales operationalise Zimmerman's and Pintrich's process components.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[mslq]]*

> [!definition] **The MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A structured markdown file acting as the coordination hub:
>

> *Permanent Note: [[the-multi-agent-plan.md-pattern|The MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md Pattern]]*

> [!definition] **Multiple Goal Adoption** **[perm-note]**
> The contemporaneous pursuit of more than one achievement goal within the same achievement context — most commonly mastery-approach combined with performance-approach. Research suggests this combination can be conditionally adaptive in competitive contexts, demonstrating that goals are not mutually exclusive and that goal dynamics are more complex than the dichotomous model implied.
> 
> **See also:** [[mastery-approach-goal]], [[performance-approach-goal]], [[goal-orientation]], [[achievement-goal-theory]]
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[multiple-goal-adoption|Multiple Goal Adoption]]*

> [!definition] **Multiple Resource Theory** **[perm-note]**
> [**Multiple-Resource-Theory**:: A theory of attention and dual-task performance proposing that interference between concurrent tasks depends on the degree to which they compete along four resource dimensions: (1) **processing stage** (perception/cognition vs. response), (2) **perceptual modality** (visual vs. auditory), (3) **visual channel** (focal/foveal vs. ambient/peripheral), and (4) **processing code** (spatial vs. verbal). Tasks that share resource types on more dimensions produce greater interference.]
> 
> *Source: walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[multiple-resource-theory|Multiple Resource Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Mutuality** **[perm-note]**
> The bidirectional quality of need support within a close relationship — both partners simultaneously functioning as sources of support for the other's basic needs. RMT and Canevello and Crocker's research on compassionate vs. self-image goals suggest that dyadic mutuality (both partners oriented toward genuinely supporting the other) produces outcomes substantially better than additive models predict — suggesting that mutual need support is not simply twice the need support of one-directional support, but that the alignment of both partners' motivational orientations creates a qualitatively...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[mutuality]]*

## _N

> [!definition] **Enactive Mastery Experience** **[perm-note]**
> Direct, personal experience of performing the target behavior, where the experience of success raises efficacy beliefs and the experience of failure typically lowers them, with the magnitude of change depending on factors including the difficulty of the task, the amount of effort required, the degree of external assistance received, and the temporal pattern in which successes and failures have occurred.
> 
> **Boundary:** Mastery experience is not merely *exposure* to the task or *attempted performance* — it requires the person's own enacted attempt and an outcome that the person interprets as ...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[enactive-mastery-experience|Enactive Mastery Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Encoding** **[perm-note]**
> Encoding is the initial process by which sensory input is transformed into a mental representation capable of being stored in [[long-term-memory|long-term memory]]. The quality of encoding — its depth, elaboration, and distinctiveness — is the primary determinant of subsequent memory performance. Encoding is not a single operation but a family of processes that vary along dimensions of intentionality (incidental vs. deliberate), depth (shallow perceptual processing vs. deep semantic processing), and modality (verbal, visual, motor, affective).
> 
> **Boundary:** Encoding refers specifically to ...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[encoding]]*

> [!definition] **Encoding Depth** **[perm-note]**
> Encoding depth refers to the level of meaning-based processing applied to new information during initial exposure. Shallow processing (attending to physical features like font or sound) produces weak memory traces with short retention. Deep processing (attending to meaning, semantic associations, and connections to prior knowledge) produces strong memory traces with substantially longer retention, a finding replicated across hundreds of studies.
> 
> **Boundary:** Encoding depth is not the complete picture — storage conditions (consolidation, retrieval-induced strengthening) matter equally. But...
> 
> *Source: motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science & educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[encoding-depth|Encoding Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Encoding Specificity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The Encoding Specificity Principle states that a retrieval cue will facilitate recall of a target item if and only if information about the cue and its relationship to the target was encoded together at the time of initial learning. More precisely: *what is stored is what is encoded, and what is retrieved depends on what was available at encoding.* Successful remembering requires that the information present at the time of testing overlap — structurally, semantically, or contextually — with the information that was encoded. Memory retrieval is therefore not a function of trace strength alon...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[encoding-specificity-principle|Encoding Specificity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Near vs. Far Transfer** **[perm-note]**
> Transfer of learning refers to the application of knowledge or skills acquired in one context to performance in different contexts. *Near transfer* involves applying learning to very similar tasks in highly comparable situations; *far transfer* involves applying structural principles across superficially dissimilar domains. Research by [[transfer-of-learning|Barnett and Ceci (2002)]] documents that far transfer is rare in naturalistic conditions but can be promoted through explicit attention to abstract structural principles. The strategies identified in this report transfer best when reade...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[near-vs.-far-transfer|Near vs. Far Transfer]]*

> [!definition] **Need Absence** **[perm-note]**
> A state in which a basic psychological need is not being satisfied in a given context without active thwarting. Distinguished from need frustration by the absence of the specific defensive responses that active thwarting produces. The distinction is operationalized in the Basic Psychological Need Satisfaction and Frustration Scale (BPNSFS; Chen et al., 2015).
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-absence|Need Absence]]*

> [!definition] **Need Balance** **[perm-note]**
> The distributional pattern of need satisfaction across the three needs, specifically the degree to which satisfaction is roughly equivalent across autonomy, competence, and relatedness rather than concentrated in one or two needs. Research suggests that need balance predicts well-being outcomes beyond the total amount of need satisfaction, consistent with BPNT's necessity claim that all three needs must be satisfied and that satisfaction of one cannot fully compensate for frustration of another. **See also:** [[Psychological-Balance]], [[Need-Satisfaction-Inventory]]
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-balance|Need Balance]]*

> [!definition] **Need Frustration** **[perm-note]**
> The active blocking or undermining of basic psychological need satisfaction, distinguished from need absence (the neutral state of neither satisfying nor frustrating a need). Need frustration produces reliable ill-being effects — anxiety, defensiveness, alienation, motivational rigidity — that need absence does not. The [[satisfaction-frustration-asymmetry]] is now understood as one of SDT's more robust empirical contributions.
