Preventing need frustration (designing environments that do not actively undermine autonomy, competence, and relatedness) is as important as promoting need satisfact...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-frustration|Need Frustration]]*

> [!definition] **Need Satisfaction** **[perm-note]**
> The state of having one's basic psychological needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness met in an environment. In the COT developmental account, chronic need satisfaction cultivates autonomy orientation; chronic need frustration produces control or impersonal orientations depending on the specific nature and pattern of frustration.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-satisfaction|Need Satisfaction]]*

> [!definition] **Need Satisfaction Ecology** **[perm-note]**
> The pattern of basic psychological need satisfaction across all three dimensions — autonomy, competence, and relatedness — within a relational context. Close relationships function as need satisfaction ecologies when they provide conditions supporting the satisfaction (or frustration) of the full basic needs set, not merely the relatedness need. High-quality relational ecologies satisfy all three needs simultaneously; pathological relational ecologies may satisfy one need while frustrating others. *See also*: [[basic-psychological-needs-theory]], [[need-frustration]]
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-satisfaction-ecology|Need Satisfaction Ecology]]*

> [!definition] **Need Satisfaction Mediational Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Need Satisfaction Mediational Model** — The theoretical mechanism posited by GCT to explain why goal content affects well-being: intrinsic goals promote well-being because their pursuit and attainment satisfies basic psychological needs; extrinsic goals fail to promote well-being (or actively undermine it) because their pursuit displaces need-satisfying activities and their attainment does not directly address need deficits. Need satisfaction mediates the relationship between goal content and well-being outcomes. See Section 3.
> **See also:** [[basic-psychological-needs-theory]], [[Autonom...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-satisfaction-mediational-model|Need Satisfaction Mediational Model]]*

> [!definition] **Need Satisfaction vs. Need Frustration** **[perm-note]**
> The distinction between the active satisfaction of basic psychological needs (autonomy, competence, relatedness) and the active frustration or thwarting of those needs. Need frustration is not merely the absence of satisfaction but involves active undermining — controlling environments frustrate autonomy, critical or chaotic environments frustrate competence, rejecting or conditional environments frustrate relatedness. This distinction has implications for COT: need frustration drives the development of control and impersonal orientations, not merely the absence of autonomy-supportive condi...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-satisfaction-vs.-need-frustration|Need Satisfaction vs. Need Frustration]]*

> [!definition] **Need Substitute** **[perm-note]**
> An activity or psychological state that partially mimics the satisfaction of a basic psychological need without genuinely fulfilling it. Characterized by compulsive or rigid engagement patterns and by the need for continuous renewal. Examples include ego-protective performance goals (competence substitute), aggressive social dominance (relatedness substitute), and obsessive passion (autonomy substitute).
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-substitute|Need Substitute]]*

> [!definition] **Need Support** **[perm-note]**
> Partner behavior that actively creates conditions for the other person's basic psychological needs to be satisfied. Autonomy support involves perspective-taking, acknowledging feelings, providing meaningful choice, and minimizing controlling language. Competence support involves providing optimal challenge, positive feedback, and scaffolding that enables genuine mastery. Relatedness support involves genuine attention, responsiveness, and investment in understanding the other person. Need support is the active form; its absence (neutrality) differs from need thwarting.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-support|Need Support]]*

> [!definition] **Need Thwarting** **[perm-note]**
> Need thwarting refers to partner behaviors that actively undermine the other person's basic psychological needs. Autonomy thwarting includes controlling behavior, psychological pressure, emotional coercion, surveillance, and conditional positive regard (withholding warmth unless the partner behaves as demanded). Competence thwarting includes chronic criticism, deprecation of the partner's abilities, and undermining their efforts in domains that matter to them. Relatedness thwarting includes emotional unavailability, dismissiveness, contempt, and communication patterns that leave the partner...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-thwarting|Need Thwarting]]*

> [!definition] **Need-Frustration** **[perm-note]**
> The active blockage of basic psychological need satisfaction by the environment — not merely the absence of need support but the presence of conditions that actively prevent needs from being met. Need frustration is distinguished from need dissatisfaction by its distinct causal profile and its more severe and less reversible consequence profile.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[need-frustration]]*

> [!definition] **The Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-level hierarchical model distinguishing a meta-level (which holds representations of the object-level cognitive system and generates monitoring judgments) and an object-level (where actual cognitive work occurs). Information flows upward from the object level to the meta level via **monitoring** — processes such as judgments of learning, feeling of knowing, and ease-of-learning assessments. Information flows downward from the meta level to the object level via **control** — decisions such as whether to continue studying, allocate more time, seek help, or deploy a different strategy. T...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-nelson-narens-monitoring-control-model|The Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model]]*

> [!definition] **Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model** **[perm-note]**
> [**Nelson-Narens-Model**:: A formal metacognitive architecture proposing that cognition operates across two interacting levels: an object-level (where cognitive activity occurs) and a meta-level (which monitors the object-level and exerts control over it). Information flows upward from object to meta-level via monitoring, and downward from meta to object-level via control (Nelson & Narens, 1990).]
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[nelson-narens-monitoring-control-model|Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model]]*

> [!definition] **Engagement-Contingent Reward** **[perm-note]**
> A reward contingent simply on engaging in or starting an activity, without reference to completion or quality. This is the most purely controlling reward type in CET's taxonomy: the contingency "if you do it, you get the reward" makes the external reason for the behaviour maximally salient. Empirically, engagement-contingent and task-contingent rewards show the strongest undermining effects in the Deci/Koestner/Ryan (1999) meta-analysis.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[engagement-contingent-reward|Engagement-Contingent Reward]]*

> [!definition] **Engram** **[perm-note]**
> The stored memory trace — the physical or functional representation in the brain/memory system of a past experience. In Tulving's usage, the engram is the residue of encoding that persists between learning and retrieval. Ecphory is the process by which the cue activates the engram. Modern neuroscience has progressed toward identifying engrammic cells (see Tonegawa & Liu, 2019 on optogenetic engram studies).
> 
> **Boundary:** "Engram" in casual usage sometimes conflates trace content with trace strength. The ESP's account treats the engram as a multi-attribute structure, not simply a strength v...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[engram]]*

> [!definition] **Non-Declarative Memory** **[perm-note]**
> [[non-declarative-memory|Non-declarative memory]] encompasses a heterogeneous collection of memory systems that operate outside conscious awareness and are expressed through performance rather than recollection. Its major subtypes include [[procedural-memory|procedural memory]] (the acquisition of motor and cognitive skills), [[priming|priming]] (the facilitation of processing for previously encountered stimuli), classical conditioning, and habituation. Non-declarative memory is phylogenetically older, more resistant to forgetting, and less dependent on the hippocampal system than declarati...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[non-declarative-memory|Non-Declarative Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Non-Recurrent Constituent Skill** **[perm-note]**
> A constituent skill component of a complex cognitive skill whose execution varies across problem situations. Non-recurrent skills require reasoning, problem-solving, or decision-making that adapts to contextual features. They are governed by schemas (cognitive frameworks) rather than by automated rules, and their performance improves through schema construction (elaboration, induction from varied examples) rather than through repetitive practice.
> 
> **Boundary:** The recurrent/non-recurrent distinction is relative to the analysis level and the domain. A skill that appears non-recurrent at one...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[non-recurrent-constituent-skill|Non-Recurrent Constituent Skill]]*

> [!definition] **Normative vs. Descriptive Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A normative model specifies what *ought* to occur under idealized conditions — what optimal performance looks like — without claiming to describe what typically occurs. A descriptive model characterizes what *actually* occurs across the population of interest. Many influential models in cognitive and educational psychology are *normative* but are *applied descriptively* — treated as though they describe typical behavior when they describe expert or ideal behavior. Post-reconstruction: the Zimmerman cycle is most accurately a normative framework for expert [[Self-Regulated-Le...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[normative-vs.-descriptive-model|Normative vs. Descriptive Model]]*

> [!definition] **Entity Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that ability is a fixed, stable trait — something one either has or does not have in a given domain. Under entity theory, performance outcomes are treated as *diagnostic* of underlying ability. Failure reveals low ability; success confirms high ability. The rational response to this belief system is to protect one's self-image by avoiding challenges and seeking easy wins. Entity theorists tend to experience criticism and difficulty as threats to identity rather than information about performance.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[entity-theory|Entity Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Entity Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> Dweck's technical term for the belief that intelligence is a fixed, stable entity. Operationalized through items like "You have a certain amount of intelligence, and you can't really do much to change it." Distinguished from incremental theory. This report finds that entity theory may function as a domain-specific attribution tendency rather than a domain-general stable belief.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[entity-theory-of-intelligence|Entity Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Entity Theory vs. Incremental Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> **Entity Theory** (also called the Fixed Mindset) is the implicit belief that intelligence and ability are fixed, stable, non-malleable properties — individuals have a certain level of ability, and experiences largely reveal rather than change this level. **Incremental Theory** (also called the Growth Mindset) is the implicit belief that intelligence and ability are malleable, developable, and responsive to effort, learning, and strategy. These implicit theories — measured by items such as "You have a certain amount of intelligence and can't do much to change it" — serve as the upstream sch...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[entity-theory-vs.-incremental-theory-of-intelligence|Entity Theory vs. Incremental Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Nuclear Fusion** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which light atomic nuclei — principally hydrogen (protons) — are combined into heavier nuclei (principally helium) at extremely high temperatures and pressures, releasing energy in accordance with the mass-energy equivalence relation. In the stellar context, fusion is the mechanism by which main-sequence stars generate the energy that sustains them against gravitational contraction for billions of years.
> 
> **Boundary:** Stellar nuclear fusion is distinct from thermonuclear fusion in weapons (which is uncontrolled) and from laboratory fusion experiments (which operate under dif...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[nuclear-fusion|Nuclear Fusion]]*

> [!definition] **Enumerative Induction** **[perm-note]**
> The simplest form of inductive inference: observing that all examined members of a class have a property, and concluding that all members of the class have that property. Example: "All the metals I have tested conduct electricity; therefore, all metals conduct electricity." Vulnerable to the single counterexample (the "black swan" problem). Distinguished from statistical induction, which explicitly quantifies the probabilistic strength of the generalization.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[enumerative-induction|Enumerative Induction]]*

> [!definition] **Nutriment** **[perm-note]**
> The metaphorical concept describing the relationship between environmental conditions and psychological need satisfaction: just as physical nutrition provides the material substrate for biological growth, psychological nutriments provide the environmental conditions for psychological growth and integration. Used to underline that psychological growth requires *active environmental support*, not merely the absence of obstruction — a somewhat weaker environment that provides positive need satisfaction will produce different outcomes than one that merely avoids need frustration. **See also:** ...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[nutriment]]*

## _O

> [!definition] **Object-Level** **[perm-note]**
> The [[Meta-Level-Object-Level-Model|object-level]] is the lower-order component of the Nelson–Narrens architecture where first-order cognitive operations occur — encoding, retrieval, problem-solving, comprehension, and other domain-specific processes. The object-level generates cues that are transmitted upward to the meta-level through the monitoring channel and receives control directives from the meta-level that alter its operations.
> 
> **Boundary:** The object-level is not "unintelligent" — it encompasses all of the sophisticated cognitive processing that the individual engages in. The ter...
> 
> *Source: nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[object-level]]*

> [!definition] **Obliterative Subsumption** **[perm-note]**
> Ausubel's account of forgetting: over time, specific details subsumed under a superordinate concept lose distinctiveness and are absorbed into the general concept, leaving only the higher-level schema. Explains the common experience of remembering gist while losing specific detail, and implies that the quality of the anchoring concept determines the persistence of the detailed learning it subsumes.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[obliterative-subsumption|Obliterative Subsumption]]*

> [!definition] **Observability Layers** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Execution traces**:
> - Which agents were invoked?
> - In what order?
> - With what inputs?
> - How long did each take?
> 
> **2. Decision logs**:
> - Why was this agent selected?
> - What alternatives were considered?
> - What confidence score triggered selection?
> 
> **3. Performance metrics**:
> - Token consumption per agent
> - Wall clock time per agent
> - Success/failure rates
> - Retry counts
> 
> **4. State evolution**:
> - How did shared state change over time?
> - Which agent made which state updates?
> - Conflicts or rollbacks?
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[observability-layers|Observability Layers]]*

> [!definition] **OIT in Education** **[perm-note]**
> [[educational-psychology]] applications of OIT examine how instructional design, teacher-student relationships, assessment practices, and school structures either facilitate or obstruct the internalization of academic values — the process by which students come to genuinely value learning rather than merely complying with its institutional demands.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[oit-in-education|OIT in Education]]*

> [!definition] **OIT in Work Organizations** **[perm-note]**
> The application of OIT to [[Work-Motivation]] examines how organizational structures, managerial practices, performance evaluation systems, and workplace culture facilitate or undermine the internalization of organizational goals — distinguishing between employees who genuinely value their work and those who comply with its requirements under various forms of external or introjected pressure.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[oit-in-work-organizations|OIT in Work Organizations]]*

> [!definition] **Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric Framework** **[perm-note]**
> Performance-based assessment using standardized rubrics enables direct calibration of self-assessment accuracy by comparing learner self-ratings to expert ratings on identical reasoning products. The **Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric** (Facione and Facione) evaluates reasoning across core dimensions including analysis quality, interpretation accuracy, evaluation rigor, inference validity, explanation clarity, and metacognitive self-regulation effectiveness, using a four-level scale distinguishing strong, acceptable, unacceptable, and weak performance.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[holistic-critical-thinking-scoring-rubric-framework|Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Tool Access Patterns** **[perm-note]**
> **Full tool access** (omit `tools` field):
> - Agent inherits all tools from main thread
> - Use for: General-purpose agents, orchestrators, implementation agents
> - Risk: Can perform destructive operations
> 
> **Read-only access** (`tools: Read, Grep, Glob`):
> - Agent can analyze but not modify
> - Use for: Code reviewers, analyzers, documentation generators
> - Safety: Cannot accidentally break anything
> 
> **Restricted write** (`tools: Read, Write(src/**), Bash(npm test)`):
> - Agent can modify specific directories, run specific commands
> - Use for: Feature developers limited to their module
> - Balance: Pro...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[tool-access-patterns|Tool Access Patterns]]*

> [!definition] **Open Society** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's term for a society organised on fallibilist principles — one that treats its institutions, policies, and governing ideas as revisable and maintains the structural conditions (free inquiry, free press, independent institutions) necessary for error-detection and correction. Contrasted with the "closed society," which treats its foundational commitments as certain and therefore inviolable, eliminating the mechanisms of self-correction.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[open-society|Open Society]]*

> [!definition] **Oral Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> The integrated ability to read connected text aloud with accuracy (low error rate), appropriate rate (words per minute), and prosody (expression, phrasing, and intonation reflecting comprehension of grammatical and semantic structure). ORF assessments, typically 1-minute timed passages, yield a Words Correct Per Minute (WCPM) score that is among the strongest brief predictors of reading comprehension. WCPM does not capture prosody and systematically underestimates fluency problems in accurate-but-slow readers.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[oral-reading-fluency|Oral Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **The Organismic Dialectical Perspective** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's metatheoretical foundation — the view that human beings are active organisms with inherent growth tendencies (the "organismic" part) that continually interact with, and are shaped by, social environments (the "dialectical" part). The perspective holds that neither biology alone nor environment alone determines outcomes; instead, the continuous transaction between the person's inherent tendencies and the nutritive or impoverishing qualities of social contexts is the proper unit of analysis for motivational psychology. This perspective explains why SDT insists on studying both person an...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-organismic-dialectical-perspective|The Organismic Dialectical Perspective]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Integration Tendency** **[perm-note]**
> OIT's theoretical claim that humans have an innate tendency to synthesize externally-sourced experiences and regulations into a coherent, autonomous self. This tendency is not an achievement but a default — it proceeds naturally when basic psychological needs are satisfied and is thwarted when they are frustrated. The "organismic" framing positions humans as active, self-organizing systems rather than passive recipients of social influence.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[organismic-integration-tendency|Organismic Integration Tendency]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Integration Theory** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's sub-theory describing the process by which externally regulated behavior is progressively internalized and integrated into the self. The internalization continuum runs from external regulation through introjected regulation, identified regulation, and integrated regulation. OIT's empirical claims about the benefits of higher-quality internalization are well-supported; its teleological claim that internalization naturally tends toward integration is the dialectically challenged element. The reconstructed version: internalization can proceed toward integration under sustained need-suppo...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[organismic-integration-theory|Organismic Integration Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Meta-Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical foundation of SDT: the view that humans are active, growth-oriented organisms with natural tendencies toward integration, internalization, and psychological coherence. These tendencies are understood as biological dispositions requiring specific environmental nutriments (the three basic psychological needs) for their expression.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[organismic-meta-theory|Organismic Meta-Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Metatheory** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical framework underlying SDT, which posits that human beings are inherently active organisms with an innate propensity toward psychological growth, integration, and the elaboration of a unified self. Unlike mechanistic models that treat behavior as the product of external forces acting on a passive organism, the organismic perspective treats the human organism as a naturally proactive agent whose developmental trajectory depends on the degree to which its basic psychological needs are satisfied by the social environment.
> 
> **Boundary:** The organismic metatheory is not a claim ...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[organismic-metatheory|Organismic Metatheory]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic-Integration-Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The sub-theory within [[self-determination-theory]] that addresses the process by which external regulations and values are internalized and integrated into the self. OIT posits an organismic tendency toward integration and identifies the environmental conditions that support or undermine the movement from external to autonomous regulation across the internalization continuum.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[organismic-integration-theory]]*

> [!definition] **Organization** **[perm-note]**
> The second process in Wittrock's SOI model, referring to the learner's active construction of internal structures — hierarchies, sequences, causal networks, categories — that give selected information relational form. Organization transforms isolated elements into structured representations, providing the architecture into which integration can occur. Analogous to the chunking process in expertise development. See also: [[chunking]], [[schema-theory]], [[mental-models]], [[Concept-Maps]]
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[organization]]*

> [!definition] **Orienting Task** **[perm-note]**
> An experimental technique, widely used in LOP research, in which participants are given a question or instruction that directs their processing toward a specific level before encountering a target word. For example: "Is this word in capital letters?" (structural), "Does this word rhyme with TRAIN?" (phonemic), or "Does this word fit in the sentence: 'The man kicked the ___'?" (semantic). Orienting tasks allow researchers to experimentally manipulate processing depth while measuring subsequent retention, enabling controlled comparison of trace durability across levels.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[orienting-task|Orienting Task]]*

> [!definition] **Orienting Task Paradigm** **[perm-note]**
> The experimental methodology developed in association with LOP research, in which participants are given a specific instruction (orienting question) that directs their cognitive processing toward a specific level before encountering target material. Used as a controlled manipulation of encoding depth. Enables incidental learning designs in which participants do not know they will be tested for memory, controlling for strategic encoding.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[orienting-task-paradigm|Orienting Task Paradigm]]*

> [!definition] **Origin-Pawn Concept** **[perm-note]**
> [**Origin-Pawn-Definition**:: Richard deCharms's (1968) metaphor for the experiential quality of personal causation. An "origin" experiences themselves as the initiator and source of their actions; a "pawn" experiences themselves as moved, directed, and controlled by external forces. CET's Perceived Locus of Causality construct is a direct formalization of deCharms's phenomenological distinction. The origin-pawn dimension captures the qualitative difference between autonomous and controlled motivation that is central to all of SDT.]
> *See also: [[perceived-locus-of-causality]], [[autonomy]],...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[origin-pawn-concept|Origin-Pawn Concept]]*

> [!definition] **Theory-Evidence Coordination** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive capacity to separately evaluate one's existing theoretical commitments and the evidence bearing on them, assigning evidential weight based on the evidence's properties rather than its fit with prior beliefs. Identified by Deanna Kuhn as a central component of scientific reasoning and one of the most difficult to develop. Learners without this capacity systematically distort evidence interpretation to confirm existing theories, undermining productive inductive reasoning.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[theory-evidence-coordination|Theory-Evidence Coordination]]*

> [!definition] **Theory-Ladenness of Observation** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical thesis that observations are not theory-neutral sense-data but are already organized and interpreted by the theoretical frameworks of the observer. "Seeing" in a scientific context is always "seeing as" — as a metal, as a bacterium, as evidence for a hypothesis. The theory-ladenness thesis has direct consequences for abduction: what counts as an anomaly, and therefore what triggers abductive inference, is determined by the observer's background theory. This makes the development of richer and more accurate background knowledge a prerequisite for, not merely complementary t...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[theory-ladenness-of-observation|Theory-Ladenness of Observation]]*

> [!definition] **Outcome Devaluation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An experimental procedure in which the rewarding value of a behavior's outcome is reduced (through satiation, conditioned aversion, or similar means) after training, to assess whether the behavior is under goal-directed or habitual control. Goal-directed behavior diminishes after devaluation; habitual behavior does not. The procedure provides the most direct behavioral evidence for the dual-system architecture.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The devaluation asymmetry — habits persist even when the person no longer wants the outcome — is the mechanism behind the difficult...
> 
> *Source: habit-formation-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[outcome-devaluation|Outcome Devaluation]]*

> [!definition] **Outcome Expectation** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that a specified behavior will produce a particular outcome, distinct from the belief that one can perform the behavior. A person may hold strong outcome expectations (the behavior works) and weak efficacy expectations (one cannot enact it), with the predictable result that the behavior is not undertaken.
> 
> **Boundary:** Outcome expectations concern *behavior-to-outcome* contingencies; efficacy expectations concern *self-to-behavior* contingencies. The two judgments can diverge sharply.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The distinction between these two expectations is the concep...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[outcome-expectation|Outcome Expectation]]*

> [!definition] **Outcome Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A goal that specifies an end-state or product rather than a process. Example: "I will complete Phase III notes." Outcome goals have their place — particularly as distant anchors — but as proximal learning goals, they can shift attention toward task completion rather than deep processing.
> 
> **Boundary:** Outcome goals are not inherently maladaptive. For novices who need structure and clear milestones, outcome goals provide motivating checkpoints. The problem arises when outcome goals crowd out process goals entirely.
> 
> **See also:** [[deliberate-practice]], [[The-SRL-Cycle-as-a-Calibration-Eng...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[outcome-goal|Outcome Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Output Style Modes** **[perm-note]**
> Three primary workflow modes in Claude Code:
> 
> **Research Mode** (`/output-style research`)
> - Focus: Deep exploration and understanding
> - Agent pattern: Single agent deep dives, sequential investigation
> - Tools: Heavy use of Explore, web search, documentation fetching
> - Output: Comprehensive analysis documents
> 
> **Planning Mode** (`/output-style planning`)
> - Focus: Architecture and design
> - Agent pattern: Architect-focused, may invoke research agents
> - Tools: Codebase analysis, design document generation
> - Output: Architectural decision records (ADRs), design specs
> 
> **Execution Mode** (`/outp...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[output-style-modes|Output Style Modes]]*

> [!definition] **Overjustification Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The specific psychological phenomenon in which adding an external justification (reward) to a behaviour that is already intrinsically motivated reduces the person's subsequent intrinsic interest in that behaviour. The term "overjustification" refers to the idea that the external reason provides more justification than is needed — and in the process, changes the person's interpretation of why they were performing the activity in the first place. Originally named by Lepper, Greene, and Nisbett (1973); theoretically explained by CET's PLOC mechanism.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[overjustification-effect|Overjustification Effect]]*

## _P

> [!definition] **Paraverbal Communication** **[perm-note]**
> The vocal qualities that accompany speech — pitch, pace, volume, rhythm, pause, and voice quality — constituting the [[prosody|prosodic]] dimension of communication. Paraverbal signals carry dense information about emotional state, speaker confidence, relational intent, and emphasis, and are processed largely below conscious awareness by receivers.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[paraverbal-communication|Paraverbal Communication]]*

> [!definition] **The Parity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> If, as we confront some task, a part of the world functions as a process which, were it done in the head, we would have no hesitation in recognising as part of the cognitive process, then that part of the world is (for that time) part of the cognitive process. The principle is explicitly functionalist in character: cognitive status is determined by causal-functional role, not by material constitution or spatial location. It does NOT entail that all external processes count as cognitive — only those meeting the functional equivalence criterion.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-parity-principle|The Parity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Parity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The central argumentative tool of Extended Mind Theory: if a process performed outside the head would count as cognitive if it were performed inside the head (meeting standard criteria of cognitive status), then it counts as cognitive when performed outside the head. The principle is explicitly functionalist: cognitive status is determined by causal-functional role, not by material constitution or spatial location. It does NOT entail that all external processes are cognitive — only those satisfying functional equivalence with internal processes. Critics argue it commits the coupling-constit...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[parity-principle|Parity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Part-Task Practice** **[perm-note]**
> Additional practice exercises targeting isolated recurrent constituent skills that require very high levels of automaticity for effective whole-task performance. Employed only when the volume of practice provided by learning tasks alone is insufficient to reach the required automation threshold. Always occurs alongside continued whole-task practice, never as a replacement.
> 
> **Boundary:** Part-task practice in the 4C/ID model is not equivalent to the atomistic decomposition of traditional training. It is a selective supplement for specific recurrent skills, embedded within a primarily whole-...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[part-task-practice|Part-Task Practice]]*

> [!definition] **Partisan Identity** **[perm-note]**
> Identification with a political party understood not merely as a summary of policy preferences but as a social identity — with the affective, motivational, and categorization properties of in-group membership. Partisan identity functions as a perceptual frame that shapes the interpretation of political events and information. The shift from understanding partisan identification as primarily a policy-aggregation mechanism to understanding it as a social identity mechanism is one of the most important developments in political psychology over the past twenty years.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[partisan-identity|Partisan Identity]]*

> [!definition] **Passive Exposure Fallacy** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The error of treating exposure to information as equivalent to acquisition of it. The Passive Exposure Fallacy operates when a learning prescription recommends increased exposure volume (more reading, more practice time, more hours) without specifying the active processing requirements that make exposure educationally productive. It is a *fallacy* because it conflates a necessary condition (some exposure is required) with a sufficient condition (enough exposure is sufficient). Active, generative processing is the rate-limiting step in vocabulary acquisition; exposure without...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[passive-exposure-fallacy|Passive Exposure Fallacy]]*

> [!definition] **Passive Externalism** **[perm-note]**
> The view, associated with Putnam and Burge, that the *contents* of mental states are partly determined by factors external to the individual — the physical environment (natural kind terms) or social community (linguistic norms) — while the *vehicles* or physical realisers of those states remain brain-bound. Active externalism is a stronger, more radical claim: it extends the vehicles themselves.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[passive-externalism|Passive Externalism]]*

> [!definition] **PATH** **[perm-note]**
> [**PATH-Environment-Variable**:: An operating system environment variable that contains an ordered list of directory paths, searched sequentially when a command is entered without a full path specification. When a user types `python`, the system searches PATH directories from first to last, executing the first `python` executable found. Misconfiguration of PATH is the most common source of "wrong interpreter" errors in Python development.]
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[path]]*

> [!definition] **PATH Environment Variable** **[perm-note]**
> The PATH is an ordered list of directories that the operating system searches through, sequentially, when asked to execute a program by name alone. When one types `python` into a terminal, the system walks through each directory in the PATH, checking for an executable with that name, and runs the first match it finds — which means that the presence of Python on the system is invisible to any terminal session unless the directory containing the Python executable appears somewhere in this list. This is the single most common source of "Python not found" errors, and it is the first thing to ve...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-practitioners-field-guide-2026-04-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[path-environment-variable|PATH Environment Variable]]*

> [!definition] **Paul-Elder-Framework** **[perm-note]**
> A comprehensive model of [[critical-thinking]] that integrates the [[the-elements-of-thought-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning|Elements of Thought]], [[intellectual-standards]], and [[intellectual-traits]] into a recursive practice of reasoning improvement. Boundary condition: strongest as a pedagogical-operational framework, less complete as a standalone empirical theory of cognition.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[paul-elder-framework]]*

> [!definition] **PENCRISAL Framework** **[perm-note]**
> Developed by Rivas and Saiz (2012) at the University of Salamanca, PENCRISAL (Prueba de Evaluación de Pensamiento Crítico en Razonamiento, Argumentación y Solución de problemas) operationalizes critical thinking through 35 situational problems requiring open-ended responses. Scoring applies a 0-2 rubric: 0 = incorrect response, 1 = correct but inadequately justified, 2 = correct with rigorous justification. Total scores range 0-70, with five dimensional subscales (14 points each):
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[pencrisal-framework|PENCRISAL Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Competence** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective sense of effectiveness in one's interactions with the environment — feeling capable, skilled, and able to produce intended outcomes. Distinct from objective competence: a person can be highly competent but have low perceived competence (as in the Impostor Phenomenon), or low competence but high perceived competence (overconfidence). In CET, perceived competence is a basic psychological need whose satisfaction by external events (through positive informational feedback) can enhance intrinsic motivation, and whose frustration (through negative feedback or task failure without i...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceived-competence|Perceived Competence]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Internal Locus of Causation** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective experience of one's own behavior as originating from internal, self-determined sources rather than external pressure or internal compulsion (introjected pressure). The technical psychological mechanism underlying the autonomy need. Contrasts with perceived external locus of causation, in which one's behavior is experienced as controlled by forces outside the integrated self.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceived-internal-locus-of-causation|Perceived Internal Locus of Causation]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Locus of Causality** **[perm-note]**
> The person's attributional sense of whether their actions originate from themselves (internal PLOC) or from external forces (external PLOC). A key mediating variable in [[cognitive-evaluation-theory]]: external events shift the PLOC from internal to external, thereby reducing autonomous motivation and intrinsic motivation. Importantly, PLOC is a perceived construct — it may not accurately reflect objective sources of causation — and is susceptible to individual differences in attribution style and [[causality-orientation]].
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceived-locus-of-causality|Perceived Locus of Causality]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Locus of Causality — PLOC** *[needs-note]*
> See full definition in Phase II. The PLOC is the subjective sense that one's behavior is self-determined (internal PLOC) or externally caused (external PLOC). In CET, controlling events shift the PLOC toward external; informational events preserve or enhance the internal PLOC. The PLOC mediates the relationship between external event type and intrinsic motivation.
> 
> **See also:** [[perceived-locus-of-causality]], [[autonomy-need]], [[autonomous-motivation]], [[controlled-motivation]], [[external-regulation]]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceived-Locus-of-Causality-—-PLOC|Perceived Locus of Causality — PLOC]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Partner Responsiveness** **[perm-note]**
> Reis, Clark, and Holmes's (2004) construct capturing the phenomenological quality of connection within a close relationship: the experience of feeling understood (the partner accurately perceives who you are), validated (the partner affirms the legitimacy of your experience), and cared for (the partner is genuinely invested in your well-being). PPR is empirically the strongest relational predictor of relatedness need satisfaction and serves as the proximate mechanism through which need-supportive partner behavior translates into felt connection.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceived-partner-responsiveness|Perceived Partner Responsiveness]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Self-Efficacy** **[perm-note]**
> People's judgments of their capabilities to organize and execute the courses of action required to attain designated types of performances. Self-efficacy is concerned not with the skills one has but with the judgments of what one can do with whatever skills one possesses.
> 
> **Boundary:** Self-efficacy is not general self-confidence, not self-esteem, not a stable personality trait, and not a global self-concept. It is a domain-specific, situationally contextualized appraisal of *capability for action* under specified conditions, oriented toward a future performance rather than a past evaluati...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceived-self-efficacy|Perceived Self-Efficacy]]*

> [!definition] **Perceptual Attunement** **[perm-note]**
> The proposed mechanism by which causality orientations produce their effects: orientations sensitize individuals to different features of the motivational environment, so that autonomy-relevant signals (interest, choice, value) are more salient to autonomy-oriented individuals and control-relevant signals (evaluation, pressure, comparison) are more salient to control-oriented individuals. Not explicitly theorized in Deci & Ryan's original COT account but derivable from the orientation construct and supported by outcomes patterns.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceptual-attunement|Perceptual Attunement]]*

> [!definition] **Perceptual Span** **[perm-note]**
> The region from which useful information is extracted during a single eye fixation during reading. Approximately 14–15 characters to the right and 3–4 characters to the left of the fixation point in alphabetic scripts. Sets the physical upper limit on information available per fixation and is not substantively modifiable through training.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[perceptual-span|Perceptual Span]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A performance goal is an achievement goal in which the person defines success as outperforming others or appearing more competent than others. The standard of reference is normative — anchored in social comparison. Performance goals are sometimes called *ego goals* or *ability goals*. The motivational consequences of performance goals are, as we will see, more complex than early theorists recognised, and they have been significantly refined by subsequent work distinguishing approach from avoidance orientations.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-goal|Performance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal / Performance-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> An achievement goal in which success is defined by normative superiority — demonstrating greater ability than others or avoiding demonstrations of inferior ability. In the 2×2 framework, the approach form seeks positive normative outcomes (outperforming); the avoidance form seeks to avoid negative normative outcomes (appearing incompetent).
> 
> **See also:** [[performance-goal-orientation]], [[performance-approach-goal]], [[performance-avoidance-goal]], [[ego-involvement]], [[differentiated-conception-of-ability]]
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-goal-performance-approach-goal|Performance Goal / Performance-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational orientation characterized by the desire to demonstrate high ability and avoid demonstrating low ability relative to others. Performance-oriented learners prioritize looking smart over becoming smart, leading to risk-avoidance and vulnerability to setback. Strongly associated with fixed mindset.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-goal-orientation|Performance Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal vs. Learning Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A performance goal specifies a desired outcome of action — a quantity to produce, a target to hit, a level to reach. A learning goal specifies a desired strategy to acquire or process to master, with outcome treated as a downstream consequence of mastery rather than as the direct target.
> 
> **Boundary:** The distinction is not the same as the [[achievement-goal-theory]] distinction between performance and mastery orientations, although the two distinctions are conceptually adjacent and partially overlap; Locke and Latham's distinction is task-strategic, while Achievement Goal Theory's is disp...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-goal-vs.-learning-goal|Performance Goal vs. Learning Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The second phase of the cycle, encompassing the learning activity itself. Contains *self-control* processes (self-instruction, imagery, attention focusing, task strategies) and *self-observation* processes ([[self-monitoring]], self-recording). The critical claim is that self-observation during performance generates feedback data that will be processed in the Self-Reflection Phase. The tight-loop assumption is most visible here: Zimmerman proposes that effective learners are *continuously* monitoring their performance against their goals, generating real-time data that will enable adaptive ...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-phase|Performance Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Performance-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Striving to demonstrate superior competence relative to others — to outperform peers, to be the best in the class, to secure the highest grade. The standard is normative; success means standing above. This is the approach pole of the performance dimension.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** PAP goals are the theoretically contested cell. They predict higher academic performance on some measures but are associated with maladaptive patterns on others (shallow processing, lower intrinsic interest, reduced deep learning). The controversy about PAP goals is the analytic epicenter ...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-approach-goal|Performance-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance-Avoidance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Striving to avoid demonstrating inferior competence — to avoid being the worst in the class, to avoid public failure, to avoid being judged as incompetent. The standard is normative, but the motivational orientation is avoidant.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** PAV goals are the most uniformly maladaptive goal type. Unlike PAP goals, their effects across outcomes, populations, and contexts are remarkably consistent in direction.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[performance-avoidance-goal|Performance-Avoidance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Person Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The subcomponent of metacognitive knowledge concerning beliefs about human cognitive processes in general, beliefs about the cognitive characteristics of other specific individuals, and beliefs about one's own cognitive strengths, limitations, and typical patterns (intrapersonal knowledge). Intrapersonal person knowledge is the most extensively studied dimension; its accuracy (calibration) is one of the primary determinants of self-regulatory quality.
> 
> **Common Misconception:** Person knowledge is not equivalent to self-concept or academic self-efficacy, though these constructs are correlat...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[person-knowledge|Person Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Causation** **[perm-note]**
> [**Personal-Causation-Definition**:: deCharms' term for the phenomenological experience of being the causal origin of one's own behavior, as opposed to experiencing oneself as an instrument controlled by external forces. The origin-pawn distinction inaugurated the concept of perceived locus of causality that CET would later formalize and operationalize.]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[personal-causation|Personal Causation]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Knowledge Base** **[perm-note]**
> A [[personal-knowledge-base]] (PKB) is a personally maintained, externalized knowledge system — typically digital — that a practitioner uses to capture, connect, and develop understanding across domains over an extended period. The defining features of a PKB that distinguish it from mere note-taking or archiving are: (1) intentional link-making between concepts rather than simple categorization, (2) an orientation toward long-term, longitudinal knowledge development rather than short-term task support, and (3) structural design choices that reflect the practitioner's model of how knowledge ...
> 
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[personal-knowledge-base|Personal Knowledge Base]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Knowledge Base — PKB** *[needs-note]*
> A [[personal-knowledge-base]] (PKB) is a structured, interconnected system for capturing, organizing, developing, and retrieving personal knowledge — typically implemented as a digital tool (such as [[obsidian]], Logseq, or Roam Research) that supports bidirectional linking, metadata tagging, and progressive elaboration. Unlike a simple note archive, a PKB is designed to grow into a *knowledge graph* where the *relationships between ideas* are as important as the ideas themselves, and where the act of building the system is itself a primary mechanism of learning.
> 
> **Boundary:** A PKB is not...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Personal-Knowledge-Base-—-PKB|Personal Knowledge Base — PKB]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Knowledge Management** **[perm-note]**
> A set of processes, tools, and practices by which an individual systematically captures, organizes, connects, retrieves, and applies knowledge relevant to their goals and projects. PKM is distinguished from passive consumption (reading, watching) by its requirement for active transformation of information into personal knowledge structures. Distinguished from organizational knowledge management by its focus on the individual rather than the institution.
> 
> **Boundary:** PKM is not equivalent to note-taking. Note-taking is a component of PKM but is insufficient without the complementary proces...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[personal-knowledge-management|Personal Knowledge Management]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Knowledge Management — PKM** *[needs-note]*
> An integrated set of practices, tools, and strategies through which an individual captures, organises, processes, connects, retrieves, and applies knowledge. Extends beyond information management to include active knowledge construction and the development of an external representation of one's conceptual understanding.
> 
> **Boundary:** PKM is not synonymous with note-taking, information hoarding, or productivity systems. The distinguishing feature is *knowledge construction* — the transformation of information into integrated understanding through active processing.
> 
> **Report-Specific Signif...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Personal-Knowledge-Management-—-PKM|Personal Knowledge Management — PKM]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Learning Environment — PLE** *[needs-note]*
> A self-curated digital ecosystem of tools, platforms, communities, and resources that a learner constructs and maintains to support their ongoing learning across domains and over time. Unlike institutional learning management systems, PLEs are owned and controlled by the learner and persist beyond any single course or program.
> 
> **Boundary:** A PLE is not simply a collection of bookmarks or tools; it implies deliberate curation and ongoing management of a learning infrastructure aligned with the learner's goals and needs.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Identified as the primary contempor...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: adult education & self-directed learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[Personal-Learning-Environment-—-PLE|Personal Learning Environment — PLE]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Responsibility Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A conceptual framework that resolves the SDL field's definitional ambiguity by distinguishing two orthogonal dimensions: SDL as a *teaching-learning process* (the degree to which the learner controls the instructional context) and *learner self-direction* as a personal attribute (the individual's dispositional orientation toward taking initiative in learning). The model clarifies that process and person are independent — one can be high on one dimension and low on the other — which explains why SDL-structured environments do not automatically produce self-directed learners, and why self-dir...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[personal-responsibility-orientation|Personal Responsibility Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Phase vs. Area — A Critical Distinction** *[needs-note]*
> Phases and areas interact, but they are not the same thing. A *phase* describes a temporal moment in the regulatory cycle; an *area* describes a domain of regulatory attention. The monitoring phase involves monitoring cognition, monitoring motivation, monitoring behavior, and monitoring context — four distinct monitoring activities happening within the same temporal window. Confusing phases with areas leads to systematically incomplete models that effectively reduce to single-area monitoring (usually cognition) spread across time.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Phase-vs.-Area-—-A-Critical-Distinction|Phase vs. Area — A Critical Distinction]]*

> [!definition] **Phenomenological Substitution** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which monitoring answers the question "how does this feel?" when the functionally relevant question is "how well is this encoded?" A consequence of monitoring's indirect, cue-based access to cognitive states.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[phenomenological-substitution|Phenomenological Substitution]]*

> [!definition] **The Philosophical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Examines the conceptual assumptions that schema theory takes for granted — its theory of mental representation, its implicit ontology of cognition, and the logical structure of its core arguments. This lens asks: What must be true for schema theory to work, and are those presuppositions defensible?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-philosophical-lens|The Philosophical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Philosophical Lineage** **[perm-note]**
> **[CT-Philosophical-Foundation**:: Critical thinking traces its intellectual roots to Socratic dialectic, Aristotelian logic, and medieval scholasticism, establishing the normative dimension where CT serves not merely as cognitive process but as epistemic virtue - the disciplined pursuit of truth through systematic questioning of assumptions, logical analysis of arguments, and commitment to evidence-based reasoning.]**
> 
> The philosophical tradition established critical thinking as fundamentally concerned with **justified belief** - not merely thinking *about* things, but thinking *well* abou...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[philosophical-lineage|Philosophical Lineage]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological Awareness** **[perm-note]**
> Phonological awareness is the explicit, conscious sensitivity to the sound structure of spoken language, including the ability to identify and manipulate phonemes, syllables, and onset-rime units. It is recognised as the strongest early predictor of reading acquisition and is the primary mechanism targeted in effective early literacy interventions.
> 
> **Boundary:** Phonological awareness is NOT the same as phonics (letter-sound correspondence instruction) or phonemic awareness (the most fine-grained subset, awareness of individual phonemes). It is an umbrella category.
> 
> **Report-Specific Sign...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[phonological-awareness|Phonological Awareness]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological Loop** **[perm-note]**
> The [[phonological-loop]] is the component of working memory that maintains verbal information in a phonological (sound-based) code through active rehearsal. It holds approximately 3-4 items for approximately 2 seconds without rehearsal. In reading, the phonological loop maintains the sound representations of recently decoded words while subsequent words are processed — making it the critical resource for sentence-level integration. Non-automatic decoding consumes phonological loop resources, leaving insufficient capacity for maintaining sentence-length phonological representations.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[phonological-loop|Phonological Loop]]*

> [!definition] **The Phonological Loop** **[perm-note]**
> The **phonological loop** is the component of working memory specialized for the temporary storage and rehearsal of phonological (sound-based) information. It consists of two subcomponents: (1) a **phonological store** — a passive buffer that can hold speech-based information for approximately 1-2 seconds before it decays, and (2) an **articulatory control process** (also called the inner voice or subvocal rehearsal mechanism) — an active process that refreshes decaying phonological representations by subvocally rehearsing them. The phonological loop evolved primarily to support language ac...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-phonological-loop|The Phonological Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological Similarity Effect** **[perm-note]**
> **Phonological Similarity Effect**: The finding that sequences of phonologically similar items (letters B, C, D, G, P, T; words cat, bat, hat, mat) are recalled less accurately than sequences of phonologically distinct items, indicating that working memory stores phonological codes rather than semantic or orthographic representations. This demonstrates that the phonological loop operates on sound-based representations, and that confusability in that dimension directly degrades recall. See: [[phonological-loop]], [[phonological-awareness]].
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[phonological-similarity-effect|Phonological Similarity Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological-Loop|Phonological Loop** **[perm-note]**
> A component of [[working-memory|working memory]] responsible for maintaining phonological traces of verbal information in an active state for approximately 2 seconds, refreshable through subvocal rehearsal. In reading, the phonological loop maintains the phonological trace of sentences to support syntactic parsing across clausal boundaries.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[phonological-loopphonological-loop|Phonological-Loop|Phonological Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Physiological and Affective States** **[perm-note]**
> Bodily and emotional signals that the person reads as information about their capability for the target behavior, where the efficacy implication depends on the person's interpretive frame for the signals. Symptoms of arousal that one person reads as anxiety another may read as readiness, and the resulting efficacy impact follows the interpretation rather than the signal itself.
> 
> **Boundary:** This source does not refer to the physiological states themselves but to their *interpretation as efficacy-relevant information*. States that the person does not consciously process, or processes witho...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[physiological-and-affective-states|Physiological and Affective States]]*

> [!definition] **Pintrich's 4×4 Matrix** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** A theoretical architecture for self-regulated learning consisting of four regulatory phases (Forethought/Planning/Activation; Monitoring; Control; Reaction/Reflection) crossed with four regulated areas (Cognition; Motivation/Affect; Behavior; Context), generating 16 cells each representing a distinct regulatory activity. The matrix is analytic — it decomposes simultaneous regulatory processes for examination — not sequential.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The matrix structure is both Pintrich's principal contribution and the source of the tractability problem identified...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pintrich's-4×4-Matrix|Pintrich's 4×4 Matrix]]*

> [!definition] **Pintrich's Framework of Self-Regulated Learning** *[needs-note]*
> A four-by-four architectural model in which four temporally ordered phases of regulation (forethought and planning; monitoring; control; reaction and reflection) cross four areas of regulation (cognition; motivation and affect; behavior; context), producing sixteen analytically distinct cells that together describe the full scope of regulatory activity during a learning episode. Distinguished from adjacent SRL frameworks by its insistence that motivation, behavior, and context are themselves regulated objects governed by the same monitoring-control logic that governs cognition.
> 
> **Boundary:...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pintrich's-Framework-of-Self-Regulated-Learning|Pintrich's Framework of Self-Regulated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Pintrich's Motivational Integration** *[needs-note]*
> Pintrich extended Zimmerman's primarily cognitive account by foregrounding [[motivational-regulation|Motivational Regulation]] as a co-equal component of self-regulation alongside cognitive and metacognitive regulation. His framework distinguishes: **[[goal-orientation|Goal Orientation]]** (mastery vs. performance approach vs. performance avoidance — which shapes not just effort level but the qualitative nature of regulatory behavior); **[[task-value|Task Value]]** (intrinsic interest, utility value, and attainment value — which determine whether regulation is initiated in the first place);...
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pintrich's-Motivational-Integration|Pintrich's Motivational Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Pipeline Stages** **[perm-note]**
> The canonical three-stage pattern:
> 
> **Stage 1: Requirements/Planning** (PM agent)
> - Input: User request, problem statement
> - Process: Clarifying questions, acceptance criteria definition
> - Output: Specification document, status set to READY_FOR_ARCH
> - Hand off: Architect agent triggered
> 
> **Stage 2: Architecture/Design** (Architect agent)  
> - Input: Specification from PM stage
> - Process: Design review, constraint validation, ADR creation
> - Output: Architecture document, status set to READY_FOR_BUILD
> - Handoff: Implementer agent triggered
> 
> **Stage 3: Implementation** (Implementer agent)
> - Inp...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[pipeline-stages|Pipeline Stages]]*

> [!definition] **The Episodic Buffer** **[perm-note]**
> The **episodic buffer** is the fourth and most recently proposed component of Baddeley's working memory model, introduced in 2000 to address a significant theoretical gap in the original three-component architecture. It is defined as a limited-capacity temporary storage system that can hold integrated, multimodal "chunks" of information — calling on both the phonological loop and the visuospatial sketchpad, as well as information retrieved from [[long-term-memory]], and integrating these into coherent, episodic representations. Unlike the slave systems, the episodic buffer does not speciali...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-episodic-buffer|The Episodic Buffer]]*

> [!definition] **Episodic Buffer** **[perm-note]**
> **Episodic Buffer** (Baddeley, 2000): The fourth component of the working memory model, added to account for multimodal integration. A limited-capacity (~4 chunks), conscious-access storage system that integrates information from the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and long-term memory into coherent, multimodal episodic representations. Connected to [[episodic-memory]], to conscious awareness, and to the temporary housing of situation models in reading comprehension. See: [[episodic-buffer]], [[mental-model]].
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[episodic-buffer|Episodic Buffer]]*

> [!definition] **Episodic Memory** **[perm-note]**
> A memory system dedicated to the storage and retrieval of personally experienced events, indexed by their temporal and spatial context. Episodic memory represents the *where* and *when* of experience, enabling what Tulving called "mental time travel" — the capacity to consciously re-experience past events. Distinguished from [[semantic-memory]] by its temporal context-specificity and by the phenomenological quality of "remembering" (as opposed to merely "knowing").
> 
> **Boundary:** Episodic memory is not simply autobiographical memory; it is the more precise cognitive-science construct of a m...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[episodic-memory|Episodic Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Action** **[perm-note]**
> An action performed on an external artifact not to advance a task directly but to change the cognitive state of the agent performing it — making thinking easier, revealing structure, or prompting new connections. Writing functions as an epistemic action insofar as the act of externalising thought reveals knowledge gaps and prompts integration that private cognition does not.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-action|Epistemic Action]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Actions** **[perm-note]**
> Physical actions performed not to achieve a practical goal directly but to change the agent's informational relationship to a problem — making information more accessible, patterns more visible, or cognitive operations easier. Distinguished from pragmatic actions, which directly advance toward a goal state.
> 
> **Boundary:** The distinction between epistemic and pragmatic actions is functional, not physical. The same physical action (e.g., writing a note) can be epistemic (trying to understand something) or pragmatic (recording information for later retrieval), depending on the agent's purpose...
> 
> *Source: pkb-as-externalized-metacognition-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-actions|Epistemic Actions]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a person's confidence in their beliefs matches the actual probability of those beliefs being true. A well-calibrated agent who says "I'm 80% confident in this" is right approximately 80% of the time across such claims. Fallibilism implies the importance of calibration: if all beliefs are potentially mistaken, then overconfidence is a systematic epistemic failing. Research in [[cognitive-psychology|cognitive psychology]] consistently shows that humans tend toward overconfidence — we are typically more certain than our evidence warrants. Calibration is thus both a theoreti...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-calibration|Epistemic Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Citizenship** **[perm-note]**
> The set of cognitive capacities, epistemic virtues, and information-engagement practices required for effective participation in democratic public life. Epistemic citizenship goes beyond knowledge of political facts to include calibrated uncertainty about contested claims, awareness of one's own cognitive biases, disposition toward evidence-responsiveness, and skill in navigating complex information environments. It is the application domain in which political reasoning research most directly meets civic education practice.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-citizenship|Epistemic Citizenship]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> An individual's beliefs, attitudes, and reasoning about the nature of knowledge and the process of knowing — including beliefs about the certainty of knowledge, the source of knowledge, and the criteria by which claims can be evaluated as more or less justified. Epistemic cognition underlies but is distinct from domain-specific knowledge; it constitutes the meta-epistemological framework within which critical thinking occurs.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-cognition|Epistemic Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Holism** **[perm-note]**
