> The thesis, associated primarily with W.V.O. Quine, that individual beliefs do not face empirical evidence in isolation but as part of an interconnected system. When experience conflicts with our expectations, we must revise our beliefs somewhere, but logic does not uniquely determine where. Holism supports fallibilism by showing that revision is always possible, and complicates Popperian falsificationism by showing that individual statements cannot be straightforwardly refuted without affecting surrounding assumptions.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-holism|Epistemic Holism]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Humility** **[perm-note]**
> The intellectual virtue of recognising the limits of one's knowledge, the possibility of one's errors, and the genuine epistemic standing of others who disagree. [[epistemic-humility|Epistemic humility]] is the dispositional correlate of the theoretical claim of fallibilism: if our beliefs could be wrong, then intellectual integrity demands that we hold them with a degree of tentativeness proportioned to our evidence, remain open to correction, and acknowledge what we do not know. Epistemic humility is not the same as intellectual timidity or the performance of uncertainty for social purpos...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-humility|Epistemic Humility]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Responsibility** **[perm-note]**
> The ethical and cognitive obligation of communicators to hold themselves to argument quality standards, distinguish legitimate influence from manipulation, acknowledge uncertainty accurately, and take responsibility for the epistemic effects of their communication on listeners. See [[critical-thinking]], [[epistemic-vigilance]], [[epistemic-humility]].
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-responsibility|Epistemic Responsibility]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Status** **[perm-note]**
> An assessment of a claim's evidential standing — including the type and strength of evidence supporting it, its position on the established-to-speculative spectrum, known counter-evidence or alternative interpretations, and the annotator's confidence level. Used in this report to make the reliability of claims explicitly visible to the reader, enabling calibrated trust rather than undifferentiated acceptance or skepticism.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-annotated-critical-analysis-2026-04-04* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-status|Epistemic Status]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Vigilance** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Epistemic Vigilance (Sperber et al., 2010)
> **Definition:** The suite of cognitive mechanisms that evaluate the reliability and trustworthiness of communicated information. Includes source evaluation, content plausibility checking, and coherence assessment. Functions as a defence against manipulation and misinformation.
> **Boundary conditions:** Can be under-deployed (gullibility) or over-deployed (pathological distrust). Requires calibration — sufficient vigilance to detect deception without becoming so sceptical that useful information is rejected.
> **Report significance:** Identif...
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-vigilance|Epistemic Vigilance]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic-Vigilance** **[perm-note]**
> Cognitive and social mechanisms used to evaluate testimony, evidence, and claims for reliability and manipulation risk. Connection: fairness, relevance, and accuracy standards provide practical vigilance prompts.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[epistemic-vigilance]]*

> [!definition] **PKB Fluency Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The paradoxical situation in which a well-organized, accessible PKB undermines metacognitive accuracy: organizational quality generates processing fluency, which is misinterpreted as genuine understanding, reducing the perceived need for effortful processing and producing a gap between apparent and actual knowledge. A specific instance of the broader fluency-metacognition paradox applied to knowledge management systems.
> 
> **Boundary:** The Fluency Trap does not imply that well-organized PKBs are counterproductive — only that organizational quality must be coupled with calibration interventio...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[pkb-fluency-trap|PKB Fluency Trap]]*

> [!definition] **PKB Schema-Fidelity** **[perm-note]**
> PKB Schema-Fidelity refers to the degree to which a Personal Knowledge Base's organizational structure mirrors the relational, hierarchical, and associative properties of [[schema-theory|schematic]] long-term memory. A high-fidelity PKB contains richly typed, conceptually meaningful links between atomic notes; hierarchical schema nodes (MOCs, index notes, framework notes) that constrain the interpretation of connected detail notes; and an associative network structure that supports spreading activation analogues (following links surfaces related concepts automatically, not just categoricall...
> 
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[pkb-schema-fidelity|PKB Schema-Fidelity]]*

> [!definition] **Planning** **[perm-note]**
> Planning is the prospective regulatory activity that occurs before task engagement and involves goal-setting, strategy selection, resource allocation, and activation of relevant prior knowledge. Effective metacognitive planning requires accurate task assessment (what does this task demand?), accurate personal assessment (what are my relevant strengths and limitations?), and adequate strategy knowledge (what approaches are likely to be effective?). Planning is the regulatory component most directly dependent on [[metacognitive-knowledge|Metacognitive Knowledge]], since strategy selection wit...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[planning]]*

> [!definition] **Political Decision Making** **[perm-note]**
> The subset of political reasoning that terminates in an action-relevant judgment: a voting choice, a donation decision, an expression of support or opposition, a policy preference. Political decision-making is the output of political reasoning processes. The distinction matters because a person may engage in extensive political reasoning without reaching a decision, and may reach political decisions with very little deliberate reasoning at all.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[political-decision-making|Political Decision Making]]*

> [!definition] **Political Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive processes by which an individual forms, evaluates, and updates beliefs about political actors, institutions, issues, and events. Political reasoning encompasses both the fast, automatic processes that generate immediate political responses and the slower, more deliberate processes that evaluate those responses against evidence and argument. It is broader than formal logical inference — it includes all the ways that humans make sense of political reality, including through narrative, analogy, emotion, and social comparison.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[political-reasoning|Political Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Power Law of Practice** **[perm-note]**
> The robust empirical finding that the time required to perform a task decreases as a power function of the number of practice trials: rapid initial improvement followed by progressively diminishing returns. In the chunking framework, the power law reflects the progressive automation of component schemas, with each practice trial consolidating and automating additional chunk-level operations.
> 
> **Boundary:** The power law describes the aggregate trajectory; individual practice sessions may show plateau effects, regression, or discontinuous jumps that reflect qualitative reorganizations of chu...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[power-law-of-practice|Power Law of Practice]]*

> [!definition] **The Practical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Investigates how the theory performs when it guides instructional design, pedagogical practice, and knowledge management. This lens asks: Where does the textbook version of schema theory diverge from what experienced practitioners discover when they apply it?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-practical-lens|The Practical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Construction** **[perm-note]**
> The novel insight from Phase IV: representation construction in reasoning is guided not by logical completeness but by pragmatic relevance — people construct the representations that are most informative and relevant given their goals and context. This explains why "reasoning errors" in laboratory tasks are often adaptive responses to realistic prior probabilities, and why everyday reasoning performs much better than laboratory deductive reasoning studies suggest.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[pragmatic-construction|Pragmatic Construction]]*

> [!definition] **The Pragmatic Maxim** **[perm-note]**
> The [[Pragmatic-Maxim|pragmatic maxim]] states that the entire meaning of any concept lies in its conceivable practical effects. To clarify an idea, ask: what difference would it make *in practice* if this idea were true versus false? If there is no practical difference, the dispute is merely verbal. For learning, this principle implies that abstract knowledge must always be connected to possible applications — understanding is tested and developed through consequential action.
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-pragmatic-maxim|The Pragmatic Maxim]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Reasoning Schema** **[perm-note]**
> A content-specific cluster of production rules organized around a recurring social or causal situation, particularly permission and obligation structures. Unlike formal logical rules, pragmatic schemas are activated by thematic content and encode the inferential patterns appropriate to that content domain. The permission schema (roughly: "if action A is to be taken, precondition P must be satisfied") explains why people reason correctly on permission-framed versions of the Wason Selection Task even without formal logical training.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[pragmatic-reasoning-schema|Pragmatic Reasoning Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Reasoning Schemas** **[perm-note]**
> Domain-specific, content-sensitive rule-like structures (proposed by Cheng & Holyoak) that govern reasoning in particular pragmatic contexts — especially permission schemas (if action then precondition must be satisfied) and obligation schemas. Represent a hybrid position: rule-like in form but content-sensitive in application. Used to explain the facilitation of Wason selection task performance with thematic materials without positing mental models.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[pragmatic-reasoning-schemas|Pragmatic Reasoning Schemas]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatism** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** [[pragmatism]] (Peirce, James, Dewey; late 19th–20th century)
> **Definition:** A philosophical tradition holding that the meaning of concepts lies in their practical consequences, that knowledge is constituted through purposive inquiry rather than passive contemplation, and that truth is best understood as the warranted assertibility achieved through communal, self-correcting investigation.
> **Boundary Conditions:** Pragmatism is not mere utilitarianism or "whatever works" relativism — Peirce's pragmatic maxim demands that practical consequences be the *conceivable* consequences of ...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[pragmatism]]*

> [!definition] **The Pragmatist Teacher Role** **[perm-note]**
> In pragmatist pedagogy, the teacher's primary role is the design and management of problem environments — the selection of genuine difficulties that are optimally challenging for the learner's current level, the provision of resources and scaffolding that support inquiry without eliminating it, and the stimulation of reflective self-awareness through productive questioning. This contrasts with the transmission model, in which the teacher's primary role is efficient presentation of organized content. The distinction maps directly onto the difference between [[formative-assessment|formative]]...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-pragmatist-teacher-role|The Pragmatist Teacher Role]]*

> [!definition] **Prediction Error** **[perm-note]**
> The discrepancy between the schema-generated predictions for a given context and the actual input received. Prediction errors serve as the update signal for schema modification: small distributed errors → accretion; consistent directional errors → tuning; catastrophic comprehensive failure → potential restructuring (if recognized). In the predictive processing reframing, prediction errors propagate upward through the processing hierarchy and drive updating at the level where the mismatch is localized.
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[prediction-error|Prediction Error]]*

> [!definition] **Principle of Truth** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that mental models represent only what is *true* in the described situation, not what is false. Initial models are "minimally" constructed: they contain only the tokens needed to satisfy the true conditions of the premises. This parsimony conserves working memory but produces systematic errors when correct reasoning requires explicit representation of false cases or consideration of multiple alternative models.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[principle-of-truth|Principle of Truth]]*

> [!definition] **Prior Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The totality of information, concepts, experiences, beliefs, and cognitive structures a learner brings to a new learning situation. Prior knowledge is not a simple inventory of facts but an organized architecture — a network of [[schema|schemas]], [[procedural-knowledge|procedures]], [[episodic-memory|episodic memories]], and [[mental-models|mental models]] — that actively shapes how new information is perceived, interpreted, encoded, and retrieved. Crucially, prior knowledge can be relevant or irrelevant, accurate or inaccurate, elaborated or sparse, and these qualities dramatically affect...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[prior-knowledge|Prior Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Prior Knowledge Moderation** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically well-established phenomenon whereby elaborative interrogation's effectiveness is conditioned on the learner possessing sufficient domain-relevant prior knowledge to generate accurate and richly-elaborated why-answers. In knowledge-impoverished conditions, EI may produce null effects or misconception formation rather than enhanced retention.
> 
> **Boundary:** Prior knowledge moderation is not merely a quantitative effect (more prior knowledge → larger EI benefit). It is a qualitative threshold effect: below a certain knowledge level, EI may actively harm learning through accurat...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[prior-knowledge-moderation|Prior Knowledge Moderation]]*

> [!definition] **Proactive and Retroactive Interference** **[perm-note]**
> Two forms of competitive memory disruption. *Proactive interference*: previously learned material impairs learning and retention of new, similar material (prior learning "interferes forward"). *Retroactive interference*: subsequently learned material impairs retention of previously learned material (new learning "interferes backward"). Both types operate primarily on declarative memories through competitive activation at retrieval — similar cues activate multiple competing traces, and retrieval of one can produce retrieval-induced forgetting of closely related competitors.
> 
> **Boundary:** In...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[proactive-and-retroactive-interference|Proactive and Retroactive Interference]]*

> [!definition] **The Problem of Induction** **[perm-note]**
> The epistemological challenge, formulated by David Hume and restated by Karl Popper, that no finite number of particular observations can logically guarantee a universal generalization. The inference from "all observed swans have been white" to "all swans are white" cannot be deductively valid — a single black swan suffices to refute it (as was discovered when Europeans reached Australia). The problem has no universally accepted solution; it motivates probabilistic, Bayesian, and pragmatic accounts of inductive reasoning that treat its conclusions as *probable* rather than *certain*.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-problem-of-induction|The Problem of Induction]]*

> [!definition] **Procedural Information** **[perm-note]**
> Step-by-step instructions, rules, and procedures that specify exactly how to perform the recurrent (consistent, routine) aspects of a complex skill. Presented precisely when needed during task execution (just-in-time), faded as the learner automates the procedure through repeated practice.
> 
> **Boundary:** Procedural information does not refer to all step-by-step content. It specifically addresses recurrent constituent skills — those that should be performed the same way regardless of context. Its defining temporal characteristic is just-in-time delivery, contrasting with the before-task-clas...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[procedural-information|Procedural Information]]*

> [!definition] **Procedural Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Procedural metacognitive knowledge — *knowing how* — is knowledge about the execution sequences, operational steps, and implementation details of cognitive strategies. Where declarative metacognitive knowledge concerns what a strategy is and what general effect it produces, procedural metacognitive knowledge concerns how to actually carry it out: the specific operations, their ordering, and their quality control checkpoints. A skilled reader's procedural metacognitive knowledge includes the specific operations involved in comprehension monitoring — the precise ways she pauses, signals confu...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[procedural-metacognitive-knowledge|Procedural Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Procedural Schema** **[perm-note]**
> A schema encoding action sequences, performance routines, and conditional rules — the knowing-how dimension of organized knowledge. Requires practice and feedback for acquisition; resists purely declarative instruction.
> *See also*: [[procedural-schemas]], [[declarative-schemas]], [[schema-automation]]
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[procedural-schema|Procedural Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Process Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A goal that specifies a *procedure*, *strategy*, or *quality of engagement* rather than a performance outcome or end product. Example: "I will generate three questions that I cannot yet answer from each Phase of this report, rather than reading for recall." Process goals focus attention on the *means* of learning rather than the metric of completion.
> 
> **Boundary:** Process goals are not activity goals ("I will read for 45 minutes") — they must specify a *learning strategy*, not merely a time allocation. Undifferentiated time-on-task is weakly correlated with learning outcomes.
> 
> **See also:*...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[process-goal|Process Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Process Goal vs. Outcome Goal** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** *Process goals* specify the strategies and behaviors to be implemented during learning. *Outcome goals* specify the end-state to be achieved. Both are useful but serve different functions: outcome goals provide direction; process goals protect self-efficacy during difficulty by providing evaluative criteria that remain under learner control.
> **Operational Indicators:** "I will pause at every section break and write a one-sentence summary" is a process goal. "I will understand this framework well enough to explain it" is an outcome goal. During early skill development with ch...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[process-goal-vs.-outcome-goal|Process Goal vs. Outcome Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Process Praise** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluative feedback that attributes performance outcomes to the learner's specific strategies, effort allocation, choices, or processes rather than to global, stable personal traits. Example: "The systematic approach you used in checking your reasoning paid off here." Distinguished from person praise, which attributes outcomes to fixed traits and has been shown to produce entity-theory orientation with associated motivational costs.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[process-praise|Process Praise]]*

> [!definition] **Processing Depth** **[perm-note]**
> The level of cognitive elaboration applied to an encoded item, ranging from shallow phonological-orthographic processing to deep semantic-elaborative processing. A core prediction of [[levels-of-processing-theory]] is that encoding durability is a continuous, monotone function of processing depth: deeper processing produces more durable, accessible memory traces.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Depth" in the levels-of-processing framework refers to semantic elaboration, not temporal duration of processing. Spending more time at a shallow level does not produce deep encoding.
> 
> **See also:** [[Levels-of-Proc...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[processing-depth|Processing Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Processing Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>Processing fluency</span> is the subjective experience of ease or difficulty associated with cognitive operations. In the metacognitive monitoring context, it serves as a heuristic cue: operations that feel fluent (easy) generate higher confidence and higher [[judgment-of-learning|JOLs]], while operations that feel disfluent (difficult) generate lower confidence. The metacognitive system treats fluency as a signal of learning strength, following the implicit logic: "If processing is easy, I must know this well."
> 
> [**Processing-Fluency**:: The subjective experie...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-monitoring-accuracy-calibration-deep-dive-2026-04-12* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[processing-fluency|Processing Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Cognitive Challenge** **[perm-note]**
> A proposed third category in the reconstructed CLT, distinct from both extraneous load (design-imposed and detrimental) and managed intrinsic load (calibrated content complexity). Productive cognitive challenge is design-introduced difficulty that deliberately maintains elevated processing demands for the purpose of strengthening long-term encoding and transfer. Its diagnostic criteria are: (a) it engages retrieval, generative, or comparative processing rather than processing irrelevant to the learning goal; (b) it is calibrated to current learner expertise — above the zone of automated per...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[productive-cognitive-challenge|Productive Cognitive Challenge]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Failure** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: An instructional design approach in which learners attempt to solve problems *before* receiving formal instruction, thereby experiencing schema inadequacy prior to receiving restructuring resources. Empirically demonstrated to produce deeper conceptual understanding than instruction-first approaches for restructuring goals, despite (because of) initial failure. NOT appropriate for all learning goals — primarily effective when restructuring rather than accretion is the target.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Provides empirical grounding for the schema-disruption-before-reso...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[productive-failure|Productive Failure]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Friction** **[perm-note]**
> The author's term for the family of cognitive resistances — articulation constraints, retrieval demands, naive audience requirements, causal questioning — that activate generative processing mechanisms when present in a writing task. The active ingredient that distinguishes knowledge-transforming from knowledge-telling writing.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[productive-friction|Productive Friction]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Struggle** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitively effortful state of grappling with genuinely challenging material without immediate access to a solution pathway. Distinguished from mere frustration (which produces withdrawal) by the learner's continued engagement and cognitive effort. Growth-mindset environments deliberately create and protect space for productive struggle, treating it as the primary site of learning rather than a failure state to be quickly resolved.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[productive-struggle|Productive Struggle]]*

> [!definition] **Progressive Approximation** **[perm-note]**
> The fallibilist model of epistemic progress as an asymptotic approach toward truth — each stage of inquiry producing theories that are more accurate, more comprehensive, and better supported than their predecessors, while remaining imperfect and in principle revisable. Associated with Nicholas Rescher's philosophy of inquiry. Preserves the objectivity and meaningfulness of knowledge claims while honouring the permanent possibility of revision.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[progressive-approximation|Progressive Approximation]]*

> [!definition] **Progressive Disclosure** **[perm-note]**
> The instructional principle of managing complexity by revealing information progressively, calibrated to the learner's current state. In metacognitive scaffolding design, progressive disclosure applies to both the complexity of the metacognitive tasks required (from simple monitoring checkpoints to richer regulatory protocols) and to the fading of scaffolding support (from high support to low support as competence develops).
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[progressive-disclosure|Progressive Disclosure]]*

> [!definition] **Propositional Representation** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive representation in a language-like format: discrete, compositional, amodal (not tied to any sensory modality), and rule-governed. Operations on propositional representations are syntactic — they apply to the form of the representation regardless of its content. The mental logic tradition (Rips' PSYCOP, Braine's natural deduction) posits that deductive reasoning operates by applying inference rules to propositional representations.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[propositional-representation|Propositional Representation]]*

> [!definition] **Propositional Text Base** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The network of semantic propositions explicitly stated by a text, representing what the text *says* at the level of sentence meanings and their immediate connections. Boundary condition: the text base is NOT the situation model; the two are distinct representational levels that are empirically dissociable.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The level of representation that the received view mistakenly treats as equivalent to full comprehension; the level of representation that the Text-Base Trap produces as an outcome masquerading as genuine comprehension.
> 
> **Operational Ind...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[propositional-text-base|Propositional Text Base]]*

> [!definition] **Propositional-Representation|Propositional Text Base** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The propositional text base is the explicit semantic content of a text — a network of propositions (subject-predicate structures) corresponding to the meanings stated by individual sentences and their immediate connections. It represents what the text explicitly says, minus the words themselves. Processing the text base is necessary for situation model construction but not sufficient for it: a reader can represent the propositional content of a passage while failing to construct a coherent situation model if they lack the prior knowledge or generate insufficient inferences t...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[propositional-representationpropositional-text-base|Propositional-Representation|Propositional Text Base]]*

> [!definition] **Prosody** **[perm-note]**
> The suprasegmental features of spoken language — including pitch variation, stress, rhythm, tempo, and phrasing — applied to oral reading. Prosodic oral reading requires simultaneous processing of syntactic structure and semantic content during production, making appropriate prosody both a marker of comprehension and potentially a mechanism that scaffolds syntactic parsing. Prosodic fluency assessment goes beyond rate and accuracy to capture the quality dimension of fluent reading.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[prosody]]*

> [!definition] **Protostar** **[perm-note]**
> A young stellar object in the process of accumulating mass through gravitational infall and disk accretion, but which has not yet achieved the central temperatures required for sustained hydrogen fusion. Protostars are typically embedded within dense envelopes of gas and dust, rendering them invisible at optical wavelengths but detectable at infrared and sub-millimetre wavelengths. The protostellar phase lasts roughly 100,000 to several million years, depending on the mass of the forming star.
> 
> **Boundary:** A protostar is distinguished from a pre-stellar core (which has not yet formed a ce...
> 
> *Source: how-stars-are-created-in-the-universe-foundational-report-2026-04-17* | *Domain: astrophysics*
> *Permanent Note: [[protostar]]*

> [!definition] **Proximal vs. Distal Goals** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** Proximal goals are achievable within a single session; distal goals are achievable across extended time periods. Research shows that proximal goals are more motivationally effective during skill development because they generate more frequent mastery experiences and provide more timely feedback on progress.
> **Operational Indicators:** A proximal goal specifies what will be achieved *today* — "I will be able to explain this section's central distinction in my own words by the time I close the text." A distal goal specifies a more distant state — "I will deeply understand meta...
> 
> *Source: srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[proximal-vs.-distal-goals|Proximal vs. Distal Goals]]*

> [!definition] **Pseudoexpertise** **[perm-note]**
> A condition in which a practitioner has accumulated extensive domain experience and developed the subjective phenomenology of expertise (confidence, fluency, the feeling of knowing what to do) without having built the underlying chunking architecture that would justify the confidence, typically because the domain provides degraded feedback that fails to correct mistaken chunks.
> 
> **Boundary:** Pseudoexpertise is not a personal failure of the practitioner but a structural consequence of practicing in domains where feedback is delayed, ambiguous, or absent. It is distinguishable from genuine e...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[pseudoexpertise]]*

> [!definition] **Pseudometacognition** **[perm-note]**
> The production of metacognitive-sounding language or behavior without genuine metacognitive functioning — accurate self-monitoring or effective self-regulation. Pseudometacognition can be inadvertently cultivated by explicit metacognitive requirements that reward the performance of metacognition rather than its substance, or when learners learn to produce appropriate responses to metacognitive prompts without genuine cognitive engagement.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[pseudometacognition]]*

> [!definition] **Psychological Disorder** **[perm-note]**
> The *Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders* (Fifth Edition), the dominant American diagnostic system, defines a mental disorder as a syndrome characterized by clinically significant disturbance in cognition, emotion regulation, or behavior that reflects a dysfunction in psychological, biological, or developmental processes underlying mental functioning. The syndrome must typically cause significant distress or disability and must not be primarily a culturally sanctioned response to a common life stressor or an expression of political, religious, or sexual minority status. Th...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[psychological-disorder|Psychological Disorder]]*

> [!definition] **Psychological Need** **[perm-note]**
> A psychological requirement whose satisfaction is necessary (not merely desirable) for optimal psychological functioning, growth, and well-being. Distinguished from *wants* (preferences that vary across persons) and *drives* (biological push states that reduce tension). In BPNT, psychological needs are characterized by their *nutriment* quality: satisfaction generates growth, while frustration generates defensive adaptation. **Boundary condition:** The necessity claim requires that there be no circumstances under which thriving is achieved in the chronic *absence* of need satisfaction — a c...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[psychological-need|Psychological Need]]*

> [!definition] **Psychological Safety** **[perm-note]**
> The shared belief within a group that interpersonal risk-taking — speaking up, admitting uncertainty, challenging decisions, sharing bad news — is safe and will not result in punitive consequences. The most important organizational variable predicting honest, complete information sharing in group contexts. Built or destroyed through accumulated communicative choices by group members, particularly leaders. See [[psychological-safety]].
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[psychological-safety|Psychological Safety]]*

> [!definition] **Psychopathology** **[perm-note]**
> The scientific study of mental disorder — its nature, causes, development, and course. A psychological phenomenon is typically classified as a disorder when it involves (1) significant distress in the person experiencing it, (2) functional impairment in social, occupational, or other major areas of life, (3) deviation from cultural norms in ways that are not merely non-conformist or politically dissident, and (4) patterns that are not better explained by substance effects or another medical condition. Note the inherent boundary conditions: psychopathology is not synonymous with deviance, ec...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[psychopathology]]*

> [!definition] **Psychotherapy** **[perm-note]**
> A structured, theoretically grounded set of psychological interventions, delivered by a trained professional in a defined relationship with a client, aimed at alleviating psychological distress, modifying problematic behavior patterns, and/or promoting personal growth and flourishing. Psychotherapy is distinguished from informal helping by its explicit theoretical basis, systematic procedures, structured setting, and trained practitioner. Critically, all credible forms of psychotherapy share certain universal features — a healing context, a rationale or explanatory framework, a set of proce...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[psychotherapy]]*

> [!definition] **Python Package** **[perm-note]**
> A Python package is a directory that contains a special file called `__init__.py` (which can be empty) and one or more Python module files. The `__init__.py` file signals to Python that this directory should be treated as a package — a namespace that can be imported from. A directory structure like `my_project/utils/__init__.py` plus `my_project/utils/file_ops.py` allows one to write `from utils.file_ops import read_csv` in a script at the `my_project` level. The `__init__.py` can also contain code that runs when the package is imported, or it can re-export names from submodules to simplify...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-practitioners-field-guide-2026-04-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[python-package|Python Package]]*

## _Q

> [!definition] **Equilibration** **[perm-note]**
> Equilibration is Piaget's proposed self-regulatory mechanism by which organisms move from states of cognitive disequilibrium (produced when assimilation fails) toward new states of cognitive equilibrium (achieved through successful accommodation). It is not a passive return to the prior state but an active, self-organizing process that produces structures at a higher level of complexity than those preceding the disequilibrium episode.
> 
> **Boundary:** Equilibration is a theoretical construct, not an observed process — it infers a functional regulatory mechanism from patterns of cognitive deve...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[equilibration]]*

## _R

> [!definition] **Trace Distinctiveness** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a memory trace is uniquely differentiated from other traces in memory — possessing specific, individuating properties that allow retrieval cues to selectively and unambiguously activate it. Distinctiveness is achieved through unusual or unique content, rich and specific contextual embedding, or elaboration that connects the to-be-remembered item to personal, unique associations rather than shared semantic features. Highly distinctive traces are more resistant to interference from similar items and more reliably accessible under variety of retrieval cues.
> 
> **Boundary:** D...
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[trace-distinctiveness|Trace Distinctiveness]]*

> [!definition] **Traceback** **[perm-note]**
> A traceback (also called a stack trace) is the diagnostic output Python produces when an unhandled exception occurs during script execution. It displays the call stack — the sequence of function invocations that were active at the moment of the error — along with the filename, line number, and code content at each level of the stack. The final line of the traceback names the exception type (such as `TypeError`, `ValueError`, `FileNotFoundError`, or `IndentationError`) and provides a human-readable description of the specific problem. The traceback is the interpreter's account of what it was...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-with-copilot-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: software engineering*
> *Permanent Note: [[traceback]]*

> [!definition] **Radical Constructivism** **[perm-note]**
> **Radical constructivism** (Ernst von Glasersfeld; also associated with Heinz von Foerster and cybernetics) holds that cognition is not a process of representing an independently existing reality but of constructing experiential reality in ways that are adaptive — that "fit" the organism's interactions with its environment. There is no mind-independent world to be correctly represented; there are only cognitive constructions that are more or less viable. This is a thoroughgoing anti-representationalist epistemology that challenges both naive realism and correspondence theories of truth in c...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[radical-constructivism|Radical Constructivism]]*

> [!definition] **Transactional Communication Model** **[perm-note]**
> A model of communication as a dynamic, simultaneous process in which both parties are simultaneously senders and receivers, each continuously interpreting and adjusting to the other's responses. Supersedes the linear [[Shannon-Weaver-Information-Theory|transmission model]] for understanding human communication by foregrounding the co-constructive, bidirectional, and relationship-embedded nature of communicative exchange.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[transactional-communication-model|Transactional Communication Model]]*

> [!definition] **Transdiagnostic Approach** **[perm-note]**
> A treatment framework that targets psychological processes shared across multiple diagnostic categories rather than developing separate, disorder-specific protocols for each DSM diagnosis. The transdiagnostic approach is motivated by (1) high rates of comorbidity in clinical practice that render single-disorder protocols unwieldy, (2) evidence for common mechanisms (such as emotion dysregulation, avoidance, and attentional biases) underlying ostensibly distinct disorders, and (3) the practical inefficiency of training clinicians in dozens of separate treatment manuals. Prominent transdiagno...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[transdiagnostic-approach|Transdiagnostic Approach]]*

> [!definition] **Transfer** **[perm-note]**
> The application of knowledge, skills, or strategies learned in one context to a different context. Near transfer involves application to closely similar situations; far transfer involves application across significantly different domains or contexts. Research on critical thinking transfer indicates that it is not automatic and requires deliberate instructional support — including explicit attention to the general principles underlying domain-specific instances of reasoning.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[transfer]]*

> [!definition] **Transfer of Learning** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The application of knowledge or skills acquired in one context to a new, different context. Distinguished as *near transfer* (application to highly similar contexts), *far transfer* (application to structurally similar but superficially different contexts), and *far-far* or *analogical* transfer (application of abstract structural principles to distant domains). Research consistently shows that [[far-transfer]] requires explicit encoding of the abstract principle, not just repeated practice in the original domain.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The FAR Transfer architect...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[transfer-of-learning|Transfer of Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Transfer-Appropriate Processing** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that memory is most accessible in contexts that match the conditions of encoding. What can be retrieved depends on how it was encoded. A writing technique that encodes knowledge in a specific form will produce retrieval most reliably in contexts that match that encoding form.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[transfer-appropriate-processing|Transfer-Appropriate Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Transient Hypofrontality Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> [**Transient-Hypofrontality**:: The hypothesis that during exercise, metabolic resources are preferentially allocated to motor and sensory cortical regions at the expense of the [[prefrontal-cortex]], producing a temporary reduction in prefrontal function. This "transient hypofrontality" is proposed as the mechanism for exercise-induced changes in consciousness (runner's high, flow), reduced self-awareness, and impaired executive function during exercise. Proposed by Arne Dietrich (2003).]
> 
> *Source: walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[transient-hypofrontality-hypothesis|Transient Hypofrontality Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Translation Window** **[perm-note]**
> The region of conditions within which the activities that produce goal achievement coincide with the activities one actually wants to be reinforced; defined operationally by the four conditions of proxy fidelity, honest attainability, measurement adequacy, and failure tolerance.
> 
> **Boundary:** Width is a property of the joint goal-measurement-reward-ability-strategy system rather than of the goal alone; engineering practices that preserve the window are precisely those the dark-side literature recommends.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Provides a unified diagnostic for distinguishing ap...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-theory-locke-latham-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[translation-window|Translation Window]]*

> [!definition] **Therapeutic Alliance** **[perm-note]**
> The collaborative and affective quality of the relationship between therapist and client, typically conceptualized as comprising three interdependent components: (1) agreement on the goals of treatment, (2) agreement on the tasks or activities through which those goals will be pursued, and (3) the quality of the relational bond between therapist and client. The alliance is the most consistently replicated predictor of therapeutic outcome across all modalities and therapeutic orientations. It is not simply a prerequisite for "real" therapy but is itself a primary mechanism of change. Disting...
> 
> *Source: clinical-psychology-foundational-report-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[therapeutic-alliance|Therapeutic Alliance]]*

> [!definition] **Reaction and Reflection as Cyclic Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory phase that converts the outcomes of a just-completed episode into inputs for subsequent episodes through cognitive evaluation of strategies, motivational attribution and updating of efficacy and value beliefs, behavioral revision of effort and time intentions, and contextual updating of environmental beliefs. Its quality determines whether the regulatory cycle produces cumulative improvement across episodes or merely repeats the same regulatory patterns regardless of their effectiveness.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reaction and reflection is not the same as rumination, which is the unprod...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[reaction-and-reflection-as-cyclic-coupling|Reaction and Reflection as Cyclic Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Reaction and Reflection Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory phase following performance in which the learner evaluates strategies, forms causal attributions for outcomes, updates efficacy and value beliefs, revises behavioral intentions, and updates contextual beliefs in ways that condition the next episode's forethought.
> 
> **Boundary:** Distinct from rumination, which lacks structure, content specificity, and terminating outputs; the framework treats reflection as a structured regulatory activity, not as undifferentiated post-task thinking.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The phase whose cyclic-coupling function makes the framework...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-self-regulation-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: educational psychology / self-regulated learning*
> *Permanent Note: [[reaction-and-reflection-phase|Reaction and Reflection Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Reading Comprehension** **[perm-note]**
> Reading comprehension is the process of constructing a coherent mental representation of a text's meaning that goes beyond the literal linguistic content. It requires the integration of information extracted from the text with the reader's existing knowledge, motivational resources, and metacognitive capacities. Comprehension is not a passive reception of meaning but an active, constructive process in which the reader continuously builds, updates, and revises a multi-level internal model of what the text is about.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reading comprehension is NOT equivalent to word recognition, a...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[reading-comprehension|Reading Comprehension]]*

> [!definition] **Reading Engagement** **[perm-note]**
> Reading engagement is a term used in the reading motivation literature to refer to the integration of cognitive, motivational, and behavioral dimensions of reading — specifically, the combination of sustained comprehension strategy use, intrinsic motivation for reading, and broad and frequent reading behavior. Engaged readers read more, read across more domains, apply more strategies, and demonstrate steeper reading achievement growth.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reading engagement is not equivalent to reading enjoyment; it encompasses strategic processing and persistence under difficulty, not merely po...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reading-engagement|Reading Engagement]]*

> [!definition] **Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> Reading fluency is the ability to read connected text with accuracy, appropriate rate, and prosody — with sufficient ease that comprehension resources are available to focus on meaning-making rather than word identification. Fluency is not merely reading speed; it integrates accuracy (getting words right), automaticity (retrieving them without deliberate effort), and prosody (reading with appropriate expression, phrasing, and intonation that reflects comprehension of grammatical and semantic structure).
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reading-fluency|Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **Receptive Vocabulary** **[perm-note]**
> Word knowledge sufficient for recognition and meaning-extraction in reading or listening contexts, without necessarily enabling production. A reader has receptive knowledge of a word when they can accurately assign meaning to it in context, even if they could not retrieve the word spontaneously or deploy it in writing.
> 
> **Boundary:** Receptive knowledge can be highly context-dependent — a reader may understand a word in one context but fail to recognize it in another. True receptive mastery implies cross-contextual recognition stability.
> 
> **See also:** [[reading-fluency|Reading Fluency]], [...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[receptive-vocabulary|Receptive Vocabulary]]*

> [!definition] **Receptive vs. Productive Vocabulary Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A fundamental distinction in vocabulary knowledge. *Receptive* (or passive) vocabulary comprises words a reader can recognise and approximately understand when encountered in context; *productive* (or active) vocabulary comprises words a speaker/writer can accurately deploy in their own output. For adult proficient readers, productive vocabulary is typically 50–70% of receptive vocabulary in size (and substantially narrower in depth). The gap exists because contextual reading provides reception-building encounters without production-building practice.
> 
> **Report-Specific Sign...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[receptive-vs.-productive-vocabulary-knowledge|Receptive vs. Productive Vocabulary Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Reciprocal Teaching** **[perm-note]**
> A structured instructional procedure in which teacher and students take turns leading text-based discussions using four metacognitive strategies (predicting, questioning, clarifying, summarizing) in a scaffolded collaborative format. Reciprocal teaching externalizes the internal metacognitive reading dialogue and makes it teachable and visible; it is the most empirically supported intervention for building comprehension monitoring skills in struggling comprehenders.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reciprocal teaching is not merely a discussion protocol; its effectiveness is entirely contingent on authentic ...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[reciprocal-teaching|Reciprocal Teaching]]*

> [!definition] **Recognition-Primed Decision Making** **[perm-note]**
> A model of expert decision making in naturalistic settings, proposing that experienced decision makers typically do not compare options systematically but instead assess the situation, recognize it as an instance of a familiar type, and thereby generate a workable course of action that can be mentally simulated and, if necessary, adapted. The model depends on the expert's library of domain-specific chunks, each encoding situation types along with their associated goals, cues, expectations, and appropriate responses.
> 
> **Boundary:** RPD describes how experts make decisions in domains with suf...
> 
> *Source: chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[recognition-primed-decision-making|Recognition-Primed Decision Making]]*

> [!definition] **Reconsolidation** **[perm-note]**
> Reconsolidation is the process by which a previously consolidated memory, upon being reactivated through retrieval, re-enters a labile state and requires renewed protein synthesis-dependent stabilisation to be maintained. Discovered experimentally by [[Karim-Nader|Karim Nader]], [[Glenn-Schafe|Glenn Schafe]], and [[Joseph-LeDoux|Joseph LeDoux]] in 2000, reconsolidation challenges the traditional view that consolidation is a one-time event, suggesting instead that memories are dynamically updated each time they are recalled. This has implications for both the therapeutic modification of mala...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reconsolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Reconstructive Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that memory retrieval is not a playback of stored records but an active process of reconstruction in which a plausible account of the past is assembled from stored fragments, schematic expectations, and contextual influences. Reconstruction enables inference and generalization but introduces systematic vulnerability to distortion and confabulation.
> 
> **Boundary:** Reconstructive memory does not mean that all memories are fabrications. Many reconstructed memories are substantially accurate. The point is that accuracy is not guaranteed by the subjective sense of remembering and t...
> 
> *Source: long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reconstructive-memory|Reconstructive Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Recurrent Constituent Skill** **[perm-note]**
> A constituent skill component of a complex cognitive skill whose execution remains consistent across problem situations. Recurrent skills are performed the same way regardless of context. They are governed by automated rules and procedures, and their performance improves through rule automation (repeated practice under consistent conditions) rather than through conceptual elaboration.
> 
> **Boundary:** Recurrence does not imply simplicity. A recurrent skill may be a complex procedure with many steps — its defining characteristic is consistency across contexts, not low cognitive demand.
> 
> **Repo...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[recurrent-constituent-skill|Recurrent Constituent Skill]]*

> [!definition] **Recurrent vs. Non-Recurrent Constituent Skills** **[perm-note]**
> *Recurrent constituent skills* are aspects of complex performance that are executed in the same way across different problem situations — they follow consistent rules or procedures regardless of the specific task context. Examples: taking vital signs in medicine, formatting citations in academic writing, conducting a safety check in aviation.
> 
> *Non-recurrent constituent skills* are aspects of complex performance that require adaptation to the specific features of each problem situation — they involve reasoning, problem-solving, and decision-making that varies with context. Examples: differe...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[recurrent-vs.-non-recurrent-constituent-skills|Recurrent vs. Non-Recurrent Constituent Skills]]*

> [!definition] **Recursive Self-Governance** **[perm-note]**
> Recursive self-governance refers to the capacity of a cognitive system to monitor, evaluate, and modify not merely its object-level performance but its own performance-monitoring-and-modification processes — a second-order metacognitive capacity. Metacognitive development understood as recursive self-governance is not limited to improving first-order monitoring but involves progressively building higher-order capacities for managing one's own cognitive self-management system.
> 
> **See also:** [[metacognition]], [[self-regulated-learning]], [[Metacognitive-Wisdom]]
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[recursive-self-governance|Recursive Self-Governance]]*

> [!definition] **Redundancy Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that adding information that duplicates already-available or already-known information increases cognitive load without aiding learning, and can actively impair learning by requiring integration of redundant elements. For expert learners, detailed worked examples can produce the redundancy effect.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[redundancy-effect|Redundancy Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Three Basic Psychological Needs** **[perm-note]**
> - **[[autonomy-need|Autonomy]]:** The need to experience one's behavior as self-endorsed, as an authentic expression of one's own values and interests, as flowing from oneself rather than being imposed from outside. Autonomy in SDT is **not** independence or self-sufficiency; it is volition, the felt sense that "this is what I want to be doing."
> - **[[competence-need|Competence]]:** The need to experience oneself as effective in interaction with the environment, capable of producing desired outcomes, growing in capability over time. Competence is satisfied not by ease but by the right level...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-internalization-continuum-deep-dive-2026-04-21* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-three-basic-psychological-needs|The Three Basic Psychological Needs]]*

> [!definition] **The Three-Dimensional Causal Taxonomy** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The classification of achievement causes along three independent dimensions — locus (internal/external), stability (stable/unstable), and controllability (controllable/uncontrollable) — that together determine the emotional and motivational consequences of causal attributions. The taxonomy replaces the simpler internal/external distinction with a structure sufficient to generate distinct predictions about pride, shame, guilt, anger, pity, hope, and hopelessness.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This taxonomy is the generative architecture of the theory — not a descriptive ...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-three-dimensional-causal-taxonomy|The Three-Dimensional Causal Taxonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Three-Tier Monitoring Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> An integrative model of comprehension monitoring distinguishing Tier 1 (lexical-syntactic monitoring), Tier 2 (propositional coherence monitoring), and Tier 3 (situational coherence monitoring). Each tier has distinct failure modes and remediation strategies. The central claim is that most consequential comprehension failures occur at Tier 3, while most strategy instruction targets Tier 1.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[three-tier-monitoring-architecture|Three-Tier Monitoring Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Reflection-on-Action vs. Reflection-in-Action** **[perm-note]**
> Schön distinguishes two temporally distinct forms of professional reflection. *Reflection-in-action* is metacognitive reflection that occurs *during* practice — the experienced professional notices an unexpected result, reframes the situation, and generates an on-the-spot experiment within the practice episode itself, often without full conscious articulation. *Reflection-on-action* is retrospective metacognitive reflection that occurs after a practice episode, through which tacit knowing-in-action is rendered explicit, patterns are identified across cases, and professional theories are rev...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology / educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reflection-on-action-vs.-reflection-in-action|Reflection-on-Action vs. Reflection-in-Action]]*

> [!definition] **Reflective Disposition** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive trait that determines how readily a person engages Type 2 monitoring of Type 1 outputs. Reflective disposition is partially independent of cognitive capacity (which Stanovich calls the "algorithmic mind") and is at least partially trainable through practices that lower the threshold for engaging metacognitive scrutiny. The dissociation between cognitive capacity and reflective disposition is what explains why high-IQ individuals can show characteristic biases.
> 
> **Boundary:** Not a fixed trait; varies across domains and contexts within an individual, and varies across indiv...
> 
> *Source: dual-process-theory-kahneman-system-1-system-2-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[reflective-disposition|Reflective Disposition]]*

> [!definition] **Reflective Judgment** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to reason well about ill-structured problems — problems in which evidence is incomplete, experts disagree, and certainty is not achievable. Reflective judgment develops through seven stages from pre-reflective (assuming knowledge is certain) to genuinely reflective (constructing well-reasoned positions in conditions of acknowledged uncertainty). A developmental prerequisite for mature critical thinking.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[reflective-judgment|Reflective Judgment]]*

> [!definition] **Reflective Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> [[Dewey's-Reflective-Thinking|Reflective thinking]] is defined by [[john-dewey]] as "active, persistent, and careful consideration of any belief or supposed form of knowledge in the light of the grounds that support it, and the further conclusions to which it tends" (*[[How-We-Think]]*, 1910, p. 6). This definition establishes reflective thinking as an active (not passive), sustained (not momentary), careful (not casual), and evidence-sensitive (not dogmatic) cognitive process.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[reflective-thinking|Reflective Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Region of Proximal Learning** **[perm-note]**
> The Region of Proximal Learning (RPL), developed from Nelson and Narens' work and extended by Metcalfe and Kornell, is the range of material difficulty for which self-paced study allocation is most effective: material that is partially learned — beyond trivial fluency but not so difficult as to be currently inaccessible. Motivated, efficient learners allocate study time preferentially to the RPL; less efficient or more affectively avoidant learners tend to over-study already-mastered material (generating fluency-based positive affect) and under-study RPL material that requires effortful pro...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[region-of-proximal-learning|Region of Proximal Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Register** **[perm-note]**
> A classification of the answer level required by an EI question. Register 1 questions can be answered from the textbase alone; Register 2 questions require situation model construction via prior knowledge integration. The register of a question is determined by the demand structure — what level of representation must be engaged to produce an adequate answer.
> 
> **See also:** [[Dual Register Diagnostic — original construct]], [[textbase]], [[situation-model]], [[elaborative-interrogation]]
> 
> *Attribution: this report's framework*
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[register]]*

> [!definition] **Regulation of Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> The ensemble of executive processes through which a learner actively manages her cognitive engagement — planning how to approach a task, monitoring the adequacy of ongoing processing, evaluating the outcomes of cognitive activity, and adjusting strategies when monitoring signals inadequacy. Regulation of cognition operates in real time, during cognitive engagement, and is responsive to the phenomenological signals of metacognitive experience and the outputs of metacognitive monitoring.
> 
> **Boundary:** Regulation of cognition is distinguished from automatic executive functions (such as attent...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulation-of-cognition|Regulation of Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Areas** **[perm-note]**
> Alongside the temporal phase dimension, Pintrich specifies four substantively distinct *areas of regulatory activity* that operate in parallel throughout the SRL cycle. *Cognitive regulation* targets the selection, deployment, and adjustment of cognitive learning strategies — elaboration, organization, rehearsal, critical thinking. *Motivation and affect regulation* targets the learner's goal orientation, self-efficacy beliefs, task value perceptions, and affective states — managing the "why" and "how it feels" of learning. *Behavioral regulation* targets effort allocation, time management,...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-model-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-areas|Regulatory Areas]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's model of motivation type ranging from amotivation through external, introjected, identified, and integrated regulation to intrinsic motivation. Each point represents a different degree of internalization and autonomy: external and introjected regulation are forms of controlled motivation; identified and integrated regulation are forms of autonomous motivation; intrinsic motivation involves inherent interest. The continuum is not a strict developmental sequence — people may operate at any point depending on the activity and context. **See also**: [[SDT's-Regulatory-Continuum]], [[Inter...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-continuum|Regulatory Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Language Spectrum** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The vocabulary of self-direction exists on a spectrum corresponding to the [[internalization-continuum]] in [[self-determination-theory]]: from *introjected regulatory language* ("I have to understand this or I'll feel like a failure") to *identified regulatory language* ("I want to understand this because it matters to my goals") to *integrated regulatory language* ("understanding this is part of what I do as a learner"). The locus and quality of causality encoded in the vocabulary signals the regulatory style of the inner speech.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This pro...
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-vocabulary-motivation-self-efficacy-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-language-spectrum|Regulatory Language Spectrum]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Mode** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A fundamental dimension of motivated behavior distinguishing whether behavioral regulation is oriented toward achieving a desired positive outcome (approach) or toward preventing an undesired negative outcome (avoidance). NOT equivalent to the distinction between positive and negative affect — both approach and avoidance can involve positive or negative affect; the distinction is regulatory, not affective.
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the primary analytic construct in this report. The regulatory mode primacy thesis claims that this dimension is more theoreticall...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-mode|Regulatory Mode]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Overhead** **[perm-note]**
> Original analytical concept developed in this report. The additional cognitive-affective burden imposed by controlled motivation (specifically introjected regulation) — the self-surveillance, performance anxiety, and contingent self-esteem management that compete with cognitive resources available for task engagement. Explains why autonomous motivation predicts not just greater effort but qualitatively deeper and more creative processing.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-overhead|Regulatory Overhead]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Phases** **[perm-note]**
> Pintrich organizes self-regulatory activity into four broad phases that describe the temporal unfolding of a learning episode: (1) *Forethought, Planning, and Activation*, the anticipatory phase in which goals are set, prior knowledge activated, and strategies planned before task engagement; (2) *Self-Monitoring*, the metacognitive surveillance of one's own cognitive, motivational, behavioral, and contextual states during task execution; (3) *Self-Control and Regulation*, the deployment of specific regulatory interventions in response to monitoring signals; and (4) *Self-Reflection and Eval...
> 
> *Source: pintrich-model-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-phases|Regulatory Phases]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Poverty** **[perm-note]**
> A state in which a learner's strategic repertoire is insufficiently developed to provide adequate responses to monitoring signals. Even accurate monitoring coupled with threshold-crossing salience produces only generic, minimally effective regulatory responses when the repertoire is thin. Distinguished from monitoring failure and threshold insensitivity as a third independent source of coupling breakdown.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-poverty|Regulatory Poverty]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Quality** **[perm-note]**
> A dimension of motivation describing the degree to which a regulatory behavior is experienced as autonomous (originating from the self, aligned with values and interests) versus controlled (experienced as compelled by external forces or internal pressure). Regulatory quality exists on a continuum from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration to intrinsic motivation, corresponding to progressively deeper internalization of motivational reasons. High regulatory quality is associated with sustained engagement, deep processing, and positive affect under difficul...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-quality|Regulatory Quality]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Quality in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Regulatory quality refers to the degree to which a reader's engagement with a text is governed by autonomous reasons (finding the text genuinely interesting, valuing the knowledge for personal growth, experiencing deep immersion) versus controlled reasons (reading to avoid negative consequences, to demonstrate compliance, or to satisfy external pressure). Autonomous regulation and controlled regulation exist on a continuum in SDT's [[organismic-integration-theory]], ranging from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration toward intrinsic motivation.
> 
> **Boundar...
> 
> *Source: motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-quality-in-reading|Regulatory Quality in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Reflexivity Problem** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The theoretical challenge created by Pintrich's inclusion of motivation as a regulated domain: if motivation is regulated by a regulatory executive, that executive must itself have some motivational basis — what motivates the motivation-regulator? The problem resolves not by positing a fifth unregulated level but by recognizing that regulatory activity is bootstrapped from dispositional motivational tendencies (trait self-efficacy, general mastery orientation) that are products of developmental history. See Phase VI Synthesis.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the r...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-reflexivity-problem|Regulatory Reflexivity Problem]]*

> [!definition] **The Regulatory Reflexivity Problem** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory reflexivity problem designates the structural challenge that metacognitive regulation, being itself a cognitive process, is subject to the same monitoring and control demands as any other cognitive process — generating an apparent regress in which the regulation of regulation would require further regulation, ad infinitum. The problem is not merely philosophical: it has practical manifestations in the difficulty of evaluating one's own regulatory effectiveness (evaluating evaluation), and in the tendency for regulatory processes to become themselves objects of meta-regulatory...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-regulatory-reflexivity-problem|The Regulatory Reflexivity Problem]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Self-Talk** **[perm-note]**
> Inner speech that carries the regulatory register — the "why am I doing this" and "how am I directing this effort" layer of motivational inner speech. SDT's regulatory language research establishes that controlled regulatory vocabulary (should/must/have to) produces a functionally different motivational state than autonomous regulatory vocabulary (want/choose/find meaningful), even when describing identical behavior.
> 
> *Source: inner-speech-attribution-self-efficacy-focused-analysis-2026-03-25* | *Domain: motivation-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[regulatory-self-talk|Regulatory Self-Talk]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Self-Talk Register** **[perm-note]**
