Encompasses four areas (cognitive, motivational/affective, behavioral, contextual) and four phases (forethought, monitoring, control, reflection).
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[self-regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Serving Attribution Bias** **[perm-note]**
> The **self-serving attribution bias** is the motivationally driven tendency to attribute success to internal, personal factors (I am skilled, I worked hard, I had a good plan) and failure to external, situational factors (the task was unfair, I had bad luck, circumstances conspired against me). This asymmetry in blame assignment vs. credit-taking serves self-enhancing and self-protective functions: it maximizes pride from success while minimizing shame from failure. The bias is most pronounced when both self-concept and social presentation are at stake, and it can be reduced or even reverse...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[self-serving-attribution-bias|Self-Serving Attribution Bias]]*

> [!definition] **Semantic Density** **[perm-note]**
> In PKM architecture, the degree to which notes encode not only information but the relationships, implications, distinctions, and contexts that make information cognitively useful without additional working memory reconstruction. A semantically dense note allows immediate resumption of thinking from where it left off; a semantically sparse note (e.g., a bare quote or title) requires the practitioner to reconstruct relational structure internally before the note can function as a cognitive resource.
> 
> **Boundary:** Semantic density is not equivalent to length or complexity. A short, precisely...
> 
> *Source: pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[semantic-density|Semantic Density]]*

> [!definition] **Semantic Memory** **[perm-note]**
> [[semantic-memory]] is the system storing general knowledge about the world — facts, concepts, vocabulary, rules, and abstract knowledge — that is linguistically organized and largely devoid of personal temporal context. It is noetic — it involves knowing about the world without the subjective experience of remembering a specific time when this knowledge was acquired. Semantic memory develops across the lifespan and reaches its peak around middle age; it is more resistant to amnesia than episodic memory and can be preserved even when episodic memory is severely impaired.
> 
> **Boundary:** Sema...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[semantic-memory|Semantic Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Sensory Memory** **[perm-note]**
> Sensory memory refers to the brief, modality-specific retention of sensory information (visual, auditory, haptic) in a relatively unanalyzed, iconic, echoic, or haptic form, lasting from approximately 250 milliseconds to several seconds after stimulus offset. Sensory memory has large capacity relative to [[working-memory]] — it may retain most or all of the current sensory scene — but decays rapidly and automatically, and its contents are largely inaccessible without attention.
> 
> **Boundary:** Sensory memory is *pre-attentive* and largely automatic; it does not require deliberate encoding an...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[sensory-memory|Sensory Memory]]*

> [!definition] **The Seven Intellectual Traits** **[perm-note]**
> Paul and Elder identify seven core [[the-seven-intellectual-traits|Intellectual Traits]] that characterize the disciplined critical thinker:
> 
> 1. **[[intellectual-humility]]** — Awareness of the limits of one's knowledge, including sensitivity to bias, prejudice, and the limitations of one's viewpoint. The opposite of intellectual arrogance.
> 2. **[[Intellectual-Courage]]** — Willingness to face and fairly address ideas, beliefs, or viewpoints toward which there are strong negative emotions, and to challenge the status quo when reasoning demands it. The opposite of intellectual cowardice.
> 3. ...
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-seven-intellectual-traits|The Seven Intellectual Traits]]*

> [!definition] **Shallow Processing** **[perm-note]**
> Encoding that focuses on the physical or acoustic properties of a stimulus rather than its meaning. For verbal material, structural processing attends to visual features (uppercase or lowercase, font, length), while phonemic processing attends to sound (does this word rhyme with another?). Neither operation requires the processor to access semantic knowledge. Traces produced by shallow processing are relatively fragile and transient.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[shallow-processing|Shallow Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Shame vs. Guilt in Attribution Theory** **[perm-note]**
> **Shame**: Negative affect arising from failure attributed to stable, internal, uncontrollable causes (inherent ability limitations). Produces identity-level self-condemnation; associated with withdrawal and avoidance. Motivationally destructive in achievement contexts.
> 
> **Guilt**: Negative affect arising from failure attributed to controllable, internal causes (insufficient effort, poor strategy). Produces behavior-level self-criticism; associated with corrective action and renewed engagement. Motivationally constructive in achievement contexts despite being aversive.
> 
> This distinction has...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: motivational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[shame-vs.-guilt-in-attribution-theory|Shame vs. Guilt in Attribution Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Shannon-Weaver Model** **[perm-note]**
> The bidirectional but sender-centered account of information transmission in which messages are encoded, sent through a channel, and decoded at the receiver end. While foundational to [[Information-Theory]] and communications engineering, its application to human communication systematically underestimates the role of interpretation, context, and relationship in the generation of meaning.
> 
> *Source: how-to-communicate-effectively-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: communication psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[shannon-weaver-model|Shannon-Weaver Model]]*

> [!definition] **Shared vs. Personal Agents** **[perm-note]**
> **Team-shared agents** (committed to git):
> - Project-specific domain experts
> - Standard workflow automation
> - Code review standards
> - Architecture validation rules
> 
> **Personal agents** (local only):
> - Individual coding style preferences
> - Personal productivity automation
> - Learning/experimentation agents
> - Developer-specific workflows
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[shared-vs.-personal-agents|Shared vs. Personal Agents]]*

> [!definition] **Short-Term Memory** **[perm-note]**
> In the Atkinson-Shiffrin model, a limited-capacity (~7 ± 2 chunks), brief-duration (~15–30 seconds without rehearsal) store holding information that is currently active in consciousness. Information in STM is maintained through **rehearsal** (deliberate repetition) and is lost through either decay or displacement when the capacity limit is exceeded. Transfer to long-term memory was theorised to depend on rehearsal duration, though this relationship later proved more complex. STM was conceived as a passive holding buffer, a conception later replaced by the more dynamic [[working-memory|Worki...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[short-term-memory|Short-Term Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Signal Threshold Failure** **[perm-note]**
> The monitoring-regulation decoupling mechanism in which a comprehension failure generates a monitoring signal, but that signal's strength does not reach the threshold required to activate regulatory action. Occurs because many real-world comprehension failures — subtle causal inconsistencies, unstated assumptions, imprecise inferences — generate weak monitoring signals that are interpreted as unfamiliarity rather than genuine failure. Primary failure mode at Tier 2 and Tier 3 of the three-tier monitoring architecture.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[signal-threshold-failure|Signal Threshold Failure]]*

> [!definition] **Silent Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to read connected text silently with sufficient speed, accuracy, and automaticity to support deep comprehension. Distinguished from oral reading fluency by the absence of prosodic production demands, and from simple reading speed by its comprehension criterion.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[silent-reading-fluency|Silent Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **Silent Reading Rate** **[perm-note]**
> Words processed per minute during silent reading with a target comprehension criterion (typically ≥70% on delayed recall or comprehension questions). Rate measured without a comprehension criterion is a speed metric, not a fluency metric.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[silent-reading-rate|Silent Reading Rate]]*

> [!definition] **Simple View of Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Reading Comprehension = Decoding × Language Comprehension. Reading comprehension is the product — not the sum — of two distinct and separable components: (1) decoding ability, the capacity to translate print to language, and (2) oral language comprehension, the capacity to understand language once it has been decoded. The multiplicative relationship implies that weakness in either component drives the product toward zero regardless of strength in the other.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[simple-view-of-reading|Simple View of Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Simple-View-of-Reading** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The theoretical model expressing reading comprehension as the product of decoding ability and linguistic comprehension ability (RC = D × LC), where both components are necessary and neither alone is sufficient. Boundary condition: the model correctly identifies decoding as a binding constraint for early and struggling readers but does not specify the cognitive operations required for situation model construction within linguistic comprehension.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The strongest empirical support for the steel-manned received view; preserved in the reconstructi...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[simple-view-of-reading]]*

> [!definition] **Situated Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The theoretical position that cognitive processes — including schema-like knowledge structures — are not solely in individual heads but are constituted by and distributed across social practices, material artifacts, and intersubjective relationships. Knowing is not a state of an individual mind but a way of participating in socially organized activity. NOT a denial of individual cognition; a recontextualization of it.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Provides the "distributed account" alternative to the schema-in-individual-heads model; pairs with embodied cognition as a ch...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[situated-cognition|Situated Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Situated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Lave and Wenger's theoretical claim that learning is fundamentally situated — that knowing and doing are inseparable from the context in which they occur. Knowledge is not an abstract, portable possession of the individual mind but is constituted by and inseparable from the practices, communities, and material environments in which it is exercised. The concept of legitimate peripheral participation captures the developmental trajectory within situated learning: novices participate at the periphery of communities of practice and develop expertise through increasing central participation.
> 
> **...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[situated-learning|Situated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Situation Model** **[perm-note]**
> A mental representation of the *state of affairs described by the text* — not the text itself, but the world, scenario, argument, or system the text is about. The situation model integrates textual information with prior knowledge, supports inference generation, and persists in long-term memory.
> 
> **Boundary:** The situation model is not simply a richer or more detailed textbase. It is a different *kind* of representation — more like a mental simulation than a list of propositions.
> 
> **Etymology:** From Latin *situatio* (position, placement) — the situation model places described events and c...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[situation-model|Situation Model]]*

> [!definition] **The Situation-Models-van-Dijk-&-Kintsch|Three-Level Model of Text Representation** *[needs-note]*
> **Definition:** Text comprehension produces three levels of representation, each dependent on but not reducible to the one below it. The **surface code** (exact words and syntax) fades rapidly. The **propositional text base** (explicit semantic content) persists longer and supports recognition and verbatim recall. The **situation model** (mental representation of the described world) persists longest, supports inference, transfer, and application, and constitutes the gold standard of comprehension. Crucially, the levels are dissociable: readers can succeed at one while failing at the next.
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Situation-Models-van-Dijk-&-KintschThree-Level-Model-of-Text-Representation|The Situation-Models-van-Dijk-&-Kintsch|Three-Level Model of Text Representation]]*

> [!definition] **Situation-Model|Situation Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A situation model is the mental representation of the situation or state of affairs that a text describes — not the text itself, but the *world* the text is about. It is constructed by integrating the propositional content of the text base with the reader's prior knowledge, goals, and inferences. A situation model is the difference between knowing what the text *says* and understanding what it *means* in relation to a coherent state of affairs. It is not stored as a linguistic structure but as an integrated, multimodal representation that can include spatial, causal, tempora...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[situation-modelsituation-model|Situation-Model|Situation Model]]*

> [!definition] **Slot / Default Value** **[perm-note]**
> A variable component within a schema that accepts specific values when the schema is instantiated in a particular context. Slots carry *default values* — the most probable or typical values in the absence of specific information. Default values drive schema-based inference, filling in unstated information during comprehension and reconstruction.
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[slot-default-value|Slot / Default Value]]*

> [!definition] **Social Constructivism** **[perm-note]**
> **Social constructivism** (Lev Vygotsky's framework) holds that higher mental functions — voluntary attention, logical memory, concept formation, deliberate problem-solving — do not arise from the individual organism's biological development but are first constructed in social interaction and cultural activity, and only subsequently internalized as individual cognitive capacities. The fundamental unit is not the individual mind but the mind-in-social-interaction; learning precedes and drives development, not the other way around (contra Piaget). Language is the primary tool of social constr...
> 
> *Source: constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: learning theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[social-constructivism|Social Constructivism]]*

> [!definition] **Sociocentric-Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Group-level analogue of egocentrism: uncritical adherence to in-group norms and narratives. Significance: explains deterioration of public reasoning in polarized contexts.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[sociocentric-thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Socratic Method** **[perm-note]**
> A dialogic pedagogical technique in which the teacher poses a sequence of open, probing questions designed to surface the implicit assumptions in a learner's beliefs, reveal internal contradictions, and motivate the reconstruction of understanding on firmer evidential and logical grounds. Named for Socrates, whose practice is documented in Plato's dialogues.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[socratic-method|Socratic Method]]*

> [!definition] **The SOI Model — Three Core Processes** *[needs-note]*
> **Selection**: The learner actively attends to and selects relevant information from the available sensory input, filtering out irrelevant or redundant material. This is not passive perception but active attentional deployment guided by prior knowledge, goals, and comprehension monitoring.
> **Organization**: The learner arranges selected information into a coherent internal structure, forming a mental representation that captures the relationships between elements (temporal, causal, hierarchical, comparative). This produces a mental model or schema fragment rather than a list.
> **Integration*...
> 
> *Source: generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: educational psychology & learning science*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-SOI-Model-—-Three-Core-Processes|The SOI Model — Three Core Processes]]*

> [!definition] **Soundness** **[perm-note]**
> A deductive argument is sound if it is both (a) valid and (b) actually has all true premises. Sound arguments guarantee true conclusions. Soundness is the gold standard for deductive arguments used to establish real-world conclusions, but evaluating soundness requires both logical skill (to check validity) and factual knowledge (to verify the premises).
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[soundness]]*

> [!definition] **Source Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process of attributing a memory to its origin — distinguishing what was actually perceived, heard, or read from what was imagined, inferred, or told. Source monitoring failures are a central mechanism of memory distortion: false recognition in the DRM paradigm, misidentification of faces in eyewitness testimony, and "cryptomnesia" (believing an idea is original when it was in fact previously encountered) all involve misattribution of source. The *source monitoring framework* (Johnson, Hashtroudi, & Lindsay, 1993) proposes that source information is retained in the memory trace...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[source-monitoring|Source Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Spacing Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The spacing effect is the empirical finding that learning sessions distributed across time produce substantially better long-term retention than equivalent time spent in a single massed session, even when total study time is held constant. Effect sizes in the literature range from moderate to large (d = 0.40 to d = 0.80+ depending on retention interval measured).
> 
> **Boundary:** Spacing advantages are maximized for relatively well-formulated knowledge units (facts, procedures, concepts) where the retention interval exceeds the spacing interval. For very novel material requiring initial compr...
> 
> *Source: motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10* | *Domain: learning science & educational psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[spacing-effect|Spacing Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Specificity-Autonomy Dialectic** **[perm-note]**
> The structural tension between goal-setting theory's core finding (specific goals enhance performance through variance reduction) and self-determination theory's core finding (autonomy-supportive contexts enhance motivational quality). In PKM, this dialectic manifests as the challenge of designing goals that are precise about the *understanding sought* while preserving the learner's sense of choice about *how that understanding is pursued*.
> 
> **Boundary:** The dialectic is productive rather than contradictory — it defines a design space to be navigated, not a problem to be solved by choosing...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-specificity-autonomy-dialectic|The Specificity-Autonomy Dialectic]]*

> [!definition] **Spectator Theory of Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> **Term:** Spectator Theory of Knowledge (Dewey's critical term)
> **Definition:** The epistemological position — attributed to the mainstream Western tradition from Plato through Descartes to early modern empiricism — that knowing is fundamentally a matter of detached contemplation: the mind passively receives or intuits truths about a mind-independent reality. Dewey argued this picture is both philosophically incoherent and pedagogically catastrophic.
> **Boundary Conditions:** The spectator theory is a family of positions, not a single doctrine — Dewey's critique applies most directly to corr...
> 
> *Source: pragmatism-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: educational philosophy*
> *Permanent Note: [[spectator-theory-of-knowledge|Spectator Theory of Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Split Attention Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The [[split-attention-effect]] occurs when learners must mentally integrate two or more sources of mutually referring information that are physically or temporally separated. The cognitive effort of mentally integrating these separated sources constitutes extraneous load that does not contribute to learning. Eliminating the split by physically integrating the information sources reduces extraneous load and improves learning.
> 
> **See also:** [[extraneous-cognitive-load]], [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning|Mayer's Spatial Contiguity Principle]]
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[split-attention-effect|Split Attention Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Split-Attention Effect** **[perm-note]**
> A CLT-derived design principle: when learners must mentally integrate two physically or temporally separated sources of information that are logically interdependent (e.g., a diagram and its text description), cognitive resources are consumed by the integration process rather than by schema formation. Integrating the sources in the instructional design eliminates this extraneous load. The split-attention effect is one of the best-replicated effects in CLT research and survives dialectical scrutiny robustly.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[split-attention-effect|Split-Attention Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Split-Attention Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The performance and learning decrement that occurs when learners must simultaneously attend to and mentally integrate multiple spatially or temporally separated sources of information that are mutually referential (neither source can be understood without the other). The split-attention effect arises because the act of holding, locating, and mentally integrating separated information consumes working memory capacity without contributing to schema formation — it is the paradigm case of extraneous cognitive load. Eliminated by physically or temporally integrating the mutually dependent inform...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-split-attention-effect|The Split-Attention Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Spreading Activation** **[perm-note]**
> [[spreading-activation]] is a cognitive mechanism in which the activation of a concept node in semantic/associative memory propagates along associative links to neighboring nodes, partially activating them in proportion to link strength. Spreading activation is the mechanism underlying priming effects, associative recall, and the fluency of expert retrieval in familiar domains. Activation spreads in parallel across multiple pathways simultaneously, reaching remote associations through successive hops along the network.
> 
> **Boundary:** Spreading activation is not deliberate or conscious searc...
> 
> *Source: schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[spreading-activation|Spreading Activation]]*

> [!definition] **Stability** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The temporal persistence of a cause — whether it is relatively fixed (stable: ability, task difficulty) or variable (unstable: effort, luck, temporary mood). Stability is the dimension that Weiner argued most powerfully determines *expectancy change* — the degree to which an outcome changes a person's prediction about future outcomes.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the analytically central dimension. Stable attributions create rigid expectancies that are resistant to disconfirmation; unstable attributions leave expectancies open to revision. The self-sealing arch...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[stability]]*

> [!definition] **Stability Dimension** **[perm-note]**
> The attribution dimension distinguishing whether a cause is perceived as fixed over time (stable: chronic low ability, consistent task difficulty) or variable over time (unstable: effort on this occasion, luck, current mood). Stable attributions for failure predict low expectancy for future success; unstable attributions for failure preserve expectancy.
> 
> **Boundary:** Stability is the *most consequential* dimension for expectancy effects, and thus for persistent motivation. The fixed/growth mindset distinction maps most directly onto this dimension: growth mindset reframes ability as unstab...
> 
> *Source: attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[stability-dimension|Stability Dimension]]*

> [!definition] **The Stability Dimension** **[perm-note]**
> The [[stability-dimension]] classifies causes as either stable (enduring, likely to persist across time and situations) or unstable (temporary, likely to change). Ability is typically perceived as stable; effort as unstable. Luck is unstable; task difficulty is relatively stable. This dimension is the primary determinant of expectancy change: stable causes produce the inference that the same outcome will recur, while unstable causes leave room for different outcomes in the future.
> 
> **Boundary:** Stability is a property of the perceived cause, not of the outcome itself. The same outcome (fai...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-19* | *Domain: social cognition & motivation science*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-stability-dimension|The Stability Dimension]]*

> [!definition] **The Stability-Expectancy Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The causal pathway by which stable attributions for outcomes (particularly failures) generate expectancy deficits — reduced estimates of future success — through the logical inference that a cause that was present and stable in the past will be present in the future. Expectancy deficits in turn reduce the perceived instrumentality of effort investment.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the analytically central mechanism of the report — the primary driver of the most educationally and clinically significant motivational outcomes.
> 
> **Operational Indicators:** Measured...
> 
> *Source: attribution-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-24* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-stability-expectancy-mechanism|The Stability-Expectancy Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Stage Characteristics and Training Implications** **[perm-note]**
> **Cognitive Stage**: Learners require explicit instruction, feedback, and conscious attention to every component. Performance is slow, effortful, variable, and error-prone. Training should emphasize understanding, provide models, and focus on accuracy over speed. **Associative Stage**: Errors decrease, performance becomes more consistent, speed increases following power law. Training should provide practice variability, maintain focus on error detection, and begin introducing realistic task conditions. **Autonomous Stage**: Performance becomes automatic, fast, reliable, and can be executed ...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[stage-characteristics-and-training-implications|Stage Characteristics and Training Implications]]*

> [!definition] **Standard of Coherence** **[perm-note]**
> The implicit configuration of expectations a reader applies as the criterion against which comprehension monitoring evaluates the current representation. Standards range from lexical (word recognition) through syntactic (grammatical parsing) and propositional (sentence-level cohesion) to structural (paragraph and discourse level) and informational (whether content delivers on rhetorical promises). The standards a reader actually applies — usually below conscious awareness — determine which kinds of comprehension breakdowns the monitoring system can detect and which it will silently accept.
> ...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: cognitive science*
> *Permanent Note: [[standard-of-coherence|Standard of Coherence]]*

> [!definition] **The standard three-load taxonomy** **[perm-note]**
> **Intrinsic Cognitive Load (ICL):** the load imposed by the inherent complexity of the material being learned, considered in relation to what the learner already knows.
> **Extraneous Cognitive Load (ECL):** the load imposed by the way the material is presented, separate from the material's inherent complexity. Reducible through instructional design.
> **Germane Cognitive Load (GCL):** the load corresponding to cognitive resources devoted to schema construction and automation. The "productive" load.
> **Total cognitive load:** typically modeled as additive: TCL = ICL + ECL + GCL, with the constra...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-element-interactivity-deep-dive-2026-04-20* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-standard-three-load-taxonomy|The standard three-load taxonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Standards Inadequacy** **[perm-note]**
> Standards inadequacy refers to the condition in which monitoring failures arise not from poor cue-utilization within a functioning monitoring system but from the learner having insufficiently specified, inaccurate, or domain-inappropriate criteria for what excellent performance looks like. Where standards are adequate, a learner can accurately detect gaps between their current performance and the standard; where standards are inadequate, even a well-functioning monitoring system cannot generate accurate assessments because it has no appropriate reference point.
> 
> **See also:** [[Metacognitiv...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-development-foundational-report-2026-04-09* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[standards-inadequacy|Standards Inadequacy]]*

> [!definition] **State Management Approaches** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Shared Markdown Files** (simplest):
> - Single truth source: MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md
> - Agents read → execute → write updates
> - Human-readable state (can inspect/debug)
> - Version controlled (git tracks state changes)
> 
> **2. JSON State Files** (structured):
> - .claude/state/features.json
> - Machine-readable for automation
> - Schema validation possible
> - Harder for humans to edit
> 
> **3. File System State** (implicit):
> - Presence of files indicates status
> - docs/requirements/X.md exists → requirements complete
> - docs/architecture/ADR-X.md exists → design complete
> - src/features/X/ exists → implementa...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[state-management-approaches|State Management Approaches]]*

> [!definition] **State-Dependent Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon whereby retrieval is facilitated when the learner's internal physiological or pharmacological state at retrieval matches the state at encoding. A special case of the ESP in which the "context" is internal (mood, arousal level, pharmacological state) rather than external.
> 
> **Boundary:** State-dependent effects are often smaller than environmental context-dependent effects, and not all studies find robust state-dependence. Methodological issues (demand characteristics, varying baseline states) complicate this literature.
> 
> **See also:** [[encoding-specificity-principle]], [[Epis...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[state-dependent-memory|State-Dependent Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Stereotype Threat** **[perm-note]**
> The situationally induced risk of confirming a negative stereotype about one's social group, which creates cognitive and emotional interference that undermines performance. Stereotype threat interacts with mindset: individuals who identify strongly with a domain and hold a fixed mindset about ability are most vulnerable to stereotype threat in that domain. Growth mindset interventions have been explored as potential buffers against stereotype threat.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[stereotype-threat|Stereotype Threat]]*

> [!definition] **Testing Effect / Retrieval Practice Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust finding that actively retrieving information from memory produces substantially better long-term retention than re-studying the same material, even when total study time is held constant. The retrieval act, not the re-exposure, is the effective mechanism.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[testing-effect-retrieval-practice-effect|Testing Effect / Retrieval Practice Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Strategic Automaticity** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive state in which a deliberately formed if-then plan operates with the speed, efficiency, and resistance to interference characteristic of habitual responses, but achieved through prior cognitive engineering rather than long behavioral repetition. Strategic automaticity exhibits three operational signatures: enhanced cue detection, accelerated action initiation, and increased shielding against competing influences.
> 
> **Boundary:** Strategic automaticity is not the same as ordinary [[automaticity]]; it lacks the deeply consolidated procedural-memory traces that distinguish true hab...
> 
> *Source: implementation-intentions-gollwitzer-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: motivation science and volitional psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[strategic-automaticity|Strategic Automaticity]]*

> [!definition] **Strategy Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The subcomponent of metacognitive knowledge concerning beliefs about cognitive strategies (techniques that directly advance cognitive goals: elaboration, organization, imagery, retrieval practice) and metacognitive strategies (techniques that monitor progress toward cognitive goals: self-testing, comprehension monitoring, error detection). Strategy knowledge in its most sophisticated form includes conditional knowledge — knowing not only what strategies exist and how to execute them, but the conditions under which each is effective.
> 
> **See also:** [[metacognitive-strategy]], [[Elaborative-I...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[strategy-knowledge|Strategy Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Stream of Consciousness** **[perm-note]**
> James's most famous psychological metaphor characterizing consciousness not as series of discrete states but as continuous, ever-changing flow: "Consciousness, then, does not appear to itself chopped up in bits. Such words as 'chain' or 'train' do not describe it fitly...It is nothing jointed; it flows. A 'river' or a 'stream' are the metaphors by which it is most naturally described." This conception revolutionized psychological thinking by emphasizing the [[continuity]], [[selectivity]], and [[personal]] character of conscious experience against earlier atomistic models treating mind as c...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-william-james* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[stream-of-consciousness|Stream of Consciousness]]*

> [!definition] **Structural Analogue** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A source domain concept whose relational structure is isomorphic to a target domain concept, enabling structural mapping. A structural analogue is not a surface similarity (sharing perceptual or superficial features) but a deep similarity (sharing the pattern of relations among components). Used in schema construction to provide temporary organizational structure for a novel domain.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Structural analogy is not explanation — it does not tell the learner what the target concept IS, only how it is organized. Overextension of structural analogies (treatin...
> 
> *Attribution: adapted*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[structural-analogue|Structural Analogue]]*

> [!definition] **Structural Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> Structural metacognitive knowledge refers to the metacognitive understanding that is *embodied in the structure of a knowledge system* rather than explicitly stated as declarative propositions. In a PKB, structural metacognitive knowledge includes the organizational hierarchy, the differentiation of note types, the linking conventions, and the review protocols — all of which encode tacit knowledge about how different types of knowledge should be processed, connected, and maintained. This concept extends Flavell's taxonomy by recognizing that metacognitive knowledge can be architecturally in...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-pkm-pkb-foundational-report-2026-04-12* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[structural-metacognitive-knowledge|Structural Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Subagents vs. Coordinator** **[perm-note]**
> **Subagents** are specialized Claude instances with their own context windows, defined in markdown files with YAML frontmatter. The **coordinator** is not a separate file—it's the main Claude agent (the "general-purpose" agent) that you interact with directly. The coordinator analyzes tasks, routes them to appropriate subagents, and synthesizes their results.
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[subagents-vs.-coordinator|Subagents vs. Coordinator]]*

> [!definition] **Subsequent Memory Effect** **[perm-note]**
> A neuroimaging finding in which activity during encoding — particularly in left inferior prefrontal and medial temporal regions — predicts subsequent memory success for the encoded item. Provides a neural-level validation of LOP predictions: deeper (semantic) encoding tasks produce greater subsequent memory effect magnitude, consistent with the claim that they produce more durable memory traces via richer cortical and hippocampal engagement.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[subsequent-memory-effect|Subsequent Memory Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Substitution Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon in which cognitive offloading reduces the internal processing that would otherwise contribute to long-term memory consolidation and schema formation, producing short-term cognitive relief at the cost of long-term learning. Named here as a design pitfall for PKM practitioners: capture that bypasses generative engagement with material (reformulation, comparison with prior knowledge, identification of implications) fails to convert working memory contents into long-term knowledge — it merely relocates the information from one temporary store (working memory) to another (PKB inbo...
> 
> *Source: pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: cognitive science / personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-substitution-trap|The Substitution Trap]]*

> [!definition] **Subsumer / Anchoring Idea** **[perm-note]**
> An existing concept in a learner's long-term memory structure that is sufficiently inclusive and relevant to provide an organizational framework into which new, more specific material can be subsumed. The advance organizer either activates an existing subsumer (comparative organizer) or creates a new one (expository organizer). When no subsumer is available, meaningful learning cannot occur and rote memorization is the only available learning mode.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[subsumer-anchoring-idea|Subsumer / Anchoring Idea]]*

> [!definition] **Supervisory Attentional System** **[perm-note]**
> **Supervisory Attentional System** (Norman & Shallice, 1986): A theoretical control system that intervenes in cognitive processing when tasks are novel, difficult, or require inhibition of habitual responses. Normal routine behavior is governed by contention scheduling (automatic schema activation); the SAS overrides contention scheduling when required, directing attention toward goal-relevant processing. Adopted by Baddeley as the provisional theoretical model for the central executive. See: [[executive-function]], [[Cognitive-Control]].
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[supervisory-attentional-system|Supervisory Attentional System]]*

> [!definition] **Supportive Information** **[perm-note]**
> The conceptual frameworks, domain models, cognitive strategies, and systematic approaches to problem-solving that support the non-recurrent (variable, reasoning-intensive) aspects of complex skill performance. Presented before or during each new task class, remaining available throughout. Bridges what learners already know to what they need to understand at the current level of complexity.
> 
> **Boundary:** Supportive information is not a synonym for "all instructional content." It specifically targets non-recurrent skill aspects — those requiring flexible reasoning rather than routine executi...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[supportive-information|Supportive Information]]*

> [!definition] **Suppression** **[perm-note]**
> The active inhibition of initially activated but ultimately context-inappropriate information during comprehension. The mechanism underlying the C-I model's integration phase. Individual differences in suppression efficiency reliably predict comprehension skill independently of vocabulary and decoding ability.
> 
> **Boundary:** Suppression is active and measurable in real time (through primed recognition paradigms showing reduced accessibility of suppressed content); it is distinct from passive forgetting.
> 
> **See also:** [[working-memory]], [[automaticity]], [[metacognitive-regulation]]
> 
> *Source: situation-models-text-comprehension-foundational-report-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[suppression]]*

> [!definition] **Surface Code** **[perm-note]**
> The verbatim or near-verbatim linguistic representation of the text — the exact words and syntactic structures encountered. The surface code is extremely short-lived, decaying within seconds under most reading conditions unless the reader rehearses or re-reads.
> 
> **Boundary:** The surface code is *not* comprehension. Holding the surface code active (reading a sentence again immediately) does not produce understanding; it merely delays decay.
> 
> **Operational Indicator:** You are primarily at the surface code level when, after reading a sentence, you can only reproduce it in the text's own word...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-focused-analysis-2026-03-29* | *Domain: reading-comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[surface-code|Surface Code]]*

> [!definition] **Surface Form** **[perm-note]**
> The verbatim representation of the text's linguistic structure — exact wording, syntactic form, and sentence-level meaning. Surface form representations are highly transient and decay rapidly from working memory, typically within seconds of reading a passage. Recognizing that "The mitochondria produce ATP" and "ATP is produced by the mitochondria" convey the same proposition requires moving *beyond* the surface form.
> 
> **Boundary:** Surface form is not the same as shallow processing — surface form is simply the first level of representation constructed, before propositional or inferential pr...
> 
> *Source: elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[surface-form|Surface Form]]*

> [!definition] **Surveillance** **[perm-note]**
> The awareness of being observed or monitored, even in the absence of explicit evaluative events. CET research (Lepper & Greene, 1975) shows that surveillance carries a controlling functional significance on its own: being watched introduces an external audience, makes the evaluative relationship salient, and tends to shift PLOC external. This finding has significant implications for digital learning environments in which learner behaviour is continuously logged and monitored, even if that monitoring is not connected to explicit rewards or consequences.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[surveillance]]*

> [!definition] **Synaptic Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> [[synaptic-consolidation]] refers to the cellular and molecular processes that stabilize individual synaptic connections following learning-related neural activity. Immediately after neural activity associated with encoding, a cascade of molecular events unfolds: neurotransmitter release triggers intracellular signaling, gene transcription is activated, new proteins are synthesized, and synaptic connections are physically strengthened — the structural basis of what Hebb famously captured as "neurons that fire together, wire together." [[donald-hebb|Hebb's postulate]] (1949) anticipated this...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[synaptic-consolidation|Synaptic Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Synergistic Ecphory** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which a retrieval cue and a stored engram interact jointly to produce a recollective experience. "Synergistic" indicates that neither cue nor trace alone determines retrieval output — the output is the joint product of their interaction. The term "ecphory" was revived from Semon (1904) to name the activation process.
> 
> **Boundary:** Ecphory is the process; the conscious recollective experience is the output. Not all ecphory reaches awareness. "Synergistic" distinguishes Tulving's formulation from simpler trace-activation accounts.
> 
> **See also:** [[endel-tulving]], [[Episodic-M...
> 
> *Source: encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology — memory science*
> *Permanent Note: [[synergistic-ecphory|Synergistic Ecphory]]*

> [!definition] **Synthesis Debt** **[perm-note]**
> The accumulated backlog of captured but unprocessed information in a PKM system — notes, clippings, or observations that have been stored but not yet evaluated, connected, or integrated into existing knowledge structures. Synthesis debt imposes ongoing extraneous load through incomplete-task intrusion and increases the future cost of synthesis by requiring re-contextualisation of stale notes.
> 
> **Boundary:** Not all captured but unprocessed information constitutes synthesis debt. Reference material intended for future lookup (e.g., a saved recipe, a boilerplate code snippet) need not be synt...
> 
> *Source: pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05* | *Domain: personal knowledge management*
> *Permanent Note: [[synthesis-debt|Synthesis Debt]]*

> [!definition] **Synthetic Model** **[perm-note]**
> In science education, a mental model that combines elements of a student's naive framework theory with partially absorbed scientific information, producing an internally consistent but scientifically incorrect model. Synthetic models arise when instruction presents correct scientific information without supporting genuine model revision: the student integrates the new information into the existing model framework rather than replacing it. The synthetic model concept explains why direct instruction on scientific facts often fails to resolve deep misconceptions.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[synthetic-model|Synthetic Model]]*

> [!definition] **System 1** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of cognition that operates automatically, rapidly, and with little or no sense of conscious effort. System 1 processes generate immediate impressions, intuitions, feelings, and impulses. In political contexts, System 1 is implicated in quick evaluations of candidates based on facial appearance, gut-level reactions to policy proposals, and emotionally driven judgments about in-group and out-group members. System 1 is not inherently unreliable — many System 1 responses in familiar domains are accurate precisely because they encode learned patterns. But in novel, complex, or contested d...
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[system-1|System 1]]*

> [!definition] **System 1 / System 2** **[perm-note]**
> System 1 processes are fast, automatic, and largely unconscious — they produce rapid intuitive responses by pattern-matching current input to stored associations. System 2 processes are slow, effortful, and consciously controlled — they enable sequential, rule-governed reasoning including formal deductive inference. In deductive tasks, System 1 often generates plausible but logically invalid responses based on belief or semantic content; valid deductive reasoning typically requires System 2 to override these responses.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[system-1-system-2|System 1 / System 2]]*

> [!definition] **System 2** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of cognition that is deliberate, effortful, rule-governed, and conscious. System 2 is invoked when we carefully evaluate arguments, check evidence against claims, or deliberately override impulsive responses. In political contexts, System 2 engagement is what is typically meant by "careful reasoning" — but it is metabolically expensive and often deployed selectively, in the service of rationalizing conclusions that System 1 has already generated. The popular assumption that more System 2 engagement always means better political reasoning turns out to be empirically fragile.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[system-2|System 2]]*

> [!definition] **Systems Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> [[systems-consolidation]] refers to the gradual reorganization of long-term memory representation across neural systems, in which initially hippocampus-dependent memory traces are progressively transferred to and stabilized in long-term neocortical storage networks over months to years. Upon initial encoding, episodic and semantic memories are rapidly bound in the hippocampus, which links distributed neocortical representations into coherent episodes. Over subsequent weeks and months, the connections between neocortical representations are progressively strengthened through repeated reactiv...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[systems-consolidation|Systems Consolidation]]*

## _U

> [!definition] **The Undermining Effect** **[perm-note]**
> [[undermining-effect|The undermining effect]] (also called the [[overjustification-effect|overjustification effect]]) refers to the phenomenon whereby introducing external rewards for an intrinsically motivating activity reduces subsequent intrinsic motivation. It is predicted by [[cognitive-evaluation-theory]] through the mechanism of the [[perceived-locus-of-causality]]: when a person attributes their engagement to the reward rather than to themselves, the experience of volition is diminished, reducing intrinsic motivation. This is among SDT's most policy-consequential claims.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-undermining-effect|The Undermining Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Undermining Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical phenomenon, first demonstrated by Deci (1971), in which the introduction of extrinsic rewards for an intrinsically interesting activity reduces subsequent intrinsic motivation when rewards are removed. Explained in CET as resulting from a shift in perceived locus of causality from internal (intrinsic interest) to external (reward). The replication literature for this effect is mixed; it is most robust for expected, tangible, contingent rewards for activities of initially moderate-to-high intrinsic interest.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[undermining-effect|Undermining Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Undermining-Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical finding that external rewards contingent on intrinsically interesting activities reduce subsequent intrinsic motivation when the rewards are withdrawn. Mechanistically interpreted in this report as an existence proof that perceived locus of causality is causally potent — changes in how people experience their own agency produce measurable downstream motivational consequences.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[undermining-effect]]*

> [!definition] **Undifferentiated Conception of Ability** **[perm-note]**
> In an undifferentiated conception of ability, effort and ability are treated as the same thing — or at minimum, as inseparable. A person who works harder is understood to be more able, and ability is seen as something that grows through effort. Under this conception, success means improving and learning, and failure means simply "I haven't learned this yet." This conception is developmentally earlier (young children tend to operate under it) and is characteristic of what Nicholls called *task involvement*. It corresponds roughly to what [[carol-s.-dweck|Carol S. Dweck]] would later call the...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[undifferentiated-conception-of-ability|Undifferentiated Conception of Ability]]*

> [!definition] **Tuning** **[perm-note]**
> A moderate form of schema change in which the *parameters* or *variable constraints* of a schema are adjusted to better fit experience, without altering the schema's fundamental structure. Tuning changes what values are expected in particular slots, or adjusts the weights of different interpretations, but leaves the schema's architecture intact.
> 
> **Example:** A new medical resident initially has a DIAGNOSIS schema with widely distributed priors (many equally likely diagnoses for chest pain). With experience, the schema is tuned so that the most common diagnoses (MI, GERD, musculoskeletal pa...
> 
> *Source: schema-and-how-they-work-deep-dive-2026-04-08* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[tuning]]*

> [!definition] **The Universal Intellectual Standards** **[perm-note]**
> Paul and Elder identify the following core standards, each accompanied by probing questions that operationalize the standard for practical application:
> 
> - **Clarity** — Is the reasoning understandable? Could it be elaborated, illustrated, or exemplified? Clarity is the gateway standard: if reasoning is not clear, its other qualities cannot be assessed.
> - **Accuracy** — Is the reasoning free from errors and distortions? Do the claims correspond to reality as best we can determine it?
> - **Precision** — Is the reasoning sufficiently specific and detailed for the purpose at hand? Precision prev...
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-universal-intellectual-standards|The Universal Intellectual Standards]]*

> [!definition] **Universal Need** **[perm-note]**
> In SDT, a need is "universal" if its frustration produces ill-being for all humans regardless of cultural context, developmental stage, or individual difference. This is distinct from the claim that its satisfaction takes the same form across cultures. The dialectical process supports the former claim (universal frustration effects) much more strongly than the latter (universal satisfaction forms). A more precise SDT formulation would state: "All humans are reliably vulnerable to motivational deterioration when their autonomy, competence, or relatedness needs are frustrated, though what sat...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[universal-need|Universal Need]]*

> [!definition] **Heutagogy** **[perm-note]**
> A theory of self-determined learning extending beyond pedagogy (teacher-directed) and andragogy (self-directed within a structure) to emphasise the learner's capacity to determine not only how they learn but what they learn and whether they have learned it. Heutagogy describes the aspirational endpoint of the developmental trajectory from other-regulated through co-regulated to fully self-regulated learning.
> 
> **Boundary:** Heutagogy assumes a level of metacognitive sophistication and motivational autonomy that must be developed rather than assumed — hence the importance of scaffolded develo...
> 
> *Source: goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15* | *Domain: educational psychology — motivation and self-regulation*
> *Permanent Note: [[heutagogy]]*

## _V

> [!definition] **Validity** **[perm-note]**
> A deductive argument is valid if and only if there is no possible scenario — no logically conceivable world — in which the premises are all true and the conclusion is false. Validity is a formal, structural property of arguments; it says nothing about whether the premises are actually true. An argument can be perfectly valid while resting on wildly false premises, and the conclusion will then be guaranteed only conditionally: *if* the premises were true, the conclusion *would* have to be true.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[validity]]*

> [!definition] **Evaluating** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluating is the retrospective regulatory activity through which the learner assesses the products and processes of a completed cognitive engagement. It encompasses assessment of goal attainment (did I achieve what I set out to achieve?), strategy assessment (was the approach I used effective?), and attribution (what accounts for the outcome?). Evaluating is the regulatory component most directly connected to the iterative improvement of regulation over time: it is through evaluation that regulatory strategies are updated, refined, or abandoned.
> 
> **Boundary:** Metacognitive evaluating is n...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-regulation-foundational-report-2026-03-31* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[evaluating]]*

> [!definition] **Evaluation** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluation is the cognitive act of assessing the credibility, accuracy, logical validity, and contextual adequacy of claims, arguments, sources, and reasoning processes. Evaluation requires the application of intellectual standards — asking whether a claim is clear, accurate, precise, relevant, deep, broad, and logically sound. Importantly, evaluation applies to one's own reasoning as much as to external sources.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[evaluation]]*

> [!definition] **Variability of Practice Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding (Paas & van Merriënboer, 1994) that training on varied problem instances produces better transfer than training on uniform instances, despite equal or higher reported mental effort. Interpreted in CLT as inducing more schema-generalization (germane) processing; in element interactivity terms, as exposing learners to a broader range of element interaction patterns.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: cognitive-science/learning-theory*
> *Permanent Note: [[variability-of-practice-effect|Variability of Practice Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Vehicle Externalism** **[perm-note]**
> The position that the physical vehicles of mental states — the material processes that carry and implement cognitive states — can extend beyond the boundary of brain and body. Distinguished from content externalism, which holds that the contents of mental states are partly environmentally determined while the vehicles remain internal. Extended Mind Theory is the most radical form of vehicle externalism. Less radical forms hold that the body (rather than the brain alone) is the vehicle of cognition — a position associated with embodied cognition.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[vehicle-externalism|Vehicle Externalism]]*

> [!definition] **Event Indexing Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A model of situation model structure proposing that readers continuously monitor and update five dimensions of the described situation: temporal, spatial, causal, intentional, and entity. Reading times increase when any dimension shifts, indicating active model maintenance. Boundary condition: the model describes competent readers; poor comprehenders show less sensitivity to dimension shifts, indicating less active model monitoring.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** Provides the structural architecture for the reconstructed situation-model monitoring strategy; the five dime...
> 
> *Source: situation-model-construction-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-29* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[event-indexing-model|Event Indexing Model]]*

> [!definition] **Verbal Efficiency Theory** **[perm-note]**
> Charles Perfetti's verbal efficiency theory refines LaBerge and Samuels by specifying the locus of the bottleneck more precisely. Verbal efficiency refers to the speed and quality of local text processes — particularly word identification and the maintenance of decoded words in [[working-memory]] long enough to be integrated into propositions and sentences. Inefficient local processes create a resource drain that impairs not just the speed of comprehension but its quality and completeness, because the products of word recognition may fade from working memory before they can be integrated in...
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[verbal-efficiency-theory|Verbal Efficiency Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Verbal Persuasion** **[perm-note]**
> One of the four sources of self-efficacy: the impact of others' (or one's own) evaluative statements on efficacy beliefs. Verbal persuasion is weaker than mastery experience as an efficacy source but is more immediately accessible; crucially, *self-directed verbal persuasion* — the inner speech one deploys when appraising one's own capability — functions through the same mechanism as external persuasion. Credibility, specificity, and process-focus (rather than outcome-focus) enhance the efficacy impact of verbal persuasion.
> 
> **Boundary:** Verbal persuasion operates most effectively when it ...
> 
> *Source: attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[verbal-persuasion|Verbal Persuasion]]*

> [!definition] **Vicarious Experience** **[perm-note]**
> The acquisition of efficacy information through observation of the performances of others, where the observer extracts judgments about their own probable capability based on the model's outcomes. The strength of vicarious effects depends primarily on perceived model-observer similarity, the diagnosticity of the modeled performance for the observer's own situation, and the cognitive accessibility of the strategies the model employed.
> 
> **Boundary:** Vicarious experience is not mere exposure to demonstrations — it requires the observer's active extraction of capability-relevant inferences from...
> 
> *Source: self-efficacy-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-20* | *Domain: social-cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[vicarious-experience|Vicarious Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Virtual Environment** **[perm-note]**
> A virtual environment is an isolated Python installation — a self-contained directory that has its own copy of the Python interpreter, its own `pip`, and its own `site-packages` directory, completely independent of the global installation and of every other virtual environment. When a [[virtual-environment|virtual environment]] is active, all `pip install` commands install packages into that environment's private directory, and all `import` statements resolve from that private directory, which means that each project can have its own set of packages at its own versions without any possibili...
> 
> *Source: python-development-in-vscode-practitioners-field-guide-2026-04-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[virtual-environment|Virtual Environment]]*

> [!definition] **Virtue-Epistemology** **[perm-note]**
> Epistemological tradition emphasizing intellectual character traits as constitutive of good knowing. Connection: offers philosophical grounding for Paul-Elder's traits component.
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[virtue-epistemology]]*

> [!definition] **The Visuospatial Sketchpad** **[perm-note]**
> The **visuospatial sketchpad** is the component of working memory specialized for maintaining and manipulating visual and spatial information. It serves as the cognitive equivalent of a mental whiteboard — a temporary workspace for mental images, spatial layouts, visual sequences, and the manipulation of objects in imagined space. Like the phonological loop, the visuospatial sketchpad is a slave system subordinate to the [[central-executive]], but it operates on a distinct representational format (visual and spatial rather than phonological) and is largely independent of the phonological lo...
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[the-visuospatial-sketchpad|The Visuospatial Sketchpad]]*

> [!definition] **Visuospatial Sketchpad** **[perm-note]**
> **Visuospatial Sketchpad**: The visual-spatial working memory subsystem, maintaining and manipulating visual and spatial information. Subdivided by Logie (1995) into the *visual cache* (temporary storage of visual features: color, shape, form) and the *inner scribe* (processing and rehearsing spatial sequences and movement). Doubly dissociable from the phonological loop through neuropsychological cases and secondary task interference paradigms. Neuroanatomically: posterior visual/parietal regions, right-lateralized for spatial processing. See: [[visuospatial-sketchpad]], [[Spatial-Cognition]].
> 
> *Source: baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03* | *Domain: cognitive psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[visuospatial-sketchpad|Visuospatial Sketchpad]]*

> [!definition] **Vocabulary Breadth** **[perm-note]**
> The count of distinct word forms or word families that a learner has some level of knowledge of, regardless of depth. Often operationalized as the number of word families a learner can correctly identify or define on standardized vocabulary size tests (e.g., the Vocabulary Levels Test). For adult educated native English speakers, estimates typically range from 17,000 to 60,000+ word families depending on methodology.
> 
> **Boundary:** Breadth counts can be misleading because they do not distinguish between partial and full knowledge. A learner may have "breadth" that is actually a collection o...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[vocabulary-breadth|Vocabulary Breadth]]*

> [!definition] **Vocabulary Depth** **[perm-note]**
> The richness and precision of knowledge for each word in the lexicon, encompassing definitional accuracy, range of meaning, collocational knowledge, and pragmatic/register constraints. Vocabulary depth is increasingly recognized as the dimension most predictive of reading comprehension among proficient readers who already have adequate breadth.
> 
> **Boundary:** Depth is not easily assessed and is often underrepresented in standard vocabulary tests, which may create an illusion that a reader has full word knowledge when they have only surface-level definitional access.
> 
> **See also:** [[Levels-...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[vocabulary-depth|Vocabulary Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Vocabulary Knowledge Depth** **[perm-note]**
> Vocabulary knowledge exists on a spectrum: from no knowledge of a word, through partial and contextual knowledge, to *rich, decontextualised knowledge* — the ability to use a word flexibly and precisely across varied contexts. Reading comprehension research distinguishes vocabulary *breadth* (how many words a reader knows at some level) from vocabulary *depth* (how thoroughly each word is known). Academic comprehension requires deep vocabulary knowledge of discipline-specific terms, not merely recognitional familiarity.
> 
> **Boundary:** Word frequency data from corpora consistently show that ...
> 
> *Source: developing-reading-comprehension-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08* | *Domain: cognitive science / reading comprehension*
> *Permanent Note: [[vocabulary-knowledge-depth|Vocabulary Knowledge Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Vocabulary Threshold Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically derived claim that fluent, unassisted comprehension of unsimplified text requires knowledge of approximately 95–98% of the running words in a passage. Below this threshold, comprehension breaks down because too many unknowns prevent the construction of a coherent [[situation-models-in-text-comprehension|situation model]]. For typical academic English texts, this corresponds to knowing the most frequent 8,000–9,000 word families.
> 
> **Boundary:** The threshold is not a fixed point but depends on text type, topic familiarity, and the reader's ability to tolerate ambiguity. Narra...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[vocabulary-threshold-hypothesis|Vocabulary Threshold Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Vocabulary-Comprehension Spiral** **[perm-note]**
> A bidirectional, mutually amplifying feedback architecture in which vocabulary knowledge and reading comprehension are dynamically coupled: vocabulary enables comprehension; comprehension quality determines the inferential context richness available for vocabulary acquisition; acquisition quality determines future vocabulary representations that support comprehension. The system has two stable equilibria: a positive spiral (high vocabulary → rich context → deep processing → richer vocabulary) and a negative spiral (thin vocabulary → impoverished context → shallow processing → minimal gain)....
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-acquisition-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Learning Science*
> *Permanent Note: [[vocabulary-comprehension-spiral|Vocabulary-Comprehension Spiral]]*

> [!definition] **Volitional Congruence** **[perm-note]**
> An alternative formulation of SDT's autonomy concept that emerges from this dialectical process. Rather than framing autonomy as self-authorship or independence, volitional congruence understands autonomy as *acting in ways that align with one's own values and relational commitments*. This formulation preserves the predictive content of SDT's autonomy need while making it coherent with collectivist cultural contexts where choosing to conform to one's group — when that choice is experienced as genuinely one's own — constitutes authentic autonomy rather than controlled compliance.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[volitional-congruence|Volitional Congruence]]*

## _W

> [!definition] **Walking-Reading Dual Task** **[perm-note]**
> [**Walking-Reading-Dual-Task**:: The concurrent performance of self-paced ambulatory locomotion and text comprehension, typically involving reading from a handheld device or printed material while walking through a familiar, low-hazard environment such as a home interior.]
> 
> *Source: walking-academic-reading-working-memory-deep-dive-2026-04-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[walking-reading-dual-task|Walking-Reading Dual Task]]*

> [!definition] **Weak-Sense vs. Strong-Sense Critical Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Weak-sense CT is the use of CT skills selectively, in service of one's own pre-existing beliefs and interests — deploying analytical tools to win arguments rather than to find truth. Strong-sense CT applies the same rigorous standards to one's own reasoning as to others', including the willingness to discover one's own errors and revise one's beliefs accordingly.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[weak-sense-vs.-strong-sense-critical-thinking|Weak-Sense vs. Strong-Sense Critical Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Weak-Sense-vs.-Strong-Sense-Critical-Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Distinction between deploying critical thinking tools to defend prior commitments (weak-sense) and applying them reflexively to one's own assumptions and identity-protective beliefs (strong-sense).
> 
> *Source: paul-elder-critical-thinking-framework-foundational-report-2026-04-01* | *Domain: critical thinking*
> *Permanent Note: [[weak-sense-vs.-strong-sense-critical-thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Whole-Task Approach** **[perm-note]**
> An instructional design philosophy that maintains the structural integrity of complex tasks throughout training, presenting learners with complete task experiences (albeit simplified and scaffolded) from the earliest stages of instruction, rather than decomposing complex tasks into isolated component skills that are taught and practiced separately before integration.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Whole-task" does not mean "full-complexity." The approach uses simplified-but-complete versions of tasks. The distinction is between structural simplification (reducing complexity while preserving integration) a...
> 
> *Source: four-component-instructional-design-model-4cid-foundational-report-2026-04-18* | *Domain: instructional design*
> *Permanent Note: [[whole-task-approach|Whole-Task Approach]]*

> [!definition] **Word Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> A multi-dimensional construct encompassing at minimum three knowledge types: *form knowledge* (spoken and written forms, morphological derivations), *meaning knowledge* (referential meaning, conceptual range, connotational range, associations), and *use knowledge* (grammatical constraints, collocational patterns, pragmatic restrictions on when and with whom the word is appropriate). Full mastery of a word requires competence across all dimensions; partial knowledge — knowing a word "somewhat" — is the normative condition.
> 
> **Boundary:** "Knowing a word" does not equate to recognition or def...
> 
> *Source: vocabulary-building-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-28* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[word-knowledge|Word Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Word-Length Effect** **[perm-note]**
> **Word-Length Effect**: The finding that fewer long words are recalled from a list than short words in immediate serial recall, when word frequency and phonological distinctiveness are controlled. The explanation: the articulatory rehearsal loop can cycle more short words than long words within the ~2-second window before phonological traces decay.
